# Crying wolf, crying foul, or crying shame: alien salmonids and a biodiversity crisis in the southern cool-temperate galaxioid ﬁshes?

R. M. McDowall

Received: 5 May 2004/Accepted: 22 August 2006/Published online: 29 November 2006 Springer Science+Business Media B.V. 2006

## Abstract

The galaxioid ﬁshes are the dominant, most speciose group of freshwater ﬁshes (with >50 species) in the lands of the cool southern hemisphere, with representatives in western and eastern Australia, Tasmania, New Caledonia, Lord Howe Island, New Zealand, the Chatham, Auckland and Campbell Islands, Patagonian South America (Chile, Argentina), the Falkland Islands and South Africa. The group is most diverse in Australia and New Zealand. Lepidogalaxiidae is found only in Australia, Retropinnidae in Australia and New Zealand, and Galaxiidae across the entire range of the group. Many species are in serious conservation crisis for a diversity of reasons, including habitat deterioration and possibly ﬁsheries exploitation, but there is enduring and pervasive information that shows that the group has been seriously impacted by the acclimatisation of salmonid ﬁshes originating in the cool-temperate northern hemisphere, particularly brown and rainbow trout. With few exceptions, where these trout have been introduced there has been major decline in the galaxioids, especially Galaxiidae, as a result of a complexly interacting series of adverse impacts from these introduced ﬁshes. In some places,

R. M. McDowall (&) National Institute of Water and Atmospheric Research, P.O. Box 8602, Christchurch, New Zealand e-mail: r.mcdowall@niwa.co.nz

centrarchids and cichlids may also have adverse impacts. In addition, there appear to have been adverse impacts from the translocation of galaxioids into communities where they do not naturally occur. In many instances it appears that displacement of the galaxioids has led to a situation where galaxioids and salmonids no longer co-occur, owing either to displacement or predation, leading to ﬁsh communities in which there is no explicit evidence for displacement. These effects are resulting in the galaxioid ﬁshes being amongst the most seriously threatened ﬁshes known.

Keywords Galaxiidae Retropinnidae Salmonidae Introductions Environmental impacts Australia New Zealand New Caledonia Patagonia Chile Argentina Falkland Islands South Africa

## Introduction

### The scope and relationships of the galaxioid ﬁshes

The galaxioid ﬁshes comprise the single most speciose element in the relatively sparse freshwater ﬁsh faunas of cool to cold temperate southern lands. About 56 species are presently recognised, with representatives recorded from southwestern

### Plate 1 Symbolic of the question of interactions of salmonids and galaxiids, a trout occupies drift-feeding space while agalaxiid peers from the shelter of the substrate (photo: Ken Miller)

and southeastern Australia, Tasmania, Lord Howe Island in the central Tasman Sea between eastern Australia and New Zealand, New Caledonia, New Zealand and some of its quite distant outlying islands such as the Chatham (650 km east of mainland New Zealand), Auckland (480 km south) and Campbell Islands (560 km south), Patagonian South America (Chile and Argentina), the Falkland Islands (Malvinas—about 600 km east of Patagonia), and the southern tip of South Africa (Fig. 1). Thus the family has a circum-temperate range that gives it very much the appearance of being an ancient Gondwanan taxon, the inclusion of southern Africa in the group’s range making it seem more ‘‘Gondwanan’’ than some other classic southern, circum-temperate groups like southern beech trees of the genus Nothofagus (Swenson et al.

- 2001) or some of the subfamilies of chironomid midges (Brundin 1966). For some time, it was also believed that there was an Indian galaxiid (Day 1888), making the group seem even more Gondwanan, though this is now discounted (McDowall 1973b).

Galaxioids are entirely absent from the tropics, and are largely absent from the sub-tropics. The single exception, New Caledonian Nesogalaxias neocaledonicus is the most tropical, being found only in a small lake at about 250 m elevation at the southern end of the island (a little south of 22 S—see Fig. 79). It is almost as if it is occupying a last, cool aquatic refuge on this ancient

continental island that was also once a part of Gondwana. Galaxioid ﬁshes are otherwise found largely south of about latitude 30 S, and their southernmost range extends well into the sub-Antarctic, including the Auckland and Campbell Islands at around latitudes 50–52 S, southern Patagonia (reaching latitude 54 S), also on the Falkland Islands at around 51 S, and the southern tip of Africa (Fig. 1). The galaxioid ﬁshes can thus be regarded as quintessential southern cooltemperate freshwater ﬁshes. The other distinctively southern and widespread freshwater ﬁsh groups are: the southern pouched lamprey, Geotria australis (f. Geotriidae), which is found in southwestern and southeastern Australia, Tasmania, New Zealand, the Chatham Islands and Patagonia; another southern lamprey family Mordaciidae, with species in Australia and Patagonia; and the percichthyid ﬁshes with several genera endemic to Australia (Macquaria, Maccullochella, Gadopsis, and perhaps Nannoperca and Nannatherina) and other genera endemic to Patagonia (Percichthys and Percilia) (Harris and Rowland 1996; Campos et al. 1998). McDowall and Lee (2005) reported ﬁsh scales from the Miocene of New Zealand that may well be percichthyids, the ﬁrst indication that this group was once present there.

The galaxioid ﬁshes have an ancient evolutionary history, being derived from amongst the lower euteleostean ﬁshes (McDowall 1969; Johnson and Patterson 1996; Williams 1996, 1997). There are

- Fig. 1 Distributions of the Salmonidae and Osmeridae in the Northern Hemisphere, and Galaxiidae, Retropinnidae, and Lepidogalaxiidae in the Southern Hemisphere

- Fig. 2 A Miocene Galaxias fossil from Central Otago, New Zealand

certain galaxiid fossils that date back into the Miocene (McDowall 1976b; McDowall and Pole 1997) (Fig. 2). Anderson (1998) described a ‘‘galaxiid’ from the Cretaceous of southern Africa, but I am dubious that it belongs in the Galaxiidae; if it does, it seems unlikely to lie close to the ancestry of modern Galaxiidae.

The galaxioid ﬁshes have excited a fair amount of interest amongst biologists. Earliest specimens were collected by naturalists with James Cook in New Zealand in 1773 (Begg and Begg 1969), while Darwin collected specimens from New Zealand, Patagonian South America, and the Falkland Islands on his travels with H.M.S. Beagle in the 1830s (Darwin 1896). In the sixth edition of the Origin of Species (Darwin 1873), Darwin discussed the broad southern distribution of the Galaxiidae as then known, and attributed this to their ability to live at sea for part of their lives, and thus to transoceanic dispersal, as did Boulenger (1902). And the group has continued to attract interest and controversy, its Gondwananlooking distribution attracting comment that the distribution pattern of the group dates back to Cretaceous or early Tertiary times, whereas the widespread presence of diadromy (regular migrations to and from the sea) has been taken to signify oceanic dispersal (McDowall 1964, 1978a,

- b, 2002c; Rosen 1974; Craw 1979; Campos 1984; Berra et al. 1995, 1996; Waters et al. 2001a, b).

There has long been debate about the scope of the galaxioid ﬁshes, and particularly about whether the various genera that are discussed in

this account form a monophyletic group, i.e. whether they share a closest common ancestry (Gosline 1960; McDowall 1969; Johnson and Patterson 1996; Williams 1996, 1997; Waters et al. 2000b). However, regardless of whether or not this is so, there is general agreement that all of the genera discussed here have a derivation among the lower euteleostean ﬁshes. The familial classiﬁcation and grouping of the galaxioid genera has been unstable for many years (Gosline 1960; McDowall 1969, 1984b; Fink 1984; Johnson and Patterson 1996; Williams 1997). Much of the debate centres around how many families ought to be recognised, and about which genera should be included in which families. These rather arcane questions do not have much bearing on the present paper, though it is necessary, for clarity, to adopt an explicit classiﬁcation here. Largely following the arrangement of Johnson and Patterson (1996), I recognise the following (most genera are shown in Fig. 3):

- 1. The family Retropinnidae comprises three genera: Retropinna (3 species—Fig. 3B), Stokellia (1), and Prototroctes (2 species—Fig. 3A); the family is restricted to Australia and New Zealand (Prototroctes is sometimes regarded as comprising a distinct family Prototroctidae, though having close afﬁnities with Retropinna—McDowall 1969, 1984b; Williams 1997).
- 2. The family Galaxiidae comprises eight genera: Galaxias (32 species—Fig. 3E, Neochanna (6—Fig. 3F, Nesogalaxias (1—Fig. 3H, Brachygalaxias (1—Fig. 3I), Galaxiella (3—Fig. 3J), Paragalaxias (4—Fig. 3G), Aplochiton (2—Fig. 3C), and Lovettia (1—Fig. 3D) (again, Aplochiton and Lovettia are sometimes regarded as comprising a distinct family Aplochitonidae, though there is no debate about whether Aplochiton and Lovettia have close afﬁnities with Galaxias—McDowall 1969; Williams 1997).
- 3. The family Lepidogalaxiidae includes only the monotypic Lepidogalaxias (Fig. 3K); its place among lower euteleosts and their evolutionary history are widely debated. Rosen’s (1974) treatment of Lepidogalaxias as a sister taxon of the otherwise entirely Northern Hemi-

- Fig. 3 The genera of galaxioid ﬁshes:

- (A) Prototroctes;
- (B) Retropinna;
- (C) Aplochiton;
- (D) Lovettia;
- (E) Galaxias;
- (F) Neochanna;
- (G) Paragalaxias;
- (H) Nesogalaxias;
- (I) Brachygalaxias;
- (J) Galaxiella;
- (K) Lepidogalaxias

sphere Esocidae is widely rejected (Fink 1984; Williams 1996; Johnson and Patterson 1996), but its placement as a sister taxon to Lovettia in a subfamily Lepidogalaxiinae, within the Galaxiidae (Johnson and Patterson 1996), is equally controversial. It was rejected on both morphological grounds (Williams 1997) and from molecular sequencing (Waters et al. 2002b), and is not accepted here. Nelson (2006) found the evidence presented by Johnson and Patterson (1996) to be strong, though I hold an alternative view (see below).

The broad, external phylogenetic relationships of the southern families are believed to be with a widely distributed co-ordinate group in the northern cool-temperate zone (especially the northern smelt family Osmeridae, but with somewhat more distant and less clear afﬁnities to the Salmonidae—the trouts, salmons, charrs and whiteﬁshes) (McDowall 1969; Johnson and Patterson 1996; Waters et al. 2002b). The northern family Esocidae (pikes) may also be involved in these rela-

tionships (Ramsden et al. 2003), despite rejection of afﬁnities between this family and Lepidogalaxias, discussed above. Galaxioids in the south, together with Salmonidae, Osmeridae and perhaps also Esocidae, in the north, thus form ‘antitropical’ (Hubbs 1952) or ‘bipolar’ groups of families (du Reitz 1940; Schuster 1969—Fig. 1). In view of these questions, it is logical to treat the galaxioids as a ‘group’, at least in the present conservation and biodiversity context.

A notable distinction between the distributions of the northern and southern groups is that the ranges of osmerids and salmonids in the north span vast areas of land (across Eurasia and North America) and are separated by lesser expanses of sea, whereas in the south, the galaxioids occupy relatively smaller areas of land (Australia, New Zealand, Patagonian South America, and Africa) that are separated by huge expanses of sea (Fig. 1).

These northern and southern families are quite similar ecological analogues, a distinctive and interesting aspect of the biology of all groups being the widespread presence of diadromy (reg-

ular migrations to and from the sea—McDowall (1988), and discussed in some detail below). This feature is pervasive across galaxioids, osmerids and salmonids, and may be an ancient and ancestral character in the wider group (McDowall 1997a, 2002a).

Conservation concern for the galaxioid ﬁshes

Among freshwater ﬁshes as a whole, the galaxioids have attracted an unusually high degree of conservation concern. In a global context, this small group of around 56 recognised species, tucked away in the often remote, cool southern temperate regions of the world, have been the focus of considerable attention from ichthyologists, ﬁsheries biologists, biogeographers and conservationists. There could be a number of reasons for the high level of conservation concern, including one or more of the following:

- 1. The perceived conservation threats may be real, in which case the threat classiﬁcations of the galaxioid ﬁshes are an appropriate response to their conservation status;
- 2. The group could be attracting a particularly high level of research interest, so that the group is becoming especially well known, and simply for that reason their conservation status is well understood—as is true of the northern Salmonidae (trouts, salmons and charrs); this level of concern applies also the Acipenseridae (sturgeons—Birstein 1993). Salmonidae and Acipenseridae are generating conservation concerns, probably owing in part to species in both families having high cultural/ﬁsheries importance. This does not apply to the same extent to the galaxioids (though see Blackburn 1950; McDowall and Eldon 1980; Fulton and Pavuk 1988). Lesser conservation concern may derive from the locally distinctive nature of ﬁsheries supported by these species, and substantially by the fact that only a few of the species in the group have ﬁsheries value. Several commentators suggest that well-known taxonomic or geographical assemblages of species tend to attract regarding their conservation. Wager and Jackson (1993) argued that, in Australia,

the level of threat tends to correlate with human population levels partly because that is where the greatest threat is identiﬁed, combined with that tending also to be where the most study has been undertaken. Moreover, high conservation concern could also be due to galaxioid biologists being passionate about their group of interest, and therefore more likely to notice declines and respond actively to perceived threats. A further aspect may be that statutory/legislative measures, and their implementation, may differ widely in the various countries where galaxioid ﬁshes are found—though it is perhaps ironic that New Zealand, where the ﬁshes are comparatively well known, provides no statutory protection for them apart from constraints on the harvesting of juvenile galaxiids as they return from the sea, as part of the country’s whitebait ﬁshery (McDowall 1984a).

If it is accepted that the threat status of the galaxioids is as serious as the various conservation listings indicate (discussed below), then there is cause for major concern. Because the group is relatively well known, it is possible to explore the reasons for conservation concern in some detail, especially in relation to local extirpations or declines, and also in the context of published evaluations of both local and broad-scale conservation issues. It is the purpose of the present account to place the apparent conservation/biodiversity crisis in the galaxioid ﬁshes in a broad perspective:

- • First, to explore its ‘setting’ within the context of freshwater biodiversity;
- • Second, to examine whether concerns about the galaxioids are well founded;
- • Third, to seek common explanations or ecological themes that may contribute to an understanding of the group’s conservation status.

Freshwater ﬁsh conservation in a global context and the question of invasive species

Invasiveness of exotic species into novel ecosystems is a growing global issue that has increas-

ingly been attracting the attention of ecologists and conservationists—who are repeatedly warning that alien species are a major biodiversity issue being faced in our generation. Invasiveness ranks at least equally with habitat degradation and exploitation among factors leading to biodiversity decline, local extirpation and global extinction. As Vitousek et al. (1995) put it, the ‘‘world’s mobile society is redistributing the species on earth at a pace that challenges ecosystems, threatens human health, and strains economies...The economic consequences are enormous, but ecological consequences may dwarf them’’.

Crosby (1986) described the introduction of continental/European biota into Southern Hemisphere lands as ‘‘biological imperialism’’, whereas Soule´ (1991) referred to species introductions and translocations as ‘‘homogenisation’’ of the global biota. Lodge (1993) listed 13 volumes on the biology of invasions published since 1973, and the interest and literature have grown rapidly since that time (Kolar and Lodge 2001; Sax et al. 2005). Introduced species that become seriously invasive are increasingly being recognised as global contributors to the decline of native species (Vitousek et al. 1997; Low 2002; Sakai et al. 2001; Kolar and Lodge 2001; Lee 2002), and many commentators have regarded invasive species as a major contributor to contemporary increase in rates of species’ extinctions. There is reference to these extinctions as a biodiversity crisis, or the third great extinction episode in earth history (Brown 1989; Pimm 2001). Fausch et al. (2001) described biological invasions as a ‘‘global crisis second only to habitat losses in causing species declines and endangerment, world-wide’’. But these are not new ideas. After Charles Darwin was in New Zealand in 1838, he referred to the extent to which British species were already invading the countryside (Darwin 1896). Much more recently, but still long ago, Charles Elton (1958) asserted that: ‘‘We must make no mistake: we are seeing one of the great historical convulsions in the world’s fauna and ﬂora’’. Simberloff

- (2001) reminded us of Elton’s prophetic warning, and noted that ‘‘not many people cared, not even ecologists’’, though some did (McDowall 1968c). Writing >30 years later than Elton, Brown (1989)

summarised that invaders have directly caused or indirectly contributed to extinction of native species, and have substantially changed the structure and dynamics of both natural and humanmodiﬁed ecosystems. Cassey and Arthington (1999) cited Welcomme (1992) as arguing that less than 10% of introductions did not meet the objectives for which they were introduced, or had shown sufﬁcient environmental impact to be pests, but they argued that Welcomme’s ﬁgure could have been ‘‘an artefact of the [broad] scale of most studies’’. Some of the movement of species around the world is as unintentional ‘‘baggage’’ (Vitousek et al. 1997). Also, there is a surprising amount of deliberate but clandestine shifting of ﬁsh, both internationally and locally (McDowall 1990b, 2004b; Arthington et al. 1999; McDowall and James 2005).

Much of conservation biology has tended to focus primarily on the biodiversity of terrestrial biomes (Leidy and Moyle 1998), neglecting aquatic biota. Wager and Jackson (1993) stated that in Australia ‘‘ﬁsh rate poorly on the public list of conservation priorities, at least compared to the larger, warm-blooded mammals’’. The current ‘biodiversity crisis’ (Wilson 1992; Cracraft and Griffo 1999) also involves rapidly growing conservation concerns for freshwater ecosystems and organisms, including freshwater ﬁshes (Allan and Flecker 1993; Stiassny 1996, 1999; McAllister et al. 1997). Cambray (2003a) described ﬁsh introductions as among the ‘‘most insidious threats to ﬁsh conservation...Alien invasive recreational ﬁsh species are now recognised as a global environmental degradation problem’’, while Vitousek et al. (1995) asserted that, ‘‘especially for birds and ﬁshes’’ introduction to new ecosystems ‘‘typically requires a greater degree of human intervention.... Nonetheless isolated islands often have more non-native species of ﬁsh than native ﬁshes’’. Englund and Eldredge (2001) report that at least 60 species have been introduced to Hawaiian waters, with 11 alien species being added to the ﬁve indigenous species. Clearly, with human interest in freshwater ﬁshing, the impacts of discarded aquarium ﬁsh, and some people’s ﬂawed idea that certain exotic ﬁshes are crucial for controlling mosquitoes, freshwater ﬁshes are spreading rapidly around the globe. A key ele-

ment in this issue relates to attitudes of ﬁsheries managers to introduced species. Dill and Cordone (1997) found that ‘‘with only a few exceptions, most of the so-called ﬁsh experts (listed as ‘‘scientists and hatchery professionals’’) supported introductions until recently. Of 53 species introduced to California, 25 were introduced by California’s Fish and Game Commission, and so that the state exhibited ‘‘greater biodiversity’’ than before; however, this ignored loss of endemics and disruption of native ecosystems. According to Moyle and Leidy (1992), 20% of global freshwater ﬁsh species were at risk of extinction but they soon afterwards described this ﬁgure as ‘‘probably very conservative’’. Duncan and Lockwood (2001) were rather more conservative, estimating that ‘‘Globally, evidence suggests that we will likely lose at least 4% of all known ﬁsh species in the near future’’ (see also Leidy and Moyle 1998). Stiassny (1996) thought that 27–35% of North America freshwater ﬁshes (arguably the best known large continental freshwater ﬁsh fauna in the world) are at risk. Some other countries were much worse, Stiassny (1996) citing 66% for freshwater ﬁsh in West Germany, 65% in Spain, and 42% across all of Europe, being at risk. This loss may have diverse drivers, including habitat deterioration, ﬁsheries exploitation, and the impacts of introduced species, though their relative roles have not been quantiﬁed.

Sheldon (1988) discussed the way isolated freshwater habitats in rivers and lakes resemble islands, and concerns for freshwater ecosystems led Stiassny (1996, 1999; Harrison and Stiassny

- 1999) to apply this analogy to issues of freshwater ﬁsh conservation, i.e. that both islands and freshwater bodies tend to be highly vulnerable to ecological perturbation and decline. Vitousek

- et al. (1997) argued that invasions of aquatic and wetland ecosystems are just as severe in their effects as island invasions. And, of course, the comparison is apt in the sense that freshwater habitats tend to be relatively small aquatic habitat ‘islands’ isolated in a ‘sea’ of ‘land’, just as islands are small terrestrial habitats isolated in an expanse of ocean (Magnuson 1976, used this allusion speciﬁcally for lakes, but it applies no less to rivers). The limited expanse of freshwater habitats may mean limited population size, there-

by increasing vulnerability to extirpation. Strong geographical isolation at the local, water-body scale can have serious conservation consequences, as small and isolated populations can suffer declines driven by stochastic effects, both ecological and genetic. Riverine populations tend to be genetically isolated within river systems, and are often small. And freshwater habitats have suffered severe impacts throughout human history, their vulnerability exacerbated by their often small size and isolation.

Damaging environmental impacts on freshwater bodies and biotas are becoming increasingly severe (McAllister et al. 1997). As the global human population rises and industrialisation intensiﬁes, especially in third world countries:

- • Catchments of rivers and lakes are increasingly developed for human habitation and for pastoral and horticultural production;
- • Landscapes formerly covered with an ancient sward of trees and herbs are now covered with cities, and their associated air and water pollution;
- • Water is heavily abstracted from rivers and lakes, for human use and for irrigating pastures and crops;
- • Ever-more rivers are impounded to meet ever-growing demand for electricity, especially in Europe and North America, but increasingly in Asia;
- • Fish and other species are translocated into new ecosystems ill-adapted to accepting such invasions.

These impacts intensify, especially:

- • As fresh water becomes increasingly scarce (plus potential impacts of global climate change); and also
- • With high human exploitation of freshwater ﬁshes (Lichatowich 1999).

River systems are often used as dumping grounds that transport toxic materials away from centres of human settlement. Damaging upstream effects are conveyed to downstream habitats in rivers owing to water ﬂowing along linear riverine channels. The tendency for human communities to use rivers for waste disposal is increasingly

creating serious problems for those same human communities, as well as:

- • For human communities downstream of those disposing of efﬂuents;
- • For the biota of rivers (the present concern); and eventually
- • For marine biotas near river mouths and beyond.

The assumption that ocean ecosystems can cope with unlimited quantities of human/industrial discharges into rivers and their transport to sea may be wrong, and it may be too late for effective remediation.

Impacts of these changes on freshwater ﬁsh species could have been predicted as severe, and they have been. Baillie and Groombridge (1996) listed 734 ‘ﬁshes’ globally as Seriously Threatened and another 358 as Lower Risk Data Deﬁcient, making a total of 1,092 ﬁshes that are causing serious conservation concerns. It could be argued that the high number of threatened ﬁshes is simply a consequence of the large number of species known, e.g., the number of ﬁshes about equals the total number of all other vertebrates together—Nelson 2006). By the time the IUCN list had been reviewed, the total of globally Seriously Threatened species (those classed as Critically Threatened, Endangered and Vulnerable) had risen to 801 (IUCN 2006). However, such comparisons do not allay concerns for their conservation status. Most listed species are freshwater ﬁshes, though perhaps the high level of concern for freshwater ﬁshes is due, in part, to their habitats being accessible and vulnerable to modiﬁcation by an increasing human population.

Grappling with the threat of biodiversity decline and extinction

There is clearly global concern about biodiversity loss in all taxonomic groups, the rising rate at which this is happening, and apparently increasing ‘knowledge gaps’ between what is and what needs to be known for effective conservation. Skelton (2002) considered that one of the practical problems for ﬁsh conservation is constitutional at the country level: ‘‘A

constitution that defends the environment is fundamental for conservation to take root and be embraced by society.... In an open and enlightened society conservation values must move beyond economic justiﬁcation’’, and this of course raises questions involving conﬂicts of interest. Skelton (2002) wrote of ‘‘collusion’’ between state and private sectors ‘‘to introduce alien predatory ﬁsh species for inland recreational ﬁsheries... under an ethical framework of autocratically administered preservationist conservation... which collapsed when the impact of the introduced aliens was too large to be ignored by the conservation agencies’’, and of course by that time most of the damage has been done, introductions are not reversible and there seems a paradox, that the better a species becomes known, the more is known about its perilous conservation status, and the greater the identiﬁed risks of losses. There are many pitfalls, among the chief of these being our limited knowledge of the biodiversity that we seek to conserve—as perhaps illustrated by recent rapid increase in the number of species thought to be threatened. Among the galaxioids, some of this increase relates to our improved understanding of species diversity and phylogenetic relationships through the use of molecular techniques (Watts et al. 1995; McDowall and Wallis 1996; McDowall 1997b; Waters and Cambray 1997; McDowall and Chadderton 1999; Raadik 2002; Raadik and Kuiter 2002; McDowall and Waters 2002, 2003—see details in next section).

The question of knowing

It seems to me that there exists a series of general ‘knowledge states’, the recognition of which has sometimes profound implications for what is, or needs to be, done, with regard to threat assessment, mitigation, and pro-active conservation management. These knowledge states can be characterised as:

1. Informed understanding—when enough is known of a species’ ecology and its conservation status, to institute effective protective or mitigative measures—though this seems a rather rare ideal.

- 2. Misinformed understanding—this applies when we think we know enough to provide protection for species or to enable effective mitigation of adverse changes to aquatic ecosystems, but we are actually deluded; this misplacedconﬁdencemaybecomeexceedingly dangerous for actual conservation outcomes.
- 3. Informed ignorance—when we are aware that our knowledge is seriously deﬁcient and that we are not in a position to institute explicit conservation protocols; the best conservation approach is probably then to apply the ‘precautionary principle’, if that can be effected. This principle urges adoption and application of conservation strategies where knowledge is insufﬁcient to make information-driven decisions but where a failure to act may have seriously adverse consequences, i.e. the imperative is to ‘do something’ when ‘doing nothing’ could be catastrophic.
- 4. Uninformed ignorance—when not enough is known to even know that we do not know enough to make conservation judgements, so that there is no known imperative to institute conservation protocols, even based on the precautionary principle; this is a state of ‘innocent’ ignorance that is more dangerous than misinformed understanding.

Issues of life history strategies of the galaxioid ﬁshes

As background to later discussion, it is critical to understand that galaxioid ﬁshes exhibit two distinctly (and importantly) different life history strategies: some species are diadromous and others are not. Diadromy, which involves reciprocal, often obligatory migrations between fresh water and the sea (McDowall 1988), is widely represented across the group. Diadromous migrations have multiple implications for conservation (McDowall 1992a, 1996a, b, 1998a, 1999a) on several grounds.

1. Diadromous species tend to be more widespread than non-diadromous congenerics (McDowall 2002c);

- 2. They tend, also, to occupy freshwater habitats at lower elevations and nearer the sea (McDowall 1998a, 1999a);
- 3. Upstream penetration varies widely among diadromous species and depends, in part, on individual species’ migratory capabilities and instincts;
- 4. Migration means that access up- and downstream in rivers must be available for species to complete their life cycles; these migrations are easily (and often) impeded by perturbations such as construction of dams and weirs, river mouth closures, and dewatering of river reaches, and so have special conservation implications; connectivity is a major conservation issue for diadromous ﬁshes (McDowall 1996a, b, 1998a, 1999b, 2000c);
- 5. Movements of diadromous species through the sea facilitates reinvasion of riverine habitats subject to major perturbation and defaunation, once that perturbation is no longer active, and so has profound implications for both breadth of distributions, and for recovery of ﬁsh populations/communities after perturbation ceases (McDowall 1996a);
- 6. The ecological phenomenon of ‘sources and sinks’ (Pulliam 1988; Dunning et al. 1992), in which reproductively positive sites (‘sources’) contribute progeny to ecologically negative sites (‘sinks’), is a mechanism by which the conservation status of diadromous species may be seriously compromised (McDowall 1999a).

Among diadromous species, migration may be facultative, and establishment of ‘landlocked’ populations is, variously, occasional to common (this facultativeness seems to be a species-distinctive characteristic). At least one retropinnid and six galaxiids are facultatively diadromous and can establish landlocked populations (McDowall 1970, 1979, 1988, 1990b; Northcote and Ward 1985; Northcote et al. 1992). In particular, Galaxias brevipinnis, in both Tasmania and New Zealand, has large numbers of landlocked populations in sub-montane lakes (McDowall 2003d). In this species there is a strong tendency for landlocked populations to undertake ontogenetically related migrations between pelagic waters

of lakes and the lakes’ tributaries (McDowall 1984a, 1990b), a pattern that closely replicates migrations of diadromous populations that move between the ocean and rivers. The implications of landlocking for conservation are discussed below. Other diadromous galaxioids are obligatorily diadromous, and this increases risks of extirpation resulting from habitat perturbation, especially where migration is impeded.

In addition to recognising that diadromy is widespread among galaxioids, it is important to understand that these migrations take several forms (Fig. 4). Some diadromous ﬁsh species are anadromous. They undertake most feeding and growth at sea, prior to migration of mature adults into fresh water to reproduce; these migratory adults either do not feed in fresh water, or any feeding is accompanied by little somatic growth. Larvae or juveniles migrate to sea at an age varying from a few hours to several years. The principal feeding and growth biome (the sea) is different from the reproductive biome (fresh water). Examples are northern lampreys (f. Petromyzontidae), sturgeons (f. Acipenseridae), salmons and trouts (f. Salmonidae), at a global scale, and southern lampreys (f. Geotriidae and

Mordaciidae) and southern smelts (f. Retropinnidae) in the cool Southern Hemisphere.

Some species are amphidromous. They breed in fresh water, and the newly hatched larvae migrate immediately to sea on hatching; they undertake early feeding and growth at sea, and then the postlarvae/well-grown juveniles (usually 20–50 mm long) return to fresh water. There is then prolonged feeding in fresh water, during which most somatic growth (juvenile to adult) occurs, as well as maturation and reproduction. There is rarely migration related to reproduction, and if there is, it is not between freshwater and the sea; in this case the principal feeding and growth biome is the same as the reproductive biome (fresh water). Examples at the global scale are sicydiine gobies (f. Gobiidae), and in the Southern Hemisphere some galaxiids (f. Galaxiidae).

Some species are catadromous. Most feeding and growth are in fresh water, prior to migration of fully grown adult ﬁsh to sea, to reproduce and in some instances die (anguillid eels); there is either no feeding after pre-spawning adults migrate to sea, or feeding is accompanied by little somatic growth. Well-grown juveniles migrate into freshwater. The principal feeding and

- Fig. 4 Patterns of migration and life history in the three forms of diadromy

growing biome (fresh water) differs from the reproductive biome (the sea). Examples at the global scale, including the cool Southern Hemisphere, are freshwater eels (f. Anguillidae) and some mullets (f. Mugilidae).

Conservation issues to consider regarding diversity of life history include:

- • The nature of the conservation response differs for diadromous species, in part, according to which form of diadromy is involved (McDowall 1988, 1999a). Though differentiating the various forms of diadromy is important for instituting effective conservation protocols, in all instances and types of diadromy: We need to address both upstream and downstream migrations;
- • We need to know the life history stages of individuals undertaking those migrations;
- • We must know the timing of migrations and perhaps the cues to migration; and
- • We must know something of the characteristics of migrating life stages to understand the nature of factors that may hinder or prevent migration, and so also understand species’ likely responses to perturbation, whether natural or man-made.

In that context, upstream migration is more likely to be perturbed than downstream migration—upstream migration barriers are often not barriers to downstream migration, though they may be. However, downstream migrants may be diverted out of river systems by abstraction of water, and carried to localities where life is threatened and completion of the life history is jeopardised. Moreover, adverse situations for ﬁsh migrating downstream have probably also created prior problems for upstream migration and, of course, if upstream migration is impeded, it is likely that there will be no life stages living upstream of such barriers to migrate downstream. Focus, then, may need to be, at least initially on facilitating upstream passage. A holistic approach is needed, which considers patterns of migration and the habitats used, and ensures unimpeded passage between them.

Upstream migrating life stages of anadromous species are always adults (by deﬁnition). Anad-

romous galaxioids are several species of retropinnid smelt (Retropinna retropinna, R. tasmanica and Stokellia anisodon), and the aplochitonine Lovettia sealii. None of these species typically penetrates far upstream, some moving little beyond the tidal estuaries. Upstream migrating life stages of amphidromous and catadromous galaxioid species are always small juveniles, ca. 20–55 mm long, and upstream penetration is sometimes substantial (hundreds of kilometres). Some Galaxias species, especially G. brevipinnis, G. fasciatus and G. postvectis in New Zealand, may penetrate hundreds of kilometres upstream, and they can climb high falls (many tens of metres—see Fig. 90) utilising splash-margins of falls (Harper 1896; McDowall 1990b).

The beginnings of conservation concern for the galaxioid ﬁshes

Leidy and Moyle (1998) thought that, apart from North America and Europe, the ‘‘status of most ﬁsh species remains poorly understood’’. In North America and Europe they summarised that species most threatened with extinction were: (1) specialised for life in larger rivers; (2) lacustrine or in inland seas; (3) found in arid freshwater areas; (4) dependent on coastal and estuarine habitats; and (5) localised endemics. Surprisingly, they omitted mention of migration, and clearly had not looked at literature from Australia and New Zealand where freshwater ﬁsh faunas are well known and migration is a common attribute of species (Merrick and Schmida 1984; McDowall 1990b, 2000a; Allen et al. 2002; Pusey et al. 2004).

Concern about conservation of galaxioid ﬁshes began many years ago. The imperilled status of the now extinct New Zealand grayling, Prototroctes oxyrhynchus, was recognised by the late 1800s (Graham 1953) and it was probably extinct by 1930 (McDowall 1990b). It was realised by the 1920s that trouts (f. Salmonidae), which were introduced from the Northern Hemisphere extensively to the cool Southern Hemisphere, and some galaxiids in Australia, were developing complementary distributions. Galaxiids were being found only upstream of falls that prevented upstream invasion by trout (Gray 1929); and ‘‘In

many streams in Tasmania (as elsewhere) the Galaxiidae have been wholly or largely displaced by the introduced Salmonidae’’ (Whitley 1935, citing EOG Scott).

In New Zealand, local ﬁsheries authorities were obsessed with expanding the ranges of introduced salmonids, and concerns about trout impacts on the native biota did not develop until the 1960s (McDowall 1968c, 1978a, 1990a, b, 2003b; Townsend and Crowl 1991; Crowl et al. 1992; Allibone and McIntosh 1999; Townsend 2003). Policy and practice with regard to introduction and liberation of salmonids (Fig. 5) in New Zealand had no regard for their ecosystem effects. In part this was due to the process being managed by amateurs, with minimal input from ﬁsheries biologists (McDowall 1994a)—not that the few New Zealand professionals exhibited much interest in native ﬁsh.

Similar attitudes prevailed in Australia. Cadwallader (1978), for instance, wrote that ‘‘incessant liberations of trout by the State ﬁsheries agencies of New South Wales and Victoria [have been] the most prevalent form of species pollution in the Murray–Darling River system...trout have been liberated into almost all waters thought to be suitable for them [and probably many that are not], but unfortunately in most instances little information is available on the native fauna before the trout introductions were made’’. The same seems to have happened in Patagonia, though the process was even less well documented. But, Pascual et al. (2002) described the faunas of southern Patagonian lakes and as rivers

- Fig. 5 Early liberations of trout into New Zealand streams by acclimatisation society ofﬁcials

‘‘poorly known and ...presently dominated by several species of [introduced] salmonids (the exclusive focus of signiﬁcant recreational ﬁsheries) ...documentation on the impacts that salmonids have had on native [ﬁsh] communities of Patagonia is scarce and largely inconclusive’’. And they thought that ‘‘Even when there are good reasons to believe that salmonids have had signiﬁcant effects on the native freshwater biota, the lack of an articulate vision of how virgin communities might have looked before introductions seriously compromises the proposal of coherent conservation goals and actions...the current management scheme simply leaves native ﬁsh out of the picture’’. All of these circumstances closely resemble what has been applicable almost throughout the temperate world (and also the sub-tropics), with salmonid ﬁsheries managers and anglers ‘‘caught up in the frenzy of acclimatization’’ (Dill and Cordone 1997, and see McDowall [1994a], for an account of acclimatisation of exotic ﬁsh species in New Zealand). Introductions continue over a wide geographical front comprising, as Moyle (1997) cynically put it, ‘‘...new introductions … still being made for the same old reasons’’.

The passion for salmonid introductions continues to be an almost global issue, as indicated by French releases of brown trout (Salmo trutta), Atlantic salmon (S. salar), rainbow trout (Oncorhynchus mykiss), Chinook salmon (O. tshawytscha), coho salmon (O. kisutch), and brook charr (Salvelinus fontinalis) on remote Kerguelen Island in the southern Indian Ocean, since 1955 (Sagar 1986; Davaine and Beall 1997), and no-one even lives there permanently! There has been persistent interest also in the Falkland Islands (Hall et al. 1979; Stewart 1973), where impacts of brown trout are of serious conservation concern (McDowall et al. 2001, 2005).

Questions of galaxioid taxonomy at the species level

The capacity to plausibly list and rank species for conservation concern depends heavily on thoroughly understanding a group’s taxonomy. If some of the actual biodiversity is obscured by inclusion of distinct taxa within species, or

if distinct evolutionarily signiﬁcant units (ESUs—Waples 1994) are not recognised, there is a danger that appropriate conservation attention may not be given to threatened but unrecognised taxa/ESUs. Leidy and Moyle (1998) raised the prospect that some widely distributed species may represent groups of species.

That introductions of alien species are a potential or actual threat among the galaxioid ﬁshes is becoming increasingly clear. The advent of molecular methods for identifying species or lineages within groups has had major impacts on our understanding of galaxioid biodiversity, and these techniques, combined with ecological studies, are revealing hitherto unrecognised diversity. There are several explicit examples of newly identiﬁed species in the galaxioid ﬁshes. One relates to the Australian form known as Galaxias olidus, within which McDowall and Frankenberg (1981) brought together a series of previously poorly deﬁned taxa. One species among these was Galaxias fuscus. Now, however, there is a strong conviction among Australian ﬁsh biologists, (contrary to McDowall and Frankenberg’s 1981 taxonomic treatment), that G. fuscus is a distinct species—hence preparation of a G. fuscus recovery plan (Raadik 1995) and the species’ inclusion in the ‘‘Threatened species of the world’’ series in the journal Environmental Biology of Fishes (Raadik et al. 1996). Raadik (1995) reported G. fuscus in sympatry with G. olidus, which is strong evidence of G. fuscus being distinct.

In addition, ongoing Australian studies are showing that McDowall and Frankenberg’s

- (1981) ‘‘G. olidus’’ probably incorporates even further distinct species (Raadik 2001; Raadik and Kuiter 2002). There may be other examples among Australian galaxioids, e.g., the demonstration that the Western Australian Galaxias occidentalis comprises two very distinct genetic groups (Watts et al.

- 1995) that may warrant recognition as distinct species. Mainland Australian retropinnids, currently referred to as Retropinna semoni (McDowall 1979), may also consist of a series of presently undescribed lineages revealed by molecular studies (M. Hammer pers. comm.)

Similar discoveries of hidden taxonomic diversity, substantially driven by molecular studies (though morphology and ecology are also

involved), are further advanced in New Zealand. The galaxiid fauna there has increased from 13 species recognised in a family-level revision about 30 years ago (McDowall 1970), to presently 22 species (McDowall 2000a; McDowall and Waters 2002, 2003). In particular, G. vulgaris (as perceived by McDowall 1970) has been subdivided into six species, with several more lineages, suggested by molecular studies, awaiting formal recognition (Waters and Wallis 2001a, b; McDowall and Hewitt 2003). Molecular studies (Gleeson et al. 1999a; Ling et al. 2001) are also suggesting that there may be more than one genetic lineage among the few populations of G. gracilis in lakes of Northland, New Zealand, implying unrecognised taxonomic diversity among populations of that species (McDowall 1990b, 2001). As well, what has become known as the ‘pencil galaxias’ species in New Zealand has increased from three to ﬁve species as an outcome of morphological and molecular studies (McDowall and Waters 2002, 2003).

Similar issues may exist in Patagonian South America, especially with regard to G. platei (McDowall 1971b). Moreover, treatment of the Chilean Brachygalaxias gothei as a junior synonym of B. bullocki by Berra et al. (1995) is inconsistent with evidence from genetics and hybridisation (Cuevas et al. 1999; Klaus Busse pers. comm.); thus B. bullocki as recognised by Berra et al. (1995) may also constitute a species complex.

Similar issues of undescribed diversity are clearly true of the presently single South African species, G. zebratus (Waters and Cambray 1997). It seems to comprise several undescribed species with multiple instances of sympatry of two or more lineages (Skelton 2002; McDowall 2001, and unpublished); pending molecular studies may reveal even more diversity, if the pattern in New Zealand and Australia is repeated in South Africa.

Given the above, our understanding of galaxioid taxonomic diversity clearly remains deﬁcient, despite (or perhaps because of), substantial, intensive, recent attention, and there is scope for conservation concern to be based on ‘uninformed ignorance’. Issues for conservation cannot even be identiﬁed, let alone clariﬁed, until the taxonomy is better deﬁned. Earlier taxonomies

were based on understanding that rapidly increasing knowledge is showing was deﬁcient—the extent of our ignorance is increasingly being revealed, and we are hopefully moving from a state of ‘informed ignorance’ to one of ‘informed understanding’.

How is conservation risk to be assessed

The question of assessing the relative levels of conservation concern among the galaxioid ﬁshes can be addressed in a number of ways:

- 1. Listings by the International Union for the Conservation of Nature. The deﬁnitive IUCN listing of threatened species (the last print issue of the list by Baillie and Groombridge 1996, and a web-based list that was last amended—2004 IUCN Red List of Threatened Species: <www.redlist.org>, IUCN

2006) included 26 galaxioid species under various categories of concern (Table 1), or 62% of the 42 galaxioids recognised when that list was assembled. Figure 6 plots the number of species per family against the number of threatened species per family across the entire diversity of ﬁshes. The Galaxiidae (arrowed) sits close to the margins of a line that would establish the upper boundary of the plot. Thus, a greater proportion of galaxiid species is of conservation concern than in all but ﬁve other ﬁsh families, and most of these have fewer species than the Galaxiidae. In only one larger family is a greater proportion of the species of conservation concern than galaxiids. Duncan and Lockwood (2001) concluded that galaxiids are among the families with ‘‘more threatened species than expected’’, listing it eighth after Acipenseridae, Belontidae, Salmonidae, Percidae, Cichlidae, Adrianichthyidae and Goodeidae. Some of these highly threatened families are small, or monotypic, or their species may tend to be more highly threatened simply because they are specialised/ idiosyncratic ﬁshes whose welfare is easily threatened, e.g. cave ﬁshes, Amblyopsidae.

- 2. Inclusion in the ‘‘Threatened species of the world’’ series in the journal Environmental

Biology of Fishes (EBF). Over the past decade or so, EBF has run a series of ‘onepage’ accounts that discuss the conservation status of ﬁsh species generating concern (Bruton 1995; Cambray 2000). With publication of volume 64 of the journal, the number of ‘threatened species’ accounts reached 126, of which 12 (9.5%) dealt with galaxioid ﬁshes. This seems a disproportionately high number of galaxioid species, given that the group comprises only ca. 56 species or about 0.2% of a ﬁsh diversity of around 25,000 species (Nelson 2006). Since most taxa in the EBF series are freshwater ﬁshes, there would similarly appear to be too many galaxioids creating conservation concern among freshwater ﬁsh, in relation to a proportion of the family’s taxonomic diversity.

I recognise that the EBF series does not pretend to provide proportional representation of globally threatened freshwater ﬁshes. And there are undoubtedly many threatened freshwater ﬁsh species in groups that are not- or are under-represented, in the EBF series. For example, the massive freshwater ﬁsh extinction events in Lake Victoria in central Africa, Lake Lanao in the Philippines, and Lake Titicaca in Peru (summarised in Harrison and Stiassny 1999) are not represented in the EBF series. Bruton (1995) reckoned that 289 cichlids were threatened, 252 of them from Lake Victoria alone. Sturgeons (f. Acipenseridae) are clearly in dire conservation crisis, and yet only 10 of the 24 species (42%) are included in the EBF series, far fewer than would justify inclusion (Birstein 1993). Leidy and Moyle (1998), for instance, considered that ‘‘25 of 27 species of sturgeons and paddleﬁshes (Acipenseridae) are threatened’’. The above issues aside, it remains true that 12 species in the galaxioids are of such conservation concern that they have been included in the EBF threatened species series. Additional species may warrant listing (Baillie and Groombridge 1996).

3. Regional summaries of conservation status and concern. Most countries that have galaxioid ﬁshes have been reviewing the conservation status of their freshwater ﬁshes.

This has been especially true in Australia, where the Australian Society for Fish Biology undertakes annual reviews of threatened species lists, and has held workshops on the subject. In addition, Australian state ﬁsheries and conservation jurisdictions have been very active in assigning conservation status rankings to species, preparing species recovery plans, and ensuring their inclusion in all sorts of conservation protocols spanning ﬁsheries biology, water management, and forestry practices (see Gaffney et al. 1992; Sanger 1993; Raadik 1995, 2002; Crook and Sanger 1997; Crook 2001; Crook and Pogonoski 2002, 2003; Threatened Species Unit 2005). New Zealand has also undertaken active review of the conservation status of freshwater ﬁshes, has ranked them for threat (Williams and Given 1981; Molloy and Davis 1992; Eldon 1993; Rowe 1994; Tisdall 1994; Hitchmough 2002); and is increasingly preparing recovery plans (Barrier 2003; Allibone and Barrier 2004; Department of Conservation 2005).

An Australian list (Crook and Pogonoski 2003) expressed some conservation concern for 16 of 25 Australian galaxioids (64%—2 critically endangered, 7 endangered, 4 vulnerable, 3 lower risk, near threatened). In an early New Zealand list, galaxioids made up 11 (55%) of the 20 taxa then recognised as threatened (Tisdall 1994). More recently (Hitchmough 2002), 15 of 22 formally

Table 1continued

Lepidogalaxiidae

Lepidogalaxias

Paragalaxias

Paragalaixas

Paragalaxias

named New Zealand taxa were a cause for conservation concern (68%). After including additional undescribed New Zealand galaxiid lineages, the ﬁgure becomes 19 of 26 (73%). The situation seems less clear in Patagonian South America (Pascual et al. 2002). However, the entire (100%) galaxioid fauna of Patagonian Chile was of conservation concern in Campos et al. (1998), and among Argentinean galaxioids, two of the four species (50%) are classiﬁed as needing conservation attention (Bello and Ubeda 1998). The Falkland Islands have just two galaxioids, one of them so seriously threatened that it has recently been afforded total protection (McDowall et al. 2001,

- 2005). The single presently recognised South African galaxiid has not been categorised as threatened (Skelton 1987; Skelton et al. 1995), although there are concerns about its conservation status at the local level (Impson et al. 2002). With the likely addition of known but undescribed species, this concern can only change for the worse as some new taxa will inevitably have more restricted distributions than the present single species. The sole New Caledonian galaxiid is not, as far as I have been able to determine, listed in any overall formal documentation of the island’s freshwater ﬁsh fauna, though Keith (2002) ranked it as critically endangered in the EBF series, discussed above.

By any standards, numerous global freshwater ﬁsh species are vulnerable to extinction (Richter

- et al. 1997). At both global and regional scales, there is substantial concern about the conservation status of the galaxioids, which are appearing in listings of threatened species substantially out of proportion to both global freshwater ﬁsh diversity

- as a whole, and local/regional freshwater ﬁsh biodiversity.

Range and extent of threats to species

Galaxioid species that are threatened throughout their ranges have tended to attract most attention, whether at the global/IUCN level or the regional/country level. They have tended also to be the species most discussed in the EBF series (which can be regarded as ‘globally threatened’). There are, however, other important threat classes. Some species, though not

globally threatened, may be threatened within signiﬁcant, sometimes discrete areas of their overall ranges (‘regionally threatened’ species). Moreover, there are instances where species are widely distributed and not regarded as generally threatened across their ranges, though there may be major local or general decline in abundance or even local extirpation (‘locally threatened’).

Mitigating threat at the local level should not be neglected for several reasons, including:

- 1. Loss of local populations may reduce species’ genetic and behavioural diversity;
- 2. Genetic diversity may represent unrecognised taxonomic entities (as is clearly true of galaxiids across the family’s range); and
- 3. Loss of a species from an ecological community may seriously perturb ecosystem functioning, especially when the species lost is a keystone species (Paine 1969, 1974; Creed 2000), or is important for the presence of some other keystone species (as, for example, as a part of its diet).

Thus, while global threat must be the primary focus, regional and local threats can still be, or lead to, serious biodiversity losses. Moreover, some focus on local threat may alert us to actual, or potential, regional or global threat. As well, the causes/mechanisms of threat may more easily be explicitly examined and identiﬁed at the lower geographic scales. Understanding local threat may be the pathway to better understanding of regional and global threat. Threat must therefore be viewed at several scales.

Part II: Scenarios of the conservation status of the galaxioid ﬁshes: a species by species analysis

Conservation concern for Retropinnines, or southern smelts

Fishes of the subfamily Retropinninae are found only in southeastern Australia and New Zealand, where they are known as smallish, silvery, shoaling ﬁshes. None usually exceed 100 mm long, and they often mature when much smaller (genera Retropinna and Stokellia—the southern smelts). They

have thin, easily dislodged scales, making them very fragile ﬁsh that die rapidly when handled or stressed. The osmerid afﬁnity of these ﬁsh is partially signalled by their having a small adipose ﬁn, though the more posterior position of the rayed dorsal ﬁn, above the anal ﬁn (Fig. 3B) differs from the northern Osmeridae, where the dorsal ﬁn originates ahead of the insertion of the pelvic ﬁns. Members in both the northern and southern families have a strong and distinctive cucumberlike odour, seemingly generated by the same complex organic compound (McDowall 1976a, 1979; McDowall et al. 1993; Berra et al. 1982).

The eastern Australian Retropinna semoni

The eastern Australian R. semoni is not well known, apart from an early account of its life history by Milward (1966). It is a small ﬁsh known to reach around 100 mm, but is usually much smaller, and in some populations it seems to reach no more than 45–50 mm (McDowall 1979). It is widespread in eastern coastal drainages from southern Queensland south through New South Wales and Victoria to around the mouth of the Murray River, and also widely through the inland Murray–Darling system and rivers associated with internal drainages into Lake Eyre (Fig. 7). At least in the Murray–Darling and internal drainages, R. semoni is non-diadromous, though it is highly tolerant of saline water. Populations in coastal drainages also seem to be non-diadromous, though this is uncertain. Across its very broad range, R. semoni abounds locally and probably seasonally in lakes and gently ﬂowing, generally open waters (Llewellyn 1983; McDowall 1996d). Seasonal ﬂuxes in abundance may be driven by a semelparous, annual life cycle, so that in the early months after spawning, there will only be larval or very small juveniles present. There have been no expressions of conservation concern for this species, though Arthington (1991) noted that Gambusia holbrooki and an unspeciﬁed Australian retropinnid (probably R. semoni) have rather similar diets, as did Cadwallader (1978). Arthington also said that the retropinnid exhibited ‘‘much lower abundances in the presence of G. holbrooki and [also another poeciliid] Xiphophorus helleri’’, but provided no data, and the

Fig. 7 Distribution of Australian smelt, Retropinna semoni in southeastern Australia

pattern of correlation demands a causal explanation. She alluded to dietary overlap with European carp, Cyprinus carpio, but commented that: ‘‘Whether any of these species has been affected by exploitation competition is not known’’. Some negative ecosystem effects are possible. Wager and Jackson (1993) blamed invasion of habitats by gambusia for reduction in R. semoni populations, though presented no data.

Fletcher (1986) also observed dietary overlap between medium-sized European carp and R. semoni and, although carp are widely recognised as having damaging ecosystem-scale effects (Miller and Crowl 2006), she did not argue for major adverse impacts of carp, nor for competition between the two species. Retropinna semoni, by all accounts, remains very widespread and locally abundant across its range. The possibility of adverse effects on R. semoni must be recognised, certainly locally from the vast populations of European carp in the Murray–Darling River system, where they may make up 60–90% of biomass at many sites, or are present at 1 ﬁsh/m2 (Roberts and Tilzey 1997; Barrett 2004).

Ongoing molecular studies are suggesting that the taxonomy of Australian Retropinna (as reviewed by McDowall 1979) may underestimate the true diversity in mainland Australia (M. Hammer pers. comm.), and this needs

clariﬁcation for implementing effective conservation protocols. As widely across the galaxioids, formal recognition of undescribed taxonomic diversity must result in species having narrower geographical ranges, an unavoidable outcome of which is heightened levels of conservation threat.

CONSERVATION RANKING: There have been no published rankings of the conservation status of

- R. semoni, which is not mentioned by Baillie and Groombridge (1996) or IUCN (2006). Tasmania’s Retropinna tasmanica

Retropinna tasmanica (Fig. 8) is endemic to Tasmania where it is widespread, especially in the north and south-east, though largely at low elevations (Fig. 9). It is the smallest of the retropinnids, occasionally reaching 70 mm, but is typically smaller. Little is known of its ecology (no one has ever studied it), and the Tasmanian Inland Fisheries Commission in the 1960s seemed more interested in introducing and establishing R. semoni as a trout forage ﬁsh, than in understanding their own endemic species (Lynch 1968b, 1969b). But it seems agreed that it is an anadromous, mid-water shoaling species, the mature adults migrating into the lower reaches of Tasmanian rivers during spring (Blackburn 1950). It is believed to spawn in the lowland, perhaps tidal reaches, though this has not been investigated. The newly hatched larvae are probably swept to sea in river ﬂows soon after hatching. Little is known of this species’ ecology. The mature adults are likely prey for marine and estuarine ﬁsh, including introduced brown trout. Though Fulton and Pavuk (1988) listed this as one of the species taken in the Tasmanian whitebait ﬁshery, they found that it is not favoured as a food ﬁsh owing to its cucumber

Fig. 8 Tasmanian smelt, Retropinna tasmanica, reaches >65 mm LCF

Fig. 9 Distribution of Tasmanian smelt, Retropinna tasmanica; note predominance of low elevation, coastal sites

odour (see also comment in this paper about possible pressure to close the Tasmanian whitebait season to make the ﬁsh available as trout food, and this may have applied to Tasmanian smelt). Lynch (1976) noted that there were lacustrine stocks of R. tasmanica in several low elevation dams (see also McDowall 1979), though there are few references to anadromy being facultative in this species.

CONSERVATION RANKINGS: There have been no expressions of serious concern about the conservation status of R. tasmanica.

The New Zealand species of Retropinna

New Zealand’s Retropinna retropinna (Fig. 10) is the largest of the southern smelts, reaching 165 mm (McDowall 1990b). It is often 100– 120 mm long, though most adults are <100 mm. The smaller ﬁsh are probably yearlings, larger ones being two- or perhaps even three-year-olds that have delayed maturation. Retropinna retropinna is typically anadromous, and is widespread (Fig. 11, n) and, though mostly at low elevations, it may move well inland (>230 km) in low

- Fig. 10 New Zealand’s Retropinna retropinna, reaches >160 mm LCF

- Fig. 11 Distribution of New Zealand’s common smelt, Retropinna retropinna; n—anadromous populations; D—lake populations. arrow

gradient rivers. It is present on the Chatham Islands, ca. 800 km east of New Zealand (Fig. 11—arrow 5). Anadromy is facultative. There are many non-migratory populations Fig. 11, D), some in low elevation, brackish lakes such as Lake Ellesmere in the eastern South Island (Fig. 11—arrow 4), and in many lowelevation to sub-montane freshwater lakes. In some lakes, again as in Lake Ellesmere, and also in lower Waikato River lakes in northwestern North Island (Fig. 11—arrow 1), anadromous and non-migratory populations co-occur, and quite

- 1—Waikato River;

- arrow 2—Rotorua lakes;
- arrow 3—Lake Taupo in the central North Island;
- arrow 4—Lake Ellesmere;
- arrow 5—Chatham Islands

possibly interbreed (McDowall 1979; Northcote et al. 1992; Northcote and Ward 1985). Populations in coastal lakes may be anadromous when lake outﬂows to sea are open and non-anadromous when they are closed.

Retropinna retropinna, when anadromous, migrates into river estuaries in huge shoals in spring and summer, the ﬁsh spawn in lower reaches of rivers on sandy shoals, and the adults die. The eggs are small and numerous (several thousand, though explicit counts have not been published for anadromous populations). The larvae hatch and are swept downstream and to sea (McDowall 1990b). Thus most of their life is spent feeding and growing at sea (as typical of anadromous ﬁsh).

Lacustrine populations occur in many lakes of the central North Island of New Zealand; some being translocated anadromous ﬁsh, which shows how facultative their anadromy is. The lake populations were established following collapse of stocks of Galaxias brevipinnis in lakes owing to

trout predation. Typically, the species reaches only about 60–70 mm in lakes. In volcanic lakes of the Rotorua district (Fig. 11—arrow 2)

- R. retropinna is a food for proliﬁc stocks of introduced rainbow and brown trout (Smith 1959); the globally famous trout ﬁsheries in Lake Taupo, New Zealand’s largest lake (Fig. 11—arrow 3), are fuelled by trout predation on R. retropinna (Stephens 1984; Cryer 1990; Greaves 2006).

Local indigenous Maori people around Lake Taupo have the right to harvest R. retropinna as food, in lieu of their former, traditional harvest of G. brevipinnis stocks in the lake. The permitted harvest of R. retropinna is small, and has been allowed only because local trout ﬁshery managers believe that harvest will not diminish availability of R. retropinna as a forage species for trout, an interesting choice of priorities that appears surprisingly often in this account of galaxioid conservation (Dedual 2005).

CONSERVATION RANKING: No conservation listings

- at global, regional, or local scales express significant concern for the conservation status of R. retropinna. It was not mentioned by Baillie and Groombridge (1996) and is not listed by IUCN

(2006), this despite both anadromous and lacustrine populations being exposed to heavy predation by brown trout in river estuaries and tidal/ brackish lakes, and by both brown and rainbow trout in freshwater lakes (Stephens 1984; Cryer 1990; Rutledge 1991). Why this species can cope with trout predation in a way that seems untrue of

- G. brevipinnis is uncertain. Populations of introduced European perch also prey on R. retropinna in some areas (Grifﬁths 1976).

Stokellia anisodon in rivers of the Canterbury coastline, New Zealand

Stokellia anisodon (Fig. 12) has narrow geographical range, occurring only in rivers of the eastern South Island of New Zealand (Fig. 13, d). It is smaller than R. retropinna, rarely reaching 100 mm, mostly 70–85 mm. It is anadromous, and anadromy seems obligatory—no lake populations are known despite presence of several lowelevation lakes within its geographical range. It

- Fig. 12 Stokell’s smelt, Stokellia anisodon, reaches 100 mm LCF

- Fig. 13 Distribution of Stokell’s smelt, Stokellia anisodon, in rivers of the east coast of the South Island of New Zealand

migrates into coastal rivers over spring in vast shoals, and only penetrates a short distance upstream (10–12 km, and mostly much less; McDowall 2000a). It spawns on low-elevation gravelly river beds, and the larvae, ca. 5 mm long, are swept to sea on hatching (McMillan 1961).

Stokellia anisodon is important in the diet of large sea-migratory and estuarine brown trout (Eldon and Greager 1983; Eldon and Kelly 1985; Rutledge 1991) and, as well, probably for diverse indigenous predators, such as kahawai (Arripis trutta—Arripidae), estuarine ﬂounders (Rhombosolea spp.—Pleuronectidae) and birds such as black-backed and black billed gulls (Larus dominicanus and L. bulleri—Laridae), blackfronted terns (Sterna striata—f. Sternidae), and shags (Phalacrocorax spp.—f. Phalacrocaracidae) (McMillan 1961). There was limited commercial

exploitation for human consumption of the

- S. anisodon population in one river estuary (McDowall 1983—Fig. 14), but this no longer happens, and any adverse impacts of exploitation were probably long ago dissipated. This species remains hugely abundant, despite predation and human exploitation.

CONSERVATION RANKING: There have been no expressions of concern for the conservation of

- S. anisodon, including from IUCN (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006), though local anglers feared that the resource might be depleted, depriving the trout of a food supply—another instance of higher priority being given to an introduced species than to an indigenous one.

Conservation concern for the Prototroctes or southern graylings

The genus Prototroctes comprises one species in southeastern Australia and Tasmania, and another in New Zealand. While the southern and northern smelts exhibit strong similarities (McDowall 1969) the southern graylings are quite different from Northern Hemisphere graylings (genus Thymallus, f. Salmonidae), Prototroctes being much more closely related to the southern Retropinna and northern Osmeridae, than to any Salmonidae (McDowall 1969; Johnson and Patterson 1996; Waters et al. 2002b).

Fig. 14 Stokell’s smelt, Stokellia anisodon being dried in the sun for sale to south-east Asia

The Australian grayling Prototroctes maraena

Prototroctes maraena is known only from: coastal drainages of central and southern New South Wales from the Grose River southwards; in eastern and southern coastal drainages of Victoria; and widely around Tasmania (Fig. 15, d). Allen et al. (2002) also reported it from King Island in Bass Strait (Fig. 15—arrow). Prototroctes has a specialised upper jaw, the blunt teeth forming a distinctive, even, comb-like row around the perimeter of the premaxilla (Fig. 16). Also, there are two full loops in the intestine (Fig. 17), compared with only one loop in other galaxioids (McDowall 1974), and this may be related to the species being at least partly herbivorous. It consumes aquatic insects as well as benthic algae, but the extent to which the insects are targeted, as opposed to being ingested with the algae, is unclear (Jackson 1976; Bishop and Bell 1978a; Berra et al. 1987). Herbivory is very unusual among lower euteleostean ﬁshes (like salmons, trout, smelts and pikes; Nelson 1994), though it is not unique as the Japanese ayu, (Plecoglossus

Fig. 15 Distribution of the Australian grayling, Prototroctes maraena in southeastern Australia and Tasmania (arrow indicates record from King Island from Allen et al. 2002)

- Fig. 16 Upper jaw shape and dentition in Prototroctes

- Fig. 17 Radiograph of Prototroctes showing double loop in intestine

altivelis—f. Osmeridae), is also a herbivore (Okada 1960). The elongated digestive tract may be seen as strengthening the evidence for herbivory.

Prototroctes maraena is an amphidromous species, the well-grown juveniles (around 50 mm long—Fig. 18) migrating into lowland rivers from the sea during spring. There are at least two reports of small juveniles being taken by Tasmanian whitebaiters (Lynch 1966, 1982), providing the ﬁrst hints that P. maraena spends part of its life at sea. Populations form large schools in river pools and runs, usually not far upstream from the sea.

Fig. 18 Juvenile of Prototroctes maraena, 35 mm long, at return to fresh water from the sea

Not much is known about reproduction, but the ﬁsh spawn in fresh water, perhaps over gravelly or sandy shoals. There are hints that habitat perturbations associated with managed river ﬂows may be signiﬁcantly contributing to a decline in the species’ abundance, speciﬁcally, that lowered water temperatures, perhaps associated with a rise in water ﬂow levels, might be implicated in stimulating spawning, and that temperatures are being inﬂuenced by ﬂow management and water abstraction (Berra 1982; Hall and Harrington 1989; O’Connor and Mahoney 2000, 2004).

There has long been concern for the distribution, abundance and conservation status of P. maraena, though the level of concern has oscillated over past decades, and has varied geographically. Greatest concerns have been in Tasmania, where a major ‘mortality event’ was reported around 1869–1879, by Saville-Kent (1888). He described P. maraena as ‘‘ﬂoating down the rivers in thousands, covered more or less extensively with a cottony fungus growth’’. [This] ‘‘epidemic [was] so virulent and exhaustive that many, more especially of the southern rivers, were more or less completely denuded of their stock of this species and have remained so up to the present date’’. Whether this was an epidemic of aquatic fungus such as Saprolegnia, or was natural mortality, perhaps following spawning, cannot now be determined. SavilleKent (1888) speculated that the fungus may have been introduced with salmonid ova which were being transported to Tasmania from Great Britain at about that time, but this cannot now be corroborated. Johnston (1883), also, mentioned that ‘‘in about 1869 ... [P. maraena] suddenly almost disappeared from many Tasmanian rivers where they were formerly plentiful’’, but this may also represent the event described by Saville-Kent (1888). There has been no reported repetition, and could be because the ﬁsh was no longer present, though this does not seem to

have been true, as indicated by repeated reports of P. maraena from diverse parts of Tasmania in later decades. Perhaps there are major natural ﬂuctuations in abundance that have not been documented.

Commentary about the conservation status of P. maraena has appeared over many years in Annual Reports of the Tasmanian Inland Fisheries Commission (see references to Lynch 1966– 1983), which provide somewhat plaintive reports backed up by few data, e.g. that ‘‘For some years there has been concern lest [P. maraena] should become extinct in Tasmania [though] ... recent reports have shown that [it] is holding its own in certain areas’’ (Lynch 1967); ‘‘only one sighting was reported this year’’ (Lynch 1971). Some individual ﬁsh were taken in 1977, for the ﬁrst time, from the Gordon River on the west coast of Tasmania, prompting: ‘‘It is hoped that there are more of this fairly rare ﬁsh in the area’’ (Lynch

- 1977). This last comment emanates from a survey of the Gordon River associated with proposals to dam the river for hydro-electricity generation; this would have eliminated P. maraena from the dammed section of the river owing to its obligatory amphidromy (see discussion of the status of Galaxias pedderensis and the new Lake Pedder). In 1979 the Inland Fisheries Commission reported that ‘‘sixty [P. maraena] were sighted feeding at the surface in the Douglas River. This is the largest number of [P. maraena] sighted at one time in Tasmania for many years. It is likely that [P. maraena] are more common in Tasmania than previously thought’’ (Lynch 1979a). And then in the Commission’s 1983 report, the comment was made that P. maraena ‘‘still has a wide distribution in Tasmania, but has undergone a major decrease in numbers since the early part of this century’’. These various statements seem largely unsubstantiated.

Frankenberg (1974) commented, by contrast, that P. maraena was ‘‘increasing in abundance in Tasmania’’, but this was also unaccompanied by any data, and subsequent events do little to corroborate this. At that time, Andrews (1973) was commenting that P. maraena was ‘‘now very rare and may possibly be extinct in Tasmania’’, but again there were no data or indications of a search for the species.

Conservation concerns have been somewhat less extreme elsewhere in southeastern Australia, though in Victoria, Castelnau (1873) wrote that P. maraena had all but disappeared from the Yarra (Melbourne). Frankenberg (1974) thought that ‘‘recent records suggest partial recovery in numbers after a long period of near extinction in Victoria and New South Wales’’, but again there were no data. Llewellyn (1983) described it as rare in coastal streams of New South Wales, where it was thought to be declining in numbers, and endangered. Occasional observations of numerous Australian grayling in New South Wales rivers have tended to allay fears for this species’ conservation, though, somewhat ironically, Bishop and Bell’s (1978a, b) reports of a mass capture (>300) of P. maraena from the Shoalhaven River in southern New South Wales, should be interpreted as a mass mortality event associated with closure of a dam in that river (1976). Clearly, large numbers of P. maraena were migrating upstream at the time, and had accumulated below a newly closed dam. And the species, of course, disappeared from above the dam, where P. maraena was one among an assortment of 10 species no longer found to be there. Thus reported ‘localised abundance’ of grayling cannot be viewed as anything but negative—it seems to have effectively been the demise of the species in much of the river. Not surprisingly, in later work, Gehrke et al. (2002) reported ‘‘virtual disappearance’’ of P. maraena in the Shoalhaven River downstream of the dam, presumably because the dam excluded the ﬁsh from the upper river, where its feeding and/ or breeding grounds may have been located. Jackson and Koehn’s comment that four ﬁsh taken in a Yarra River tributary in 1981–2 were the ﬁrst seen there since 1873 is a telling reﬂection on this species’ status. Berra (1982) collected hundreds from the Tambo River, also in Victoria, so P. maraena was clearly locally abundant at that time. The fact that he was permitted to do so seems surprising, given the conservation status of the ﬁsh.

In their review of the status of P. maraena in Victorian waters, Jackson and Koehn (1988) discussed lack of data on the species’ distribution

and abundance, but did mention that it can, in some rivers, be captured in relatively large numbers, and has been caught ‘‘consistently’’ in the Barwon and Tambo Rivers. They knew of P. maraena in some degraded Victorian rivers, but noted that ‘‘What is not clear is whether the species can spawn successfully in these rivers or whether these stocks come from other less degraded rivers systems... [and further that] all rivers known to contain [P. maraena] are susceptible to some form of habitat degradation’’. These views raise the prospect of a potential ‘source and sink ecology’ scenario (Pulliam 1988; Dunning et al. 1992). Jackson and Koehn (1988) pointed to major gaps in knowledge of this species’ ecology, and were concerned that none of the major catchments known to contain P. maraena were within the boundaries of National Parks, as a result of which ‘‘no ... populations in Victoria are adequately protected’’.

A wide range of factors has been listed as contributing to this species’ decline (Jackson and Koehn 1988; Ingram et al. 1990; Jackson 2000c), including:
