- • Impoundments obstructing migrations—as shown by events on the Shoalhaven River in New South Wales, discussed above);
- • Flow regulation—as O’Connor and Mahoney

(2004) reported instances of wild stocks of P. maraena resorbing their ovaries. They monitored reproductive development in the species in the Barwon River in southern Victoria, and over a two year period found no evidence for breeding in the population. They observed resorption of ovaries in many females, and hypothesised a connection between ovarian maturation and/or ovulation, and ﬂows in the river; thus, spawning may be inhibited by river regulation (the lack of ﬂood ﬂows);

- • The presence of brown trout has been blamed for decline, with suggestions that trout might occupy a similar niche to P. maraena—although there is no evidence of competition or predation. Jackson and Koehn’s (1988) experience was that trout and P. maraena were seldom in the same stretch of river. They recommended that ‘‘Until such time as...research is undertaken

- it would be unwise to stock streams containing valuable [P. maraena] populations with introduced species, particularly brown trout’’. Ingram et al. (1990) also suggested competition with introduced trout. Koehn and O’Connor (1990) thought that this species may suffer predation from trout, including adults but especially migratory juveniles—Morris et al. 2000);
- • Invasion of habitat by European perch has also been suggested as an adverse impact, presumably perch predation;
- • Deforestation and the loss of riparian vegetation and consequential siltation, perhaps modifying foods available, especially if herbivory is important to the ecology of the ﬁsh;
- • Conversion of land to agriculture may lead to siltation (Jackson and Koehn 1988; Ingram et al. 1990);
- • River regulation may affect reproductive maturation and development;
- • Hall (1983) found the copepod parasite Lernaea cyprinacea on P. maraena from the Tambo River, noted that overseas this parasite causes mortalities in ﬁsh populations, but thought this unlikely in ﬂowing waters. Wager and Jackson (1993) considered this a potentially harmful development, and thought that the copepod may have been introduced to Australia ‘‘with salmonids or redﬁn perch’’. Association with salmonids seems unlikely as these were typically brought to Australia as eyed ova (Clements 1988). Lernaea is known to infect other Australian ﬁshes, such as Gadopsis marmoratus and Maccullochella peeli, providing diverse sources for infection. Hall

(1983) found no Lernaea on other ﬁsh species sharing habitat with P. maraena, and the source of the parasite remains unknown, as does its impact.

Ingram et al. (1990) added to all of the above ‘‘the species’ general sensitivity to habitat disruption’’, though what this was meant to convey is unclear, though they certainly suggest that the species is vulnerable to habitat change.

Conservation concerns for P. maraena have been expressed frequently and have been highly varied. Lake (1971) described it as ‘‘one of the

most seriously threatened species in Australia’’. Kennedy and Burton (1986) regarded it as a highest priority species for conservation among all Australian freshwater ﬁshes, though Fulton (1987) dismissed this listing because he regarded the ﬁsh as known widely around Tasmania, though other, opinions on the status of this ﬁsh have not supported this conclusion (Lynch 1966– 1982). Unfortunately a widespread range of a vulnerable diadromous ﬁsh species could be its downfall owing to wide dispersion of recruits into non- or marginally viable habitats.

CONSERVATION RANKING: According to Michaelis

- (1986) the Australian grayling was ranked as ‘‘endangered’’ by the Australian Council of Nature Conservation Ministers. Jackson and Koehn (1988) regarded it as Vulnerable in Victoria, this ranking applying to ‘‘taxa believed likely to move into the Endangered category in the near future if the factors causing the demise continue operating’’. Morris et al. (2000) also regarded the

- species as Vulnerable in New South Wales. Wager and Jackson (1993) and Ingram et al. (1990) classed it as Potentially Threatened at the national level, while Wager and Jackson (1993) called it Vulnerable, proposing a substantial research programme to determine what was affecting abundance. O’Connor and Mahoney (2004) reported that P. maraena was ranked Vulnerable under Australia’s Federal Environment Protection and Biodiversity Conservation Act 1999.

The broad, very inclusive, range of unfocussed impacts, nominated by various commentators as affecting the P. maraena probably contributed to it being classiﬁed by Crook (2001) as Data Deﬁcient, i.e., possibly insufﬁcient is known about it to permit rigorous determination either of its conservation status, or of the causes of any changes in its abundance. Perhaps, too, levels of concern for P. maraena, over many years, have been accentuated by knowledge that its only congener, P. oxyrhynchus from New Zealand, has long been extinct (see below). Prototroctes maraena is ranked Vulnerable by IUCN list (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; 2006 IUCN Red List of Threatened Species).

The New Zealand grayling, Prototroctes oxyrhynchus

The only galaxioid species ofﬁcially recorded as Extinct (McDowall 1990b; Tisdall 1994; Baillie and Groombridge 1996) is the so-called New Zealand grayling, Prototroctes oxyrhynchus

- (Fig. 19). The decline and extinction of P. oxyrhynchus

took place so early in New Zealand’s colonial history that little is known of its ecology or life history. It was once probably New Zealand wide

- (Fig. 20). Like the Australian species, P. oxyrhynchus appears to have been amphidromous, Clarke (1899) recording capture of juveniles in large numbers in the New Zealand West Coast whitebait ﬁshery (based primarily on amphidromous species of Galaxias that migrate into rivers from the sea during the spring—McDowall 1984a, 1990b). In the absence of detailed and consistent information on the ecology of P. oxyrhynchus, it seems parsimonious to consider its behaviour as similar to the Australian species. What little is known about the New Zealand species is consistent with the two species having similar habits.

Prototroctes oxyrhynchus was reportedly very common at European colonisation of New Zealand in the mid 1800s. Hector (1872) observed that ‘‘At certain seasons they assemble in the streams in immense numbers’’, while colonial explorer Charles Douglas told of them being present ‘‘not in hundreds but in thousands’’ (Pascoe 1957). Moss (1958) related how in 1869 a mill wheel was brought to a standstill because the intake channel was choked with thousands of ﬁsh. Drummond (Otago Daily Times Newspaper, 23 March, 1910) said that the rivers once ‘‘swarmed with these ﬁsh’’, making it ‘‘the most common freshwater ﬁsh

Fig. 19 New Zealand grayling, Prototroctes oxyrhynchus, reached >300 mm, perhaps 450 mm

Fig. 20 Distribution of the now extinct New Zealand grayling Prototroctes oxyrhynchus (derived from Allen (1949) and other literature sources)

in many parts of New Zealand’’ being formerly taken by the ‘‘cartload’’ from the Wairau River, in Marlborough (Rutland 1878; Phillipps 1923). Though Phillipps’ ranking of the species as that abundant may be dubious, it nevertheless appears that the species was both widespread (Fig. 20), and sometimes very common.

Despite disparaging commentary about ﬁsh species available to anglers in New Zealand prior to the introduction of various salmonids in the last third of the 19th century (McDowall 1990b,

- 1994a), P. oxyrhynchus had appeal both to anglers and as a food ﬁsh. Spackman (1892), having declared New Zealand rivers to be worthless for anglers, then churlishly admitted that P. oxyrhynchus could provide ‘‘capital sport’’. Thomson (1922) considered that before trout were established there was no sport for freshwater anglers in New Zealand ‘‘unless he lived near a stream where the grayling abounded ... a ﬁne sporting ﬁsh’’.

Although there was clearly ambivalence by ‘leaders’ in the colonial New Zealand freshwater ﬁsheries scene of the mid 1800s, and prior to the establishment of trouts and salmons (f. Salmonidae), P. oxyrhynchus was nevertheless clearly quite highly regarded by some settlers, who found it a once common and highly valued food ﬁsh. Several early accounts described P. oxyrhynchus being taken in large numbers by colonial explorers and travellers (Brunner 1848; Hursthouse 1861; Hector 1872). Hector referred to ‘‘Our most skilful brother of the rod, Mr. H.J. Smith [who] caught last year 1,152 of these ﬁsh in 58 ﬁshing days, taking in one day 93’’. Smith must have had high regard for the ﬁsh, judging by the time he spent ﬁshing for them. Legendary late 19th century explorer and surveyor, Charlie Douglas (see McDowall 1980; Pascoe 1957), ranked it ‘‘as an eating ﬁsh...far above any of the [other] freshwater ﬁsh native to Westland’’, describing it as ‘‘much sought after and looked upon as a delicious ﬁsh food, the ﬂesh being white and delicate...and much in demand’’. Douglas, known for his wry observations about life in early colonial New Zealand, would have known what P. oxyrhynchus was like to eat, and he would have mentioned any reservations about its quality as a food, as he did, with other types of ‘‘bush tucker’’ (McDowall 2005b); moreover, he depended for most of his life on what he could garner from the forests and rivers (Pascoe 1957), so although this was all he wrote about the species, he must have caught it, and have looked forward to eating it, from time to time.

Sometimes it was netted in large numbers, e.g. Thomas Brunner (1848) during his long and arduous journey down the Buller River, in Westland, reported several instances of catching scores to hundreds of P. oxyrhynchus in a seine net that he and his Maori guides had made. They caught hundreds and dried them in the sun for later consumption.

Opinions are divided about how, or even whether, it could be caught on a hook. Informants told anthropologist Elsdon Best (1929) that they would not take a bait, though Best reckoned that they ‘‘took the ﬂy readily and provided good sport, were strong ﬁghters; the ﬁnest tackle and small trout-ﬂies were required for them’’. Hector’s (1872) account of the catches of angler

- H.J. Smith (see above) indicated that it was taken on hook and line. Several early accounts, including Hector (1872), mention capture of P. oxyrhynchus on the small red worm that abounded beneath cattle excrement. David Graham (1953) related how easily he caught them as a lad in rivers of the West Coast of New Zealand’s South Island around 1900 ‘‘using any piece of stick, household string, a small hook, and a cork as a ﬂoat’’ and using such baits as small worms, ﬂies, grasshoppers, crickets and maggots, outdoing older anglers who caught few ﬁsh with more sophisticated ﬁshing gear, and how they unhappily observed Graham and his friends ‘‘going home, as we often did, with enough ﬁsh to feed a family of four or more’’. Capture of grayling on a baited hook points to a role of aquatic and perhaps terrestrial animals in the species’ diet.

Sadly, this ﬁsh is now extinct, having last been recorded in the 1920s (or perhaps early 1930s—McDowall 1990b) and its disappearance remains a mystery. There is a record of a specimen apparently ‘obtained’ after 1930, and now in the Natural History Museum in London. It was given by the New Zealand Marine Department to scientists on the Discovery, which was in New Zealand seas in the 1930s (Norman 1935), but this ﬁsh could have been in the Department’s archive for years previously. If we set aside that record as of dubious date, amongst the last published records of captures of P. oxyrhynchus are two ﬁsh taken from the Turanganui River, near Featherston in 1922 (Phillipps 1923, 1940a).

Another late record is by New Zealand Maori anthropologist te Rangi Hiroa, also in the 1920s. With astonishing serendipity, visited East Cape in 1923 and asked the local Maori people to demonstrate how they caught P. oxyrhynchus. They built a ﬁshing weir according to traditional design and caught 30–40 ﬁsh (Hiroa 1926) (Fig. 21); this, perhaps along with the record by Phillipps (1923), may be among the last veriﬁed catches of the species.

Reports of the decline of P. oxyrhynchus vary geographically and temporally. It appears to have begun its decline in some areas as early as the 1870s (Allen 1949; Graham 1953; McDowall 1990b). Best (1929) mentioned that it had ‘‘disappeared since the ‘seventies’ in the Bay of Plenty in north-

Fig. 21 Maori demonstrating how they used to catch New Zealand grayling, Prototroctes oxyrhynchus (photo from Museum of New Zealand)

eastern New Zealand, and alluded to one that was ‘‘brought into one of the hotels at Opotiki as a curiosity’’ in1904.Graham (1953) commentedthat Bay of Plenty Maori knew it as ‘‘abundant in local rivers some time prior to 1900’’. The ﬁsh reportedly had ‘‘disappeared from the Waikato River following a heavy ﬂood in 1874’’ (Best 1929) and Drummond (1910, Otago Daily Times newspaper) advised that by 1884 it no longer ‘‘swarmed’’ in the Buller and Inangahua Rivers on the West Coast of the South Island. Best (1929) also commented that the ‘‘Otaki, Waiapu [Rivers], and other places’’ reported a few each season. And although Phillipps (1923) ranked the species as ‘‘still fairly common in certain streams near East Cape’’ in the early 1900s, he later (1940) wrote that it had been ‘‘comparatively rare’’ for 60 years (and so in the late 1870s or early 1880s), Phillipps adding that it was then ‘‘rarely seen except in isolated streams and rivers remote from settled areas’’.

Reasons for this species’ decline and extinction are poorly understood (as so often). An oftensuggested threat was introduction of trout into New Zealand, from the late 1860s and rapidly gathering momentum in the late 19th and early 20th centuries (McDowall 1990b, 1994a). Spackman (1892) reckoned that the trout seemed ‘‘too much for them’’, and Rutherfurd (1901) attributed their decline to the impacts of trout. W. Stevenson, the owner of a food canning ﬁrm, wrote to New Zealand’s Marine Department (out of concern for decline in catches of Galaxias whitebait, which his company canned), complaining that trout were ‘‘eating out our native

ﬁsh...they will ﬁnish the whitebait as they have the greyling [sic]’’ (McDowall 1984a).

Phillipps (1940a) described P. oxyrhynchus as ‘‘highly esteemed not only by man but also by trout’’, but did not document trout predation. He stated (1940b) that: ‘‘There appears to be little doubt that under the rigid system of Maori conservation of animal and plant life...a ﬁsh such as [P. oxyrhynchus] could and did survive the inroads on its number as made by the common enemy ‘‘man’’—when close seasons are gone and a strange new trout makes its appearance to compete in the struggle for existence, it is small wonder that ... [P. oxyrhynchus] has for the most part gone’’. Young and Foster (1986) argued that ‘‘a number of freshwater ﬁsh including the paneroro [P. oxyrhynchus] which has not been recorded in New Zealand since the 1920s’’, had been ‘‘killed from the [Wanganui] river’’ by ‘‘the introduction of trout in the last century’’. However, P. oxyrhynchus appears to have disappeared from rivers that trout had not yet invaded (though they spread widely around the country through coastal seas).

An additional part of the decline scenario may have been that New Zealand’s grayling, like the Australian one, appears to have been partly, if not substantially, a herbivore (McDowall 1974,

- 1976a; Bishop and Bell 1978a, b; Berra et al.

- 1987), both having specialised dentition and a much-lengthened digestive system (Fig. 19), which is important for digestion of plant material in herbivorous ﬁshes. Its dentition might have been adapted for scraping algae from the substrate gravels of New Zealand streams (Fig. 16), a habit that was reported in some early literature, e.g. the Maori people of New Zealand could tell where ﬁsh had been feeding from scrape marks in the periphyton on stones in streams where P. oxyrhynchus was known (Best 1929).

Deforestation of river catchments happened rapidly in much of New Zealand during the last half of the 19th century (Park 1995), and this could have inﬂuenced the decline of P. oxyrhynchus, but the ﬁsh also disappeared from rivers whose catchments were still forested, especially in the upstream reaches of the hill-country river valleys. Thus a partial explanation for its demise may have been that increasing sunlight reached the streams once forest had been removed, and

that this led to change in the periphyton that grew on the stones, probably a shift from encrusting red or brown algae or diatoms to ﬁlamentous green algae where more light was reaching the stream beds. The large, gravel, braided rivers of the eastern South Island would not have been affected in this way, but the grayling was seldom reported from them.

Another perspective is that almost all 19th century commentators regarded this ﬁsh as highly migratory, and it is possible that modiﬁcation of downstream river reaches may have been too serious, even if upstream catchment remained little modiﬁed. The migrations probably included some of the life cycle spent at sea, as is known for the Australian species (Lynch 1966, 1982; Berra 1982). Colonial New Zealand surveyor, Francis Clarke (1899), reported juveniles being taken in West Coast rivers by whitebaiters, contributing to what he described as ‘‘the heterogeneous collection that made up the so-called ‘whitebait’ at the end of the inanga [G. maculatus] season in the Westland rivers’’. And he thought it: ‘‘piteous at those times to see the enormous quantities of young grayling which were destroyed’’. However, it is hard to imagine that exploitation of P. oxyrhynchus amongst the whitebait was a major contributor to its early decline, though that could perhaps have been just one more population pressure that accelerated the species’ disappearance.

If larval and juvenile life of P. oxyrhynchus was marine, the concept of ‘‘sources and sinks’’ advocated by some ecologists (Pulliam 1988; Dunning et al. 1992—as discussed earlier) may have contributed to decline—essentially, that progeny from increasingly sparse, reproductively successful populations (sources) were being dispersed widely, many into habitats where life and/or reproduction for the P. oxyrhynchus was becoming impossible (sinks). This sort of dispersion of a species already in conservation crisis could have rapid and fatal effects, especially if the returning ﬁsh do not home to natal habitats (and there is no reason to think that P. oxyrhynchus did so). This would have meant that rivers producing large numbers of ﬁsh would have been ‘subsidising’ recruitment into populations in rivers that were failing to produce any, or enough, young for

returning back into fresh water, leading to the progeny being spread over a wide area.

Suggestions that New Zealand’s Prototroctes species’ disappearance was possibly related to introductions of diseases and parasites by Harrison and Stiassny (1999) are not supported by documentation. They may have mistakenly applied to the New Zealand species comments by Saville-Kent (1888) about the Australian species. Nor can I authenticate the assertion that it was ‘‘largely due to unsustainable commercial harvesting of the seafaring whitebait stage’’ (Allen et al. 2002), a statement that may derive from Clarke’s (1899) comment on the capture of juvenile P. oxyrhynchus among ﬁsh taken in the New Zealand whitebait ﬁshery.

This is all conjectural, and is made more paradoxical by the survival of the Australian sister-species, Prototroctes maraena, sometimes in locally high numbers, in southeastern Australia—despite concerns about its conservation status there (listed as vulnerable in Crook and Pogonoski 2003).

From time to time, over many decades since the 1930s, there have been reports of ‘‘P. oxyrhynchus’’ in New Zealand rivers, most famously in the late 1940s, at a time when there was considerable uncertainty over whether the species was still extant. A report reached Wellington of shoals of ﬁsh being observed in a river in a remote and inaccessible part of South Westland—a report almost certainly related to a bridge construction programme to improve access to that, then remote, region. In response to this report, scientists from New Zealand’s Marine Department rushed south to search for the ﬁsh, but without success. Similar reports still emerge, but none has been veriﬁed.

Some uncertainty has been voiced about the ﬁnality of the extinction of P. oxyrhynchus. Harrison and Stiassny (1999) raised the prospect that it may still persist somewhere, and this appears to derive from Day (1981), whose popular account was entirely derivative. If, as supposed, there was a sea-living juvenile phase in P. oxyrhynchus, it is likely that any surviving population would have been discovered, as the ﬁsh would probably have turned up over a wider geographical range. Even if it was able to home

(return to the river of birth) as some salmonids do (Quinn 1993; Quinn and Dittman 1990), it seems likely that there would have been enough straying for ﬁsh to have turned up elsewhere and have been reported. The data now available on the distributions of New Zealand’s freshwater ﬁshes from very extensive ﬁeld collection in New Zealand over the past 30–40 years (in excess of 25,000 sites are recorded in the New Zealand Freshwater Fish database—see Fig. 22) there is no doubt among local biologists that the ﬁsh disappeared long ago (McDowall 1990b, 2000a). The last local hopes were dashed when ﬁeld collecting was undertaken in Fiordland, in the far southwest or the South Island, where forests remain undisturbed, and there is no vehicular access; it is as virgin an area as could be imagined. Two expeditions, one into Dusky and Doubtful Sounds (McDowall 1981), and another into Chalky and Preservation Inlets (Bonnett and James 1988), revealed no P. oxyrhynchus. Thus, notwithstanding expressed uncertainties about the cause of the extinction of P. oxyrhynchus, there are no grounds for uncertainty that it is extinct, though occasional, rapidly dwindling numbers of increasingly elderly people claim to have seen the species alive (McDowall 1992b).

Fig. 22 Distribution of sampling sites in the NIWA New Zealand Freshwater Fish Database (>25,000 sites)

CONSERVATION RANKING: The ﬁnality of the disappearance of P. oxyrhynchus, and its ranking as Extinct, were accepted by Baillie and Groombridge (1996) and in the 2004 IUCN listing (2004 IUCN Red List of Threatened Species: <www.redlist.org>) and ultimately by Harrison and Stiassny (1999), just as by New Zealand conservationists for many years (Stokell 1955; McDowall 1978a; Williams and Given 1981; Molloy and Davis 1992; Tisdall 1994; Hitchmough

- 2002). It was gone probably by the 1930s, including from areas retaining unmodiﬁed forest and where trout were only beginning to invade in a major way may, and the reason(s) for its disappearance may always remain a mystery. There may well have been no single, primary cause. Gerald Stokell (1955), though an amateur naturalist, was during the mid-20th century the local authority on New Zealand’s freshwater ﬁsh, and

- as far as I have been able to determine, he never saw a grayling alive. He counselled that: ‘‘Anyone knowing its whereabouts...would do well to keep the locality secret, and so protect that ﬁsh from interference’’, an indication of his disaffection with ﬁsheries bureaucrats in New Zealand. He thought the ‘‘position of the ﬁsh constitutes a blot on the history of wildlife administration in New Zealand’’. Conservation concerns for the Galaxiidae

Galaxias maculatus: among the most widely distributed of all freshwater ﬁshes

Galaxias maculatus (Fig. 23A) is probably the best known of the galaxioids. It is a small slender species that grows to 190 mm though it typically reaches around 80 mm long (McDowall and Fulton 1996). It is a predominantly shoaling species, this making it distinctive (though not unique) in the family, most of which are cryptic and solitary (occasional other species exhibit shoaling behaviour, whilst species form shoals

- at juvenile life stages, up to ca. 30–55 mm length when they become cryptic and solitary). G. maculatus may well be neotenic (Benzie 1968a) in retaining schooling behaviour that juveniles of many other galaxiid species abandon as they grow (McDowall 1990b).

Fig. 23 Two slender, mid-water swimming New Zealand galaxiids, (A) Inanga, Galaxias maculatus (reaches ca. 150 mm LCF; (B) Dune Lakes galaxias, G. gracilis, to 80 mm LCF.)

This is a ‘marginally’ catadromous species with a distinctive life history (McDowall 1968d, 1990b). Huge shoals of mature adults migrate downstream into river estuaries during autumn, their movements being synchronised with high spring tides that are associated with full and/or new moons. How this behavioural synchrony is established is unknown. Spawning usually takes place in small streams ﬂowing into river estuaries and largely upstream of the salt wedge incursion into river estuaries on the rising tide (McDowall 1990b). The salt wedge penetrates upstream along the ﬂoors of estuaries with the rising tide whereas outﬂowing fresh water forms a surface layer over the heavier salt water. The shoals of ﬁsh penetrate and reproduce amongst estuary bank vegetation (Fig. 24) that is inundated during the high tides, and the eggs become stranded on the estuary margins when the tide recedes (Fig. 25). The adults generally die after spawning so that the species is effectively semelparous—though some post-spawners may survive and spawn again, and others, always females, may delay ﬁrst maturation until their second or third year (Burnet 1965). Embryonic development takes place in the moist atmosphere amongst the bases of the estuary bank vegetation. The eggs hatch when they are inundated by a later series of high spring tides. If ambient temperatures are warm enough, hatching may take place at about 14 days after spawning, but cooler temperatures delay development, and larvae may not hatch until a second, or even later, series of spring tides after fertilisation (Benzie 1968b). Delays in hatching may affect larval survival if energy stored in the egg is exhausted

Fig. 24 Estuarine spawning habitat of inanga, Galaxias maculatus (photo: D.K. Rowe)

Fig. 25 Fertilized eggs of inanga, Galaxias maculatus, stranded on estuary-bank vegetation

prior to hatch. Eggs held under experimental conditions still hatched six weeks after fertilisation, though the larvae derived from this longdelayed hatch would probably have been seriously starved by this time and their viability low

- (McDowall 1968d). The tiny larvae (<10 mm long)

are swept to sea as the receding tide ﬂushes from the estuary. They feed and grow at sea over the winter, probably in the coastal marine plankton, for around ca. 21–23 weeks, and return to fresh water, in vast shoals, as well grown but still entirely transparent juveniles around 50–55 mm long (Fig. 26; McDowall 1984b; McDowall et al. 1994).

Galaxias maculatus is very widespread on lands around the southern ocean (Fig. 27), with populations in:

- • Western Australia, largely in the south, and to the east of the range of G. occidentalis, another widespread Western Australian species (Morgan et al. 1996) (see Fig. 58, m);
- • Eastern Australia from around Brisbane (latitude 27 S), south and west in coastal drainages as far as Adelaide (see Fig. 57, d);
- • The islands in Bass Strait between mainland Australia and Tasmania to the south;
- • Widely in Tasmania (McDowall and Frankenberg 1981);

- Fig. 26 The sea-migratory ‘whitebait’ juvenile of the inanga, Galaxias maculatus ca. 53 mm LCF)

- Fig. 27 The broad, circum-southern distribution of Galaxias maculatus

- • Lord Howe Island in the mid-Tasman Sea between Australia and New Zealand (Allen et al. 1976);
- • Throughout New Zealand including the Chatham Islands (McDowall 1972, 1990b);
- • Patagonian South America from north of Valparaiso (around latitude 33 S) south to near Cape Horn (McDowall 1971b, 1972), largely west of the Andes, but also in some Andean lakes in both Chile and Argentina; and
- • The Falkland Islands (McDowall 1972; McDowall et al. 2001).

It is thus amongst the most widely, naturally dispersed, freshwater ﬁshes known. Despite its wide geographical range, this species is not a strong upstream migrant, cannot climb falls, has difﬁculty penetrating swift rapids, and so tends to have low inland penetration of rivers (Fig. 28, d), and is found largely at low elevations—though it may reach 245 km inland in low gradient rivers (McDowall 2000a).

This is a highly proliﬁc species that has long sustained very heavy ﬁsheries exploitation of its juveniles as they return from the sea, at least in Tasmania, New Zealand and Chile. In New Zealand there has been a ﬁshery for more than a century, though catches are thought to have declined substantially in recent decades across much of the country (there are no catch data since the 1970s and earlier data are at best, erratic). With that caveat, the data available show that peak catches were taken in the mid-1950s, when the greatest recorded annual catch from the West Coast of the South Island of New Zealand (the most productive region) was around 320,000 kg (Fig. 29), or around 600 million ﬁsh for the season (August–November) (McDowall 1984a, 1990b). Decline in the ﬁshery has been attributed, as much as anything, to habitat deterioration, and especially wetland drainage and the degradation of estuarine spawning grounds (McDowall and Eldon 1980; McDowall 1984a, 1990b—estuaries are highly vulnerable habitats in New Zealand, as globally, owing to the common establishment of towns and industries nearby). Galaxias maculatus is exposed, also, to heavy predation by introduced brown trout, especially sea-run/estuarine-living trout, almost throughout its range, and especially

Fig. 28 Distribution of inanga, Galaxias maculatus in New Zealand (note the general lack of inland penetration by this diadromous species)

around the southern coasts of New Zealand where ambient temperatures are lower and brown trout may be more anadromous (McDowall and Eldon 1980; McDowall 1990b); the same is probably also true in Tasmania. It is sometimes suggested, at least in New Zealand, that trout predation has driven the decline in G. maculatus, but this seems likely to be less inﬂuential than the huge numbers caught in the whitebait ﬁshery (McDowall 1984a, 1990b).

Taylor (1988) suggested that G. maculatus and brown trout have mutually exclusive distributions in rivers of the West Coast of New Zealand’s South Island. He found G. maculatus to be scarce in streams not stained by tannins from forest wetlands, and hypothesised that un-coloured waters of other streams may facilitate predation by trout on a pelagic shoaling species like G. maculatus, whereas the tannin-stained streams might provide them with a type of cover. Minns (1990) found a signiﬁcant negative association between the presence of G. maculatus and brown trout in an analysis of more than 6,000 site records then available in the NIWA New Zealand freshwater ﬁsh database (McDowall and Richardson 1983), and this seems unlikely to be due to different habitat preferences. Allibone and McIntosh (1999) observed that G. maculatus has

Fig. 29 Historic record of whitebait catch, from rivers on the West Coast of the South Island, based on the seamigratory juveniles of ﬁve Galaxias species

‘‘unexpectedly low co-occurrence with trout’’, but attributed this probably to sampling methods, and called for further study. In addition, brown trout are not common in the estuaries of northern New Zealand rivers, where summer water temperatures may be too high for salmonids, which are likely to retreat upstream into cooler, higher elevation streams. Glova (2003) presented evidence, from behavioural studies in a small stream simulator, that the number of G. maculatus declined when they shared the stream habitats with brown trout 255–390 mm long, and also that the galaxiids shifted their microhabitat with trout present. Presumably, this resulted in the galaxiids occupying less favourable microhabitats for drift feeding on invertebrates. Bonnett and McIntosh

- (2004), however, found that, when sharing a simulator stream with smaller brown trout (65– 115 mm long), G. maculatus (51–100 mm long) suffered no identiﬁable impacts. In Australia, Koehn (1986) blamed absence of G. maculatus from a small Victorian stream on the presence of large brown and rainbow trout. Even though ﬁsheries exploitation, as well as habitat change and the advent of salmonid predation, may have had strong adverse impacts on populations of G. maculatus, it is not regarded as under serious conservation threat in any part of its broad range.

Although G. maculatus is generally best known as a diadromous species, there are

numerous landlocked populations, especially in southeastern Australia and in Patagonia, and occasionally in New Zealand (McDowall 1972). Landlocked populations, especially in small lakes, can be highly vulnerable to deterioration in lake water quality (and perhaps more so when the lakes have low inﬂows and are in catchments with high human use). Also, their genetic/ecological isolation from the wider gene pool of the diadromous stocks may increase local vulnerability. In one such landlocked population, in Lake Purrumbete in southern Victoria, Australia, the galaxiid is an important forage species for introduced rainbow trout and Chinook salmon. Cadwallader and Eden (1982) observed a decline in the number of galaxiids in trout stomachs over about ten years—from ca. 25 per ﬁsh in 1967 to ca. 10 a decade later, and suggested that: ‘‘The phenomenal growth rate [of rainbow trout] in Lake Purrumbete in the past reﬂects an excellent food supply, and there is evidence to suggest that [reduced growth rates and ﬁsh condition] may be attributable to the reduced availability of forage ﬁsh, particularly G. maculatus’’ where anecdotal reports suggested that G. maculatus was much less abundant in Lake Purrumbete than formerly. A decline in the abundance of G. maculatus in the diet of the salmonids was associated with a signiﬁcant decline in the ﬁshery. Cadwallader and Eden (1981) pointed out that large releases of Chinook salmon were also being made into the lake during the same time period, and that they too had been preying on Lake Purrumbete’s G. maculatus. Thus the mechanism for decline is not simply rainbow trout predation. However, rather wryly, Cadwallader and Eden (1981) noted that since neither rainbow trout nor Chinook salmon spawn in Lake Purrumbete (there are no suitable spawning gravels), their numbers depend ‘‘solely on releases of hatcheryproduced ﬁsh. Under these circumstances introduced salmonids are unlikely to eliminate the galaxiids from the lake unless the lake is grossly mismanaged by overstocking with trout or salmon or both’’. Gross mismanagement cannot be discounted, given attitudes towards introduced salmonids and indigenous galaxiids in Australia (Cadwallader 1996).

In Patagonian Argentina, landlocked populations of G. maculatus comprise a major food item for introduced salmonids—especially for large brown trout, sometimes for brook charr, and also for some indigenous predatory ﬁshes (f. Percichthyidae). Macchi et al. (1999) found that the galaxiid remained abundant in lakes where predation was taking place, including predation by indigenous species, leading them to conclude that predation was therefore ‘‘not a novelty’’ for G. maculatus. It is one thing, however, for predation to not be a novelty, but possibly something altogether different for the prey to cope with a novel (and additional) predator. The status of the galaxiid populations in these Patagonian lakes therefore needs to be watched with care. Here, again, salmonids seem to earn higher management priority than native ﬁshes (McDowall et al. 2005).

CONSERVATION RANKING: Morris et al. (2000) recommended that G. maculatus should be classed as Data Deﬁcient in Australia mostly to stimulate further study, and they cited habitat deterioration as a cause for concern. I am unaware of any other explicit rankings of G. maculatus as under conservation threat. While this species remains locally abundant across its broad range, it seems, nevertheless, to have suffered from exploitation and habitat degradation. There are hints, from differences in adult ﬁsh size distributions, that G. maculatus also suffers impacts from trout predation in Falkland Islands streams (McDowall et al.

- 2001).

The dune lakes galaxias, Galaxias gracilis, in northern New Zealand

A series of small lakes in former coastal sand dune topography along the west coast of northern New Zealand (Fig. 30, d) contain dune lakes galaxias, G. gracilis (Fig. 23B), which is a landlocked derivative of the catadromous G. maculatus (McDowall 1967, 1970; Ling et al. 2001). There are two series of lakes, Kai Iwi Lakes in the north (Fig. 30—arrow 1) and Poutu Lakes further south (Fig. 30—arrow 2). It grows to about 80 mm, but in some populations ﬁsh mature at

30 mm long (McDowall 1972). Spawning behaviour and habits are undiscovered. The species is entirely lacustrine, as the lakes in general lack stream tributaries. It undergoes ontogenetic habitat shifts associated with different feeding niches, with larvae and juveniles being pelagic and mature adults moving into shore habitats as they grow (Rowe and Chisnall 1996). During the 1950s and 1960s these small lakes were regarded by local anglers as having potential for development as recreational ﬁsheries (Cunningham et al. 1953; Fish 1966), in an area that offered few freshwater angling opportunities. New Zealand lacked suitable game ﬁsh to liberate in lakes that were unsuitable for brown or rainbow trout. After a prolonged literature search, and consultation with overseas authorities (Allen n.d.), North American largemouth bass, Micropterus salmoides (f. Centrarchidae), were chosen for stocking these lakes, and approval was close to being granted.

Substantial objections to this proposal were expressed, however. Then leading, New Zealand freshwater ﬁsh naturalist, Gerald Stokell (1963), thought that ‘‘The project originated in an angler’s brainwave’’, and he pointed out that the faunas already present in the lakes, into which it was proposed to liberate the bass, were unknown.

Fig. 30 Distribution of dune lakes galaxias, Galaxias gracilis, in northern New Zealand: arrow 1—Kai Iwi Lakes; arrow 2—Poutu Lakes; arrow 3—a translocated population outside species’ natural range)

Furthermore, he thought that ‘‘For all the Freshwater Research [the New Zealand Government’s ﬁsheries research and management agency] knows, its bass might soon be exterminating unknown local species which would then be lost to science’’, a speculation that proved correct (see below). Thus several, inter-twined issues beset the bass proposal:

- 1. What would the bass eat?
- 2. How would this affect populations of forage species; and
- 3. What might their impacts be on other biota in the lakes?

A senior New Zealand Government bureaucrat at the time, G.L. O’Halloran, Secretary for Marine (who administered ﬁsheries research and management in New Zealand), responded testily to Stokell, mentioning a ‘‘14 year’’ research programme being undertaken by his staff in one of the lakes to examine its suitability for trout (Fish 1966). Apart from the fact that the research programme lasted nothing like 14 years, O’Halloran claimed that: ‘‘very full consideration has been given [to the effects] in any water to which [largemouth bass might] spread’’. This statement was untrue. As well, he failed to deal with the question of potential impacts on indigenous ﬁshes in the lakes which, as Stokell surmised, did include a then undescribed native ﬁsh species—Galaxias gracilis (McDowall 1967). Neither O’Halloran nor his staff even knew the species was there. Neither did they know that this undescribed species was already under serious threat from his department’s research programme. It was perhaps too much to expect him to know that an undescribed species was threatened if he or his staff did not know it existed! But, as Stokell (1963) remarked, O’Halloran’s response was ‘‘silent on the most important point...the possible destruction of native ﬁshes’’.

At the time that this furore was developing, I was a graduate student at an American University (rather ironically on study leave from O’Halloran’s Marine Department). After discussing this issue with North American biologists, I grew seriously concerned about the superﬁciality of the ‘‘environmental impact assessment’’ (such as it was) undertaken with regard to the proposed

largemouth bass introduction, and particularly about the potential impacts of a large piscivore like largemouth bass in New Zealand ecosystems. Of major concern was the absence of forage ﬁshes for the bass in the target lakes. This, in turn, raised the additional likelihood that there would then be pressures for introductions of smaller ﬁsh species to provide food for the bass. And, at that very time, I was in the process of describing a new Galaxias species from the proposed bass lakes (McDowall 1967). And so I undertook a critique of the documentation prepared to support the case for bass introductions to New Zealand, and I attempted to review ecological theory relating to potential impacts of introduced ﬁsh species (something that had not been done before for introductions of ﬁsh species, as far as I am aware—McDowall 1968b, c). With considerable trepidation, as a junior staff member on paid study leave, I sent two manuscripts back to my employing department in New Zealand for review and, I hoped, approval. Rather to my surprise (and relief) I was given permission to publish both papers. In particular, the paper directly confronting the bass introduction proposal was published in the New Zealand Government’s ‘‘New Zealand Journal of Marine and Freshwater Research’’, and there is (to me at least) a pleasing footnote in that paper, added after proof reading and without my consultation, stating: ‘‘Since this paper went to press the Government has declined to authorise the introduction of largemouth bass into the coastal dune lakes of North Auckland (Cabinet decision of 6 May, 1968’’) (McDowall 1968b). There the matter of bass introduction ended, and thankfully the species has never been introduced to New Zealand, though occasionally it has been mooted anew by those with only a superﬁcial knowledge of history (McDowall 1994a).

The question of additional introductions of small forage ﬁsh to support recreational angling ﬁsheries for other introduced species might seem innocent enough. What damage could such forage ﬁshes do? Well, perhaps much, since reported work by Johannes and Larkin (1961) long ago described ecosystem-wide, damaging effects of introducing Richardsonius balteatus (f. Cyprinidae) into a Canadian rainbow trout lake as a

forage ﬁsh, in an ill-advised attempt to broaden the food base for the trout in that lake.

As a prelude to the wider use of these lakes for recreational angling, an experimental programme was initiated to explore their ﬁsheries potential. Four hundred rainbow trout were liberated into Lake Waingata (a lake in the southern series—Fig. 30, arrow 2), annually, beginning in 1960. At the beginning of Fish’s (1966) study in 1962, ‘‘large shoals’’ of G. gracilis were seen around the shoreline of the lake outside the weed beds (pers. comm. from A. Parker, Marine Department ﬁsheries technician involved in the research: Rowe and Chisnall 1995). Galaxias gracilis was found to be present in the diet of 76% of rainbow trout examined in 1962, but it became so scarce that none could be found in the lake in 1963 or 1964, and it was no longer present in the trout diet, at all (Fish 1966). Thus it took only a couple of years to cause this major decline in the species’ abundance in the lake. All this was happening at about the same time as the Marine Department’s chief, O’Halloran, was pontiﬁcating about the ‘‘need to protect our interesting native ﬁsh species, and particularly those which are not yet known to science’’, yet by that time an undescribed galaxiid was near extirpated in the lake.

Rainbow trout could not breed in Lake Waingata owing to its lack of coarse gravel substrates in which the ﬁsh could dig redds at spawning time, and so maintenance of a ﬁshery depended on continual liberations. These ceased with completion of the research project many years ago, and so the trout population soon died out. Galaxias gracilis has again been recorded in Lake Waingata since that time though it seems never to have recovered its former high abundance there (McDowall 1972; Rowe and Chisnall 1995).

The process of rainbow trout introductions and probably consequential decline in the abundance of G. gracilis was repeated in the Kai Iwi Lakes north of Dargaville (Fig. 30—arrow 1). Cudby and Ewing (1968) and Cudby et al. (1969) reported that they saw many large schools of G. gracilis around the shallow margins of Lake Taharoa, and at that time the ﬁsh was present in the diets of 22% of stocked rainbow trout in the lake. But the galaxiid had completely disappeared

from the trout diet by the following year, and was described as rare by 1971 (Allen et al. 1971). Numbers of G. gracilis plummeted, disappeared from the diet of the trout, and almost from the lake itself, as with Lake Waingata.

An additional, more recent, and apparently growing threat to G. gracilis is rapid spread of Gambusia afﬁnis (Poeciliidae) into Galaxias gracilis habitats. The long-known damaging impacts of gambusia appear to be occurring where this species has been (illicitly) introduced into northern coastal dune lakes. Rowe (1998) documented major damage to Galaxias gracilis in the lake by gambusia. In one study lake, Rowe observed gambusia ‘‘attacking both the head and tail regions of healthy, active [Galaxias gracilis] in the shallows [of the lake]... hundreds of dead and dying [G. gracilis] were found ﬂoating around much of the shoreline’’ and more than ‘‘80% of the 160 ﬁsh examined exhibited severe physical damage to either the tail or head region. Most ﬁsh had no tail ﬁns and could not swim, while others were missing one or both pectoral ﬁns, or had one or more damaged eyes’’.

At the time that reports of Fish and Allen were written (Fish 1966; Allen et al. 1971) there was no equivocation that decline in G. gracilis in Lake Waingata and the Kai Iwi Lakes was due to rainbow trout predation, and there seems no reason now to reconsider that view. It was suggested (McDowall 1990b) that although G. gracilis had not yet had IUCN listing, it probably justiﬁed as much attention from conservationists as any other New Zealand species—its limited distribution of habitats, and the small sizes of the lakes in which it was found, rendered this species vulnerable to decline under pressure from introduced ﬁsh predators.

Rowe (1998) speculated that before decline of G. gracilis in Lake Waikere, rainbow trout had controlled the population of gambusia there, thereby limiting competitive and other impacts between G. gracilis and the gambusia. However, there is no evidence that gambusia was present during the time of studies by Cudby (Cudby and Ewing 1968; Cudby et al. 1969) or Allen et al. (1971); furthermore, gambusia is typically found around the marginal shallows and among shore

vegetation, where rainbow trout would have little access to them. The question of the role of gambusia in the diet of trout certainly needs to be investigated before making any strong conclusions about the role of trout in controlling gambusia populations.

Stocks of G. gracilis have since collapsed in others of the series of dune lakes, into which trout have not been released, and these later declines have been coincident with a series of other environmental changes in the lakes (Rowe and Chisnall 1995; Gleeson et al. 1999a). However, eels have been liberated into some lakes (illicitly) in an effort to increase production of the commercial eel ﬁsheries, and further exotic ﬁsh such as gambusia, rudd—Scardinius erythrophthalmus, and tench—Tinca tinca (both Cyprinidae), have been released illicitly by anarchic coarse ﬁsh anglers. Anguillid eels are unable to enter the lakes owing to their lack of streams draining from them to the sea, and so G. gracilis has never had to cope with eel predation. Exotic aquatic macrophytes have also taken over some lakes, an event often associated with eutrophication of lake waters, and probably relating to increasingly intensiﬁed pastoral farming in the lake catchments—where there have been serious problems with farm cattle wading in the lakes’ shallows, discharging body wastes into the water and contributing to a decline in water quality.

And further, there has been development of exotic forestry plantations in the catchments of some, speciﬁcally Pinus radiata (Pinaceae). These have potential to signiﬁcantly reduce runoff derived from rainfall (Hicks et al. 2004) thus reducing inﬂow of water into these lakes with very small catchments and generally lacking, or having very small, intermittent, tributary streams. This results in reduced lake levels and slower replenishment of water in the lakes. All of these effects may have become compounded causing extirpation of G. gracilis in some lakes, and major reductions in abundance in others. The responses of the galaxiid populations to these anthropogenic impacts seem erratic and remain unexplained (Rowe and Chisnall 1995, 1997; McDowall and Rowe 1996; Rowe 1998), even though the role of rainbow trout predation in the initial major, rapid

declines in abundance of G. gracilis in Lakes Waingata and Taharoa seems unarguable.

A somewhat contradictory scenario, however, applies to a population of G. gracilis in an additional, nearby lake. Despite G. gracilis having suffered population collapse that can be attributed to trout predation in several lakes, local ﬁshery managers translocated stocks of the galaxiid into Lake Ototoa, nearby (Fig. 30—arrow 3) (Thompson 1989), with the intention of providing a new food source for rainbow trout stocked there. Any reasonable pre-release assessment would have concluded, on the basis of the experience of major and rapid decline of G. gracilis in Lake Waingata (Fish 1966) and the Kai Iwi Lakes (Cudby and Ewing 1968; Cudby et al. 1969; Allen et al. 1971), that translocated dune lakes galaxias stock would not survive trout predation in Lake Ototoa, but that assessment (had one been undertaken) would have been wrong. Galaxias gracilis has thrived in an unexpected way in Lake Ototoa, providing an enduring forage resource for the trout population there, contrary to experience elsewhere. An explicit explanation for this paradox is not available, but it may be that Lake Ototoa has an extensive fringe of emergent rushes around its marginal shallows that may provide a refuge for the galaxiid and allows its successful occupation of the lake, despite trout predation in open waters of the lake. Low stocking rates of trout in Lake Ototoa may be inﬂuential. European perch have recently (illegally) been introduced into Lake Ototoa (Syron 2003), and it will be interesting to see whether G. gracilis in Lake Ototoa can cope with an extra and behaviourally different ﬁsh predator. Experimental manipulation of ﬁsh populations of small ponds containing perch and common bullies (Gobiomorphus cotidianus, f. Eleotridae) indicate that perch signiﬁcantly deplete the bully populations (Closs et al. 2003); perch would predictably have similar, and perhaps more adverse, impacts on Galaxias gracilis.

CONSERVATION RANKING: Complexities in the taxonomic recognition of these populations may pose additional threats to conserving the full taxonomic and genetic diversity in this species

(Ling et al. 2001). That question aside, an outcome of events discussed above has been the classiﬁcation of G. gracilis: ﬁrst as Category C (third priority), then Category B (second priority) by the New Zealand Department of Conservation (Molloy and Davis 1992; Tisdall 1994), and most recently Serious Decline (Hitchmough 2002). The

- 1996 IUCN listing had this species as Vulnerable (Baillie and Groombridge 1996) and this remained unchanged in the 2004 IUCN Red List of Threatened Species (IUCN 2006).

The Department of Conservation treats the populations from two groups of lakes (Kai Iwi Lakes and those on the north head of the Kaipara Harbour (Fig. 30) as distinct taxa owing to their apparently diphyletic relationships with the diadromous G. maculatus; a recovery plan providing for management strategies to ensure the survival of these ﬁsh has been prepared (Allibone and Barrier 2004)

The New Zealand and Australian Galaxias brevipinnis: a winner and a loser

Galaxias brevipinnis (see Fig. 31A) is one of the larger galaxiids, reaching a length of about 290 mm; it is a strongly built benthic/cryptic ﬁsh found typically as an adult in swift-ﬂowing bouldery streams, often in steep landscapes of mountain foothills. Typically, it is found in streams with indigenous riparian forest (McDowall 1990b; Eikaas et al. 2005a, b). It is very widely distributed, being found in coastal drainages of eastern Australia from around Sydney southwards, west along Australia’s south coast to near Adelaide and on Kangaroo Island, widely in Tasmania, and also in New Zealand, the Chatham Islands about 800 km east of mainland New Zealand, and in the Auckland and Campbell Islands, well to the south (Fig. 32).

This species spawns during elevated ﬂows in small streams with riparian native forest; the eggs are laid over temporarily inundated marginal stream gravels, sometimes several metres from the stream channel at normal ﬂows, and when the elevated ﬂows abate, the eggs are left stranded, to develop in the humid atmosphere among the gravels (O’Connor and Koehn 1998; Allibone and Caskey 2000). Development takes several weeks

Fig. 31 Large, slender-bodied New Zealand species of Galaxias: (A) G. brevipinnis (reaches >295 mm, the only diadromous species among this group); (B) G. vulgaris (>150 mm); (C) G. depressiceps (>105 mm); (D) G. anomalus (>140 mm); (E) G. pullus (>140 mm); (F) G. gollumoides (>75 mm); (G) G. eldoni (>105 mm)

(depending on ambient temperatures), and the eggs hatch during another period of elevated ﬂows that inundate the marginal spawning gravels—a seemingly risky strategy that depends on arrival of ﬂoods at appropriate times for the eggs to hatch. Quite probably the eggs need substantial

Fig. 32 Distribution of Galaxias brevipinnis, in southern Australia, Tasmania, New Zealand, and associated islands in the Southern Ocean

agitation to stimulate hatching, as otherwise heavy rainfall would do so, and that would often leave the larvae stranded in the stream-side gravels. The larvae are swept to sea by the elevated stream ﬂows (this perhaps being optimised by stream ﬂows being elevated), and the larvae feed and grow at sea for 17–20 weeks before returning in spring as well grown whitebait juveniles. Again, in New Zealand and Tasmania the returning juveniles are exploited in the whitebait ﬁsheries (McDowall and Eldon 1980; McDowall et al. 1994).

Galaxias brevipinnis reaches its favoured freshwater habitats by migrations upstream from the sea, being known as far as 400 km inland. Migration is probably largely by juveniles. Galaxias brevipinnis has long been known as a very proﬁcient climber of falls, and penetrates upstream past what appear to be formidable barriers to ﬁsh passage (Harper 1896; McDowall 1990b: Fig. 33). This explains why Australians call this ﬁsh ‘‘climbing galaxias’’ (Green 1979; Allen et al.

- 2002). Climbing is probably most successful by small individuals, whose higher surface area to weight ratio helps them to adhere to the wetted rock surfaces in marginal splash zones lateral to the main ﬂows of falls. Downward facing pectoral and pelvic ﬁns provide surface contacts that make adhesion and climbing possible. The presence of G. brevipinnis in Boulder Lake, at an elevation of nearly 1,000 m in the mountains inland from Nelson, in New Zealand, which discharges via a fall ca. 65 m high provides an explicit indication of the ability of this ﬁsh to climb and this may not be unusual, given the widespread presence of this species in many lakes of the Southern Alps in New Zealand and the high elevation plateau of

central Tasmania. It climbs the walls of a velocity barrier inserted into a power canal to prevent upstream migration of ﬁshes (Fig. 33). Climbing is facilitated by the presence of downward facing paired ﬁns (Fig. 34A) with distinctive rugosities (Fig. 34B) on the ventral surfaces (McDowall 2003a). We can only speculate, but it seems likely that the corrugations originally evolved to assist the ﬁsh in holding their place in very swift stream ﬂows (where they typically live); and if this is so, the corrugations may then have been a preadaptation that facilitates climbing.

Amphidromous migrations in G. brevipinnis are highly facultative, and landlocked populations are common and widespread in lakes of upland, southeastern, Australia, Tasmania, and New Zealand. Galaxias brevipinnis can complete its life cycle within lakes that lack any inﬂowing tributaries, and so must spawn somewhere in the lakes themselves. In many lakes there is a pattern of migrations between lakes and their tributaries that mirrors migrations between the sea and rivers in amphidromous populations. There is also evidence, in some lake populations, of a shift in the timing of reproduction, from late autumn and winter in amphidromous populations, to

Fig. 33 A velocity barrier constructed to prevent upstream migration of ﬁshes (ﬁsh can be seen clinging to vertical concrete wall of structure) (photo: E.J. Cudby)

spring in lake populations. This may have happened to optimise growth and survival rates, structured around the best time for larval ﬁsh to be feeding and surviving in the lake plankton (study of this question is needed).

Because of its capacity for inland/upstream penetration in river systems, G. brevipinnis is one of the few galaxiids found in many inland lakes, and has numerous landlocked populations in higher elevation/submontane to montane lakes throughout the species’ distribution in both New Zealand (D in Fig. 35) and Australia. Though

Fig. 34 Pectoral ﬁn of Galaxias brevipinnis: (A) Showing down-facing lamina of ﬁn, ellipse encloses area of ﬁn with ventral rugosities; (B) Close up of rugosities on ventral surface of ﬁn (photos: Nelson Boustead)

G. brevipinnis is generally regarded as favouring coarse substrate, swift-ﬂowing streams, in some lakes there are populations both in adﬂuvial streams and in/on the beds of the lakes themselves. The adﬂuvial and lake-bed populations in any lake appear to comprise a single genetic stock (Young 2002).

The status of Galaxias brevipinnis in New Zealand: There are few published observations about the conservation status of riverine, amphidromous populations of G. brevipinnis in New Zealand. There can be no doubt that the whitebait juveniles (along with the juveniles of other whitebait galaxiids) migrating into river estuaries from the sea during spring (McDowall and Eldon 1980), have been heavily harvested in the whitebait ﬁshery for 150 years (McDowall 1984a). Allibone and McIntosh (1999) thought that predation by trout on juveniles of G. brevipinnis was also likely to be particularly common, especially during the long-distance inland penetration of the juveniles. Initially this will be by large, estuarineliving/anadromous brown trout that are feeding intensively to recover condition following the previous winter’s spawning (McDowall 1990b).

Fig. 35 Distribution of Galaxias brevipinnis in New Zealand (note substantial inland penetration by this diadromous species (d); many of the inland sites (D) are landlocked populations in submontane lakes). Arrow 1—Rotorua lakes; arrow 2—Lake Taupo

There may also be predation by native estuarineliving ﬂounders of the genus Rhombosolea, most especially R. retiaria, which also penetrates substantial distances inland (known to 250 km inland—McDowall 2000a), as well as by the largely marine Arripis trutta (f. Arripidae), and possibly also by abundant, piscivorous, estuarine birds such as gulls, terns and cormorants.

Long-distance invasions of New Zealand rivers by G. brevipinnis mean that during migration they may have to run a gauntlet of habitats populated with high densities of large trout, though this will depend on their migratory behaviours—about which little is known. Once upstream beyond the estuaries, their migrations may no longer be in large shoals (McDowall and Eldon 1980), and there are hints that the migrating juveniles may become benthic and semi-cryptic once beyond the river estuaries, at least if the rivers have coarse gravel substrate. If this is so, then increasingly cryptic behaviour by the migrating juveniles may minimise their availability to predators like trout and various birds.

Impacts on G. brevipinnis populations by the combination of ﬁshery exploitation, and trout, ﬂounder and other predators, as well as estuarine aquatic birds, and other large marine ﬁsh around river mouths and estuaries, are not documented, though it is clear that production from the whitebait ﬁshery declined substantially during the 20th century (McDowall 1984a). This reduction is likely to have involved declines in the abundance of G. brevipinnis, though it still makes up a substantial proportion of the whitebait catch in some rivers, especially those that are glacierfed, and in which spring ﬂows are turbid from snow melt (McDowall and Eldon 1980).

Adverse impacts on G. brevipinnis, other than ﬁsheries exploitation and trout predation, are likely. Contemporary observations suggest that G. brevipinnis is largely nocturnally active (Glova and Sagar 1989b; McDowall 1990b). Hayes (1996a), however, observed adult G. brevipinnis feeding on insect drift by day in a river free of brown trout, in much the same manner as brown trout drift feed. He hypothesised that this open, daytime foraging behaviour may have been more common before introduction of salmonids (in the last third of the 19th century—McDowall 1994a),

and the competition for both space and food that this engenders (in addition to any novel predation that may have resulted from those introductions). The implications of Hayes’ (1996a) observations are that trout may be provoking changes in feeding behaviour/locations of G. brevipinnis in much the same way as in related, though nondiadromous, ﬁshes of the G. vulgaris species complex in southeastern New Zealand (McIntosh 2000a, b; McIntosh and Townsend 1996, 1998).

The diet of G. brevipinnis is based extensively on stream benthic insects (Glova and Sagar 1989b), as is also true of brown and rainbow trout. Terrestrial invertebrate foods may also be important (Main and Winterbourn 1987). Glova (1989) observed behavioural interactions of G. brevipinnis and brown trout in a stream simulator, and he found that the two species tended to feed at different times, G. brevipinnis at night and brown trout by day. He speculated that these ‘‘behavioural differences [were] obviously of beneﬁt’’ to their coexistence, arguing that competition for food would be minimised, and be ‘‘indirect’’. He did not, however, observe or comment on the behaviour of the galaxiids in the absence of the trout and the nocturnal feeding behaviour of the galaxiids ﬁsh may have been an outcome of negative interactions with trout as hypothesised by Hayes (1996a), and demonstrated by McIntosh for the related G. vulgaris. Glova speculated that the galaxiids were living in a ‘‘trout-dominant society’’, which made it difﬁcult for the G. brevipinnis to ‘‘acquire suitable feeding and resting sites during the day’’, but considered this evidence ‘‘circumstantial’’, and concluded that ‘‘native and salmonid ﬁshes are not the best neighbours’’. He then postulated that predation was more likely to be important than food competition (Glova 1989), and he later reiterated this view (Glova 1990). How he drew such conclusions from his data is unclear and, quite possibly, both predation and a shift in feeding behaviour are important. Possibly, too, the outcome of interactions drove occupation of separate niches or habitats, minimising the conﬂict for resources.

Glova and Sagar (1991) later studied the diet of G. brevipinnis in the Ryton River, a tributary of Lake Coleridge, in the headwaters of the Rakaia

River—a lake that carries stocks of rainbow trout, brown trout, and landlocked Chinook salmon, and which is an important recreational salmonid ﬁshery. They again described overlaps in the diet of G. brevipinnis and the two trout species, but found that there were diel differences in feeding activity and habitat that they considered might segregate the feeding ﬁsh both temporally and spatially. Night feeding would increase the availability of aquatic insects in the drift, since this is mostly nocturnal. However, once again, the spatio-temporal differences between G. brevipinnis and ﬂuvial trout could have been driven by, rather than minimising, species interactions, a question that was not addressed.

Main et al. (1985) in a survey of ﬁsh populations in the rivers of Westland (New Zealand) found G. brevipinnis to be absent from streams containing brown trout, and concluded: ‘‘It is...almost certain that [G. brevipinnis] has been affected by negative interactions with salmonids, and may even be restricted to rifﬂe refuges’’. Though there is no documentation of any changes in the abundance of amphidromous populations, it can be conjectured that deforestation has seriously affected the populations owing to this species favouring streams with riparian forest (Taylor and Main 1987; Main and Winterbourn

- 1987; Eikaas et al. 2005a). But, because G. brevipinnis favours quite steep, swiftly ﬂowing streams, it is possible that impacts from deforestation have been less serious than for other ﬁsh species in more lowland streams. Though there has been no explicit documentation of any changes in the abundance of amphidromous populations, it can be conjectured that deforestation has seriously affected the populations.

Galaxias brevipinnis, smelt introductions and the Lake Taupo trout ﬁshery: Prior to European colonisation of New Zealand, which began about the mid 19th century, G. brevipinnis, and especially its shoaling juveniles, was a food source of great importance to indigenous Maori people who lived near inland lakes. This was especially true where Maori communities had no easy access to marine foods as was true of Lake Taupo, New Zealand’s largest lake (Fig. 35—arrow 2) in the central North Island, but also in other areas (Hiroa 1921; Best 1929; Grace 1959). Accounts tell of Maoris taking this ﬁsh by the ‘hundredweight’ (Armstrong 1935, = 112 lbs, or ca. 50 kg) using huge seine nets around the shores of Lake Taupo, or even between canoes in mid-lake (Angas 1847—Fig. 36; Burstall 1983).

Early European explorers’ descriptions of travels through New Zealand include references to their being given meals, based on juveniles of G. brevipinnis, by Maori tribes living around lakes (Cooper 1851; Frame 2002). Bidwell (1841) described capture of juvenile G. brevipinnis from Lake Rotoaira (just a few kilometres south of Lake Taupo: Fig. 35—arrow 2), and related how Maori people around the lake spread the ﬁsh out on stones to dry in the sun and, having ‘‘vast quantities’’, would cook them ‘‘by making them into a kind of soup’’. Similar patterns of usage around Taupo are certain as the Maori people around both lakes belonged to similar tribal groups (Grace 1959). And the same appears to have been true of lakes of the Rotorua area further north (Fig. 35—arrow 1) (Fletcher 1919a, b; Cowan 1930; Andersen and Petersen 1956).

Fletcher (1919a) described how bundles of brush or fern were tied together and then to each

Fig. 36 Painting by the 19th century painter G.F. Angas showing Maori men in a canoe ﬁshing for juvenile koaro, Galaxias brevipinnis, netting ﬁsh from Lake Taupo, probably from the 1830s or 1840s

other with ropes. These were submerged in the depths of Lake Taupo using stone anchors, with sometimes ‘‘as many as thirty bundles of fern...attached to the main line. The bundles would be lifted during the daytime, and very great care taken to pull the bundles up one at a time in such a way that no sudden jar was given to them. The bundles were either shaken into the canoe, or a net...was passed under the bundle to catch the [Galaxias] as they fell from the fern’’.

Galaxias brevipinnis was once so abundant in Lake Taupo that Fletcher (1919b) wrote of travelling across the lake by boat and passing through what he assumed was an ‘‘enormous shoal of [introduced] trout. We were nearly ten minutes passing from one side to the other side of the shoal... [and he further remarked] One calm day two years ago, when crossing from Taupo to Tokaanu [a distance of over 50 km] I was not out of the sight of rising ﬁsh’’. However, Armstrong (1935) deduced, and probably correctly, that the rising ﬁsh that Fletcher had seen were not trout, but were ‘‘Galaxias feeding on Chironomus [midges], and accompanied and attacked by the trout’’. Other early reports tell of G. brevipinnis being so abundant in Lake Taupo that local people could walk around leeward lake shores after major wind storms, and collect adult ﬁsh thrown ashore by the waves, in numbers large enough to constitute a recognised food source (Frame 2002).

Brown trout were introduced to Lake Taupo in 1886, and rainbow trout in 1903 (Burstall 1983), leading to a ﬁshery that became rapidly and justiﬁably famous (Fletcher 1919b). These two exotic salmonid predators exploded in abundance to produce an outstanding recreational trout ﬁshery, visited by anglers from throughout the world—notably including American novelist Zane Grey (Grey 1982), and the Duchess of York (later British Queen Elizabeth as wife of King George VI, and after whom the Duchess Pool in the Tongariro River, a major Taupo tributary, is still named—Hickling 1960). The Lake Taupo trout ﬁshery reached a zenith in the early 20th century (Burstall 1975), and there are stories of huge angler catches of very large trout. Fletcher (1919b) and Armstrong (1935) described

proliferation of rainbow trout in Lake Taupo in the late 1800s and early 1900s. Fletcher (1919b) reported that they ‘‘grew so rapidly and so large that in 1906 they had been caught up to and over 20 lb in weight’’, and he mentioned how rainbow trout had taken over from the previously introduced, and very abundant, brown trout. All this led Armstrong (1935) to enthuse that ‘‘From the numbers reported it was quite evident that they were thriving in a most amazing manner’’.

Invasion of the lake by such huge numbers of large predators could not have happened without substantial impacts on the indigenous biota, and there were. During the early 1900s, the trout ﬁshery deteriorated very sharply, and the lake became seriously overpopulated with very large numbers of trout in poor condition (Burstall 1975, 1983: Fig. 37). Fletcher (1919b), for instance, described how trout in the lake were ‘‘beginning to feel a shortage of food. This is shown by their voracity and the number of thin ﬁsh caught early in the season’’. Phillipps (1924), not surprisingly found that the huge shoals of juvenile G. brevipinnis in Lake Taupo were the major food resource for generating trout growth. And it needs to be remembered that Phillipps’ work was undertaken after a prolonged period (>35 years) of trout predation, by which time galaxiid abundance was probably already greatly reduced, as a consequence. And, although there are no quantitative data (as is so often true), it seems certain that changes in the abundance of

Fig. 37 Fluctuations in average weight of trout taken from Lake Taupo in the central North Island of New Zealand, a decline that parallels decline in lake population of Galaxias brevipinnis (from Burstall 1983)

G. brevipinnis in Lake Taupo that have clearly taken place, can be attributed directly and at least substantially, to trout predation. There seems no other logical cause. Similar conclusions can be drawn for other lakes (Hayes 1986, in Lake Alexandrina, for instance).

Wilson (1995) has attributed the impacts on G. brevipinnis primarily to predation by brown trout, and argued for the decline to have taken place prior to introduction of rainbows. This is an interesting if somewhat ingenuous idea that may warrant further exploration, but it needs to be recognised that brown trout were only in Lake Taupo for a decade before rainbow trout arrived (Burstall 1983). And, in the context of galaxiid conservation, it does not much matter which species had the greatest impacts. The key element,

- as far as the galaxiid is concerned, was that it underwent very substantial decline in abundance around the end of the 19th and beginning of the 20th centuries, as a result of ‘‘trout’’ predation (and see discussion below on the alleged impacts of rainbow trout in nearby Lake Rotoaira).

What had clearly happened in Lake Taupo was that stocks of G. brevipinnis that had hitherto provided a much valued food supply for local Maori communities living around the lake shores, had been so ‘‘eaten down’’ that the trout were starving. The reproductive output of trout in the lake’s tributaries was clearly greatly exceeding the capacity of the lake to fuel growth of the trout to maturity and maintains them in good condition (McDowall 1990b). Fletcher (1919b) thought deterioration in the condition of the trout was due simply to a lack of food: ‘‘As the small native trout [which he mistakenly called Galaxias fasciatus] is becoming scarce and the brown trout is becoming rare, the lake must either be stocked with suitable trout food or the number of trout considerably reduced’’.

Trout had become so abundant that Fletcher (1919b) suggested that ‘‘2000 or 3000 tons ...could be taken out of the lake every year [and] no harm would be done, but rather the remainder would be the better for such thinning out...An army of men could be employed in netting, cleaning and smoking trout for the breakfast tables of New Zealand’’. Interestingly, Fletcher (1919b) also hypothesised that reduction in the Galaxias pop-

ulations of the lake by the trout had also resulted in the shags (or cormorants, Phalacrocorax spp.) around the lake taking ‘‘toll of the young trout much more than they did a few years ago’’. There were clearly thought to be some major ecosystemscale effects going on.

Armstrong drew a contrast between the observations of Fletcher (1919a, b) and his own in the 1930s, noting that by 1935, ‘‘one rarely hears of even a single trout rising in deep water’’ and, as a result, Armstrong (1935) lamented that ‘‘more remarkable still has been the almost complete destruction of Galaxias [brevipinnis] in the lake...the trout rapidly brought about its complete extermination in the deeper waters of the lake. In shallow waters round the edge of the lake the shoals of Galaxias may still be seen, but harried by the shag, and in active competition with the young trout for the available food supply, they are steadily decreasing in numbers’’. Notably, this was at a time when there had been trout predation there for several decades. Armstrong (1935) reported on his examination the stomach contents of 200 trout in 1926 and in no case found Galaxias in their stomachs, whereas analyses by Phillipps (1924) revealed large numbers of galaxiids. It is interesting that in a review of the relations between salmonids and the native freshwater fauna in New Zealand, Allen (1961) stated that ‘‘In lakes...small ﬁsh are mainly eaten by the larger salmonids [brown and rainbow trout], the particular species consumed varying according to the composition of the local fauna...many of our lakes contain numerous small native ﬁsh which provide good trout food; and he speciﬁed ‘‘bullies’’ (f. Eleotridae) and smelts (f. Retropinnidae). He provided no data, nor did he make any mention of galaxiids, and of G. brevipinnis in particular. Possibly by 1961, G. brevipinnis in the trout lakes was so seriously diminished that it no longer contributed to trout food.

Today, about 70 years after the time of Armstrong’s (1935) observations, G. brevipinnis appears to be vastly reduced in abundance in Lake Taupo. Adult galaxiids are no longer swept ashore by storms and, in fact, seem to be virtually absent from the shallows around the lake shores. Even shoals of ‘whitebait juveniles’, which

migrate into lake tributaries for growth to adulthood and for reproduction, are no longer so abundant around the lake shores, and neither adults nor whitebait juveniles are available as a food for the Maori communities that still live around the shores of the lake.1

Not all the apparent loss of galaxiids from Lake Taupo can be attributed to predation by adult trout in the lake. Kusabs (1989) reported that in lake tributaries ‘‘the population density of G. brevipinnis is negatively correlated with trout density’’, and he observed predation by juvenile trout on galaxiids in the streams. Kusabs and Swales (1991) also observed broad overlap in the diets of G. brevipinnis and trout in tributaries of Lake Taupo. They thought that the trout may have out-competed the galaxiid, displacing them from some habitats and preying upon them in others, to the point of extinction. And they found the galaxiid to be now rare in the food of trout owing to its scarcity, as ‘‘only remnants remain in the lake and its tributaries’’. The galaxiid is now, it seems, common only in habitats where it is able to avoid direct competition from trout.

Nor is trout predation on the whitebait juveniles the only impact, Phillipps (1940a) reporting the capture of a trout taken in Lake Rotoiti, in the stomach of which there was a large G. brevipinnis >180 mm long.

Though it seems clear that predation by two introduced trout species on G. brevipinnis populations has contributed initially, and substantially, to the decline of the galaxiid, other factors seem likely to have entered the equation, as indicated by ongoing events. As noted before, to alleviate the decline in food available for trout, an indigenous retropinnid, the common smelt, Retropinna retropinna (another galaxioid), was liberated into Lake Taupo. Despite R. retropinna stocks having apparently come, originally, from an anadromous

- 1 As discussed for Retropinna retropinna above local Maori communities have been arguing for rights to harvest the ﬂourishing populations of landlocked Retropinna retropinna, which has become established in the lake, as a replacement forage species for the lake’s trout populations, this right being asserted as a form of compensation for the loss of their traditional food supply, the lake-living juveniles of G. brevipinnis—Anon 1996.

(sea migratory) stock in the lower Waikato River, the species rapidly establishes landlocked populations in lakes.
