It quickly developed high abundance in Lake Taupo and, for reasons that are not understood, remains abundant in the lake, and has proved capable of withstanding long-term trout predation to the extent that it now forms the major source of food (Smith 1959; Stephens 1984; Cryer 1990; Greaves 2006) for a trout stock that has substantially recovered, restoring its international reputation as a ﬁshery of world class (even though the huge trout captured during the founding years are rare).

Perhaps the ultimate irony relating to decline in galaxiid abundance in the lake was a complaint by Phillipps (1924), that the local Maori people were causing ‘‘continuous destruction of trout food’’, when the lake had, before trout were introduced, been their subsistence ﬁshery. Centuries old ﬁsheries rights seem to have been subjugated to allow the ﬁsh to support a ﬁshery based on an introduced species (McDowall 1990b; Anon 1996). Another perspective might be that when stocks in the lake were decimated by trout predation, the Maori people lost access to a valued food resource (McDowall 1990b). Moreover, though European settlers might have thought that replacement of the galaxiid by trout might have made a better food source for the Maori people, that was not their opinion on the matter—they preferred the galaxiid.

Extirpation in Lake Rotopounamu: A scenario from another small lake close to Lake Taupo bears on our understanding of these events. Galaxias brevipinnis remained abundant into the late 1980s in Lake Rotopounamu, a tiny tarn (0.04 square km) set in forest, on a hill saddle a little south of Lake Taupo (Kusabs 1989). Some unknown individual unlawfully liberated R. retropinna into Lake Rotopounamu (perhaps as a planned prelude to an unauthorised liberation of trout there), and within a year to two, the G. brevipinnis population had completely disappeared. Rowe (1993) concluded that introduction of R. retropinna had led to this loss of the galaxiid. The cause-and-effect conclusion he reached was based entirely, but quite reasonably, on the coincidence of G. brevipinnis extirpation following rapidly after R. retropinna introduction. There is no other logical explanation.

Lake Rotopounamu lies in virgin forest, there is no human population, agriculture, active forestry, or industry anywhere in its catchment, no discharge of pollutants into the lake, and no other reasonable cause of the extirpation of G. brevipinnis can be imagined. Though R. retropinna is usually a small species in landlocked lake populations, rarely reaching much more than about 60–70 mm in Lake Taupo, it appears likely, nevertheless, that sub-adult and adult R. retropinna, which are at times pelagic in the open waters of lakes, have simply preyed upon the pelagic larvae and juveniles of the G. brevipinnis in Lake Rotopounamu, to extirpation.

If the above scenario correctly reﬂects what has happened in Lake Rotopounamu, it may have signiﬁcant implications for understanding the present, highly impoverished, status of G. brevipinnis stocks in Lake Taupo. Given the widely distributed and persistent high abundance of R. retropinna in Lake Taupo, it is entirely conceivable that the now greatly depressed state of G. brevipinnis populations there is being exacerbated by pervasive R. retropinna abundance and predation—logically, again, predation by adult R. retropinna on pelagic/planktonic larvae and small juveniles of G. brevipinnis. Perhaps there has also been competition in the surface waters of the lake by both species for a common source of food in the lake plankton.

Can we, then, look forward to extirpation of G. brevipinnis in Lake Taupo? There is no sign of this being imminent, though it remains a distinct possibility and could easily happen, almost unnoticed. I know of no quantitative information on the abundance of G. brevipinnis in Lake Taupo. Jeppeson et al. (1997) thought that densities of larval Retropinna in New Zealand lakes may be high enough to inﬂuence plankton community structure—and if this is so, then there could be dual effects from introducing Retropinna, in Lake Taupo, which could be outcompeting the larval G. brevipinnis for plankton-sourced food, as well

- as preying on galaxiid larvae in the lakes.

Galaxias brevipinnis in Lake Rotoaira: The status of G. brevipinnis populations in lakes elsewhere in New Zealand is even less clearly understood, but it seems likely that G. brevipinnis

numbers in lakes have declined seriously across the species’ range. Perhaps in some lakes, trout numbers are limited by spawning habitat availability, and so impacts of G. brevipinnis may be less than elsewhere. Rowe et al. (2002) explored abundance of G. brevipinnis in Lake Rotoaira, immediately south of Lake Taupo, which also traditionally supported a traditional Maori ﬁshery for G. brevipinnis (Grace 1959; Cudby 1984). The rainbow trout has long been present in that lake, but R. retropinna has not been introduced there. Rowe et al. (2002) found G. brevipinnis to be ‘‘common’’ in some tributaries of Lake Rotoaira but ‘‘rare’’ in others. Sometimes it shared streams with juvenile trout, but sometimes had complementary distributions with trout, the galaxiid then being present only upstream beyond the upper limits of the range of rainbow trout. In general, G. brevipinnis was rare where rainbow trout were abundant (and so here again emerges this persistent theme in discussion of the distributions of trout and galaxiids, throughout the range of galaxiids). Galaxias brevipinnis was also sometimes found in streams ‘‘too small to support trout’’. Rowe et al. (2002) noted a sharp contrast between ‘‘virtual absence’’ of G. brevipinnis in streams where trout were abundant and lesser abundance in other streams where trout were common. He concluded that O. mykiss can reduce lacustrine stocks of G. brevipinnis by two, or possibly three, mechanisms.

- • First, trout may displace adult G. brevipinnis from streams, and this can be expected to reduce recruitment of larvae.
- • Second, decline may result from direct predation by trout on galaxiid juveniles in the lake (and dietary studies illustrate the importance of the galaxiid in the food of trout in the 1920s—Phillipps 1924). Rowe (1990) noted that galaxiid larvae are surface living in the lake, but that the smelt, by day, are found at depths of several metres; however, the smelt migrate into the surface waters of lakes, at night, and predation on the galaxiid may occur then.
- • Third, competition between larvae and juveniles of R. retropinna and G. brevipinnis for the same zooplankton foods in these

lakes may have inﬂuenced decline of the galaxiid.

To all of these may also be added a fourth possible adverse trout impact—the trout may prey on the galaxiid juveniles as the latter migrate upstream in lake tributaries inhabited by both young and adult O. mykiss, as reported in several tributaries of Lake Taupo by Kusabs and Swales (1991).

A yet further, and more subtle, cause of decline in G. brevipinnis in Lake Rotoaira is possible. The galaxiid seems to have remained abundant in Lake Rotoaira into the 1960s despite the presence of rainbow trout. Decline of G. brevipinnis since that time may have been driven, in part, by subsequent use of the lake as a reservoir for hydro-electricity production. Diversion of water from two substantial rivers (upper reaches of the Wanganui and Tongariro River, some water in the latter also derived from the Rangitikei) into and through Lake Rotoaira, and then through a power station on the shores of Lake Taupo, will have increased the rate of water turnover in the lake, and this may, in turn, have entrained G. brevipinnis larvae in the surface waters of the lake in ﬂows out of the lake through the power station, and into Lake Taupo.

The scenario in the Rotorua lakes: Rowe et al.

- (2002) thought that brown trout, introduced into Lake Rotorua in 1888, had also instigated a decline in the Maori ﬁshery for G. brevipinnis in that lake, and the same almost certainly applies to all of the many lakes in that area (Fig. 35—arrow 1). Rowe was not alone in blaming trout for the decline. Maori communities in the Rotorua district, as at Taupo, had long regarded G. brevipinnis as a traditional food resource, considering it as ‘‘much more appetizing than the introduced trout’’ (Hiroa 1921; Burstall 1983). Makeriti Papakura (see Penniman 1938) described how the ‘‘old Maori was an expert in taking freshwater ﬁsh, and the people who lived inland had an enormous supply... until the Europeans introduced the trout and other ﬁsh. Now our freshwater ﬁsh have almost entirely disappeared’’. The Maori people complained to the Government, about the changed abundance of G. brevipinnis

following introduction of trout, and partly because, having lost access to this formerly proliﬁc, valued, and freely available food resource, they then found that they were required to purchase an angling licence to catch trout that had probably caused this loss (Stafford 1986). And so the local Maori community requested local government authorities that no more trout should be introduced into the lake, in part in the hope of protecting their traditional galaxiid ﬁshery. Their protests were ignored and, as far as brown trout were concerned, it was already too late. As well, and despite their pleas, rainbow trout were also released in the lakes in 1898 and, as Rowe (1990) commented, the native ﬁshery has never recovered. It is unclear which of the two trout species has had the greatest impact. Though, as noted above, Wilson (1995) described brown trout as the main villain, it makes no difference as far as both the galaxiid and the Maori people were concerned.

As in Lake Taupo (as discussed above), the presence of R. retropinna enters the scenario in Lake Rotorua. Studies of the composition of the diet of rainbow trout in Lake Rotorua, and in other nearby lakes such as Okataina and Rotoiti, show that in 1921 the food of the trout consisted substantially of G. brevipinnis. Comparable data for 1959 show that in all lakes, R. retropinna predominated in the trout’s diets (Smith 1959), with no G. brevipinnis recorded from Lake Rotorua, virtually none from Lake Okataina, and few (ca. 10%) from Rotoiti (Fig. 38). Rowe (1990) asked:

- • Did trout [by 1957] prefer smelt and simply ignore juvenile koaro [G. brevipinnis]?
- • Or had juvenile koaro virtually disappeared from the lakes and been replaced by smelt?

He clariﬁed these questions by undertaking seine net sampling around the shores of Lakes Rotorua and Rotoiti, when he ‘‘caught many smelt but not a single juvenile galaxiid’’. His predictable conclusion was that G. brevipinnis had ‘‘virtually disappeared...Today, juvenile [G. brevipinnis] are rare in the central North Island lakes. Adults persist in inlet streams and are sometimes found in the lakes’ littoral zones, at

Fig. 38 Changing diet of salmonids in central North Island lakes in 1921 and 1957, showing replacement of Galaxias by Retropinna in the food of the ﬁsh (from Rowe 1990)

night. Juvenile G. brevipinnis have almost vanished from the lakes’’.

Though it seems clear that trout predation has had substantial impacts on galaxiid populations in the lakes, Rowe (1990) argued, as he had for Lakes Taupo and Rotoaira, that R. retropinna in the lakes (as a replacement forage species for the trout) has had additional, cumulative, negative impacts on the galaxiid populations. The galaxiid remains quite abundant in some lakes with trout but no R. retropinna, and attributed decline of galaxiids in lakes, shared with rainbow trout, to smelt predation on larval and juvenile galaxiids. Young (2002) investigated aspects of the ecology of G. brevipinnis in several lakes in the Rotorua area, but the most telling aspect was her apparent inability to obtain enough koaro from the lakes for her study!

Other New Zealand lakes: Declines in abundance of lake populations of G. brevipinnis are even less well quantiﬁed elsewhere in New Zealand, though they do share many lakes with salmonids, almost always brown and rainbow trout, but in some lakes also landlocked stocks

of Chinook salmon. Other potential predators are Atlantic salmon in Lakes Manapouri and Te Anau, and sockeye salmon (Oncorhynchus nerka) in lakes of the upper Waitaki River system, though both are now close to local extirpation. They therefore pose no continuing threat to the indigenous species in the lakes (though Atlantic salmon Salmo salar have probably been displaced by rainbow trout in Lakes Te Anau and Manapouri—McDowall 1994a).

An observation was made, during the 1980s, by New Zealand botanist Peter Williams, of G. brevipinnis taking insects from the surface of a small alpine lake in which there are no salmonids (McDowall 1990b). This is reminiscent of what once was commonplace (Fletcher 1919b; Armstrong 1935) but is now rare enough to attract comment (see also similar comments on G. brevipinnis in Tasmania).

Galaxias brevipinnis was once the basis for a small ‘whitebait’ ﬁshery in tributaries of South Island’s Lake Wanaka, an analogue of their migrations from the sea into coastal rivers. However, the ﬁshery was banned many decades ago, ostensibly owing to a decline in abundance of the galaxiid, though combined with (and most probably driven by) a desire to ensure that the galaxiid remained available as a food for the trout (McDowall 1984a)—again, an interesting choice of conservation priorities.

Galaxias brevipinnis remains abundant in some lakes where introduced salmonids are present, though there are no quantitative data, and why they have persisted has not been elucidated (in fact the whole cause/effect relationship between trout species and G. brevipinnis, and what contributes to it, is effectively unstudied)—there could be factors relating to how G. brevipinnis use the lakes, the role of Retropinna in lake ecology, where it is present, either naturally or translocated, and the mix and size of salmonid populations in the lakes. In the early 1980s, I caught within about an hour, two Atlantic salmon a brown trout and two rainbow trout, when angling from shores of Lake Fergus in the upper Eglinton valley, above Lake Te Anau. I watched salmonids in a feeding frenzy in the lake’s surface waters, and the ﬁsh caught had all been preying on G. brevipinnis. Thus, clearly, on occasion,

there are enough G. brevipinnis in some lakes to provide a food resource for salmonids, though the ecologies of this predation, of both prey and predator, are very poorly understood; how many survive predation to reach maturity, and reproduce, is not known. In the meantime, populations of G. brevipinnis seem seriously threatened in New Zealand lakes by combinations of introduced salmonids and translocated native R. retropinna.

Implications for lake-living birds: Another, little recognised factor in all these ecological interactions extends to the birds of New Zealand’s oligotrophic, often glacial, high country lakes. Falla (1975), when discussing bird faunas of these lakes, wrote that: ‘‘Apart from insects associated with the water, the main food resource for birds under earlier conditions were native ﬁsh, mainly Galaxias and Retropinna’’. Knowing the ﬁsh faunas of lakes as we now do, the contribution of Retropinna to bird diets in most of these lakes was probably slim, as few lakes had natural populations of this species (its presence in most lakes is an outcome of releases during the early third of the 20th century by trout ﬁsheries managers—McDowall 1990b). Falla (1975) cited a comment from naturalist Richard Henry (1903) that: ‘‘Just where the Waiau [River] enters [Lake] Manapouri from [Lake] Te Anau, nearly all the grebes [Podiceps cristatus—Podicipididae] would sometimes congregate, perhaps once a year, but for what purpose I do not understand, because out on the lake they were always in pairs and appeared mated for life; they lived wholly on minnows’’ almost certainly G. brevipinnis. Falla (1975) adds: ‘‘Since that time, the ‘minnows’ have had to sustain, as well, a growing population of three species of introduced salmonid predators [brown trout, rainbow trout and Atlantic salmon], and he attributed decline in grebe numbers to salmonid predation on the ‘‘minnows’’, as well as increasing frequency of speeding boats on the lakes. Similar patterns might also apply to grebe populations on other high country lakes where trout have been introduced. From the perspective of the present account, we appear to be looking at salmonid predation on G. brevipinnis being so severe that aquatic birds such as crested grebes

have been supplanted as the primary galaxiid predators.

Conservation implications of further translocated lake populations of Retropinna retropinna: There was recently an irruption of R. retropinna in Lake Brunner on the South Island West Coast (McDowall 2002b). This may have resulted from a movement downstream of R. retropinna from Lake Poerua (where it was liberated by trout ﬁsheries managers as a potential trout food—McDowall 1994a). The establishment and spread of Retropinna may have serious implications for the stocks of G. brevipinnis in Lake Brunner that are yet to emerge (given events discussed above in Lakes Taupo and Rotopounamu). The same concerns may apply to G. argenteus and G. fasciatus, both of which also have landlocked populations in Lake Brunner, whose larvae almost certainly live in surface waters of the lake. They, too, may suffer from predation by brown trout as well, possibly, as predation by the newly established populations of Retropinna.

The passion for trout ‘‘everywhere’’ in New Zealand: There has been growing concern among New Zealand conservationists that most of the country’s cool sub-montane lakes have trout in them, leading to measures to provide protection for the few lakes in which G. brevipinnis has not suffered trout predation. Three lakes have been afforded statutory protection under a New Zealand Government statute as ‘faunistic reserves’, with the explicit intention of protecting virgin galaxiid populations. One of these lakes was Lake Rotopounamu, discussed above, where G. brevipinnis appears to have been driven to extinction by introduction of R. retropinna. Another is Lake Christabel, in headwaters of the Grey River on the West Coast of the South Island. The galaxiid population of this remote lake has hitherto been protected from invasion by trout that live downstream in the Grey River, by outﬂow from the lake ﬁltering through a large porous landslide that blocks its outlet. However, brown trout have recently been found in the lake, and it seems that, at times of ﬂood ﬂows, surface water may

pass across the landslide, allowing trout to invade the lake. The future status of the galaxiids of Lake Christabel is thus at risk, though it will depend on how proliﬁc trout become. Efforts are being made to eliminate any trout in the lake and/or to exclude them from spawning streams entering it. Only in a third of these three protected lakes, Lake Marion, is the natural animal community apparently intact, though there are additional lakes (mostly small tarns high in the Southern Alps), that have landlocked populations of G. brevipinnis, and lack introduced ﬁsh of any sort. Pressures by angling interests to establish trout in virtually ‘every suitable lake’ have been almost relentless in New Zealand, and in several instances against the law. Lake Macrae is another remote lake high in the mountains of the northeastern South Island where some individual illegally released a few brown trout several decades ago; not many years later trout up to 15 kg in weight were taken by anglers—repetition on a minor scale of what happened in the early 1900s in large New Zealand lakes. With that ‘‘success’’ in mind local people proposed to liberate rainbow trout there (again illegally), and it is not presently known whether this happened. Only under protest from conservationists was a proposal to release trout into Lake Chalice in the mountains of the Wairau River valley of the northeastern South Island turned down by Government. The ultimate irony is that some of the lakes are accessible only by helicopter or long mountain walks, and so are of limited interest to anglers.

New Zealand’s premier wildlife conservation and management agency until the late 1980s was the Wildlife Service of the Department of Internal Affairs. Staff of the Wildlife Service illegally released rainbow trout into Lake Orbell, in Fiordland National Park without statutory authority, and despite a clause in New Zealand’s National Parks statute that required extermination of exotic biota from within National Parks, and so yet another lake lost its ‘virgin status’ owing to actions of the country’s premier wildlife conservation agency. It is ironic that the Wildlife Service had no responsibility for conservation/ management of native ﬁsh, and seemingly did not care about them.

Pastoral farmers in upper reaches of the Rangitata River in the eastern/central South Island owned small, high-elevation tarns that they thought might make interesting ﬁsheries for brook charr, and they sought statutory approval to release charr there, which was refused on two grounds: ﬁrst, that beneﬁt from potential charr ﬁsheries would accrue only to the landowners as no one else would be allowed access to the tarns; and secondly, that there were populations of G. brevipinnis in these tarns that were regarded as of biodiversity value owing to the relatively small number of lakes not subject to trout introduction and predation. Having been refused authority to make the releases, it is my understanding that the farmers did so, anyway—salmonids once more being given precedence over native species.

Amphidromous and lacustrine Galaxias brevipinnis in Australia: Less is known about the history and status of G. brevipinnis in Australia, though both amphidromous and lacustrine populations are widespread on eastern and southern coasts (Fig. 39, d). A decline in its abundance was long ago attributed to trout invasion, e.g. Gray (1929), found the species plentiful above a waterfall in a New South Wales stream but only rainbow trout below it. Morris et al. (2000)

Fig. 39 Distribution of Galaxias brevipinnis in southeastern Australia and Tasmania

reported range fragmentation and fewer populations of G. brevipinnis in New South Wales, and reported it only in places inaccessible, or hostile, to salmonids.

Andrews (1976) observed the range of G. brevipinnis in Tasmania to be highly fragmented, with the ﬁsh abundant in localities lacking trout. Where trout were present he found that G. brevipinnis was, yet again, present only upstream of falls impassable by trout. Renowden (1968) surveyed streams in southern Victoria and reported that though G. brevipinnis was abundant in streams of the Otway Ranges before trout were introduced, since their introduction galaxiid numbers had become greatly reduced. Jackson and Williams (1980) also surveyed streams in the Otway Ranges, and found that brown trout was the most frequently recorded ﬁsh species. Galaxias brevipinnis was present at the only three sites where brown trout was absent. In contrast, they found that streams on Wilsons Promontory (also in southern Victoria) had no trout, though galaxiids, including G. brevipinnis, were abundant. Merrick and Schmida (1984) mention that G. brevipinnis once contributed ‘‘a minor portion of the Tasmanian whitebait ﬁshery; however, it is now of no commercial signiﬁcance’’. It is unclear whether this was intended to imply that ﬁshery exploitation has contributed to this change.

Galaxias brevipinnis was once present in Lake Tarli Karng, in inland Victoria (Lucas 1892), but both Williams (1964) and Timms (1974) later found brown trout to be the only species present. Williams thought that this comprised circumstantial explanation for galaxiid extirpation by brown trout, while Timms concluded that: ‘‘so far man’s only inﬂuence has been the introduction of trout, which has possibly caused the local extinction’’ of G. brevipinnis. Frankenberg (1966), similarly, attributed the species’ loss from that lake to stocking of trout, and implied general loss of galaxiids from Victorian streams as a result of trout releases. Unlike Williams, Frankenberg found the galaxiid still present in tributaries of Lake Tarli Karng, but brown trout were common in streams close to the lake and the galaxiid was present only upstream of penetration by trout.

The lacustrine galaxiid juveniles would presumably have suffered trout predation and would also have had to run a gauntlet of stream trout as they migrated upstream from the lake to the tributary headwaters—where adult G. brevipinnis persisted.

Davies and Fulton (1987) recorded G. brevipinnis in the diet of brown trout in Great Lake, central Tasmania, but expressed no concerns about impacts from trout predation on the galaxiid populations. Moreover, Sloan and French (1991) identiﬁed no explicit differences in relative abundance in inland Tasmanian lakes (trout and G. brevipinnis were both abundant in some lakes, and both sparse in others). But they did observe that galaxiids were ‘‘more conspicuous in the open surface waters’’ of lakes where trout were rare or absent, although where trout were abundant, the galaxiids were ‘‘more conﬁned to the lake margins, the rocky shorelines, and shallow bays dominated by emergent weeds’’ (cited from Cadwallader 1996). This is reminiscent of observations, discussed earlier, of G. brevipinnis occasionally being seen feeding at the surface in New Zealand lakes.

An interesting feature of the history of G. brevipinnis in Australia is its appearance in the inland, Murray–Darling system, particularly above Hume Reservoir (Morison and Anderson 1990; Waters et al. 2002a). What seems to have happened is that G. brevipinnis, became established in Hydro-Lakes Eucumbene and Jindabyne following diversion of water from the Snowy River, and that the ﬁsh has spread downstream into tributaries of Hume Reservoir, lower in the Murray River. Whether the species’ presence in Hume Reservoir is a result of continual spread downstream from the hydro lakes, or there is a self-sustaining population in Hume Reservoir and its tributaries, or both, is unknown. Morison and Anderson (1990) alluded to comments by Wayne Fulton, former Commissioner of the Tasmanian Inland Fisheries Commission, expressing concern about the proliferation and spread of G. brevipinnis, in the context of its potential impacts on other native ﬁshes, particularly for potential impacts of G. brevipinnis on G. olidus in tributaries of the Murray–Darling River.

Galaxias brevipinnis as an invasive species: Having explored alleged or likely impacts of various salmonid species on G. brevipinnis, it is important to note, also, discussion in the sections on Galaxias pedderensis and the G. vulgaris species-group regarding the status of additional Australian and New Zealand Galaxias species in the context of proliferation of G. brevipinnis in lakes formed by impoundment for hydro-electricity generation.

Furthermore, Barrett (2004), listing G. brevipinnis as among the species not naturally present in the waters of the Murray–Darling River system, viewed this as a symptom of declining native ﬁsh populations in the river—mentioning these immediately after mention of ‘‘alien ﬁsh species’’ such as carp, gambusia, weatherloach, trouts, and other taxa.

CONSERVATION RANKING: Overall, it appears that almost everywhere in New Zealand that trout have been introduced into lakes, stocks of G. brevipinnis are locally threatened, though lacustrine G. brevipinnis, as a whole, in New Zealand is not considered to be imperilled despite the impacts of salmonid and retropinnid introductions discussed above (and notwithstanding its former listing as Category C threatened (third rank) by the New Zealand Department of Conservation (Molloy and Davis 1992; Tisdall 1994). Subsequently; however, concern has lessened, and the species is no longer classed as Threatened in the latest listing (Hitchmough 2002). Despite these assessments, it is clear that G. brevipinnis has declined substantially in abundance, especially in inland, submontane lakes throughout its range. Allibone and Barrier (2004), in a conservation recovery plan that included this species, stated that this ﬁsh is widespread in New Zealand and its offshore islands, but ‘‘has declined greatly’’ in some lakes, especially in the central North Island.

Despite alleged negative impacts of trout predation in Australia, and certainly some local declines in abundance, G. brevipinnis remains widespread there, and has not been listed as Threatened by the Australian Society for Fish Biology (Crook 2001), though Morris et al. (2000)

ranked it as Data Deﬁcient in New South Wales. This species was not mentioned as under threat in the Tasmanian galaxiid recovery plan (Threatened Species Unit 2005).

The Galaxias vulgaris species-group of New Zealand non-migratory galaxiids

The eastern South Island of New Zealand has a series of non-migratory (non-diadromous), mostly sub-montane, exclusively stream-living galaxiids (Fig. 31B–G) that are believed to be derived from the diadromous G. brevipinnis (Fig. 31A) (see McDowall 1970, 1997b; Allibone et al. 1996; McDowall and Wallis 1996; McDowall and Chadderton 1999; Waters and Wallis 2001a, b). For many years populations of these non-migratory derivative species were regarded as a single highly variable, eastern South Island species, G. vulgaris (see McDowall 1970, 1990b) (Fig. 31B). A long-term ecological study of the Taieri River in eastern/central Otago (Townsend and Crowl 1991), however, revealed that several eco/morphotypes were subsumed within G. vulgaris (sensu lato as then recognised), and subsequent genetic studies pointed to a much higher level of species diversity than was then recognised (Allibone et al. 1996; Wallis et al. 2001; Waters and Wallis 2001a, b). It is therefore now accepted that populations hitherto treated as G. vulgaris (McDowall 1970, 1990b), comprise a complex of species, and a series of new species has been described from that part of the southeastern South Island to the south of the Canterbury Plains and the Waitaki River (Fig. 40) (McDowall and Wallis 1996; McDowall 1997b; McDowall and Chadderton 1999). Molecular studies have revealed even further lineages that have not yet been formally recognised (Waters and Wallis 2001a, b; Waters et al. 2001a; McDowall and Hewitt 2003), so that additional species may await description—there are perhaps 10 distinct species, of which 6 are formally described. At times it is not possible to reconcile the recently published, more speciose, taxonomy with older publications, and for this reason some of the discussion below refers to the species-complex, rather than to the various lineages.

- Fig. 40 Distributions in New Zealand of the Galaxias vulgaris species complex. (A) arrow 1—rivers of the northern South Island; 2—widespread across the Canterbury Plains east of the Southern Alps; 3—coastal drainages south of the Waitaki River; 4—upper Taieri River in Central Otago; 5—Waiopori River of the lower Taieri’

- 6—widespread in western Southland and on Stewart Island; 7—widespread through the Clutha River system;

- (B) arrow 8—upper Taieri River and Manuherikia River;

9. narrow range in Teviot River, mid Clutha and headwaters of the Taieri River; 10—widespread through the Waipori River, lower Taieri; 11—widespread across the Southland Plains and on Stewart Island

The present, revised, published taxonomy recognises the following species (formally described) or lineages (undescribed): There is one lineage, informally referred to as ‘northern ﬂathead galaxias’ or Galaxias ‘northern’ in the northern

South Island, in upper reaches of the Maruia and Rappahannock Rivers (draining west via the Buller River), in the north ﬂowing Motueka, Wairau and Awatere Rivers, and the predominantly east ﬂowing Clarence River (Fig. 40A—arrow 1). Galaxias vulgaris (Fig. 31B) is present in rivers south of those listed above for Galaxias ‘northern’—in rivers draining east from the Southern Alps, as far south as the Waitaki River

- (Fig. 40A—arrow 2), and then a little further south in coastal drainages to the south-east ﬂowing from the Kakanui Mountains (Fig. 40A—arrow 3).

In the area to the south and west of the range of G. vulgaris (sensu stricto) as just outlined, at least ﬁve related species are now formally recognised: G. depressiceps (Fig. 31C) is found primarily in upper reaches of the Taieri River (Fig. 40A—arrow 4); G. anomalus (Fig. 31D) also occurs in the upper Taieri River system, as well as in the Manuherikia River, a Clutha River tributary

- (Fig. 40B—arrow 8); G. pullus and G. eldoni (Fig. 31E,G) are mostly in the lower Taieri and Waipori Rivers but spreading a little south into tributaries of the lower Clutha River (Fig. 40A—arrow 5 and 40B—arrow 10); and G. gollumoides (Fig. 31F) is largely in rivers of Southland and on Stewart Island (Fig. 40B—arrow 11) (Allibone et al. 1996; McDowall and Wallis 1996; McDowall 1997b; McDowall and Chadderton 1999; Waters and Wallis 2001a, b). Again, however, molecular data indicate additional undescribed lineages in the Clutha River valley (Galaxias ‘species D’Fig. 40A—arrow 7; Galaxias ‘teviot’—Fig. 40Barrow 9), and another in the south-ﬂowing rivers of Southland and on Stewart Island (Galaxias ‘southern’; Fig. 40A—arrow 6).

The conservation status of members of this species-complex is discussed below, together, partly because they are so similar that this makes sense and is easier to understand, and partly because studies were undertaken before the taxonomic diversity represented had been identiﬁed, some of them still undescribed.

The ﬁsh in this complex are of moderate size, growing to ca. 150 mm, and are characteristic of submontane streams that are usually swift-ﬂowing with coarse gravel/cobble substrates. They are all nondiadromous, spawn in spring, have a few

hundred to a thousand or more large eggs, and these may be deposited in groups beneath large cobbles, or sometimes amongst the root wads of stream-side vegetation. Their larvae and juveniles form small shoals in pools and runs during spring and summer, until they reach around 30–40 mm, when they become cryptic like the adults, and are rarely seen in the open, by day.

Results of several recent studies are informative about the distributions, abundance and conservation status of some of these taxa, including a Department of Conservation recovery plan (Allibone and Barrier 2004). Their distributions have implications for the species’ conservation status and for their ecological/behavioural interactions with other ﬁsh, both indigenous and exotic, and they contribute to an understanding of possible causes of declines or fragmented distributions. In particular, several studies are informative of a long-held view that galaxiids and trout have complementary distributions—that where trout, especially brown trout, invade streams, galaxiids tend to decline or disappear (McDowall 1968c,

- 2003b). Cadwallader (1975a, b) studied G. vulgaris

sensu stricto, in the Glentui River a tributary of the upper Waimakariri River in inland Canterbury, and he concluded that similarities in habitat of the galaxiid and brown trout may help to explain a decline in abundance of the galaxiid. The galaxiid was observed to consume similar foods to brown trout and at the same time of the day providing, in Cadwallader’s opinion, a strong case for competition in explaining apparent incompatibility of the two species. Competition for food, however, was not demonstrated. Furthermore, as so often, there is a problem that past interactions may have led to increasingly complementary distributions, and that mutual exclusion by the two species has led to avoidance or reduction of mechanisms of interaction, which can be elucidated only by detailed and demanding ecological studies.

McIntosh (2000a) recently examined distributions and habitat characteristics of G. vulgaris and brown trout on a broad scale in the Waimakariri River, and he found trout to be present in medium to larger streams but absent from small ones. Galaxiids were present across a much wider

range of stream sizes and habitat stabilities, though they were typically absent from larger streams with more stable substrates, if these have larger trout. However, galaxiids were present in these streams if there were no trout, so that physical conditions do not explain absence of galaxiids from streams with large trout—though where trout and galaxiids were both present, trout were never more than about 150 mm long. Allibone and McIntosh (1999) noted that there was little evidence for predation by trout on the galaxiid, but attributed this to the two species having limited range overlaps as an outcome of their interactions. These trends, relating ﬁsh presence to stream size and stability, suggest that galaxiids tend to co-occur with trout primarily in medium-sized streams that have intermediate bed stability. McIntosh (2000a) concluded that the variable that ‘‘best explained the presence of galaxiids at a site was the absence of large trout’’. He considered it likely that species native to New Zealand conditions, which have ‘‘evolved in unpredictable and ﬂood-prone New Zealand rivers (Winterbourn et al. 1981) may be less affected by ﬂoods than trout’’, i.e., exotic ﬁsh are less ‘‘well adapted to disturbance regimes’’ than native ﬁsh (Minckley and Meffe 1987; Baltz and Moyle 1993). This may mean that, in New Zealand streams inhabited by populations of large trout, native species are marginalised into sub-optimal habitats—with food less available, lowered feeding efﬁciency, slower growth, lower survival, and lower individual fecundity. Biotic characteristics that McIntosh (2000a) thought likely to better adapt galaxiids than trout to unstable streams included: (1) Small size; (2) Relatively slim, tubular body shape; (3) Negative buoyancy; (4) Benthic behaviour; (5) Lower food requirements; and (6) An ability to burrow into substratum interstices (and for the latter, see Hartman 1990).

Though native species may be better adapted than trout to unstable conditions, this does not mean that these conditions are optimal for them—perhaps only that galaxiids are better able than introduced trout to survive in conditions that are sub-optimal or marginal for both species. This conclusion is consistent with McIntosh’s (2000a) ﬁnding that though trout of all sizes were absent from ‘‘unstable gravel-bed rivers with braided

channels...galaxiids were present in small numbers in most unstable streams’’ (present author’s emphasis).

Townsend and Crowl (1991) reported on quantitative sampling of G. vulgaris (sensu lato)

- at 198 sites in the more southern Taieri River system, where they found: (1) No ﬁsh present at 54 sites; (2) Only brown trout at 69 sites; (3) Only Galaxias species (later identiﬁed as G. anomalus, G. depressiceps and G. eldoni—McDowall and Wallis 1996; McDowall 1997b) at 63 sites; and (4) Trout and galaxiids co-occurring at 9 sites. Virtually mutually exclusive distributions of trout and galaxiids are obvious (Fig. 41). Townsend and Crowl (1991) showed that ‘‘the occurrence of waterfalls, at least 3 m high, downstream of a sampling site [was] the best predictor of G. vulgaris occurrence’’. This was attributed to falls preventing penetration of brown trout upstream into habitats where galaxiids were present. Townsend and Crowl (1991) interpreted absence of the galaxiid immediately below falls as an indicator of negative trout impacts, ﬁnding no other reason for repeated absence of galaxiids downstream of falls. In their analysis, they controlled for land use patterns at sampling sites, and found that land use was not linked to the fragmentation of galaxiid populations. Nor was

- Fig. 41 Plot demonstrating virtually mutually exclusive distributions of galaxiids of the Galaxias vulgaris species complex, and brown trout, Salmo trutta, in the Taieri River southeastern New Zealand (from Townsend and Crowl 1991)

it related to habitat features. The ﬁnding of galaxiids living mostly upstream of falls impassable to trout engenders the spectre of larger, predatory trout occupying streams below falls, with galaxiids breeding upstream of falls, the galaxiid larvae and juveniles being, in some measure, swept (or even deliberately shifting) down stream, and ending up as food for trout awaiting them below the falls.

In the few streams where Townsend and Crowl (1991) found galaxiids and trout to co-occur in the Taieri River, habitats were described as ‘‘predominantly cobble substrate [in] an unconstrained braided channel’’. Presumably unstable, braided streams are sub-optimal for trout, allowing galaxiids to ﬁnd some refuge there, albeit at low densities (McIntosh 2000a). Townsend and Crowl (1991) recognised that their study did not ‘‘deﬁne the mechanism for the fragmented population structure of G. vulgaris in the Taieri River’’, though they did argue that their results had ‘‘implicated brown trout as a causal agent’’, and they considered that their data allowed rejection of an alternative interpretation that loss of galaxiids resulted from environmental degradation. Townsend (1996) concluded that the best predictor of presence of galaxiids was absence of trout. Townsend and Crowl (1991) ‘‘speculate[d] that the unstable nature of the bed in these sites promotes the co-occurrence (at very low densities) of trout and G. vulgaris’’, as did McIntosh (2000a) in the Waimakariri River.

A complex pattern of largely complementary trout/galaxiid distributions applies also to ﬁsh faunas in tributaries of Lake Mahinerangi, in inland, southern/central South Island of New Zealand. This lake was formed as a hydro-electric impoundment on the Waipori River—a southern, inland tributary of the Taieri River that Townsend and Crowl (1991) did not study. The impoundment was developed in a series of stages, beginning in 1911, culminating with construction of the largest and existing dam in 1942. McDowall and Allibone (1994) reviewed knowledge to that time of the galaxiid fauna of tributaries of Lake Mahinerangi, and noted presence of G. brevipinnis in tributaries of the lake upstream of the dam, though this species was rarely found elsewhere in the Taieri River system. However, non-migratory

species that are widespread through other parts of the Taieri catchment, including streams just downstream of Lake Mahinerangi, seemed to be largely absent from Waipori River tributaries upstream of Lake Mahinerangi that had then been sampled (McDowall and Allibone 1994). It was hypothesised that when the dam was formed, stocks of G. brevipinnis in streams above the dam site became landlocked within the lake, proliferated there, and invaded the lake’s tributaries, eliminating any non-migratory galaxiid species present. Brown trout were also established in the lake, and formed the basis for a recreational ﬁshery.

Allibone (1999) re-examined this hypothesis following widespread sampling of 46 streams, either Lake Mahinerangi tributaries (Fig. 42), or tributaries of the Waipori River upstream of the lake. By the time of Allibone’s study, some of the taxonomic problems regarding these non-migratory species of galaxiid had been resolved, allowing the populations to be distinguished, and identiﬁed as either G. eldoni (Fig. 42, h) or G. pullus (Fig. 42, +) (McDowall 1997b) (Fig. 31E, G). Allibone (1999) demonstrated a dominance hierarchy in ﬁsh faunas of these streams. With regard to brown trout (Fig. 42,

), G. brevipinnis (Fig. 42, ·) and the two nonmigratory galaxiids present in upper reaches of the system (G. pullus and G. eldoni), Allibone found:

- 1. No ﬁsh in three stream sections sampled;
- 2. Brown trout present alone in eight streams;

- 3. G. brevipinnis alone in seven;
- 4. G. pullus alone in 12;
- 5. G. eldoni alone in 10;
- 6. Brown trout and G. brevipinnis together in four;
- 7. Brown trout and G. pullus together in one stream; and:
- 8. G. brevipinnis and G. pullus together in one.

Galaxias eldoni was never found in the same reach of stream as any other species, and coexistence of any combinations of more than one species were rare (6 of 46 streams). In no stream sections were three species, of what ever combination, found together. Brown trout almost universally penetrated the lake’s tributaries until they encountered an impassable barrier, and they were always numerically dominant when present with any other species (usually G. brevipinnis).

Differing distributions of various ﬁsh species present in this area relate partly to behavioural differences. Whereas trout tend to penetrate past barriers by jumping, G. brevipinnis are poor jumpers, but they are very proﬁcient climbers, being able to move upstream past falls tens of metres high by climbing up the wetted rock margins of the stream ﬂows (McDowall 1990b, 2003b). Galaxias brevipinnis populations, when present in Lake Mahinerangi tributary streams, were almost always upstream of brown trout. This galaxiid spends its larval life in the lake, typically

- Fig. 42 Distributions of ﬁsh species in the Waipori River, a tributary of the Taieri River in southeastern New Zealand (+—Galaxias pullus; ·—G. brevipinnis; h—G. eldoni; s—Salmo trutta; D—no ﬁsh taken) (from Allibone 1999)

migrating upstream into lake tributaries as juveniles ca. 40–45mm long (McDowall 1990b). Thus, G. brevipinnis presumably occupies tributary habitats by juveniles migrating upstream from the lake, often through brown trout-occupied reaches, and it can be conjectured that they suffer trout predation when ‘running this gauntlet’. Fish that escape predation form the G. brevipinnisoccupied zones that extend up the streams above the ‘trout zone’, in general, until there were barriers to further penetration—typically waterfalls that G. brevipinnis juveniles can not climb (perhaps having a distinct overhang), or areas of wetland through which G. brevipinnis did not penetrate. Populations of non-migratory species, G. pullus and G. eldoni, were often present only upstream of barriers that excluded both G. brevipinnis and trout. The dominance hierarchy discovered by Allibone (1999) was thus: brown trout > G. brevipinnis > G. pullus or G. eldoni.

Brook charr have also been liberated into tributaries of Lake Mahinerangi, and were present at two sites in Allibone’s study, but there is insufﬁcient evidence to make a judgement on any impact of this species on galaxiid populations. Rainbow trout, stocked into Lake Mahinerangi over a period of years (Scott 1964, 1968, 1970, 1971), were not observed by Allibone, implying that stocking of this species was a failure, avoiding further complications to the story of ﬁsh populations of Lake Mahinerangi and its tributaries.

However, charr became established in the Munro’s Dam lake (on a tributary of the Waipori River a little downstream of the Mahinerangi Dam in the 1960s), and it has moved into streams ﬂowing into the dam. Allibone and McIntosh (1999) related that in 1964, prior to brook charr stocking into the Waipori River, Otago Acclimatisation Society ﬁeld staff had collected specimens of what later proved to be G. pullus (McDowall 1970, 1997b). Present day surveys in the Munro Dam Stream showed that brook charr have displaced G. pullus from all areas where brook charr maintain self-sustaining populations. Allibone and McDowall (1997) found that brook charr and G. pullus rarely co-occur there, the galaxiid being ‘‘restricted to the steeper, lower reaches of the stream where

brook charr are largely absent’’. The galaxiid is also present in reaches of stream that brook charr have been unable to colonise by moving upstream from the dam. Thus there is concern that G. pullus has lost ‘‘signiﬁcant areas of refuge habitat during ﬂood ﬂows’’, and also that with time, ‘‘brook charr will reduce further the section [of stream] occupied by’’ G. pullus (Allibone and McDowall 1997).

All of these observations of apparently, and widely pervasive, complementary distributions of brown trout, brook charr and galaxiids of the G. vulgaris species-complex, with galaxiids tending to be present upstream of, and separated from trout, by barriers impenetrable by trout, corroborate a view expressed long ago (McDowall 1968c) that exotic trouts and indigenous galaxiids are incompatible, and that where trout occupy streams, galaxiids tend to disappear.

Overall, Allibone and McIntosh (1999) summarised evidence relating to the whole G. vulgaris group, indicating that ‘‘disjunct distributions, competitive interactions, dietary overlaps [and] predatory behaviour of salmonids are all impacting upon’’ these non-migratory galaxiid species. However, reasons for these complementary distributions are probably complex, and vary among species and across river systems. The fact that distributions tend to become complementary makes it difﬁcult to explicitly discover causation, especially to distinguish between competitive interactions and predation, since whatever interactions have contributed to complementarity have created a scenario where the causal mechanism is eventually limited or avoided.

The series of studies outlined above does, however, create a powerful scenario describing how trout and galaxiids establish complementary distributions where trout invade galaxiid habitats, and some data point to the probability of predation and competitive exclusion as causal mechanisms. Other studies, however, suggest that interactions may be both more complex and more subtle, and in the ﬁnal analysis, unravelling these interactions may be elusive.

Diets, particularly, are similar across the diversity of these galaxiids. They are generalised invertebrate predators, their prey comprising primarily the stream benthos, plus some surface

terrestrial foods, and they strongly prefer softbodied prey organisms (Cadwallader 1975a, b; Glova and Sagar 1989a, 1991, 1993; Glova et al. 1992; Crowl et al. 1992; Allibone and McDowall

- 1997; Allibone and Townsend 1998). The galaxiids tend to be substantially benthic in habit, foraging around the substrate often detecting prey using mechanical cues. They will also rise into the water column to take prey organisms from the invertebrate drift. Galaxiids are often nocturnal (though this may, in part be induced by presence of trout; see below). Nor are they always nocturnal, and may be less so at higher elevations. Allibone (Allibone and McDowall 1997; Allibone and Townsend 1998) reported various species of the G. vulgaris species complex to be active diurnally. Allibone and McIntosh (1999) suggested that, in the absence of trout, galaxiids foraged at all times of the day, depending on food availability, and that there is a change in foraging behaviour in the presence of trout. Moreover, Edge et al. (1993) found that the galaxiids consumed food at a lower rate when trout were present.

The four species of salmonid (brown and rainbow trout, Chinook salmon, and brook charr), that are widespread in New Zealand, and present extensively across much the same areas as galaxiids, have similar diets to the galaxiids. Salmonids prey on both benthos and surface terrestrial insects, though they tend to be visual drift feeders, and probably prey more strongly on the drift than the galaxiids, though more benthic habits and diets of the galaxiids may also result from the inﬂuence of trout (again, see below). Salmonids are territorial, and compete for optimal foraging positions in the water column, and grow substantially larger than galaxiids (which are typically <150 mm long) (McDowall 1990b; McIntosh and Townsend 1995). There is thus clearly potential for negative interactions between galaxiids and salmonids that relate variously to feeding behaviour, timing and location, and the organisms eaten.

Predation seems to be a likely source of trout impacts, and there may also be prey competition. Glova (1990) showed that, in a stream simulator, trout ca. 150 mm long prey upon shoaling juveniles of ﬁshes of the G. vulgaris species-complex—he found the mid-water swim-

ming galaxiids more vulnerable to trout predation than benthic-living bullies (species of Gobiomorphus—f. Eleotridae). Townsend and Crowl (1991) also considered predation the most likely cause of decline in range and abundance of native species.

McIntosh et al. (1994) found that densities of galaxiids, particularly when small, were lower in streams containing large brown trout, and later (McIntosh 2000a) that, in an experimental stream channel, trout >150 mm long consumed galaxiids of all sizes offered (48–94 mm long). He concluded that, in his study streams, there was ‘‘no size refuge for galaxiids from predation by large trout, the galaxiids never became so large that they were no longer vulnerable to trout predation at sites where there were large trout’’. McIntosh (2000a) summarised his results by pointing out that ‘‘the strength of biotic interactions leading to galaxiid extirpation vary with trout size, but the distribution of trout and, therefore, potential for biotic interactions, is limited by abiotic factors’’ (such as stream size and bed stability). This is reminiscent of Closs and Lake’s (1996) interpretation of impacts of biotic conditions on the distributions of rainbow trout and Galaxias olidus in Victoria, Australia, though in that instance, upstream range of trout into the galaxiid-occupied habitats was determined by water temperatures.

Other ecological factors may be important for galaxiid/salmonid relationships and interactions. The various salmonid species spawn large eggs (5–7 mm in diameter) in cold, gravelly, headwater streams, from late autumn through into spring; the eggs develop over several months and salmonid alevins emerge into the streams, during spring, at a length of around 30–35 mm, forming loose shoals around slack margins of pools and runs.

Non-migratory galaxiids spawn much smaller eggs (ca. 2 mm) during late winter and spring (Cadwallader 1976; McDowall 1990b; Allibone and Townsend 1997). These eggs develop in about 4–6 weeks and, though spawning and hatching times of galaxiids vary by species and also with the elevation of streams in which they live, they are hatching and delivering larvae, usually <10 mm long, into streams at about when

much larger salmonid alevins are emerging from the stream gravels. The newly hatched galaxiids also form loose shoals around slack margins of pools and runs over spring and summer, before becoming cryptic amongst substrate cobbles when about 30–40 mm long (McDowall 1990b). Even though some of galaxiids will be hatching before salmonid alevins emerge, the salmonids will always have a substantial size advantage over the galaxiid larvae throughout spring and early summer. McIntosh (2000a) discussed potential for brown trout only 50–70 mm long to prey on galaxiid fry in captivity, but there are no data from natural habitats. Small trout and galaxiids do co-occur (McIntosh 2000a), and a role for predation by small, young of the year, trout on newly hatched galaxiid fry seems likely. Predation by juvenile salmonids on larval galaxiids may be an additional stressor of the galaxiid populations, and is a topic in need of study.

As Allibone and McIntosh (1999) pointed out, salmonid predation on these galaxiids is not much reported, but this may be due, at least partly, to their increasingly complementary distributions—in large measure driven by continuing impacts of trout on galaxiids, i.e., there may not be any (or many) galaxiids left for the trout to prey upon apart, perhaps, from continual supply/ delivery of small galaxiids carried from headwater tributaries, downstream in tributary stream ﬂows, into trout-occupied zones, where the galaxiids succumb to trout predation. Quantifying the effects of such predation is likely to be difﬁcult. Predation studies are needed that focus on the spring–summer period, when larval/juvenile galaxiids abound, before becoming cryptic in the stream substrate. Nor should it be assumed that all predation is on juveniles (McIntosh 2000a, b); trout anglers report adult galaxiid ﬁshes of this species group in the diet of large brown trout (D. Kelly pers. comm.)

An additional, as yet largely undiscussed impact on galaxiids may relate to effects of substrate disturbance by spawning salmonids, particularly large anadromous Chinook salmon. Especially where spawning salmon populations are large, there is capacity for spawning activities to cause widespread and extensive disruption of

substrate gravels in high elevation gravelly headwater streams along eastern ﬂanks of the Southern Alps of the South Island of New Zealand, where G. vulgaris and other high-country galaxiids are widespread. The rifﬂe/run structure of streams can be completely reorganised by redddigging activities of spawning Chinook salmon (Field-Dodgson 1987) (Fig. 43). Anecdotal observations suggest that these headwater salmonspawning streams, which are expected to support populations of galaxiids, either lack them, or small numbers of galaxiids may persist only in gravelly bars and stream margins undisturbed by salmon. Bryophyte communities growing on stream cobbles may indicate habitat stability where galaxiid may persist. Similar impacts of salmonid spawning have been described for North American streams—digging of redds may reduce or dislodge benthic invertebrate faunas, and disturb populations of small ﬁsh species, especially those living within substrate gravels (Hildebrand 1971; Krueger and May 1991) in similar manner as galaxiids do in New Zealand streams. There are potential impacts on both galaxiid ﬁsh and their food supplies.

These examples are, conceptually, the relatively simple and straight-forward interactions, but they do not exclude other mechanisms of interaction, and further more subtle and/or complex interactions seem likely, including feedback mechanisms.

Allen’s (1951) now classic study of brown trout in the Horokiwi Stream, in New Zealand,

Fig. 43 A small sub-montane New Zealand stream in which spawning of chinook salmon, Oncorhynchus tshawytscha has completely restructured the stream substrate gravels

generated data suggesting that observed productivity of the stream’s trout population could not be driven by energy available from foods derived from the stream’s benthic invertebrate production. Huryn (1996) called this ‘‘Allen’s paradox’’, and addressed the question in streams in southern New Zealand (Huryn 1996, 1998)—as it was impossible to replicate Allen’s (1951) study in the Horokiwi Stream whose trout population is defunct owing to habitat degradation (Jellyman

- et al. 2000). Huryn’s studies also revealed that brown trout were consuming all available benthic insect production in his study reaches, and that estimated trout production became possible only when energy inputs of terrestrial foods, hyporheios, and trout cannibalism were included. This was in contrast to galaxiid populations in similar, nearby streams, where Huryn (1996) found that galaxiids were consuming only a modest proportion of benthic insect production—and there was plenty of energy available to fuel observed ﬁsh growth. Thus, apart from direct impacts of trout relevant to galaxiid ﬁshes, there are also clearly severe negative impacts by trout on benthic invertebrate communities in some New Zealand streams, the biodiversity implications of which cannot be ignored. Further work by McIntosh (2002) described how ﬁshless streams, and those with only G. vulgaris in them, had biomass and densities of a mayﬂy species important as trout food, which were two or three orders of magnitude higher than in similar streams carrying brown trout.

That trout predation is having a signiﬁcant effect on the benthos of New Zealand streams is not a new notion. Tillyard (1920) long ago observed that trout predation was having major impacts on stream benthic insect communities. He looked explicitly at stoneﬂies (Plecoptera), mayﬂies (Ephemeroptera), dragonﬂies (Odonat-

- a), and caddisﬂies (Trichoptera), all of which are important food for trout, and in all instances he found that there had been serious reduction in insect abundance, using expressions like: ‘‘massive decline’’, ‘‘very considerable diminution’’, ‘‘much decreased’’, ‘‘complete loss’’, ‘‘practically eliminated’’ and ‘‘quite extinct’’ to describe his ﬁndings for various named insect taxa. Summarising, Tillyard (1920) thought that ‘‘...the balance

of nature has been completely upset by the introduction of the trout...it appears certain that the rainbow trout has had a greater share in the eating-out of the insect fauna than has the slower and less greedy brown trout...Many of the streams...are almost totally ‘eaten out’ and the amount of food in the lakes is woefully deﬁcient’’.

As discussed above for galaxiids, Tillyard (1920) found that some stream benthic insects had been extirpated, except in reaches upstream of falls impassable to trout. He attributed these impacts, in part, to New Zealand streams, and their faunas, lacking a history of predation on their native benthic insect communities, by what he labelled ‘‘rapacious ﬁsh...The introduced trout...gorged themselves at leisure upon the rich food...No attempts were made to conserve or renew the food supply. On the contrary, the only idea seemed to be to put in more and more [trout] fry’’.

Furthermore, Hayes (1996b; Hayes et al. 2000) has much more recently sought to explain how New Zealand’s brown trout obtain enough food to generate observed growth rates, and which produce the large ﬁsh in New Zealand rivers—particularly, how they can reach such size, maintain condition, and spawn year after year, without resorting to piscivory. The discussion above indicates that they do resort to piscivory, but this can persist only until the trout have extirpated or displaced their ﬁsh prey and it appears that piscivory by trout on galaxiids in upland New Zealand rivers and streams is not an effective and enduring feeding strategy, to achieve high growth rates and to continue to reproduce. The galaxiids cannot sustain this.

Flecker and Townsend (1994), McIntosh and Townsend (1996) and Biggs et al. (2000) showed that when benthic insect populations in New Zealand streams are heavily preyed upon by trout, this leads to community-wide ecological impacts. Intensive trout predation on stream insectsleads to a trophic cascade, the outcome of which is that periphyton proliferates on stream substrates in the absence of heavy grazing by herbivorous stream insects. This heavy periphyton grazing does not happen in streams that either have no ﬁsh—where the benthic grazing insect populations are unchecked by ﬁsh predation, or only have galaxiids—populations of which prey less heavily on

benthic insect populations than trout and leave a relatively proliﬁc insect population to graze the periphyton. Paradoxically, though Allen (1951), found that there was not enough food in the Horokiwi Stream to account for the growth of thetrout,helater(Allen1961)describedNewZealand streams as very productive, and thought it unlikely that food would be limiting trout populations in stream communities.

There are additional, consequential ecological impacts of trout predation intensity. McIntosh and Townsend (1996, 1998; McIntosh 2000a, b) have shown that presence of trout has led to modiﬁed behaviour of stream benthic insects—presence of predation by introduced trout results in aquatic insects spending less time on exposed surfaces of substrate stones than they do when only native galaxiids are present, which may result in reduced grazing of periphyton, further strengthening the trophic cascade. Derivative effects are that benthic insects, in becoming increasingly secretive in the presence of trout, may have reduced growth and reproductive output, leading to less prey available for galaxiids that remain in trout-occupied streams—creating additional ecological pressure on the galaxiids.

Yet further impacts have been demonstrated. McIntosh (2002) described a change in stream benthos composition, with a shift to a greater proportion of cased caddisﬂies (a result perhaps of predators selecting soft-bodied prey). Nystrom and McIntosh (2003) showed a reduction, or even elimination, of larger invertebrates in trout streams, whereas Huryn (1998) found invertebrates to be smaller. And Drinnan (2000) showed that benthic invertebrate drift in trout streams is increasingly nocturnal.

Moreover, McIntosh et al. (1994) observed a habitat shift by galaxiids of the G. vulgaris species-group into slower ﬂow velocities in streams, attributing this to a combination of interspeciﬁc competition for habitat, and predation by trout. This habitat shift means that galaxiids are driven into less favourable feeding sites, adding further to the trout impacts. McIntosh’s study also showed that when trout are present, galaxiids (as well as insects) spend less time foraging on upper substrate surfaces, where they would be more exposed to trout predation.

These various shifts in both habitat and behaviour, of both benthic prey and ﬁsh, are likely to mean reduced and less efﬁcient food intake. Alternatively, if galaxiids are sufﬁciently hungry, they may risk exposure to trout predation to get enough food—a trade off between growing and surviving—rapid growth, high fecundity but risk of predation mortality, versus slower growth, lower fecundity and better survival.

Thus for a whole mix of reasons there appears to be a series of compounding, consequential effects of the presence of trout on G. vulgaris sensu lato that are additional to direct effects of predation (Simon and Townsend 2003). These include:

- 1. Reduced benthic invertebrate populations in streams owing to trout predation;
- 2. Proliferation of periphyton communities as a result of less grazing owing to reduced benthic invertebrate densities;
- 3. Periphyton proliferation is further accentuated by altered behaviour of benthic invertebrates, which reduce their substrate-surface exposure so as to escape trout predation and thus have less optimal grazing opportunities;
- 4. This can be assumed to reduce growth of benthic insects and therefore benthic insect production, and resulting in less food available for ﬁsh consumption;
- 5. Less surface activity by benthic insects means that they are less available to predatory ﬁsh, including galaxiids, thereby reducing galaxiid growth rates/survival;
- 6. There is predation by trout on galaxiids;
- 7. This may provoke less open activity by galaxiids as a result of predation threat, the galaxiids perhaps becoming more nocturnal than they would be in absence of trout, and they have to invest more effort in obtaining food;
- 8. Galaxiids may also be being displaced from ideal foraging sites as a result of competition/ predation threat from trout, and their growth and survival are further reduced, and reproductive output is compromised;
- 9. Or varying combinations of all of these.

There has been a tendency to regard predation as the primary and most serious effect of introduced

trout on species of the G. vulgaris species complex (Townsend and Crowl 1991), and it may be, though explicit data are sparse. All other potential effects indicated by research discussed above will be at least additive, and some of them may be crucially important to galaxiid survival. As a consequence of these changes, survival and production of galaxiid populations seem likely to be reduced, sometimes seriously compromised. In essence, galaxiid populations, in addition to being preyed upon by trout, are being pushed into suboptimal habitats, with food less available owing to competition with trout, also as a result of both habitat shifts by benthic invertebrates and reduced benthic production. The tradeoff between better survival but slower growth may result in galaxiids becoming severely food limited in the presence of trout.

The context of these changes in behaviour and growth needs to be understood. Many streams in which galaxiids of the G. vulgaris species-group live are at high elevations, suffer very cold winters, and water temperatures can remain low during much of the year. Low temperatures (the streams may experience marginal icing in winter) will likely affect ﬁsh behaviour in ways that can further compromise activity, feeding and survival. Some salmonids are known to disappear into cobble/ boulder substrate of streams when water temperatures are low, e.g. Rimmer et al. (1983) showed this in Atlantic salmon when temperatures dropped below 10 C, and that is not really cold for submontane streams in New Zealand. Rimmer et al. (1985) attributed this to their inability to hold position on the substrate, against stream ﬂows at low temperatures—an effect of cold temperatures on muscular activity and efﬁciency. Because galaxiids spawn in spring, soon after the winter, it is probably essential for them to sequester as much energy as possible over summer and autumn, to fuel gonad growth before going into winter. It appears that galaxiids may become increasingly diurnal at higher elevations (R.M. Allibone pers. comm.), and this may reﬂect their difﬁculty in obtaining enough food during the short, summer nights in higher southern latitudes, to see them through the winter and leave them with resources to maintain and produce gonads for spring spawning. Therefore anything that compromises the

ability of the galaxiids to obtain food and to minimise the effort needed to do so, may have adverse impacts on the viability of galaxiid populations—their survival, growth, and reproductive output—quite apart from, or in addition to, predation-caused mortalities. Moreover, according to Allibone and McIntosh (1999) ‘‘habitat structure and stability [play] a signiﬁcant role in the interaction between the galaxiids and salmonids, with coexistence more common in braided, disturbed habitats than stable single thread streams’’. These, perhaps, are streams that are already sub-optimal for trout and galaxiid foraging, though galaxiids, being rather smaller than trout, may be better suited to these habitats. Shuter and Post (1990) have explored problems of life in cold climates and, in particular, the question of progeny from one breeding season being sufﬁciently large to permit them to live through the next winter, and this could well be a signiﬁcant issue for galaxiids that live in montane streams of Central Otago, in southern New Zealand.

Galaxias vulgaris (sensu stricto) remains widely present and locally abundant, including presence in some areas that carry self-sustaining populations of brown trout. There is, nonetheless, an oft-repeated perception that reach-scale distributions of galaxiids and brown trout are in places complementary, and are perhaps becoming increasingly so. Baker et al. (2003), after detailed measurement of micro-habitats of G. depressiceps (one of the G. vulgaris species complex), found (as Townsend and Crowl 1991, had reported at a greater scale) that this galaxiid and brown trout rarely live together, and that at small spatial scale ‘‘relatively small numbers of...[G. depressiceps] were found co-existing with trout’’. They concluded that ‘‘trout might be inﬂuencing [G. depressiceps] abundance, [though] comparisons in habitat use between streams with and without trout should be regarded as tentative because of small sample size’’. In streams where there were no trout, Baker et al. (2003) reported that adult G. depressiceps were found in depths greater than 0.3 m, even though deeper water was sampled with similar effort to that in streams that did not contain trout—suggesting that adult G. depressiceps were avoiding water deeper than 0.3 m with trout present.

The process of galaxiid displacement by introduced salmonids may be slow and be still continuing, and although some populations/species in the G. vulgaris species-group are presently regarded as non-threatened—notwithstanding the apparent and varied negative impacts of introduced trouts discussed above—there is realistic concern that they may not retain that status indeﬁnitely (Allibone and Barrier 2004). Some historic evidence is suggesting loss of G. vulgaris from certain streams where they were present 30– 40 years ago, and the process of marginalisation or extirpation of galaxiids may still be occurring, largely unobserved, and certainly unquantiﬁed. There is plainly no reason to believe that presence of trout in New Zealand, even though now for well over a century (McDowall 1990b,

- 1994a), has resulted in establishment of a stable equilibrium between trout and galaxiid distributions and abundances.

CONSERVATION RANKING: The various described non-migratory Galaxias species in the G. vulgaris species-complex, as well as several genetic lineages that are not yet formally described, are variously a cause for conservation concern, partly because their distributions and abundances are not well known. Galaxias anomalus, G. depressiceps, G. eldoni, and G. pullus are listed as in Gradual Decline and one undescribed lineage, referred to as Galaxias ‘sp. D’, is listed as Nationally vulnerable (Hitchmough 2002). The New Zealand Department of Conservation recovery plan for these species (Allibone and Barrier

- 2004) points to G. anomalus suffering range reduction; G. eldoni being range restricted with many populations being small and vulnerable to local extirpation; G. pullus having fragmented ranges, G. depressiceps having range fragmented by presence of brown trout in downstream reaches of streams; G. gollumoides also having fragmented range; while Galaxias ‘teviot’ is known only from a small area. Thus the conservation status of these southern lineages is fragile. None of the species/lineages in this species complex are mentioned in IUCN rankings (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006).

The Galaxias olidus species group of Australian non-migratory galaxiids

Galaxias olidus (Fig. 44), described as widespread in the eastern Australian mountain ranges by McDowall and Frankenberg (1981), constitutes a species complex that is at present under taxonomic study (Tarmo Raadik pers. comm.; Raadik 2001; Raadik and Kuiter 2002; Kuiter 2003). The complex as a whole comprises smallish ﬁsh (to ca. 135 mm, but usually smaller—McDowall and Fulton 1996) often found swimming in the water column of pools and runs, mostly of small, lower order foothill streams in the mountains of eastern Australia.

Galaxias olidus in eastern Australia: As deﬁned in the most recent formal taxonomic treatment of Australia’s galaxiids (McDowall and Frankenberg 1981), G. olidus is present in streams draining the Great Dividing Range in eastern Australia, from southern Queensland, south through New South Wales and then south and west through Victoria, with additional disjunct populations in hill country around Adelaide in South Australia (Fig. 45, d). Ongoing studies are clearly showing that recognition of additional taxa is needed (Tarmo Raadik, pers. comm.), though how many, and how such taxa eventually relate to names treated as synonyms of G. olidus by McDowall and Frankenberg (1981) has yet to be determined. They are non-migratory (non-diadromous) ﬁshes that complete their whole life cycles in upland streams, with spring spawning, moderate-sized and numbers of eggs, and larvae forming loose shoals in pools and runs near adult habitat and spawning sites. Galaxias olidus does not have a reputation for being a good climber (compared, that is, with G. brevipinnis).

Fig. 44 Australian mountain galaxias, Galaxias olidus, reaches 135 mm LCF

- Fig. 45 Widespread distribution in eastern and southern Australia of populations belonging to the Galaxias olidus species complex

Until the species composition of this complex is resolved, it is impossible to address conservation issues that apply to the (now apparently) several species within the complex, although local ﬁsheries biologists have already done so, to some extent, with their recognition of barred galaxias, G. fuscus, as a distinct taxon (Raadik et al. 1996, and discussed below). When a fully revised taxonomy reveals the full diversity in this complex, there will inevitably be several more, highly localised species that will have heightened conservation threat simply because of their narrower ranges (as already with G. fuscus). These likely comprise a case of ‘uninformed ignorance’ progressing with time to ‘informed ignorance’.

Over several decades there have been repeated expressions of concern about the conservation status of populations of this species group, focussing especially on impacts of trout introductions. In most instances, conclusions that trout are affecting populations of G. olidus are based on circumstantial evidence—typically that trout and galaxiids have complementary distributions, yet again, and/or that galaxiids are present at lower densities where the two groups co-occur.

Frankenberg (1966), for example, found that, in streams on the Bogong High Plains in inland Victoria, brown trout ‘‘occupy the main body of the stream while galaxiids [G. olidus] are only found in situations inaccessible to trout, such as above waterfalls or in small tarns adjacent to the main stream...Assuming that the galaxiids were once continuously distributed, trout appear to have fragmented their range into a number of small isolated [headwater] populations. The two have been taken together only in sluggish lowland streams with abundant weed growth, providing a more complex environment and less favourable conditions for trout’’.

Cadwallader (1978) studied the distribution of G. olidus in Victoria, and reported that: ‘‘Trout have fragmented the ranges of some species of galaxiids into a number of small, isolated populations’’. He found brown trout at 19 sites, mostly in the main river channels and also below waterfalls in parts of the Seven Creeks system, whereas G. olidus was present at 22 sites, primarily above waterfalls, presumably impassable to trout. Both species were present at only three sites. Cadwallader (1979) again described fragmentation of the range of G. olidus by brown trout invasion in Seven Creeks, as did Jackson and Davies (1983). Llewellyn (1983) described G. olidus as ‘‘often abundant in loose shoals where trout are absent’’ in New South Wales streams. Jones et al. (1990) observed a negative association between trout and G. olidus in a stream in the Australian Capital Territory. Similarly, Morison and Anderson (1990) surveyed numerous tributaries in the Murray–Darling River, where they found both S. trutta and G. olidus at only four sites, but S. trutta present alone at 23; presence of S. trutta was regarded as a signiﬁcant negative effect on G. olidus.

Jackson and Williams (1980) examined distributions of ﬁsh in upper reaches of the Yarra River system, near Melbourne, the Otway Ranges southeast of Melbourne, and Wilson’s Promontory west of Melbourne. In 49 sites in the Yarra and 38 sites in the Otways, brown trout were present but no galaxiids, though based on other knowledge they expected that G. olidus to be present. This was interpreted as exclusion of galaxiids by brown trout. Koehn (1986) observed

similar distributions in the Yarra. By contrast, Jackson and Williams (1980) found no trout in Wilsons Promontory streams, though there was always at least one Galaxias species (though it was not G. olidus—galaxiids present were G. maculatus, G. brevipinnis and G. truttaceus).

Not all evidence is as circumstantial as the above, however. Tilzey (1976) undertook a long-
