- term study of trout ecology in Lake Eucumbene, in inland New South Wales, a lake created as a hydro-electricity reservoir in 1957 at an elevation of 1,164 m. It was stocked with brown and rainbow trout, which established self-sustaining populations, browns being more successful than rainbows (Tilzey 1977). Mature trout migrate into the lake’s tributaries to spawn, and these act as nursery streams for juvenile trout, as well as supporting some adults that fail to drop back to the lake after spawning. Tilzey (1976) surveyed ﬁsh populations of 27 tributaries of Lake Eucumbene, ﬁnding the following:

- 1. Nine streams carried only brown trout;
- 2. Two streams had only rainbow trout;
- 3. Fourteen streams had both brown and rainbow trout, browns much more numerous than rainbows in all but one instance (in 11 instances <20% were rainbows);
- 4. Two streams had brown trout, rainbow trout and G. brevipinnis (referred to as G. coxii by Tilzey), numbers of galaxiid being small (<7%);
- 5. Two streams had rainbow trout and G. brevipinnis, in one instance the galaxiid predominating; and
- 6. One stream had only G. olidus.

Both galaxiids and trout were present in only 4 of 30 streams. Moreover, in streams with both present, trout were found in easily accessible downstream reaches, whereas galaxiids typically were found only in upstream reaches. These patterns had developed rapidly, over several years since the lake was formed and trout introduced.

Tilzey’s (1976) study was undertaken during invasion by trout of the stream that had a population of G. olidus after the impoundment was created. Prior to trout invasion, biomass of G. olidus was evenly distributed at about 8 g or

2 ﬁsh/m2, both above and below a waterfall. After trout invasion there were no galaxiids below the waterfall, whereas biomass above the fall was unaltered. Tilzey (1976) considered that, since nothing else had changed, loss of the galaxiid downstream of the fall was a consequence of trout invasion, though the mechanism of trout impacts was unclear. Though Tilzey observed some dietary overlap between rainbow trout and G. olidus, other interactions would seem to be needed to provoke changes in the distribution of the galaxiid, within the time scale noted—possibly predation or competitive displacement.

The pre-impoundment species composition and distribution of galaxiids in the Lake Eucumbene system is unknown, but Tilzey thought that galaxiids would have been widespread, as they were widely present in inland, higher elevation streams of the Great Dividing Range of eastern Australia (McDowall and Frankenberg 1981). It seems likely, to me, however, that G. olidus would have been the more widespread species, with G. brevipinnis rarer, as G. brevipinnis is typically amphidromous, and streams in the Eucumbene catchment are a very long distance up-river from the sea (ca. 400 river km via the Snowy River). This is not beyond the migratory powers of G. brevipinnis though the numbers reaching this elevation and distance upstream would probably have been small. Moreover, abundance of what must formerly have been amphidromous populations G. brevipinnis (preimpoundment) this far upstream in Australian rivers is unusual (other than in lakes).

The outcome of formation of an impoundment on the Waipori River, with G. brevipinnis proliferating in Lake Mahinerangi, in New Zealand, discussed previously (McDowall and Allibone 1994), and also Tasmanian experience following formation of Lake Pedder, where there was unexpected rapid proliferation of G. brevipinnis following impoundment (discussed below), prompts the view that the same scenario may have applied in Lake Eucumbene following its formation, i.e., that the formerly rare G. brevipinnis would have invaded the lake from upper, diverted tributaries of the Snowy River, was proliferating rapidly in the new Lake Eucumbene, and was perhaps in the process of

displacing G. olidus from Lake Eucumbene tributaries at the time of Tilzey’s study. That question cannot now be resolved with certainty, but displacement of G. olidus by invasion of trout seems, as Tilzey concluded, unequivocal. Whether the G. brevipinnis population that developed in Lake Eucumbene has withstood trout predation since Tilzey’s (1976) study is another question that, as far as I know, has had no attention.

Fletcher (1979) undertook experimental studies of G. olidus populations in a series of small Victorian streams. She recorded G. olidus upstream of waterfalls, but in four streams where trout were present downstream of falls, G. olidus was either absent or present at low densities (perhaps as expatriates from populations above falls). She observed dietary overlap between the two species. Following introduction of trout into a screened experimental section of one streams, she observed negative impacts on G. olidus—which became less abundant and at lower biomass. The galaxiid density in Watchbox Creek, a tributary of Seven Creeks, for example, dropped from 2.4 to

- 0.9 ﬁsh/m2, and biomass from 3.6 to 2.1 gm/m2. This implies that impacts were greatest on juvenile G. olidus, suggesting either size-selective predation by trout on young-of-the-year galaxiids (Fletcher 1979, 1986), or competitive displacement. Dietary overlap may also have created competition for food (or perhaps for optimal feeding sites) as the larger galaxiids were in poorer condition than those in a control section upstream of the experimental section.

Fletcher (1986) later reported that in streams where brown trout were present, the composition of the stream invertebrate community was altered, with several taxa (Plecoptera and Trichoptera) eliminated or less abundant. She contrasted this with the response of the benthos to galaxiid predation where she observed an increase in diversity as well as more abundant Chironomidae, Ephemeroptera and Trichoptera. She concluded that introduction of trout had subtle effects on the benthos, where trout have replaced galaxiids, with cumulative negative impacts of trout on G. olidus

- at two levels—trout and G. olidus have tended to develop complementary distributions driven partly by competition for food (owing to overlap

in food preferences), and also possibly interference competition resulting from aggressive and territorial behaviour of the trout. These observations were short-term, and there may have been more serious, longer-term implications for G. olidus.

That trout do prey upon G. olidus was demonstrated by Bishop and Tilzey (1978) in a study of ﬁsh populations at the site of a proposed dam on the Shoalhaven River in southern New South Wales. They found that the galaxiid contributed more than 20% of the organisms in the trout’s diet in a tributary stream, and predicted, that after the lake was formed, ‘‘G. olidus will probably be a food item of initial signiﬁcance to the lentic trout populations but will then decline in abundance’’, and that the ‘‘future survival of G. olidus in the ... impoundment [would] probably [be] threatened by the presence of predatory game ﬁshes’’, viz. brown and rainbow trout. However, they thought that G. olidus has higher temperature tolerances than trout, which would enable it to persist in small, upstream tributaries of the lake, where elevated summer water temperatures would exclude year-round occupation by trout. This conclusion was consistent with a further study (see below).

Closs and Lake (1996) observed upstream expansion of the range of brown trout in the Lerderderg River, in central Victoria, over four successive years, and a coincident upstream contraction in the range of G. olidus. A short zone where ranges of the two species overlapped also retreated upstream, year by year. Then, a summer drought led to high trout mortality at the upper end of their range as the stream became restricted to isolated pools and water temperatures became elevated, whereupon the galaxiid population spread downstream again, re-occupying upper reaches of the former trout zone. When the stream became restricted to a series of separate pools, Closs and Lake (1996) found that G. olidus populations in each pool would extirpate nektonic food organisms there, whereas the trout died as a result of elevated temperatures. It seems that there was an unstable equilibrium in the ranges of trout and galaxiids in the river that shifted with the survival/mortality of trout from year to year, depending on ﬂows and weather conditions in

summer. G. olidus found enduring refuge in upper, small, headwater streams that were too small to support trout and/or from which trout were excluded by high summer temperatures. This placed the galaxiids in an advantageous position to reinvade further downstream when trout were extirpated by high temperatures and/or droughts. An implication of this conclusion is that galaxiids were continually dispersing downstream from headwater refuges but were unable to become established if trout occupied the downstream reaches (presumably owing to trout predation and/or competitive exclusion). Biotic factors may thus have been constraining the downstream range of G. olidus (i.e. impacts of trout), while physical factors (low ﬂows and oxygen and temperature levels) were controlling the upstream range of trout. Severe, but ﬂuctuating, impacts of trout on G. olidus seem clearly demonstrated, yet again. This is a good example of complementarity—mutual, but temporally and spatially shifting shifting exclusion of trout and galaxiids driven by mechanisms that result in cessation of observable, albeit ﬂuctuating, interactions between the two species. Lake (2003) reported another similar scenario, where even European carp became extirpated, though G. olidus survived in reaches of a stream impacted by drought, when ﬂow was reduced to small separate pools with depleted oxygen concentrations and elevated temperatures. Galaxias olidus can thus be ‘tough’ and ‘resilient’, but not when predatory trout are present.

Raadik (2003) reported on effects of a bush ﬁre in forests of the Australian Capital Territory, which had an interesting outcome. Trout populations were worse affected than galaxiids, so that some streams that previously supported healthy trout populations subsequently carried signiﬁcant numbers of G. olidus, ostensibly because the galaxiid, from residual headwater populations, or in reaches inaccessible to trout, was better able to cope with drought and reduction of streams to isolated pools following the ﬁre. This is reminiscent of Closs and Lake’s (1996) study, discussed above. Raadik reported that consideration was being given to removing remaining pockets of trout and inserting barriers into streams to obstruct reinvasion by trout from more perennial downstream reaches.

Lintermans (2000) studied interactions of G. olidus and rainbow trout in Lees Stream, a sub-montane stream in the Australian Capital Territory, with telling results. Rainbow trout had become established above and below a fall that inhibited upstream movement by ﬁsh. Galaxias olidus was found primarily in a tributary that connected to Lees Stream via a 15 m long pipe of 30 cm diameter, which passed beneath a road. Trout could reach the outfall of the pipe but were unable to move up the pipe to colonise the tributary. Galaxiids were rarely found downstream in the rainbow trout zone.

Lintermans (2000) removed all ﬁsh from Lees Stream, above the fall but below the pipe, by rotenone treatment. Following trout removal, he found that G. olidus rapidly occupied the vacated reach of Lees Stream above the fall but below the lower end of the pipe, where trout had formerly been present. Recolonization was almost entirely by 0+ year-class galaxiids, which Lintermans hypothesised were more prone to downstream displacement than older ﬁsh (presumably through the pipe). A year later, Lintermans (2000) found that two galaxiid year classes were present in the former trout zone, but could not distinguish between this being due to:

- 1. further downstream emigration of 0+ galaxiids from the tributary, or
- 2. reproduction by galaxiids in the formerly exclusively trout reach of the stream by the previous year’s emigrants from the upstream tributary.

At the time of reporting, Lintermans (2000) had observed re-occupation of the previous trout reach of Lees Stream, by G. olidus, for six years, and noted that trout extermination had made available 9 km of stream from which galaxiids had been excluded. The role of trout in excluding G. olidus from Lees Stream seems unequivocal, though the explicit cause remains uncertain.

All of these scenarios support Cadwallader and Backhouse’s (1983) summary that: ‘‘introduced trout appear to have fragmented the range of mountain galaxiids into small isolated [headwater] populations. Trout usually occupy the main body of a stream while mountain galaxiids are often found only in situations apparently inacces-

sible to trout, such as above waterfalls or other obstacles’’, and they pointed to the likelihood of competition for food and space, as well as predation as likely adverse impacts. Arthington (1991) similarly reviewed published information and concluded that ‘‘decline and fragmentation of ... G. olidus ... may be due to exploitation competition [with brown trout] since their diets are similar’’. There is thus a mass of circumstantial evidence as well, sometimes, as strong explicit evidence for serious impacts on the G. olidus species complex from invasion of their habitats by trout—especially brown trout but also rainbows, on a wide geographical front in southeastern Australia. Because the G. olidus complex is so widely distributed there is probably no explicit concern for its conservation, though there ought to be concern for its genetic diversity; moreover the conservation status of various taxonomic components/lineages of the species-group is unknown, and may eventually be viewed very differently. If the processes of repetitive population decline and disappearance followed by reinvasion persist, there is a serious prospect of genetic bottlenecks, and reduced genetic variability, a potentially adverse scenario for survival of populations. The threatened status of G. fuscus (see below), may be a portent of things to come.

Bond and Lake (2003) made an interesting observation about the ecology of G. olidus, remarking that it ‘‘appears to have widely dispersive young...which partially decouple adult abundances from localized reproductive success’’. This may have been an effective way for the species to continually be spreading downstream to broaden its range, and may also have helped to minimise casualties in headwater streams that may cease to ﬂow. With arrival of piscivorous salmonids, it may have turned into a ‘food-delivery mechanism’ for trout.

Lintermans et al. (1990; Lintermans 1993) also examined the impacts of oriental weatherloach, Misgurnus anguillicaudatus (f. Cobitidae—

- Fig. 46) on presence of G. olidus in streams of the Australian Capital Territory. In one stream he found weatherloach largely present only with other exotics; at only two sites was it recorded with any native ﬁsh species, and despite the galaxiid being locally widespread, the weatherlo-

Fig. 46 The alien cobitid weatherloach, Misgurnus anguillicaudatus, which is implicated in decline of populations of the Australian mountain galaxias, Galaxias olidus

ach was found at only one of these with G. olidus: at one site a solitary G. olidus was present with weatherloach. In another stream, G. olidus, which was common approximately 150 m up stream from where weatherloach cut out, was never found where weatherloach were present (Lintermans et al. 1990). They found, also, that weatherloach preyed on G. olidus, and concluded that the large size and habitat diversity of streams where the two species co-occurred may occasionally have allowed G. olidus to co-occur with weatherloach. They saw immense potential for the spread of weatherloach in streams across eastern Australia, and thus considerable potential for negative impacts on small native ﬁsh. This species was introduced as an aquarium ﬁsh (from where it presumably escaped into the wild), but Raadik et al. (2005) placed it in the ‘‘SHAME FILE: ALIEN Creature Feature’’, noted that its importation was banned in 1986, and that it had been declared noxious in most states, ‘‘but continues to spread’’.

Another potential threat for G. olidus, which seems to derive from an exotic species, is an apparently pathogenic virus, epizootic haematopoietic necrosis, that is carried by European perch, but which is highly pathogenic for G. olidus (Langdon 1989).

CONSERVATION RANKING: Despite the discussion and investigation associated with the distribution of G. olidus, and the impacts of land use and alien species invading their habitats, G. olidus has never been classiﬁed as Threatened, with the proviso, however, that what is now recognised as ‘‘G. olidus’’ almost certainly comprises a species complex, some components of which may need classiﬁcation as threatened at some level. <www.redlist.org> did not listed this species.

Barred galaxias, Galaxias fuscus, in inland Victoria: Although Galaxias fuscus was treated as a junior synonym of G. olidus in McDowall and Frankenberg’s (1981) the local opinion has since ﬁrmed (and is accepted here) that it represents a distinct species (Raadik et al. 1996). This conviction is driven in part by discovery of locations where G. olidus and G. fuscus co-occur (Raadik

- 1995). Sympatry, plus the seriously imperilled conservation status of the few known G. fuscus populations, has led to its inclusion in local faunal lists, despite lack of formal taxonomic reinstatement (Allen et al. 2002; Kuiter 2003). Such, perhaps premature, practice can be justiﬁed, if for no other reason, by the urgent need to draw attention to conservation threats to G. fuscus populations, which have declined seriously over most of their narrow range in headwater tributaries of the Goulburn River, this again owing largely to impacts of invasive rainbow and brown trout (Koehn and O’Connor 1990).

Galaxias fuscus is another small (to ca. 130 mm—Raadik 1995, 2002), mid-water to cover-frequenting galaxiid that is found in small cool, submontane streams with coarse cobbleboulder substrates, often in forested landscapes, inland from Melbourne, Australia. It has narrow geographical range, in headwater tributaries of the Goulburn River, a tributary of the inland Murray River drainage, typically at elevations above 400 m. It spends its whole life in river headwater tributaries, often ﬁrst and second order streams. It does not seem to climb falls.

Though, according to Raadik (1995), the natural range of G. fuscus was always restricted, he considered that it was probably once substantially more widespread than presently known—from about 25 km of stream channel in about eight stream systems. When summarising knowledge of the status of G. fuscus, Raadik (1995) reported that of the 13 recorded populations, 5 were considered extirpated, 5 endangered, and 3 vulnerable (Fig. 47). He found that brown trout, and more particularly rainbow trout, had invaded nearly the entire range of G. fuscus, and he thought that predation by these trout species was the major threat to the species’ persistence. He feared that continuing trout expansion, may lead to further contraction in the range, and thus

Fig. 47 Status of populations of the Australian barred galaxias, Galaxias fuscus (from Raadik 2002)

abundance, of G. fuscus. Complementary distributions of trout (primarily rainbow trout) and the galaxiid were (again) demonstrated, and attributed to predation—the galaxiid was recorded in rainbow trout diet (Raadik 1995). In a species recovery plan for G. fuscus, Saddlier and Raadik (1995) described several instances of ‘real time’ decline in abundance of G. fuscus, which they attributed to trout, e.g. they noted that an electric ﬁshing survey in November 1990 showed a density of 0.98 ﬁsh/m2 in one stream, but that a re-survey in January 1994 ‘‘saw a dramatic reduction in the population density of [G. fuscus] at the same site’’ to 0.03 ﬁsh/m2 ‘‘due to the presence of trout, which had been progressively moving upstream towards the temporary barrier constructed during 1993’’. Shirley (1991) reported that competition for foraging sites and dietary overlap was also present. ‘‘The paucity of information on the life history and ecology of this species does not preclude the identiﬁcation of trout predation as the most serious threatening process facing the existing Barred Galaxias populations...The threats posed...are exacerbated [by] the small size and high degree of fragmentation of extant galaxias populations. There is the risk of losing entire populations through catastrophic events (such as ﬁre, toxic spill, etc.) ...The typical scenario is for a pattern of parapatric distribution of Barred Galaxias and Rainbow Trout to develop, followed in some cases by the local extinction of the galaxias population. Where no physical barriers are present to stop this advance [of rainbow trout] ...the range of Barred Galaxias is contracting rapidly’’ (Raadik 1995). Here, again, we have a scenario that would easily lead

to genetic bottlenecks, and adverse scenarios for survival of populations. Raadik considered invasion of trout to be still going on, commenting that: ‘‘...Taggerty River itself supports a large population of Barred Galaxias upstream of the Upper Taggerty Road, and a small fragmented population amongst a dense population of Rainbow Trout downstream of the road. The trout are currently colonising further upstream, slowly moving through the culvert under the Upper Taggerty Road....Rainbow trout have expanded their range into nearly all of the extant populations [of G. fuscus] and are continuing to colonise further upstream with a consequent contraction of the range [of G. fuscus], and where there are no barriers impeding upstream movement by the trout, colonisation is happening rapidly’’ (Raadik

- 1995). In one stream a population present in 1986 had been replaced by rainbow trout in 1997; other populations were isolated in headwaters by rainbow trout populations further downstream (again!) and yet others were upstream of falls, with dense populations of rainbow trout below the falls. An interesting observation about trout in these streams is that the small trout are old, stunted ﬁsh, rather than young ﬁsh, and that therefore their contribution to angling is negligible. Thus, although trout have been in Australia for nearly 150 years (Clements 1988), the species’ range is still increasing and its impacts are still emerging in some habitats.

A recovery plan for G. fuscus (Raadik 1995) nominated invasion by rainbow and brown trout, leading to predation and competitive displacement, as the chief concerns for conserving this species, though there were also concerns about habitat damage from forestry (sedimentation; loss of riparian vegetation), mining, roading, as well as a chemical (cyanide) spill (Rich 1986; Wager and Jackson 1993; Raadik 1995). Reports of G. fuscus being a drift feeder at the upper ends of pools in streams might indicate one area of interaction with trout that forage in similar places—predation or competitive exclusion of some sort again

- seem likely. Certain sites, where this ﬁsh formerly was recorded, no longer have populations, and this includes the type locality of G. fuscus in the Rubicon River, though Raadik (2002) reported its presence further upstream in a tributary where

trout were excluded by a 3.5 m high natural rock barrier. Similar scenarios have been described for other populations of G. fuscus, in some of which stomach analyses of trout demonstrated predation on G. fuscus (Raadik 1995).

Summarising, Raadik (1995) blamed competition for feeding sites, and possible dietary overlap (see also Shirley 1991; Shirley and Raadik 1997), but regarded predation by both brown and rainbow trout as the major threat to the future of G. fuscus. Recovery actions proposed included:

- 1. Obtaining a better understanding of the species’ distribution;
- 2. Monitoring of known populations;
- 3. Insertion of barriers to upstream migration by trout, to limit encroachment by trout; and:
- 4. Removal of trout by electric ﬁshing or use of rotenone (Raadik 1995).

Morris et al. (2000) similarly attributed decline of G. fuscus to negative interactions with trout—primarily predation on juvenile G. fuscus, and competition for habitat and food for the adults. A Melbourne newspaper (Sunday Sun Herald, 10 April, 1994) reported a proposal to use rotenone poison to remove rainbow trout from a stream carrying G. fuscus but subject to rainbow trout invasion. The report discussed ‘‘culling thousands’’ of trout that had moved upstream from angling reaches further downstream.

In the latest report on this species, Raadik (2002) reported discovery of several further populations in remote areas in Victorian mountains from which the Goulburn River ﬂows. He described G. fuscus as ‘‘restricted to the very headwaters of upland tributaries of the Goulburn River system... [and] ... threatened at a state and national level’’. Many populations are limited to a few upstream kilometres of a catchment, their vulnerability increasing because the populations are not close enough together to seriously contemplate attempts to reconnect them by excluding predators from downstream reaches. Raadik (2002) reported mostly successful establishment of exclusion barriers to inhibit further upstream movement by trout, followed by removal of residual trout from reaches upstream of the barriers. Remnant populations of G. fuscus were generally stable and recruitment was demon-

strated in most of them, leading to slow recovery of populations and some spread into exclusion zones formerly inhabited by trout. Populations nonetheless remain restricted to headwater streams with trout present downstream.

Lintermans and Raadik (2003) reported that in 1994–95: ‘‘Barriers to trout reinvasion were ﬁrst constructed...after which...a total of 20 km of stream length in seven small streams in the Goulburn River catchment in Victoria were treated with rotenone’’ to remove trout. They summarised that there were now only 12 known populations, 7 of them in small streams. Four populations were known to have become extinct; predation by trout on juvenile galaxiids and, to a lesser degree, adults, was identiﬁed as the process most threatening survival of G. fuscus.

CONSERVATION RANKING: Galaxias fuscus is ranked as Critically Endangered by the Threatened Species Committee of the Australian Society for Fish Biology (Crook and Pogonoski 2003), and Endangered Nationally by both the Australia and New Zealand Environment and Conservation Council (ANZECC) and the Victorian State Government, with the comment that ‘‘Predation on adult and juvenile [G. fuscus] by rainbow and brown trout is

- seen as the major threat to the persistence of the species (Raadik 1995). IUCN lists it as Critically Endangered (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006).

Pedder galaxias, Galaxias pedderensis, and the formation of the new Lake Pedder in Tasmania

The formation in 1972–1974 of the (new) Lake Pedder in central southern Tasmania (Fig. 45,

- Plate 2) precipitated one of the most intense environmental debates in Australian history (Johnson 1972; Burton et al. 1975; Gee 1978). This has, for some interest-groups and individuals, remained a ‘sore point’ ever since (Lake 1998, 2001; Walker 2002). Ingram et al. (1990) called it the ‘‘catalyst that triggered the recent ‘greening’ of Tasmanian and Australian politics’’, its inundation occurring ‘‘amid much outcry and protest’’ (Lake 1998).

Implicated in a proposal to dam the Gordon, Huon and Serpentine Rivers was the ﬂooding and thus obliteration of Lakes Pedder a small montane tarn at nearly 300 m elevation (Buckney and Tyler 1973), and also nearby Lake Maria. Lake (1998) described the original Lake Pedder as ‘‘renowned for its setting and for the beauty of its remarkable beach’’, and (Lake 2001) as ‘‘very beautiful...in a dramatically inspiring way. There were many moods of the lake, from balmy stillness of autumn, to the swirling cold storms of any time of the year. To many, the lake was the natural centrepiece, the symbol of sanctity of the southwest wilderness; in destroying it the wilderness shrinks’’. It was from Lakes Pedder and Maria that G. pedderensis and G. parvus, had recently been described by Frankenberg (1968; Fig. 48). Though potential impacts of the proposed impoundment on these ﬁsh were only part of the concerns of conservationists who were opposing the dam (Bayly et al. 1972), they were nonetheless a signiﬁcant aspect of the move for environmental protection, and are of particular relevance here. Lake (1998) stated that ‘‘Ofﬁcial investigation of the lake [Pedder] failed to detect the unusual nature of the ﬂora and fauna’’, and that Lake Pedder’s ‘‘very signiﬁcant biological values were not realized until the grand scheme to ﬂood the lake had become entrenched in government policy’’. Moreover, it was ﬁnally the ‘‘voluntary efforts of committed biologists’’ who described the unique diversity of this lake. Protests by conservationists led to a Commission of Enquiry, and Lake (2001) lamented that, despite the enquiry recommending that ‘‘funds be set aside for an urgent and intensive program of investigation to determine the possibilities of natural re-establishment of the species in the new environment of the enlarged impoundment...this was never done’’. Moreover, some of those clamouring for reparation were described as having exaggerated the impacts, one commentator alleging that doing so was ‘‘probably just as common as obscured or diminished impacts [being identiﬁed] by developers’’. This commentator also alleged that assessment of impacts exaggerated the harm, and that such assessment is a subjective matter of which ‘‘unequivocal examples are not easy to ﬁnd. I would offer the alleged

Plate 2The original Lake Pedder, said by some to have galvanised environmental debate in Australia, the lake now obliterated by a large hydro dam

(photo: Kevin Leeson). Inset:Galaxias pedderensis, now evidently extinct in its natural habitat

detrimental impacts of the ﬂooding of Lake Pedder on the ﬂora and fauna (Bayly et al. 1972) as an example’’.

Lake (1998, 2001) was so distressed at the events that he could not bring himself to call the new impoundment ‘‘Lake Pedder’’, a name being used by the developers ‘‘Almost as a gesture of repentant propaganda...the new impoundment ...was called Lake Pedder even though it bore little resemblance to the original lake, and ﬂooded two other lake systems as well’’. Lake (1998, 2001), resolutely and consistently referred to the water body formed as the ‘‘Huon-Serpentine Impoundment’’. And just as volunteer efforts were largely responsible for discovering the biological novelties in the original lake, it was Sam Lake and some of his colleagues, in lieu of an ofﬁcially sanctioned and funded monitoring program, who organised their own monitoring program ‘‘through the unofﬁcially and frugally funded efforts of scientists’’. Lake (2001) even wrote of proposals to remove the dam and restore the lake, something that he thought could ‘‘serve

- as a great symbol to show that if we put our effort into it we can bring back to their former value, some parts of the natural world that we have needlessly destroyed’’. And, so, discovery of Galaxias pedderensis was symbolic of the values of a scenic and biological gem in the Tasmanian high country. As we shall see, the fate of this galaxiid has been nearly as lamentable as the fate of the lake whose name the species perpetuates.

Galaxias pedderensis is another non-migratory galaxiid found originally at submontane elevations only in a small lake in the headwaters of the Huon River. The ﬁsh is little studied, but is cryptic and benthic as an adult, grows to about 160 mm, and has low fecundity with a few hundred to over a thousand eggs. The ‘original’ Lake Pedder was a small shallow lake surrounded

- Fig. 48 The critically threatened Tasmanian Pedder galaxias, Galaxias pedderensis (reaches 160 mm LCF)

by swamps and peat plains. The lake appears to have acted as a nursery habitat for juvenile G. pedderensis, which formed shoals around the lake margins, whereas adults were mostly in the gently ﬂowing, meandering adﬂuvial streams. If these various habitats are crucial to the well-being of G. pedderensis, it would seem that the species must be able to move between lake tributaries and the lake pelagial zones. G. pedderensis is a poor climber of falls.

Following formation of the ‘new’ Lake Pedder G. pedderensis underwent a ‘‘population explosion’’ during the late 1970s in the vastly increased area of lake (Hamr 1992a, b); ‘‘large schools [were] ...observed throughout the lake’’ an example of ‘‘trophic upsurge’’ (Lake 2001). Brown trout were liberated into the new lake soon after its formation, and the stock of extremely large ﬁsh that developed (described by Lake [2001] as ‘‘monstrous’’) attracted intense attention from anglers. In addition, G. brevipinnis rapidly proliferated after Lake Pedder was ﬁlled (as discussed elsewhere for a New Zealand impoundment). Galaxias brevipinnis had not previously been reported from the catchment of the old lake (Gaffney et al. 1992; Hamr 1992a, b), but was recorded from the Serpentine River prior to impoundment. The Tasmanian Inland Fisheries Commission (Lynch 1972) expressed interest in the prospect that G. pedderensis might provide food for trout being stocked into the lake (yet another galaxiid species debased as trout fodder) and, if one believed that other Galaxias species lived in balance with brown trout in Tasmanian lakes, there would seem (maybe) no reason to be concerned. And it does appear that growth of trout in the new Lake Pedder was driven initially by predation on the abundant stocks of both G. brevipinnis and G. pedderensis that developed (Hamr 1992a), the rapid expansion of populations of both Galaxias species perhaps (Lake 2001) being driven by high productivity (‘‘trophic upsurge’’) that is a common feature of a newly formed lake.

Galaxias pedderensis populations in Lake Pedder peaked in 1977 (about 5 years after the lake formed), declined rapidly during the late 1970s and it had disappeared from catches of ﬁsh from the lake by 1980 (Lake 1998, 2001; Threatened

Species Unit 2005). In a review of the status of threatened Tasmanian galaxiids, Lynch (1983) commented that G. pedderensis was already a matter of ‘‘some concern’’, having become abundant during the new lake’s early years, but Lynch said that it had ‘‘decline[d] considerably in recent years’’, an understatement given above-mentioned comments by Lake (1998). In the following year Lynch (1984) reported angler concern about a ‘‘sudden decline’’ in numbers of G. pedderensis (i.e., trout fodder), as a result of which ‘‘the matter has been under investigation by research staff...Only a few specimens...were recorded, where sampling in previous years would have produced signiﬁcant numbers...there has been a serious depletion in the stocks’’. Lynch offered ‘‘No deﬁnite explanation for the scarcity of the galaxiids’’. Numbers of G. pedderensis remained

- so low that during Inland Fisheries Commission surveys in 1983–1989, the species was not found in the lake itself, was becoming extremely rare in the lake’s tributaries, and was clearly endangered.

According to Sloan (1989), anglers expressed concern at the reduction in the numbers of galaxiids [species unstated] in Lake Pedder, though he did not explicitly state whether they were worried about the galaxiid, or about lack of food for trout (it would not be hard to guess!). A survey of populations was conducted and, to the surprise of few, numbers were still extremely low in all lake sites visited, though some ‘‘galaxiids’’ were found in most of the creeks examined; however, their abundances varied and G. pedderensis was found in low numbers, in only four creeks, and was not present at any swamp or lake site. Sloan (1989) commented that ‘‘This species was formerly very common in the lake and was responsible for the large galaxiid schools which were observed in the 1970s. Its numbers appear to have declined seriously to the extent that it is now in danger of extinction. The reasons for the decline are not immediately obvious and the [Tasmanian Inland Fisheries] Commission intends to examine the problem further next summer’’. Galaxias pedderensis, which at one stage was the most common species in the lake, was now ‘‘extremely rare, even in creeks ﬂowing into Lake Pedder’’. The populations had, in fact, collapsed to the point where no more than 200

ﬁsh remained in two tributaries of Lake Pedder (Gaffney et al. 1992). Also, the species was present only in the ‘‘lower, well-shaded, meandering sections of stream’’ (Hamr 1992a), whereas G. brevipinnis was by then abundant and had ‘‘taken over [in the] shallower and fasterﬂowing upper reaches’’. Hamr (1995) further summarised that G. pedderensis was then ‘‘found in the lower meandering portions’’ of two streams draining into Lake Pedder ‘‘up to 2 km upstream from the impoundment. It is most abundant in forested sections where the stream is deeply convoluted with deep pools, relatively slow ﬂow, sandy bottom and abundant cover’’. Galaxias brevipinnis was now the dominant galaxiid in many streams possibly owing to the altered character of streams that entered the lake; the low-gradient, gently ﬂowing streams that formerly entered the old lake had been replaced by much steeper and more swiftly ﬂowing streams in a hilly landscape that would have suited G. brevipinnis (Threatened Species Unit 2005).

Fulton (1987) criticised suggestions that G. pedderensis was threatened, stating that a species ‘‘should only be listed in a category of some signiﬁcance if it has received sufﬁcient study, adequate collections or observations have been made to conﬁrm its identity and determine its distribution with reasonable surety’’. This seems a curious attitude, given the well-documented, massive change in abundance of G. pedderensis in the now maturing, new Lake Pedder, which was by then in a phase of ‘‘trophic depression’’, the reciprocal of ‘‘trophic upsurge’’ discussed earlier. In so arguing, Fulton implicitly rejected the role of the ‘precautionary principle’ in helping to stem the decline of possibly threatened species. Fulton (1987) also argued that the Tasmanian Inland Fisheries Commission had adjusted its trout stocking policy to avoid conﬂict with sensitive native ﬁsh species, and had asked other Tasmanian Government agencies to take account of the distributions and habitat needs of threatened species. As well, he asserted that G. pedderensis had been afforded some protection by the inclusion of Lake Pedder (despite being a highly modiﬁed lake), within a World Heritage Area—Lake 1998, 2001), though it seems that this was far too late, as trout and

G. brevipinnis were both well established and very widespread in the new lake, and G. pedderensis was in catastrophic decline. The lake’s trout population was by then self-sustaining. Nor did inclusion of the lake in the World Heritage Area have value for G. pedderensis conservation, though the species was then only Potentially Threatened in the Australian Society for Fish Biology’s ranking of Australian freshwater ﬁshes. Additionally, should there have been enough information to make such provisions for the protection of G. pedderensis by adjusting trout stocking rates (which is unlikely), it would seem surprising that there was apparently inadequate information to permit an informed, at least interim, ranking of the species’ conservation status.

This series of events thus created serious conservation concern among many Tasmanian biologists and conservationists (Sanger 1988; Gaffney et al. 1992): Hamr (1992a) described the status of G. pedderensis as ‘‘precarious’’ and classed it as the ‘‘most endangered ﬁsh species in Australia and likely to become extinct within ﬁve years if recovery actions [were] not undertaken immediately’’. Crook (2001) reported concern that the species was ‘‘in danger of becoming the ﬁrst Australian freshwater ﬁsh species to become extinct’’, and that, as far as then known, it was no longer present in what remained of its natural habitat. Decline was attributed, at least in part, to predation by trout and G. brevipinnis and, though Hamr (1992b) could ﬁnd no G. pedderensis in predator stomachs, he noted ‘‘numerous anecdotal reports of large trout preying on G. pedderensis during the ﬁrst 10 years following the ﬂooding of the original Lake Pedder’’. It seems that, by the time of Hamr’s study, G. pedderensis was so reduced in numbers that there were not enough to form a signiﬁcant item in the food of predators (another example of impacts appearing to cease as a result of mutually exclusive distributions that result from species’ interactions).

Adverse factors, other than predation, thought to be affecting G. pedderensis included displacement from habitat by invading species (trout and G. brevipinnis), plus the loss of suitable habitat occasioned by formation of the new lake

(Gaffney et al. 1992)—though habitat sustainability seems unlikely to be a core issue for G. pedderensis conservation, given its earlier abundance in the lake. Hamr (1992b) concluded that ‘‘both G. brevipinnis and brown trout have replaced G. pedderensis in most of the tributaries of the new lake. G. brevipinnis is of particular concern because it has invaded even the smaller streams (which are not suitable for S. trutta) and [has] apparently displaced G. pedderensis in these streams’’. Its climbing ability allows it to invade streams beyond the reach of trout, and so G. brevipinnis had the potential to greatly accelerate and accentuate trout impacts downstream.

An additional potential impact was identiﬁed by Sanger (1996)—who raised the prospect that European perch could migrate from Lake Gordon to Lake Pedder via an artiﬁcial canal, and considered that perch are ‘‘very effective predators of native ﬁsh, in particular galaxiids. ...Very few galaxiid species can coexist with perch’’. This risk remains un-evaluated, though G. pedderensis populations potentially under threat from perch predation have gone, anyway.

The Tasmanian Inland Fisheries Commission gave consideration to assisting recovery of populations through habitat protection/restoration and/or exclusion of invasive species, but this was complicated by concerns that Pedder galaxias may need access to the lake as larvae and juveniles which, if true, of course increased its vulnerability to predation by lake populations of trout and juvenile G. brevipinnis, and stream populations of both species, especially by G. brevipinnis in small, headwater streams. Proposals were developed to attempt captive reproduction of G. pedderensis, and to establish a new population by translocation of ﬁsh into a lake that lacked trout (though obviously both options were subject to the proviso that ﬁsh for translocation could be found). A ﬁsh few were eventually located in tributaries of the new lake, and captive reproduction was attempted with limited and only very short-term success—small numbers of eggs were fertilised by artiﬁcial stripping of ripe ﬁsh and ‘‘Only 11 individuals were raised through to juvenile size’’ (Hamr 1995). These few ﬁsh, plus others recovered from

natural habitats (a total of 34 ﬁsh—Threatened Species Unit 2005), were translocated into Lake Oberon, a ‘‘ﬁsh free lake in the Western Arthur Range’’ of southwestern Tasmania—Hamr 1995). This was successful (Anon 1997; Jackson 1999b, 2001a, 2002b; Hardie 2003b)—even though Lake Oberon is very different from the original Lake Pedder, being deep with steep and rocky shores. Crook (2001) reported that a survey in Lake Oberon revealed at least 500 adult G. pedderensis

- as well as numerous juveniles, the ﬁsh being found mostly in the lake. The species had also spread downstream into another small lake.

To provide further assurance of survival of this ﬁsh, 74 adults were then translocated from Lake Oberon into a ﬁsh free dam ‘‘near Strathgordon in south-west Tasmania in March 2001’’ (Crook 2001; Threatened Species Unit 2005). This dam was ﬁrst drained to ensure that it was ﬁsh free, the outﬂow was modiﬁed to exclude upstream migration of ﬁsh into the dam, and modiﬁcations were undertaken to improve its suitability for G. pedderensis, including formation of a small stream entering the lake (Threatened Species Unit 2005). These translocated populations provide the best prospect for survival of what was at one stage among the most threatened of Australian freshwater ﬁshes. A research imperative for this species’ conservation is to determine whether both lakes and adﬂuvial streams are essential for it to complete its life cycle. Also there has been mention of reintroducing it into Lake Pedder, though this would seem to require establishment of protected areas that exclude brown trout and possibly G. brevipinnis. There are also concerns about genetic variability of residual stocks, with prospects that survivors and translocated stocks are from a narrow gene pool.

Despite all the above, Wager and Jackson (1993) argued that reasons for decline of G. pedderensis were still ‘‘Not conclusively known’’, though they admitted that interactions with G. brevipinnis and brown trout ‘‘may be responsible’’. With trout predation and G. brevipinnis proliferation taking place simultaneously, it is impossible to determine which, if either, or to what extent both factors, had the prime effects on populations of G. pedderensis. A signiﬁcant aspect of the threatened status of G. pedderensis

has been its original very limited geographical range (only original Lake Pedder), but regardless of the speciﬁcs of threat, the outcome of forming the new Lake Pedder was catastrophic for the galaxiid.

Hamr (1995) reported that ‘‘All of the remaining habitat and populations (original and translocated) are found within the South-West National Park/World Heritage Area... [and although] Introduced species captured in ...remaining habitats were removed... invasion and proliferation of exotics’ remained a major problem, thus reiterating Fulton’s (1987) comments.

CONSERVATION RANKING: Galaxias pedderensis is listed as Endangered by Tasmanian authorities (Anon n.d., Threatened Species Unit 2005), who reported it as now ‘‘apparently extinct within its native range’’, and as Critically Endangered in Australia (Crook 2001). Crook and Pogonoski (2003) reported the nomination of G. pedderensis as Extinct in the Wild, but the Threatened Species Committee of the Australian Society for Fish Biology decided to retain its rank as Critically Endangered, subject to reconsideration by the International Union for the Conservation of Nature. Its survival depends on a self-maintaining ex situ population in Lake Oberon. IUCN also listed it as Critically Endangered in 1996 (Baillie and Groombridge 1996) and 2004 (IUCN 2006).

Three large bodied Victorian and Tasmanian species

Southern Victoria and Tasmania have three distinctively large and stout-bodied galaxiids that probably form a closely related species-group (Ovenden and White 1990; Ovenden et al. 1993), which is dealt with, below.

Amphidromous and lacustrine Galaxias truttaceus in southern Australia: Galaxias truttaceus (Fig. 49A) is one of the larger Australian galaxiids, reaching a length of ca. 200 mm. It is found mostly in pools, being cryptic among woody debris or beneath overhanging stream banks;

- Fig. 49 Three stout bodied galaxiids from southeastern Australia and Tasmania: (A) Spotted galaxias, Galaxias truttaceus (reaches >200 mm LCF); (B) Golden galaxias, G. auratus (>235 mm LCF); and (C) Saddled galaxias, G. tanycephalus (>145 mm LCF)

and it is typically amphidromous, with sea-living whitebait juveniles returning to freshwater from the sea in spring. These are captured in the Tasmanian whitebait ﬁshery (Fulton and Pavuk

- 1988). It is found mostly at low elevations, near the sea, and has no reputation as a climber. It spawns in autumn (as typical of diadromous galaxiids), producing several thousand smallish eggs that are laid amongst root wads along stream margins (Humphries 1986). It is widespread in southern Victoria, primarily in coastal drainages, present in streams on King and Flinders Islands in Bass Strait, and widespread in Tasmania (Fig. 50); there are a few outlier populations in Western Australia (McDowall and Frankenberg

- 1981; Morgan 2003) (Fig. 51). Particularly in Tasmania, but also occasionally

in Victoria, there are landlocked populations of G. truttaceus. Western Australian populations are in catchments that seldom if ever ﬂow to sea, so they too will be non-amphidromous.

Within the above ranges, G. truttaceus occurs widely in both streams and lakes inhabited by brown trout (and to a lesser extent, rainbow trout), but opinions on impacts of brown trout are

inconsistent. Renowden (1968) reported that, in the Otway Ranges in southern Victoria, G. truttaceus was rarely found in main streams where trout were often present, but instead occupied largely marginal areas inaccessible to brown trout or in stream reaches upstream of barriers that prevent their invasion. Moreover, whereas G. truttaceus was reportedly abundant there before brown trout were introduced, it was considered to have since become much rarer, though data are elusive. Renowden (1968) hypothesised dietary overlap between G. truttaceus and brown trout, but also concluded that as the galaxiids ‘‘are often exceedingly scarce in streams possessing little cover...predation rather than competition, has been the major factor in the decline of the galaxiid’’. The logic of this discussion of cause and effect is somewhat elusive and, in addition, deforestation or other forms of habitat degradation, may have contributed to a decline. Jackson and Williams (1980), however, identiﬁed no negative effects on G. truttaceus from presence of brown trout in Otways streams (though they suggested impacts on other species of Galaxias).

Lacustrine populations of G. truttaceus provide food for brown trout in upland Tasmanian lakes, Davies and Fulton (1987) commenting on knowledge that there are four galaxiids in Great Lake, in central Tasmania, ‘‘certainly removes the need for any forage ﬁsh introductions to the system...However, from a conservation point of view the populations are in a very healthy state and there is no concern [about] risk from trout predation’’. What data this conclusion was based on was not revealed, and one may hope they were correct.

The inﬂuence of trout may, however, sometimes be subtle, as demonstrated by Ault and White’s (1994) comparison of abundance/habitatuse of G. truttaceus and brown trout in Tasmanian streams where both, and when only one of these was present. Their comparisons of habitat use by three size classes of G. truttaceus provided evidence for habitat displacement of G. truttaceus in streams where present with trout. They thus claimed ‘‘strong evidence that brown trout adversely affect populations of G. truttaceus’’, with presence of trout more important in deter-

- Fig. 50 Distributions of the stout bodied galaxiids: (d)—spotted galaxias, Galaxias truttaceus; (w)–Golden galaxias, G. auratus, and: ( )—Saddled galaxias, G. tanycephalus, from southeastern Australia and Tasmania

- Fig. 51 Very restricted distribution of spotted galaxias (d) Galaxias truttaceus in southern Western Australia

and that when about 25 mm long, the juveniles move into the river.

Galaxias truttaceus spawns in autumn in Western Australia (Morgan 2003), as do diadromous stocks in eastern Australia, but unlike spring-spawning lacustrine stocks in eastern Australia (Humphries 1989), and here we may have local adaptation in Western Australian populations, in which open-water, pelagic larval phases are timed to exploit surface waters of lakes in autumn and winter when water temperatures may be at their minima, and also when lake levels are higher (Morgan 2003).

Morgan (2003) found that the diet of adult G. truttaceus in Western Australia largely comprised terrestrial insects (Coleoptera and Hymenoptera), thinking this to be driven by ‘‘resource partitioning of limited food resources in southwestern Australian streams’’, though he made no mention of the diets of other co-occurring ﬁsh, so the question of resource partitioning in these waterways is evidently intuitive rather than based on data. Diverse galaxiids depend on terrestrial foods, making it an almost family-wide character (Main and Lyon 1988; McDowall et al. 1996a, b), for which many galaxiids appear to be specially adapted (Halstead 1994; McDowall 1997e). Mor-

mining abundance of G. truttaceus than habitat suitability.

Galaxias truttaceus in Western Australia: Morgan (2003) thought that the cold-temperature preferences of G. truttaceus meant that in Western Australia G. truttaceus achieved negligible growth in warmer months, and that this would seem to make the species highly vulnerable there. He reported that the population in the Goodga River had a distinctive, migratory life history, with larval and juvenile rearing taking place in Moates Lake, downstream from adult habitat,

gan (2003) also mentioned unpublished results of molecular studies, which show the Goodga River population in Western Australia to exhibit ‘‘substantial genetic differences’’ from those in eastern Australia, these being ‘‘greater than those between populations of other Australian galaxiids’’ (the explicit meaning of this is unclear, but we are perhaps looking at another instance of greater species’ diversity than is recognised in the present taxonomy—McDowall and Frankenberg 1981). There is concern in Western Australia (Anon

- 2002) that ‘‘stocking of trout into waterways inhabited by this species would threaten the small populations’’ of G. truttaceus. The impacts may depend substantially on whether trout populations become self-sustaining, and/or on stocking rates.

Morgan (2003) reported infestations of Wes-

- tern Australian G. truttaceus by the cestode parasite Ligula intestinalis. He concluded that infestation takes place through consumption of copepods during the pelagic larval life of G. truttaceus, and noted that the parasite occurred only in galaxiids <80 mm long. Infested ﬁsh exhibited ‘‘gross disﬁgurement’’, and Morgan

(2003) interpreted the lack of large galaxiids as indicating that the ﬁsh failed to grow larger because disablement of galaxiids by the parasite rendered the ﬁsh more vulnerable to predation by aquatic birds (which host the deﬁnitive tapeworm). Morgan (2003) explored possible routes of invasion by L. intestinalis, and speculated that it may have arrived in Western Australia with ‘‘brown or rainbow trout releases in the 1950s and 1960s’’. He cited no instances that these salmonids carry the parasite, and for this to happen there must have been ﬁsh imported rather than the more likely ova. Otherwise, as may seem more likely, Ligula could have arrived with an infected avian host dispersing from elsewhere. The tapeworm is known in galaxiids in eastern Australia (Pollard 1974). Similar, seemingly ad hoc, invasions by L. intestinalis into New Zealand ﬁsh ecosystems have been reported (Weekes and Penlington 1986; McDowall 1990b).

Galaxias truttaceus as an invasive species: Somewhat surprisingly, a population of G. truttaceus has recently been found in parts of the inland

Murray–Darling River system (Littlejohn 2000); this seems likely to result from translocations from lakes in Victorian coastal drainages, possibly by anglers using the species as bait (McDowall 2000c). Whether the typically amphidromous G. truttaceus has, or will, spread more widely in this river system, as G. brevipinnis has (Waters et al. 2002a), is unknown. There might be scope for larvae to become entrained in marginal, stationary waters or in billabongs. Barrett (2004) in an account of ﬁsheries values in the Murray– Darling did not view presence of this galaxiid in the river as an asset. As with G. brevipinnis (see above), he regarded G. truttaceus as an indicator of declining values in the river, and regarding the two galaxiids, only slightly less adverse than presence of distinctly noxious species like gambusia, carp, weatherloach, and salmonids.

CONSERVATION RANKING: Galaxias truttaceus has not been listed under any conservation threat category. Threat seems, perhaps, greatest in Western Australia, since the populations may be vulnerable to elevated temperatures. Morgan (2003) recommended that ‘‘In order to protect this rare [Western Australian] population (species?) of native galaxiid it is imperative that the non-native trout species, Oncorhynchus mykiss and Salmo trutta, or the European/redﬁn perch, Perca ﬂuviatilis, or any other large predatory or competitive ﬁsh (e.g. mosquitoﬁsh, Gambusia holbrooki) are not inadvertently or deliberately liberated into the streams or lakes where G. truttaceus is present in Western Australia’’. Trout were not introduced into these waters but were self-maintaining and were maintained only by stocking. But Morgan (2003) claimed that rainbow trout had elsewhere been shown to have had serious impacts on galaxiids, including G. truttaceus, through predation. He recommended that the entire habitat available to G. truttaceus in the Goodga River/Lake system should be added to a nature reserve, and noted that ‘‘the Natural Heritage Trust of Australia has provided funding to build a ﬁsh ladder [that] will not only open up an area of potential habitat that is presently devoid of ﬁsh, but will also reduce predation below the weir’’.

Galaxias auratus in central Tasmanian lakes: Galaxias auratus (Fig. 49B) is regarded as a lacustrine, non-diadromous derivative of G. truttaceus (McDowall and Frankenberg 1981; Ovenden et al. 1993). It is among the largest Australian galaxiids, reaching about 240 mm. Knowledge of this species derives largely from work of Hardie (2003a; Hardie et al. 2004, 2005, 2006a, b). This species is largely lacustrine, and known primarily from two modiﬁed upland lakes in central Tasmania (Fig. 50, w). It is native to Lake Sorrel, and became established in Lake Crescent after emigrating via a canal that now connects the two lakes (Allen et al. 2002). The Tasmanian Inland Fisheries Service has translocated G. auratus into a series of additional lakes that lack salmonids, in an attempt to increase the protection of G. auratus (Threatened Species Unit 2005).

Spawning is in winter-spring, with several

- to many thousands of small eggs. Juveniles form shoalsinopenwater,butmoveinshoreandbecome cryptic when about 40 mm long. Adults are found mostly in rocky shore habitat. Brown trout, have occupied these two lakes for many years, providing a valued recreational ﬁshery, and Fulton

(1990) said that this galaxiid is the ‘‘preferred bait [used by] trout ﬁshermen in Lake Crescent, who catch it around the margins of the lake’’. The lakes are managed for irrigation and town water supply, as well as trout ﬁshing, and some marginal areas oflake marsharelostduetoﬂuctuatingwater levels; these may be used as nursery areas for the ﬁsh. Also some areas of rocky shore are periodically exposed.

Hardie (2003a) recorded a 10-fold difference in abundance of G. auratus between the two lakes, thinking this was likely owing to different levels of competition and predation. He found overlap of food and habitat between juvenile brown trout and G. auratus in the clearer Lake Sorrel, but he thought that poorer natural recruitment in the turbid Lake Crescent might reduce competition and inhibit trout predation. He postulated that trout were ‘‘controlling the size of the G. auratus populations’’, though he considered that the two species appear to coexist. Decline in the galaxiid populations could have been exacerbated by habitat changes associated with the presence of European carp in Lake Crescent, particularly loss

of macrophytes and increased turbidity, though there has been a major investment in reducing the carp populations. There has been mention of a small population of G. maculatus in the lake, though it is not thought to be reproducing, and so will soon disappear (Hardie 2003a; Threatened Species Unit 2005). Additional suggested effects are climatic change, and reductions in water levels in the lake. As well, high turbidity in L. Crescent has been blamed for past ﬁsh kills (Anon 1994). Hardie (2003a) recommended restrictions on trout stocking in the lakes to limit predation on G. auratus. The Tasmanian Inland Fisheries Service has undertaken habitat enhancement activities, such as exclusion of stock from the catchment and protection of native vegetation. Also, ‘‘The landowner has entered into a Conservation Management Agreement with the Tasmanian State Government to formalise the protection of this area’’ (Threatened Species Unit 2005).

Despite heavy trout predation on G. auratus (Stuart-Smith 2001), the galaxiid remains abundant in both Lake Sorrel and Crescent. However this species’ very restricted range has generated concern. Conservation issues, other than trout predation, relate to management of lake levels, and monitoring is undertaken.

CONSERVATION RANKING: The Tasmanian Inland Fisheries Commission (Jackson 2000d) listed this species as Rare under the Tasmanian Threatened Species Protection Act 1995. Crook and Pogonoski (2003) discussed outcomes of a meeting of the Threatened Fishes Committee of the Australian Society for Fish Biology, where a recommendation was received that this species should be classed as Vulnerable. The Committee recommended that it remain as Endangered owing to the narrow range of waters where it is found.

Galaxias tanycephalus in central Tasmanian lakes: The third stout-bodied Tasmanian species is G. tanycephalus (Fig. 49C), which is probably another non-diadromous derivative of G. truttaceus (Fulton 1978a; Ovenden et al. 1993). Galaxias tanycephalus is of modest size, reaching around 150 mm long but is often no more than 75 mm. It

is primarily lacustrine, though is occasionally found in the lower reaches of streams ﬂowing into the lakes, and is cryptic amongst shoreline boulders, except for the juveniles which form shoals in the surface waters of lakes. It possibly spawns over a longish period in summer, and fecundity is moderate with several hundred to several thousand small eggs. It was described from Woods and Arthurs Lakes, two small lakes in close proximity on the elevated, central Tasmanian plateau—both of them natural lakes, though both have raised levels caused by construction of dams (Fig. 50, ) (Fulton 1978a). Galaxias tanycephalus co-occurs with brown trout in both lakes, and is said to be abundant in the more turbid Woods Lake, but ‘‘extremely rare’’ and in decline in the clear Arthurs Lake (Anon

- 1989). Examination of ‘‘gut contents of brown trout from Woods Lake showed that [G. tanycephalus] formed a signiﬁcant part of its diet’’. Possibly, lower abundance in the clear waters of Woods Lake is due lower productivity and/or easier predation by a visual predator like brown trout. However, G. tanycephalus has survived in the lakes with the trout present for >100 years (Threatened Species Unit 2005). This suggests that the galaxiid has been less affected by trout than some other galaxiids. Its rarity in Arthurs Lake is nevertheless a conservation concern. There are also fears that this is another instance where European perch may ﬁnd their way into the lakes, and the addition of this additional piscivore (at least at large size) would have unpredictable, but possibly serious, effects on the galaxiid. To limit the impacts of trout populations, Sanger and Fulton (1991) suggested that there should be no action to enhance the trout population, nor modiﬁcations to increase or improve trout spawning habitat, and this view was endorsed by the Tasmanian Threatened Species Unit (2005), which proposed translocation to additional waters lacking trout.

In summarising the status of threatened Tasmanian galaxiids, Lynch (1983) noted two attempts to re-collect G. tanycephalus in May, 1979, that yielded only six specimens. He thought that ‘‘Further investigations may discover a wider distribution [though it hasn’t], but the fact that it was not discovered until 1976

and that it is rare in its known range suggests that it may be endangered’’ (some curious logic here). It was later suggested that ‘‘this species has a free swimming larval stage, which must make it prone to predation in the clear waters of Arthurs Lake’’ (Anon 1989). Wager and Jackson (1993) were concerned that ‘‘a water diversion scheme adding nutrient rich water to Woods Lake may be a potential threat’’, compared with the more oligotrophic Arthurs Lake. There thus seem to be at least two distinctions between the lakes—productivity and turbidity—that may explain the differences between G. tanycephalus populations in the two lakes, but which may be linked in various ways. It is conceivable that higher water turbidity in Woods Lake, rather than being a threat, may actually protect the galaxiid from trout predation, perhaps explaining its greater abundance there. Or, on the other hand, the bigger population in Woods Lake may be due to the lake being more productive and having richer plankton stocks (Anon. n.d.).

Measures proposed for management to ensure survival of this species included:

- 1. Preventing additional exotic species ﬁnding their way into the lakes, especially European perch;
- 2. No increase in spawning production of trout; and
- 3. Examination of genetic diversity of the populations (Threatened Species Unit 2005).

CONSERVATION RANKING: Michaelis (1986) reported that the Australian Council of Nature Conservation Ministers listed this species as Endangered, but the Tasmanian Inland Fisheries Commission declared it Vulnerable (Anon 1989), as did Wager and Jackson (1993), Crook (2001) and a later Tasmanian evaluation (Anon. n.d.; Threatened Species Unit 2005). The 2003 ranking of this species by the Threatened Species Committee of the Australian Society for Fish Biology also listed it as Vulnerable (Crook and Pogonoski 2003), and IUCN gave it the same ranking in both 1996 (Baillie and Groombridge 1996) and 2004 (the 2004 IUCN Red List of Threatened Species, IUCN 2006).

Three large, stout-bodied, amphidromous New Zealand Galaxias species

New Zealand also has three, large (some >ca. 300 mm long), stout-bodied Galaxias species (McDowall 1970: Fig. 52) that resemble the Australian stout-bodied species discussed above, though relationships between the New Zealand and Australian groups are uncertain (and irrelevant here). The three New Zealand species are amphidromous (sea migratory), and all contribute

- at varying levels to the whitebait ﬁshery, where juveniles are caught as they migrate back into fresh water from the sea during spring (McDowall 1984a; McDowall and Eldon 1980).

Galaxias argenteus, the largest of the Galaxiidae: Galaxias argenteus (Fig. 52A) is recorded to 580 mm length and 2.8 kg in weight (Clarke 1899; reports by Charles Douglas in Pascoe 1957) making it much the largest species in the family. It is widespread around New Zealand (Fig. 53A, d), but is not reputed to climb falls, and tends to be

- Fig. 52 Three large, stout-bodied galaxiids from New Zealand: (A) Giant kokopu, Galaxias argenteus (reaches >550 mm LCF); (B) Shortjaw kokopu, G. postvectis (>350 mm LCF); and: (C) Banded kokopu, G. fasciatus (>295 mm LCF)

distributed at low elevations and near the sea. Nevertheless, it has been found up to 170 km upstream. It is a long-lived species, perhaps exceeding 20 years, and is highly fecund when large, producing many thousands of small eggs. Spawning is undiscovered, though is believed to be in late autumn or winter (McDowall and Kelly 1999), and may involve egg deposition at elevated water levels in marginal riparian vegetation around streams (McDowall and Charteris 2006).

There has long been concern about this species’ conservation status for several reasons—particularly habitat loss (especially wetland drainage), ﬁsheries exploitation (McDowall 1978a, 1984a; Molloy and Davis 1992), and the effects of introduced brown trout (McDowall 1968c, 1990b; Williams and Given 1981). A further potential impact is mortality resulting from being taken as a by-catch by commercial eel ﬁshermen, even though the ﬁsh are not retained. This concern has lacked a quantitative foundation, but remains an issue.

As discussed elsewhere for New Zealand’s Neochanna species, wetland drainage has been both very widespread and intensive in New Zealand, beginning with European colonisation around 150 years ago—probably 90% of New Zealand’s wetlands have gone (Park 1995; Cromarty and Scott 1996). Thousands of hectares of wetland have been drained, sometimes in areas where there is now no hint of wetland, and irrigation is needed to permit pastoral agriculture (McDowall 1998b). As a result G. argenteus has virtually disappeared from some extensive areas where it was formerly well known, such as the eastern South Island (Fig. 53A—arrow 2) (Anderson 1916; Studholme 1940). Haast (1873), for instance, wrote of this species being taken from extensive wetlands once associated with Lake Ellesmere (Fig. 53A—arrow 1), a coastal, semi-tidal lake in the eastern South Island, but the species has not been seen there in decades. Behaviours and habitats associated with spawning in G. argenteus remain undiscovered, making for difﬁculties in implementing protocols for conserving this species.

Bonnett and Sykes (2002) studied habitat preferences of G. argenteus in streams, ﬁnding that it depends on the availability of riparian and/

- Fig. 53 Distributions of the large, New Zealand stout bodied species: (A) Giant kokopu, Galaxias argenteus: arrow 1—Lake Ellesmere; arrow 2—sparse presence in eastern South Island; arrow 3—widespread on west coast of South Island; (B) Shortjaw kokopu, G. postvectis; (C)

Banded kokopu, G. fasciatus—arrow 1—the Auckland City area; note the general paucity of sites along eastern coastlines, a likely result of habitat perturbation, wetland drainage and deforestation in particular) (M signiﬁes lake populations)

or instream cover, their conclusion being that ‘‘presence of some form of cover was more important than its composition’’. Cover types used included instream woody debris, overhanging tree buttresses and root wads along stream margins, or coarse boulder/cobble stream substrates. Part of this cover dependency was clearly to provide daytime refuges (the ﬁsh tends to be nocturnal, though it does forage by day during summer, if undisturbed—David and Closs 2002). In addition, however, it has a predilection for foods of terrestrial origin, often insects and spiders, taken from the water’s surface. Haast (1873), for instance wrote of how this ﬁsh is ‘‘easily caught with the hook baited with the grasshopper during the summer, and at any time of day’’; and Clarke (1899) wrote of giant kokopu

inhabiting ‘‘deeper streams, lying close to the surface at times, sometimes sheltering under a patch of foam at the foot of a miniature cascade or fall, and rising to the surface in the evening and taking the ﬂies or moths’’. More recent data conﬁrm exploitation of surface foods (Jellyman 1979; Main and Lyon 1988; West 1989; Bonnett and Lambert 2002), and its preference for such foods may inﬂuence its dependency on cover.

The whitebait juveniles of G. argenteus form a minor component of the whitebait ﬁshery (McDowall 1984a, 1990b), but typically do not appear in catches until around the second week in November (McDowall and Eldon 1980). As the statutory West Coast whitebait ﬁshing season ends on 15 November, exploitation of the species takes place usually over a brief period.

Thus, given the late migration of G. argenteus it is conceivable that most of its migration takes place after ﬁshing has ceased. Nevertheless, the ﬁshery’s manager, the Department of Conservation, proposed earlier termination of the season (31 October), to offer G. argenteus additional protection from exploitation. Despite the proposed change only, on average, reducing the seasonal whitebait catch by around 10% (McDowall 1994b), whitebait ﬁshermen objected strongly, leading to a review of the decision by the Regulations Review Committee of the New Zealand Parliament. The committee instructed the Department of Conservation to rescind the decision to curtail the ﬁshing season (Northey 1994). G. argenteus, despite at times being listed

- as threatened in New Zealand, continues to be harvested in the whitebait ﬁshery (making for an interesting conﬂict of interests for its conservation management).

Galaxias argenteus is known to establish lakelimited populations quite freely and, especially in the West Coast and Southland, there are populations that have abandoned migrations to sea (Fig. 53A, D); they are not always in landlocked lakes, and it is possible that their failure to migrate is due to low rates of water turnover in lakes which impedes entrainment in outﬂows and transport to sea of newly hatched larvae. A landlocked population in Horseshoe Lagoon, in the central eastern South Island, is the only known population along the east coast until around Otago Peninsula in the south. Presence of these lake populations may contribute to biodiversity preservation, though with the caveat that such populations do not reﬂect the species’ full behavioural, and probably genetic, diversity.

As if habitat loss and ﬁsheries exploitation were not enough, G. argenteus has long been thought subject to exclusion by introduced brown trout (McDowall 1968c, 1978a, 1990b). Taylor and Main (1987; Main et al. 1985) undertook ﬁsh surveys in a substantial area of the West Coast of New Zealand’s South Island, one of the least developed areas of New Zealand, where G. argenteus remains relatively widespread (Fig. 53A—arrow 3). But they reported its capture only rarely from locations where brown

trout occurred. Bonnett and Sykes (2002) addressed this question from data on the two species’ distributions and co-occurrence (New Zealand Freshwater Fish Database—McDowall and Richardson 1983), and concluded that G. argenteus and brown trout distributions are ‘‘not mutually exclusive, [though] they are less frequently found together than might be expected from chance alone’’. Key features relating to presence of this species are instream and riparian cover, water depth, low ﬂow velocity, and proximity to the sea (Bonnett et al. 2002).

David et al. (2002; David 2003) examined the distribution of G. argenteus around Lakes Waihola/Waipori and nearby streams, part of the lower Taieri River system in the southeastern South Island of New Zealand. Although they found that brown trout and G. argenteus were both widespread through this system, and though both were ‘‘regularly present in the same streams, their distributions rarely overlapped at the local scale’’. They thought it unlikely that ‘‘the low abundance of trout from reaches supporting [G. argenteus] were unsuitable for trout’’, found it ‘‘conceivable that habitats occupied by [G. argenteus] were suitable for trout’’, but that trout were ‘‘being excluded from them by large’’ G. argenteus. They recognised that this idea ‘‘would contrast with the dominant paradigm that native galaxiids are competitively inferior to introduced salmonids [and that] ...exclusion of trout by large [G. argenteus] in pools remains to be tested’’. And though they found that G. argenteus was common in some streams, ‘‘The seemingly low numbers of [G. argenteus] ...coupled with the fragmented or isolated distributions... suggest that these populations are vulnerable to extirpation’’. David (2003) observed that habitat preferences were rather narrower for G. argenteus than trout, and this difference has the potential for adverse impacts if trout are forcing the galaxiid into less optimal habitats, perhaps despite the galaxiid being highly territorial. Development of riparian lands for agriculture may, perhaps, tip the balance in competition for sites in favour of trout. David (2002) summarised that decline in this species was linked to a loss of suitable habitat, and possibly introduction of trout, but

thought that additional study was needed (as usual!)

CONSERVATION RANKING: Concern about the conservation status of G. argenteus dates back many years. It was not listed in the International Union for the Conservation of Nature Red Data Book (Fishes) (Miller 1977), but was regarded as Indeterminate in the New Zealand Red Data Book (Williams and Given 1981). New Zealand Department of Conservation rankings have been Category B (second priority) (Molloy and Davis 1992; Tisdall 1994), and then Gradual decline (Hitchmough 2002), meaning that it is regarded as chronically rather than critically threatened. Baillie and Groombridge (1996) listed it as Indeterminate., but in the 2004 IUCN Red List of Threatened Species it was ranked as Vulnerable (IUCN 2006).

Galaxias postvectis a species with declining threat status: Another of this species group, G. postvectis (Fig. 52B), grows to ca. 350 mm (though rarely). This species is an aggressive inland migrator, and can climb high falls, though probably as the juvenile, and is known 200 km upstream from the sea. No landlocked lake populations are known, so that it seems to be obligatorily amphidromous. It is almost always found in forested streams and is highly secretive, though is occasionally seen out foraging in the open by day. When disturbed, it retreats rapidly into deep instream cover, making its extraction using electric ﬁshing difﬁcult. It has also generated considerable conservation concern (McDowall 1978a; Williams and Given 1981). Issues for its conservation are somewhat similar to those generated by G. argenteus.

Galaxias postvectis spawns in small streams where it is customarily found, and does so in late autumn or early winter, at times when stream ﬂows are elevated, covering the rocky/gravel stream margins. The eggs settle amongst the marginal substrates and forest litter and remain there when ﬂows subside. They develop in the humid atmosphere, and hatch during another period of elevated stream ﬂows, when the stream margins are again inundated; the larvae are then carried downstream to sea (Charteris et al. 2003;

McDowall and Charteris 2006). This may be a key element in this species’ well-recognised restriction to streams with riparian forest (McDowall 1990b; McDowall et al. 1996a, b; Goodman 2002; Bowie 2002).

Galaxias postvectis is rare in eastern areas of New Zealand, from East Cape of the North Island to the Catlins area in the southeast (Fig. 53B, d), but it may never have been there in high abundance owing to a lack of suitable habitats. Extensive areas of soft rock geology, at least along the east coast of the North Island, may be inﬂuential.

Studies of habitats occupied by G. postvectis showed a high preference for streams in which riparian forest is typically podocarp/broadleaf hardwood (Wardle 1991) rather than Nothofagus forest (McDowall et al. 1996b; McDowall 1997c).
