It is of interest that McCaskill (1954) reckoned that ‘‘Few environments are more niggardly of food than pure beech [Nothofagus] associations’’, and though McCaskill was probably not thinking of aquatic fauna in saying this, aquatic biota has strong linkages to terrestrial biotic inputs, especially as insect foods for shortjaw kokopu (McDowall et al. 1996a, b). This distinction has been challenged by ﬁndings of Bowie (2002) and Goodman (2002), both of whom found G. postvectis to be present quite commonly in streams ﬂowing through a mixed Nothofagus fusca/podocarp-broadleaf forest community, though not in pure Nothofagus. The critical issues may relate to the species of Nothofagus present, or perhaps the proportion of Nothofagus present is important; or the presence of trees other than Nothofagus species in the forest communities may be decisive in determining suitability for G. postvectis. These questions aside, presence of riparian forest along streams seems critical to its presence (McDowall et al. 1996a, b; McDowall 1997c; Bowie 2002).

Micro-habitats of G. postvectis seem to be idiosyncratic—adults are often found in deep cover beneath large boulders or instream wood debris, where either of these creates a small pool below a rifﬂe—almost as if the eddy formed in this pool is a place were terrestrial foods become entrained. G. postvectis preys extensively on terrestrial invertebrates (McDowall et al.

- 1996b). The availability and abundance of appropriate terrestrial foods, may be mediated by the presence of suitable forest types, and this may inﬂuence presence of G. postvectis. It also appears to be highly cryptic, and issues of cover are probably involved. Whatever the functional dependency of G. postvectis on riparian forest (of whatever ﬂoristic composition), the need for forest is clear, and it can safely be concluded that deforestation has contributed substantially to its threatened status.

Main (1988; Main et al. 1985) found no habitat overlap between G. postvectis and brown trout in a broad-scale survey of South Island West Coast streams. Similarly, a wider analysis of sites where G. postvectis is known, which compared such sites for the presence or absence of brown trout, revealed no clear evidence that trout and G. postvectis have mutually exclusive distributions (McDowall et al. 1996a). Possibly ﬁner (reach or pool) scale analysis may have revealed that local differences in distribution are present, Allibone and McIntosh (1999) thought G. postvectis was amongst the most vulnerable of all galaxiids to competitive interactions with trout, and observed that it is widespread in forested streams draining from Mount Taranaki that rarely contained brown trout, but not, or rarely, in nearby stream reaches where trout were present. An impact of brown trout is uncertain and the question needs further study.

Galaxias postvectis is also involved in the whitebait ﬁshery, but it has proved impossible to distinguish whitebait juveniles of G. postvectis from those of G. brevipinnis (McDowall and Eldon 1980). The certainty that the former does have a whitebait juvenile comes from captive rearing of post-migratory juveniles taken from the ﬁshery, through until adult characters and colour patterns are sufﬁciently developed to enable reliable identiﬁcation (McDowall 1966). Spatial and temporal variables relating to migrations of G. postvectis whitebait are thus unknown, and the species’ idiosyncrasies cannot speciﬁcally be accommodated in management of the ﬁshery and/or in mitigating the effects of exploitation on the populations.

Research in the last decade has seen the level of conservation concern for G. postvectis reduced.

Recent events and discoveries have suggested that this species is both more widespread, and locally abundant, than had hitherto been thought—alternative collecting techniques, such as spot-lighting in streams at night and use of stationary traps, are revealing greater local abundance than more traditional techniques used in New Zealand, such as electric ﬁshing (Bowie 2002; Charteris 2002; Goodman 2002).

CONSERVATION RANKING: This species was once regarded as one of New Zealand’s most seriously threatened species. The IUCN Red Data Book (Fishes) listed it as Indeterminate (Miller 1977), as did the New Zealand Red Data Book (Williams and Given 1981), in both instances owing to inadequate knowledge of the species. Rankings by the New Zealand Department of Conservation have varied from Category B (second priority) (Molloy and Davis 1992), Category A (top priority) (Tisdall 1994), and Gradual Decline (Hitchmough 2002). It was classed as Vulnerable in the IUCN lists in both 1996 (Baillie and Groombridge 1996) and 2004 (in the 2004 IUCN Red List of Threatened Species, IUCN 2006). These changes are an instance of increasingly ‘informed ignorance’—where application of the precautionary principle is appropriate, and where increasing knowledge has permitted downgrading of conservation concern (Hitchmough 2002).

Galaxias fasciatus: widespread but mostly where there is forest: The third species in this trio is G. fasciatus (Fig. 52C), another large galaxiid, known to grow to ca. 260 mm. It remains very widespread and locally abundant, especially in northern New Zealand (Fig. 53C, d), and is common even near major centres of human population like Auckland, New Zealand’s largest city (Fig. 53C—arrow 1). This is a distinct contrast when compared with the distributions of G. argenteus and G. postvectis (Fig. 53A, B). It is a small stream species with large adults often found in streams less than a metre wide, often consisting of small pools separated by small, tumbling cascades among rocky substrates. Spawning in G. fasciatus resembles that in G. postvectis (discussed above), in that it takes

place in autumn and winter, and when the small streams where this species is found, have elevated ﬂows. The eggs are discharged over inundated stream margins and settle amongst forest litter and gravels; when elevated ﬂows recede, the eggs are left to develop in the humid atmosphere in the litter. They take several weeks to develop, and hatch when elevated ﬂows again cover the stream margins, and agitate the eggs. The larvae are swept downstream to sea. This curious spawning habit is risky, and must make these species highly vulnerable to the failure of streams to ﬂood in autumn. On the other hand, perhaps ensuring that the eggs hatch at times of elevated ﬂows may make for rapid transport to sea, minimising starvation mortalities and predation on the way (Mitchell and Penlington 1982; Charteris 2002; Charteris et al. 2003; McDowall and Charteris 2006).

Main et al. (1985), in their survey of ﬁsh populations in largely forested West Coast South Island river systems, concluded that G. fasciatus seldom co-occurred with brown trout, and that the galaxiid was ﬁnding refuge by occupying habitats unsuitable for trout. This is consistent with observations by Chadderton and Allibone (2000) that G. fasciatus occupies a rather broader range of habitat types in Stewart Island streams, where there are no trout. West et al. (2005) reafﬁrmed the association between presence of this species and presence of riparian forest, and adependencyrelatedtotheroleofterrestrialfoods in the species’ diet (see also Main and Lyon 1988).

Galaxias fasciatus is heavily exploited in the whitebait ﬁshery (Fig. 54), especially in forested streams draining the lowlands and foothills, but there is no clear evidence that its abundance has been seriously compromised. Galaxias fasciatus is another highly skilled climber that penetrates well inland (known to nearly 180 km upstream), and migrates (climbs) upstream past very substantial vertical falls (tens of metres high). This ability may protect it from some of the inﬂuences of trout invasion as it allows the ﬁsh to move upstream into localities inaccessible to trout (though, as is true of all ‘whitebait’ species, the juveniles do have to run the gauntlet of predatory trout in the downstream reaches). Galaxias fasciatus is known to occasionally establish lakelimited populations.

Fig. 54 Sea migratory juvenile of banded kokopu, Galaxias fasciatus (ca. 45 mm LCF)

Though the species does not rank as severely threatened it, too, is dependent on riparian vegetation. Typically it is found in small forested streams, but presence of scrub, low bushes, or even rank, undeveloped pastoral vegetation overhanging streams sometimes seems sufﬁcient to allow inhabitation by G. fasciatus. Eikaas et al. (2005b), for instance, found abundance much greater in streams well inside forest cover, and ﬁsh found outside forest cover were smaller. The reasons for cover dependency in G. fasciatus are not clariﬁed, but likely relate partly to the species’ use of cover, especially by day. Perhaps, also, its exploitation of surface terrestrial foods is a key element in its choice of forested streams, and this may be controlling distribution (Phillipps 1926; Hopkins et al. 1966; Main and Lyon 1988; West 1989). There is no debate that the breadth of distribution of G. fasciatus has been restricted by deforestation and conversion of land to pasture. Main (1988; Main et al. 1985) found no overlap in the distributions of G. fasciatus and trout. It is uncertain whether this lack of overlap results from choice of different habitats, often in deeply tannin-stained forested streams and wetlands, where brown trout tend to be much less common (McDowall 1990b), or mutual exclusion. Minns (1990) found that in general, G. fasciatus does not share habitats with trout even though both species may share streams at a broader scale. There are many populations in lakes (Fig. 53B, D), but how many are lake-limited is unknown.

CONSERVATION RANKING: Galaxias fasciatus was somewhat surprisingly listed as Category C by the New Zealand Department of Conservation (Molloy and Davis 1992; Tisdall 1994), but was not listed by Hitchmough (2002), has not been listed elsewhere (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN

- 2006), remains very widespread (Fig. 53C), and is often abundant.

Among these three large, stout-bodied galaxiids, predation risk is related, in part, to the distance that their whitebait juveniles migrate upstream, the extent to which they are exposed to predatory trout, and the time it takes them to reach a size-refuge from trout predation.

A miscellany of Australian non-migratory galaxiids

In this section a series of southern Australian/ Tasmanian species is dealt with; species that are in general either poorly known because there has been little work on their natural history, and/or because little is written about even their conservation status (Fig. 55).

The Clarence galaxias, Galaxias johnstoni: Galaxias johnstoni (Fig. 55A) is one of a complex of non-diadromous Galaxias species that are widespread across eastern Australia and Tasmania (others are G. olidus, G. pedderensis, G. fontanus), at least some of which are nondiadromous derivatives of G. brevipinnis in Australia. These ﬁsh tend to be found in coarse

- Fig. 55 Tasmanian slender non-diadromous galaxiids: (A) Clarence galaxias, Galaxias johnstoni (reaches 140 mm LCF); (B) Swan galaxias, G. fontanus (to 135 mm LCF);

- (C) Swamp galaxias, G. parvus (to 100 mm LCF)

substrate, rifﬂy waters, largely cryptic in behaviour, and are typically spring spawners with smallish numbers of eggs. Galaxias johnstoni is known from a few waterways and lagoons in the upper, higher elevation reaches of the Derwent River, including the Clarence, Nive and Little River systems in central Tasmania (Fig. 45, m). It is non-migratory, and seems to spend its entire life in streams, lakes and wetlands, the young being mid-water shoaling ﬁshes, and the adults cryptic, living within the substrate, at least by day.

A Tasmanian review of this species’ status, by Lynch (1983), included that ‘‘Until 1976 there were only a few specimens of [G. johnstoni] ... despite the fact that it was ﬁrst described in 1936...at least two recent efforts have failed to collect it... [and] ...because of its reduced distribution and the fact that so little is known of it, some doubts can be expressed as to its ability to survive’’. Precisely what this was intended to convey is uncertain. In some ways this prognosis has proved too pessimistic, as the species is now known in a number of other waterways and lagoons in the same catchment. On the other hand, other factors have entered the ‘conservation equation’ of G. johnstoni.

A recent Tasmanian evaluation summarised that G. johnstoni ‘‘is no longer found in the lower Clarence catchment due to invasion of brown trout ... which are thought to out compete and/or prey upon Clarence galaxias’’ (Anon. n.d.). As a consequence, the Tasmanian Threatened Species Unit (2005) expressed concerns about the inﬂuence of brown trout, and added that G. johnstoni ‘‘does not [co]occur [naturally] with any other native [Tasmanian] ﬁsh species, although G. truttaceus and G. brevipinnis are widespread in the area’’. An interesting observation was that G. johnstoni is found only in streams and lagoons outside the areas of central Tasmania that were affected by ice during the last glacial advance (ca. 18–20,000 years ago—Davies 1974), presumably having evolved there. In contrast, G. brevipinnis and G. truttaceus, are said to be present in areas that were glaciated, and the implication of this contrast may be that G. brevipinnis and G. truttaceus are found in waterways that the retreat of the ice sheets have made invasible. If that is so, then another implication of the com-

plementary distributions of these two species, relative to G. johnstoni, could be that habitats of the latter were hitherto inaccessible to the two other species and/or that G. johnstoni cannot cope with the presence of these other galaxiids. It is not known whether the complementary distributions of the several galaxiids are (Jackson 2000a):

- 1. A habitat preference issue;
- 2. A competitive displacement issue (or both); or
- 3. Result from the ability of various galaxiids to spread across central Tasmania after retreat of ice following the last glacial advance 18– 20,000 years ago, or from chance.

Perhaps there are elements of all three involved. Wager and Jackson (1993) nominated no other cause of decline. Crook and Sanger (1998) reported, rather more explicitly, that G. johnstoni is ‘‘absent in areas...that have been colonised by brown trout... [and is] ... no longer present in the lower part of the catchment... [where its] distribution has been fragmented’’. They did ﬁnd, however, that it remained ‘‘quite common within its range’’, including one population of G. johnstoni that coexists with brook charr. They described the main threat as predation by brown trout, and Jackson (2000a; Jackson and Crook

- 2002) concurred, with Tasmania’s Threatened Species Unit (2005) reiterating concerns about brown trout, and adding that rainbow trout are also a threat. Moreover there are, yet again in Tasmania, concerns about the potential for European perch to invade habitats of G. johnstoni.

It has been observed that as trout penetrated relevant waterways, G. johnstoni has disappeared, Sanger and Fulton (1991) noting that ‘‘the most striking feature of the present distribution of Galaxias johnstoni is that the larger populations are in remote locations [that] don’t contain brown trout’’. Its ‘‘primary environmental requirement ... is isolation from brown trout ... the clear delineation [between the galaxiid and brown trout] suggests that declines could occur should the galaxiid be exposed to brown trout predation’’. In some places trout invasion into G. johnstoni habitat is prevented only by a series of steeply cascading sections of stream, causing concern that such barriers may not always exclude trout. It is

notable that brook charr do not seem to be as harmful to the galaxiid as brown trout are (Sanger and Fulton 1991; Crook and Sanger 1998; Threatened Species Unit 2005), and the Tasmanian Inland Fisheries Service has adopted a policy of allowing the presence of brook charr, thinking these preferable to having to deal with pressures from anglers to have brown or rainbow trout introduced. Whatever limits the range of G. johnstoni, its conservation status has generated considerable concern. As is related for galaxioid ﬁshes throughout this paper, the downstream limits of G. johnstoni often coincide with presence of trout (or habitats are unsuitable).

Jackson (2002a) reported an illegal release of rainbow trout into the habitat of G. johnstoni, prompting Crook and Pogonoski (2002) to lament that ‘‘Galaxias conservation work suffered a major setback...when it was discovered that domestic-strain rainbow trout had been illegally introduced to a lagoon containing the endangered Clarence galaxias. ...This species survives only in trout-free waters’’. The Threatened Species Unit (2005) of the Tasmanian Inland Fisheries Service planned to try and remove them.

A recovery plan for G. johnstoni speciﬁes a number of measures intended to ensure the species’ survival, including:

- 1. Monitoring known habitats to ensure absence of invading predators and presence of G. johnstoni;
- 2. Exclusion and/or removal of trout, including the recent illicit liberations mentioned above;
- 3. Habitat management;
- 4. Possibly some translocation should there be suitable habitats free of other ﬁsh species; and:
- 5. Captive breeding trials.

Genetic studies are needed to determine the genetic diversity present, and for the management of any translocations.

CONSERVATION RANKING: Galaxias johnstoni has disappeared from parts of its known range, including the type locality, and was declared Endangered by the Australian Council of Nature Conservation Ministers (Michaelis 1986). It is protected under

both Australian Commonwealth and Tasmanian State statutes. The Tasmanian Inland Fisheries Commission expressed concern that this species did not ‘‘appear to be able to co-exist with trout’’ and designated it as Endangered (Anon 1989; Threatened Species Unit 2005). It was classed as Critically Endangered’’ by the Threatened Fishes Committee of the Australian Society for Fish Biology (Crook 2001), and Crook and Pogonoski (2003) reported that the Committee had responded to a recommendation that the species status should be changed to Endangered by suggesting that that question should be reconsidered after clariﬁcation of the adequacy of measures designed to protect the populations from trout. IUCN listed it as Critically Endangered (Baillie and Groombridge1996), asalso didthe2004IUCN Red List of Threatened Species (IUCN 2006).

The Tasmanian Swan galaxias, Galaxias fontanus: Galaxias fontanus (Fig. 55B) is a smallish (to 135 mm long, but usually much smaller) openand midwater-living, stream-dwelling species at all life stages, with very narrow range in eastern central Tasmania, where it is restricted to several small waterways that are parts of the Swan and Macquarie Rivers (Fig. 45, w). Unlike most galaxiids, this species is not associated with lakes or wetlands, and its entire life cycle is in small headwater streams that are prone to major ﬂuctuations, ﬂows sometimes ceasing. Other ﬁsh are not naturally present in habitats apart from the eel, Anguilla australis (Anguillidae). Galaxias fontanus is described as frequenting gently ﬂowing waters around woody instream debris. It is not diadromous. It is another spring spawner with low fecundity (a few hundred largish eggs). It is said to be tolerant of elevated temperatures and low oxygen levels, and survives when ﬂows decline and streams become a series of isolated pools, with no or minimal ﬂows.

From its ﬁrst description (Fulton 1978b), this species’ narrow range has generated conservation concern, Lynch (1983) summarising that ‘‘Its limited distribution and unknown life history [were a] cause for concern’’. In recent years, exploratory surveys showed the species to be somewhat more widespread than originally thought, with Sanger and Fulton (1991) reporting

four additional populations near the original site. In 1989 it was also found in ‘‘small numbers’’ in upper reaches of several tiny tributaries of the Macquarie River (Anon 1989).

Trout were not known in the habitat of G. fontanus, and Lynch (1983) thought it vulnerable to trout predation. Thus, from the beginning there were conservation concerns, and it was soon realised that downstream limits of the distribution of G. fontanus coincided with natural barriers that delineated upstream limits of brown trout. As now understood, the species’ distribution is very fragmented, conﬁned to small headwater tributaries of several distinct river systems, with most populations now isolated by trout populations downstream in each of them. These trout populations restrict the availability of downstream refuges at times of low ﬂows in upstream habitats and prevent gene ﬂow among the populations, many of which may be small and exhibit limited genetic diversity. There was also particular concern that juvenile G. fontanus form schools in open water, making populations particularly vulnerable to predation by trout (Anon. n.d.).

Sanger and Fulton (1991) expressed concerns that barriers preventing upstream invasion by exotic species were not strongly exclusive, in some instances being a marsh or a small cascade, and they thought that occasional trout could penetrate upstream past them. Moreover, an unauthorised liberation of brown trout upstream of the falls, that had hitherto isolated the originally described population, had immediate adverse impacts on the galaxiid population, conﬁrming Lynch’s (1983) fears, and adding to the threat. Michaelis (1986) recommended creation of reserves in headwaters of the Swan River, for the protection of G. fontanus, and Fulton (1987) said that the Tasmanian Inland Fisheries Commission was then doing so, ‘‘in conjunction with the [Tasmanian] Forestry Commission’’.

Jackson (1999a) summarised the status of G. fontanus to that time, ﬁnding that:

- 1. There were only three surviving natural populations;
- 2. Three other known populations had disappeared after either trout or European perch invaded the streams, and

- 3. Translocations had resulted in the possible establishment of nine new populations.

More recently, Jackson (2000b), and the Tasmanian Threatened Species Unit (2005) reported ﬁsh

- at a site where G. fontanus was once regarded as extirpated, and discussed construction of verticaldrop weirs designed to prevent upstream movement by trout in lower reaches of the stream.

Early concerns about trout impacts have persisted, the Tasmanian Threatened Species Unit (2005) describing predation and competition from introduced ﬁshes (primarily trout and European perch) as the major threat. It summarised that G. fontanus is ‘‘unable to coexist with these species, as shown by its occurrence only in trout-free streams, all [known] naturally occurring populations being limited by the downstream presence of trout’’. Wager and Jackson (1993) nominated damaging effects of forestry as an additional threat. Fulton (1978b) thought that all entirely freshwater galaxiids, in general, could not coexist with trout, and Sanger

- (1996) concurred, considering G. fontanus ‘‘very vulnerable to predation by both brown trout and perch’’, adding that one small natural populations had been completely eliminated by invasive perch.

A recovery plan has been compiled for this species (Sanger and Fulton 1991), and this has recently been updated for a further decade (Threatened Species Unit 2005). Measures introduced by the Tasmanian Inland Fisheries Service to ensure conservation of this species included:

- 1. Monitoring and survey;
- 2. Translocations to secure habitats;
- 3. Construction of barriers to prevent upstream invasion by trout;
- 4. Removal of trout from above barriers, natural or human constructed;
- 5. Establishment of captive populations in managed refuges, and
- 6. Public awareness of the species’ imperilled status and biodiversity value.

Breeding trials were undertaken with limited success, resulting in only four juveniles (Jackson and Harvey 2000), and the Tasmanian Threatened Species Unit (2005) emphasised the critical

conservation value of residual natural populations.

Translocated populations have resulted in the species being present in ca. 30 km of stream in addition to the 11 km of natural populations, but all populated reaches have trout lower downstream. Nothing is known of genetic diversity of any populations and there are concerns for bottleneck effects caused by reduction of small, isolated population; genetic studies are needed (Threatened Species Unit 2005).

CONSERVATION RANKING: This species is provided with statutory protection under all manner of Tasmanian State and Australian Commonwealth statutes. The Australian Council of Nature Conservation Ministers listed G. fontanus as Endangered (Michaelis 1986), as did the Tasmanian Inland Fisheries Commission (Anon 1989; Threatened Species Unit 2005). Amongst concerns are those deriving from wood harvesting and associated perturbations, and some populations of G. fontanus that are found within forests that have been designated as special management zones, so that fauna protection takes priority over wood production (Threatened Species Unit 2005). Crook and Pogonoski (2003) reported a recommendation to the Australian Society for Fish Biology Threatened Species Committee that this species’ threat ranking should be eased to Endangered, but the Committee decided that it should remain as Critically Endangered, subject to provision of addition information on the stability of translocated populations. Baillie and Groombridge (1996) gave and IUCN listing as Critically Endangered, as did the 2004 IUCN Red List of Threatened Species (IUCN 2006).

Tasmania’s swamp galaxias, Galaxias parvus: Galaxias parvus was the second species described from Lake Pedder in Tasmania by Frankenberg (1968). It is a small, rather chubby ﬁsh that grows to about 105 mm (Fig. 55C). Its life history is virtually unreported, apart from the fact that it clearly must spend its entire life in fresh water, and it appears not to have a lacustrine life stage. Fulton (1990) thought that it breeds in the spring (as usual for non-migratory Galaxias species).

Fecundity is low with usually only a few hundred eggs spawned (Threatened Species Unit 2005). The habitat preferences of this species seem less explicitly described, with the roles of the lake, its adﬂuvial streams, and nearby wetlands, at best uncertain.

In contrast with the fate of G. pedderensis when the original Lake Pedder was inundated by construction of a large dam and the new, enlarged lake populated by brown trout and G. brevipinnis (discussed above), the implications were evidently rather less harmful for G. parvus—at least initially. Fulton (1990) suggested that numbers of G. parvus increased initially, but it seems that this was less obvious than that described for G. pedderensis. Allen et al. (2002) have also suggested that it increased in abundance after formation of the new Lake Pedder in 1977, as did Lake (2001), but they were mostly gone by 1978. Nor did G. parvus undergo such massive subsequent decline as that described (above) for G. pedderensis. Fulton (1990) noted that it is not often found in the main lake, preferring streams and swampy lake margins, and as a result it is unlikely to have suffered the heavy predation that was so adverse for G. pedderensis. And, as with G. pedderensis, Fulton (1987) argued that inclusion of Lake Pedder in a Tasmanian World Heritage Area offered protection for G. parvus, but this beneﬁt depends entirely on what is allowed to happen in such areas and, speciﬁcally, is only available if the galaxiid is not subject to predation by trout and G. brevipinnis. Otherwise, it is difﬁcult to see what protection a World Heritage Area designation affords G. parvus. Lake (2001) blamed competition and predation by trout for a decline, as well as by G. brevipinnis that proliferated in the lake.

Wager and Jackson (1993) thought that G. parvus ‘‘may not have declined seriously’’, though Sanger (1988) expressed particular concerns that European perch might invade wetlands where G. parvus is found. It has no pelagic lacustrine life stage, and this may reduce the threat from lacustrine predators (compared, for instance, with G. pedderensis). Possible threats include modiﬁcation of the original Lake Pedder and associated wetlands by ﬂooding of the lake, and competition and predation by brown trout and G. brevipinnis. Issues for ensuring survival

and/or encouraging recovery of the populations of G. parvus have included:

- 1. Obtaining a better understanding of its distribution and ecology;
- 2. Ensuring security from additional invasive ﬁsh predators;
- 3. Translocation into additional waters; and
- 4. Trials at captive breeding.

Six sites have been described as secure for this species (Threatened Species Unit 2005).

CONSERVATION RANKING: The Tasmanian perspective (Anon. n.d.), is that this species is Rare; it is considered to have ‘‘declined in recent years and no longer occurs in the [Lake Pedder] impoundment itself, but is found [only] in marginal wetlands associated with the lake’’. Jackson (2000d, Threatened Species Unit 2005) listed this species as Rare under the Tasmanian Threatened Species Protection Act, 1995. Crook (2001) listed G. parvus species as Lower Risk—Near Threatened’’, but as a result of its listing as Data Deﬁcient by Wager and Jackson (1993) it was delisted under the Australian Endangered Species Protection Act. However, Crook and Pogonoski (2003) listed it as Endangered, and the Tasmanian Threatened Species Unit (2005) endorsed this ranking. The 1996 IUCN listing is nevertheless, Data Deﬁcient (Baillie and Groombridge 1996), and this remained unchanged in the and in the 2004 IUCN Red List of Threatened Species (IUCN 2006).

Flathead galaxias, Galaxias rostratus, in the Murray–Darling River, eastern Australia: The ﬂathead galaxias, G. rostratus, is a midwater swimming species (Fig. 56B) that closely resembles G. maculatus, and is of small size (to about 120 mm). But it is non-diadromous and has a life cycle that is conﬁned largely to gently ﬂowing waters, billabongs, and wetlands of the huge Murray–Darling River system of inland easterncentral Australia (Fig. 57, m). A population of galaxiids in Lake Hiawatha, in drainages of northern New South Wales may be a coastal isolate of this species, but this ﬁsh needs careful study. Galaxias rostratus is not well known,

- Fig. 56 Two slender, mid-water swimming Australian galaxiids: (A) Western minnow, Galaxias occidentalis (reaches >165 mm LCF); (B) Murray jollytail, G. rostratus (>120 mm LCF)

- Fig. 57 Distribution in southeastern Australia and Tasmania of common jollytail, Galaxias maculatus—d; Murray jollytail, G. rostratus—m (note coastal distribution of the diadromous G. maculatus, but inland distribution of G. rostratus

probably, partly because it attracts less interest than it deserves on account of its rather ‘ordinary’ appearance, and also because of its somewhat sporadic presence in larger waterways. Very few concerted studies have addressed either this species’ natural history or its conservation. It appears to occur intermittently and sporadically across a very broad area. It is possibly most prevalent in waters that are not often or easily sampled, as a result of which little is known about

it (Cadwallader and Backhouse 1983). Llewellyn (1983) recorded it as ‘‘occasional’’ in the Murray– Darling and found in turbid dams, billabongs and slow-ﬂowing reaches of rivers.

Lloyd and Walker (1986) presented a summary of the status of the smaller ﬁsh species of the Murray River, and said that they had found too few G. rostratus to permit explicit description of its distribution and habitat preferences. They thought it had never been common, based on museum records, though low interest and sampling difﬁculties may have led to this assessment, rather than the species’ actual abundance. They found Gambusia holbrooki to be present at most sampling sites, the inference being that this may contribute to low numbers of Galaxias rostratus.

Cadwallader and Backhouse (1983) reported that G. rostratus is found in the diet of European perch, and speculated that it is probably ‘‘also a forage species for other large predatory ﬁsh in the Murray–Darling River system’’, including species native there. Fletcher (1986) observed that the diet of medium-sized European carp overlapped with that of G. rostratus, and though she did not argue for major impacts, it has to be recognised that there may have been adverse effects on the galaxiid by the vast populations of European carp, both from food competition and habitat deterioration attributed to carp (Roberts et al. 1995; Roberts and Tilzey 1997; Khan et al. 2003).

Barrett (2004), in an analysis of the status of the ﬁsh fauna of the Murray–Darling River, reported that ‘‘galaxiids ... are declining at an alarming rate’’. He did not provide any data, nor did he specify which species, but it can be surmised that G. rostratus may have been among them, as this is the only recognised Galaxias species present in the Murray–Darling River system beyond the hill streams of eastern Australia.

CONSERVATION RANKING: Crook and Pogonoski (2002) reported that the entire aquatic ecological community of the lower Murray River has been declared endangered under the New South Wales Fisheries Management Act, 1964; in so doing, some measure of protection is offered to Galaxias rostratus, though it remains to be seen what this might mean for that species’ conservation.

Wager and Jackson (1993) did not regard G. rostratus as under any threat, whereas Morris et al. (2000) described it as ‘‘now restricted and intermittent’’ in the Murray River region; they classed it as Vulnerable in New South Wales. Allen et al. (2002, p. 106) described it as ‘‘not particularly common and more likely to be found

- at low elevations’’ but I rather doubt that its habits and habitats are well enough known to make a deﬁnitive judgement (this seems to me like ‘uninformed ignorance’).

Crook and Pogonoski (2003), in the Threatened Species Committee of the Australian Society for Fish Biology’s threatened species list, ranked G. rostratus as Vulnerable, though its status, and the reasons for any decline, seem poorly explored. IUCN also listed this species as Vulnerable in both 1996 (Baillie and Groombridge 1996), and 2004 (the latest IUCN Red List of Threatened Species, IUCN 2006).

The western minnow, Galaxias occidentalis: This species (Fig. 56A), which is found widely in southern Western Australia, is one of the few galaxiid species in which shoaling behaviour is retained through adult life (others are G. maculatus and G. rostratus). As with these other species, it is an open-living, midwater species that is relatively slender with a forked caudal ﬁn. It reaches about135 mm inlength.Itspendsitsentire life in fresh water, spawning in winter-early spring, and is probably largely an annual species, though some individuals may survive for two or more years, occasionally for ﬁve (Pen and Potter 1991a,

- b). Surface terrestrial foods contribute substantially to its diet (Pen and Potter 1991b).

Galaxias occidentalis is widespread in southwestern Western Australia, from well north of Perth (around latitude 30 S), and south and east around the southwestern corner of Australia to a little west of Albany (Fig. 58, d).

Both brown and rainbow trout are present in a few southwestern Western Australian fresh waters, especially the latter, but these populations depend largely on hatchery releases (Morgan

- et al. 1998), and as a result they probably do not compare in ﬁsh size, population density, or invasiveness with naturally reproducing populations of trout elsewhere in Australia, or in most

Fig. 58 Distributions of western minnow, Galaxias occidentalis (d) and jollytail, G. maculatus (m) in Western Australia

other parts of the range of the galaxioid ﬁshes. Morgan et al. (1998) noted that the trouts, perch and gambusia ‘‘have been shown to inﬂuence, through interference competition, and/or predation, the distribution of freshwater ﬁsh in Australia and other parts of the world’’. They reported observations of gambusia attacking G. occidentalis which were observed to have a ‘‘higher degree of ﬁn damage’’ in habitats where the two species co-occurred. European perch also sometimes shared habitats with Galaxias occidentalis (Morgan et al. 1998).

CONSERVATION RANKING: This species remains abundant across its range according to Morgan et al. (1998), and Allen et al. (2002) classed it as the ‘‘most common and widespread [freshwater ﬁsh] in south-western Australia’’. However, alternative views exist, as another report (Anon 2002) expressed concern that G. occidentalis may be at ‘‘high risk of losing localised populations to trout’’ which ‘‘may have implications for the conservation of the genetic diversity of this species’’. I suspect that, once more, not enough is known to make deﬁnitive judgements. No formal conservation ranking has been assigned

to this species, one of few in the family about which this can be said. Genetic studies (Watts et al. 1995) demonstrate more than one molecular lineage in the populations, so that more than one species may be represented in what is presently known as G. occidentalis.

The ‘‘pencil-galaxias’’ species—small, slender sub-alpine species in New Zealand

New Zealand has a series of apparently closely related small, very slender, pencil-shaped species (Fig. 59) that are widely distributed. These species tend to be found mostly in small cobbly to shingly tributary streams and rocky river margins often well back into foothills of New Zealand’s mountain ranges (Fig. 60) (McDowall 1970, 1990b; McDowall and Waters 2002, 2003). Most of them are found in streams that have never had riparian forest, at least since the mid-1800s colonisation of New Zealand.

- Fig. 59 New Zealand Galaxias species of the ‘pencil galaxias’ species group: (A) Alpine galaxias, G. paucispondylus (reaches >105 mm); (B) Bignose galaxias, G. macronasus (>75 mm); (C) Dwarf galaxias, G. divergens (>85 mm); (D) Upland longjaw galaxias, G. prognathus (>85 mm); (E) Lowland longjaw galaxias, G. cobitinis (>90 mm)

Galaxias paucispondylus—widespread along the Southern Alps: Galaxias paucispondylus (Fig. 59A) is present in headwaters tributaries of many rivers of the eastern ﬂanks of the Southern Alps (Elkington and Charteris 2005) (Fig. 60B, ·—arrow 10), largely within the intermontane valleys, rarely extending far to the east, out on to the Canterbury Plains. It is the largest of this species group, despite reaching only 112 mm (McDowall 1990b). This species inhabits coarse cobble/gravel swift-ﬂowing rifﬂes, and perhaps most often in smaller side-braids rather than main ﬂows of the larger braided rivers (Fig. 61). It is a spring spawner, though spawning behaviour and site are undiscovered. The eggs are relatively large for so small a ﬁsh (ca. 2 mm diameter), and number only a few hundred. The larvae and juveniles are probably among those that are found in slack margins of pools and runs during spring and early summer, and can be expected to disappear into the gravels when 30–40 mm length. Little is known, and they are elusive ﬁsh that are not easy to study (Bonnett 1990, 1992).

There is no suggestion that G. paucispondylus has declined noticeably in range or abundance owing to habitat deterioration, as most habitats are in hill country where environmental effects are minimal. Though there have been no suggestions of effects from invasion of streams by trout, this may be a case of ‘uninformed ignorance’—we may not yet know enough to enable reliable judgements. Adoption of that view is supported by observations of McIntosh (2000a) that, though G. paucispondylus occurs across a broad range of habitats in upland streams, it is absent from habitats that support trout >150 mm long, an absence that he attributed to trout predation. The open-living, pool run-margin dwelling juveniles of G. paucispondylus may be especially vulnerable to predation by young-of-the-year ﬁngerling brown trout during spring and summer. At present insufﬁcient is known to determine whether there is predation and/or whether there are signiﬁcant impacts on this species’ recruitment processes. However, Dunn (2002) suggested that G. paucispondylus can survive ﬂow reductions by retreating into the substrate, something that brown trout cannot do; he suggested, there-

- Fig. 60 Distribution of the New Zealand pencil galaxias species complex: (A) dwarf galaxias, Galaxias divergens m; upland longjaw galaxias, G. prognathus d; lowland longjaw galaxias, G. cobitinis—+; arrow 1—northern isolates of G. divergens; arrow 2—gap in range relating to volcanic ash discharges; arrow 3—West Coast South Island populations; arrow 4—G. prognathus in the Hurunui River; arrow 5—absence of G. prognathus from the Waimakariri River catchment; arrow 6—presence of members of this species group upstream of a series of

glacial lakes; arrow 7—G. cobitinis in Hakataramea River; arrow 8—low elevation populations of lowland longjaw galaxias in Kauru and Kakanui River; arrow 9—McKenzie Basin of upper Waitaki River system. (B) Alpine galaxias, G. paucispondylus—·; bignose galaxias, G. macronasus—d; arrow 10—G. paucispondylus widespread across eastern South Island; arrow 11—G. macronasus widely across Mackenzie Basi; arrow 12—G. paucispondylus present above glacial lakes

fore, removal of predatory trout by lowered ﬂows may beneﬁt galaxiid survival (a process not unlike protection of G. olidus from trout invasion, discussed above, but using different stream ﬂow and species’ attributes).

McIntosh’s (2000a) work may be a warning that distribution and abundance of this species needs to be watched. Though trout have been in New Zealand rivers for 130 years (McDowall 1990b, 1994a) they may still be spreading, and their effects on indigenous ﬁshes may still be emerging. Sagar and Eldon (1983) found substantial dietary overlap between G. paucispondylus and juvenile brown trout, thinking this strong evidence for competition (but did not demonstrate that food was a limiting resource, so that competition is uncertain).

Proposals to build water storage dams in the intermontane valleys of the eastern Southern

Alps pose risks to populations of G. paucispondylus as the marginal braids and spring-fed valleyﬂoor tributaries where it is present will be ﬂooded, excluding the galaxiid. Whether similar habitats would become available at higher elevations where streams join such lakes is unknown. Another unknown is how far upstream into the mountain valleys populations of G. paucispondylus go. This species is present upstream of some of the major glacial lakes of the eastern Southern Alps (Fig. 60B—arrow 12).

CONSERVATION RANKING: Galaxias paucispondylus is not listed as under threat by the New Zealand Department of Conservation, (Hitchmough 2002), being to some extent protected from human-caused effects (other than trout impacts) by its sub-montane distribution. It is not listed by

IUCN, either (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006).

Galaxias macronasus: a Mackenzie Basin endemic: Galaxias macronasus (Fig. 59B) is only recently described (McDowall and Waters 2003), and is known from a series of streams and drains often associated with wetlands quite widely across the Mackenzie Basin of New Zealand’s South Island (upper Waitaki River system—Fig. 60B, d—arrow 10) (Elkington and Charteris 2005). It grows to around 80 mm, commonly to 60 mm long. It is another non-migratory ﬁsh that appears to spawn during spring, probably in or near normal adult habitats, though little is known about this ﬁsh, which has restricted range within the broader range of G. paucispondylus (which it quite closely resembles) (Fig. 59A, B)—though habitats pref-

- Fig. 61 Shallow rifﬂe habitat in submontane catchments, characteristic of alpine galaxias, Galaxias paucispondylus and upland longjaw galaxias, G. prognathus

erences are quite different (Bowie 2005). Galaxias macronasus is thus a further New Zealand galaxiid with narrow range, and the small streams it inhabits are vulnerable to habitat degradation, especially wetland drainage and/or ﬂooding by impoundments.

This species’ recent discovery has considerable irony. The farmer through whose property the type-locality stream ﬂows became frustrated with a small area of wetland where his farm vehicles became bogged, and so (without obtaining requisite statutory approvals), he drained the wetland. Having done so, he found that there was then insufﬁcient soil moisture to grow grass, and so he applied to the local water management authority for a permit to pump water from the stream that had replaced the wetland. To obtain that permit, there was a requirement that local Department of Conservation ofﬁcers should survey the stream to identify biodiversity values in the stream, and the new species was discovered during that survey. Subsequent surveys of tributaries of the Waitaki River across the Mackenzie Basin have revealed that G. macronasus is quite widespread there, though intermittent, most often in small shingly streams associated with perched wetlands (Elkington and Charteris 2005; Bowie 2005). Some habitats are in close proximity to populations of G. paucispondylus in the bigger, swifter streams and river margins, but spatial separation at a very local scale of the two species seems consistent and reﬂects their differing habitat preferences.

Elkington and Charteris (2005) reported this species from 12 sampling sites across the Mackenzie Basin, but as a result of ongoing Department of Conservation surveys nearly 40 sites are now known, at most of which salmonids are not recorded, though some sites have only occasional brown or rainbow trout, though they are typically common and widespread in nearby streams. Partly, this separation may relate to habitat preferences, as G. macronasus is most common in small, shingly wetland streams, where trout would not be expected. However, wetland drainage may have facilitated upstream access by trout which encounter no major physical barriers to their invasion of G. macronasus habitat.

So little is known about this species that informed management of its conservation poses problems. Attention needs to be given to potential impacts from invasion of habitats by G. brevipinnis living in hydro-lakes downstream in the Waitaki River (given the dominance hierarchy demonstrated by Allibone (1999) for non-migratory galaxiids in the Waipori River and tributaries of Lake Mahinerangi). Galaxias brevipinnis is recorded from at least one stream having G. macronasus (McDowall and Waters

- 2003).

CONSERVATION RANKING: Study of this species is needed, and it is appropriately ranked as Data Deﬁcient (Hitchmough 2002—listed as Galaxias

- sp. from the Waitaki River). Ongoing surveys are revealing additional populations across the Mackenzie Basin (Elkington and Charteris 2005; Bowie 2005), where it seems to be quite widespread. Recent description of this species (McDowall and Waters 2003) means that it has no IUCN ranking.

Galaxias divergens across central New Zealand: Galaxias divergens (Fig. 59C) is broadly distributed across central New Zealand—central and southern North Island and north and west of South Island (Fig. 60A, m). It grows to only about 87 mm (McDowall 1990b) and lives largely within gravel-cobble substrates in small streams and in river rifﬂe margins. Yet again, we are dealing with a non-migratory species, and a spring spawner in small mostly headwater tributary streams. Eggs are largish, and number just a few hundred; the spawning site is undiscovered.

Galaxias divergens tends to be sporadic in occurrence across its quite broad range, which might suggest historic range fragmentation. Some of this (note the isolated northern localities in Fig. 60A—arrow 1, and its absence from central North Island—arrow 2) can clearly be attributed to Quaternary to Recent volcanism. An area of New Zealand, roughly centred on Rotorua-Taupo in the central North Island, was profoundly inﬂuenced by a series of relatively recent, and sometimes huge, volcanic eruptions lasting over ca. 50,000 years, the last major eruption being

around 186 A.D. (Wilson and Walker 1985; Froggatt and Lowe 1990; Wilson and Houghton 1993). Substantial impacts on terrestrial biota have been described or postulated, and effects on freshwater biota are equally likely. These effects may have been both more widespread and persistent than those affecting terrestrial biota, owing to erosion of ash into river headwaters, and its downstream ﬂushing far beyond the original deposition zones (Cudby 1977; Spiers and Boubee´ 1997). Repeated and massive impacts of this volcanism on stream biotas seem certain (as discussed elsewhere in detail—McDowall 1996a), and probably for longer than terrestrial impacts. All life was probably exterminated from streams within the ash-fall area, and downstream in rivers draining major areas of volcanic deposition as these were eroded.

As a result of the 186 A.D. eruption, vast ash showers were spread across the central North Island, spreading northeast from the eruption centre in Lake Taupo due, presumably, to strong southwesterly winds at the time (Fig. 62). This eruption must have caused massive biotic extinction across a wide area that seems to be still reﬂected in ﬁsh species’ distributions (McDowall 1996a). More localised eruptions, as of Mount Tarawera in 1886 (Cole 1970; Healy 1992), seems less likely to have affected the distribution of G. divergens.

There is a perception that G. divergens has a distribution that is complementary with that of brown trout (Hopkins 1965; McDowall 1968c, 1990b). Hopkins (1971) found G. divergens to be present only above waterfalls impassable to trout in several tributaries of the Ruamahanga River in the southeastern North Island (this seeming to mirror, yet again, the scenario for populations of many New Zealand Galaxias species, especially in the G. vulgaris species complex, and also the Australian G. olidus complex in Australia). Protection of G. divergens populations, even above falls, seems sometimes in jeopardy, as Hopkins (1971) reported that ‘‘three times during [his] study period, juvenile trout were found to have surmounted the waterfall...to penetrate hundreds of metres [upstream] through the [reach] occupied by G. divergens. Without the physical barrier offered by falls, trout would probably extend

- Fig. 62 Areas of ignimbrite deposition and volcanic ash fall from the 186 AD Taupo volcanic eruption

further through the system and perhaps cause changes in production levels of the ﬁsh populations’’. Minns (1990), in an analysis of species associations at over 6,000 sampling sites around New Zealand found that G. divergens and brown trout clustered together at the regional scale, but not at the local/reach scale, and he thought that the contrast between regional and reach scales might result from ‘‘exclusion owing to competition and/or predation, or as reﬂecting the occurrence of different species in different habitats within regions’’. The contrast between these two scales might be interpreted as making G. divergens vulnerable to invasion by brown trout.

Though this species seems to have declining range, explicit data are few. It seems to have disappeared from the type locality in headwaters of the Buller River near the Lewis Pass, though it is still present in some nearby streams. Also, attempts in 2000–2001 to re-collect specimens from the site of Hopkins’ study (1971, and discussed above) were unsuccessful. The effect

of trout invasion generally across this galaxiid’s range is not well documented and the distribution and status of G. divergens need study.

CONSERVATION RANKING: Galaxias divergens was not listed as in any way threatened by early IUCN and Department of Conservation accounts (Miller 1977; Williams and Given 1981; Molloy and Davis 1992; Tisdall 1994), but it is now classed by the Department as in Gradual Decline (Hitchmough 2002); it was not listed by IUCN (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006).

Galaxias prognathus: the upland longjaw galaxias of the eastern South Island: This species is one of two very elongate, slender galaxiids with distinctly protruding lower jaws—presumably with a close common ancestry (McDowall and Waters 2002) (Fig. 59D). Galaxias prognathus is certainly another non-migratory spring spawner. Little is known about it, although it is known to spawn just a few hundred largish eggs (ca. 2 mm diameter) (Bonnett 1992). It is a cryptic species that lives largely within the substrate rifﬂes (Fig. 61) in several major braided rivers that drain eastwards from the high Southern Alps (Fig. 60A, d)—Rakaia, Rangitata and Waitaki Rivers, but not the Waimakariri River (Fig. 60A—arrow 5). Of rivers further north it is known only from the Hurunui (Fig. 60A—arrow 4). It has not been found in smaller rivers of the central South Island that arise in the valleys of the hill country marginal to the high alps (Ashley, Selwyn, Ashburton, Orari, Hinds, Opihi, Waihao, etc.). It’s restriction to the main rivers seems to have nothing to do with proximate habitat suitability. Galaxias prognathus is typically found in small, shallow braids and spring-fed streams at the margins of large rivers, often in water so shallow that upper surfaces of the substrate cobbles are dry. These habitats are very abundant in the upper mountain valleys of rivers like the Rakaia Rangitata, and Waitaki.

Galaxias prognathus, like G. paucispondylus and G. vulgaris, is found upstream of some of the glacial lakes of the Mackenzie Basin in the eastern ﬂanks of the Southern Alps (Fig. 60-

A—arrow 6), but how it achieved this distribution is unclear. It may date back to declining years of the last glacial advance (ending about 10,000 years ago), when there may have been streams ﬂowing down the glacial valleys, and along the ﬂanks of the glacial moraine, prior to substantial ice-melt that would have led to these valleys being impounded by moraine, thus forming the lakes. It seems unlikely, from its habitat preferences that this ﬁsh could have penetrated upstream beyond the lakes by migrating through the lakes themselves.

The habitat of this species may be highly vulnerable to impoundment of the upper river valleys and gorges for water storage for hydroelectricity generation and farm irrigation (as has been proposed for both the Rangitata and Rakaia Rivers). Filling the impoundments would ﬂood the small, spring-fed streams that arise from the alluvial terraces along the margins of these large braided rivers, where G. prognathus is found (McDowall 1990b).

An additional conservation concern for G. prognathus is that this species appears to have disappeared from a tributary of the Maruia River (upper Buller River system near the Lewis Pass), this being the only population known west of the Southern Alps, and from the same site from which G. divergens has also disappeared (Fig. 60A—arrow 3: McDowall 1970, 1990b). Reasons for these disappearances are unexplored, but the streams are now ‘full’ of brown trout. Elkington and Charteris (2005) found this species to seldom co-occur with trout in rivers of the Mackenzie Basin, upper Waitaki River; they thought that the distribution of brown trout in the basin was limited only by barriers to upstream migration from the lower river and associated lakes.

CONSERVATION RANKING: Galaxias prognathus has not attracted much conservation concern, but was listed as Category C by the New Zealand Department of Conservation (though only by Tisdall 1994) and the latest ranking for this species is Gradual Decline (Hitchmough 2002). This ranking probably reﬂects its rather sporadic occurrence rather than local rarity. The few rivers from

which this species is known should alert us to careful monitoring of its distribution and abundance. There is no IUCN ranking (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006).

Galaxias cobitinis: a new lowland species of longjaw galaxias: Galaxias cobitinis, is another newly recognised species (McDowall and Waters 2002) (Fig. 59E) that seems clearly to share a common ancestry with the upland longjaw species, G. prognathus (Fig. 59D.). It is a nonmigratory, winter–spring spawner, with only a few hundred eggs, though little is known. Discovering more of its early life is not helped by the fact that juveniles (<ca. 20 mm) lack the protruding lower jaw of the adults (McDowall and Waters 2002) (as is probably true of G. prognathus). This makes it hard to distinguish young G. cobitinis from various other, sub-montane smallstream galaxiids of the Canterbury Plains (G. vulgaris and G. paucispondylus and G. macronasus, with which it often shares streams).

The population at the type locality of this species—the Kauru River, a small, low-elevation, tributary of the Kakanui River (Fig. 60A, +, arrow 8)—has been known for >25 years (Wing 1978), but its recognition as distinct from G. prognathus is very recent (hitherto another example of ‘uninformed ignorance’). The enigma of this ﬁsh, especially prior to recognition of its taxonomic distinctness, has always been the unexpected presence of a typically submontane ‘longjaw galaxias’ in a small, low-elevation stream that is heavily abstracted for pastoral irrigation, including the reach from where the ﬁsh is primarily known. It is also known from the Kakanui River mainstem just downstream of the Kauru conﬂuence, this perhaps a result of expatriation from the Kauru population. A somewhat dated (1989) record from the Hakataramea River, a tributary of the lower Waitaki River (Fig. 60A—arrow 7) has recently been augmented by discovery of populations in upper reaches of that tributary (S. Bowie pers. comm.). The Waitaki River is near enough to the Kakanui/Kauru Rivers for the latter to have once, conceivably, been connected to the Waitaki. Continued exploration of streams of the Waitaki River system by Department of Con-

servation staff has revealed additional populations of G. cobitinis in tributaries of the Waitaki River further upstream, in the Otematapaio Stream in hills behind Otematata, and quite widely in streams across the Mackenzie Basin of the upper Waitaki River system (Fig. 60A—arrow 9: Elkington and Charteris 2005), and so it now seems to be a quite widespread, largely Waitaki River endemic, with the Kauru/Kakanui populations something of an enigma, rather than being typical.

The Kauru River has a small catchment in an area of low rainfall, and is subject to natural low ﬂows in summer and autumn. Heavy abstraction for pastoral irrigation is typically at the same time of the year as it has natural low ﬂows—for the simple reason that a lack of rainfall is critical for both pastoral farming and for maintaining stream ﬂows. A key element in ﬂow management in the Kauru River is that abstraction restrictions are determined by ﬂows in the larger Kakanui River (of which the Kauru is a tributary). This means that even though the Kakanui has still not reached its prescribed minimum ﬂow, ﬂows in the Kauru may sometimes be totally abstracted and the Kauru River is sometimes reduced to a series of isolated pools. This typically happens in summer and early autumn, when insolation may elevate stream temperatures to more than 20 C. Such temperatures would probably be fatal for most galaxiids, let alone a form that seems otherwise restricted to cold, sub-montane streams. There were serious concerns over the summer of 2000–2001 that abstraction and elevated temperatures would extirpate the Kauru River population of G. cobitinis, even before it was formally described.

Observations of habitats occupied by G. cobitinis in the Kauru River showed that the ﬁsh were possibly ﬁnding cold ground-water upwelling from the stream gravels (McDowall and Waters

- 2002), and survival of the Kauru population over the particularly stressful 2000–2001 summer probably can be attributed to this upwelling. It is probable, also, that long-term persistence of this

- species at low elevations in the Kauru, and its very restricted distribution there, have been a consequence of its ability to locate such cold upwelling. It can penetrate deep into the sub-

strate into areas of upwelling (see Hartman 1990). Recent information (P. Ravenscroft, Department of Conservation, Dunedin), suggests that it may also spawn in these upwellings, as much as 150 mm down into the stream substrate.

CONSERVATION RANKING: Clearly, G. cobitinis is highly vulnerable and was once ranked as New Zealand’s most threatened freshwater ﬁsh—leading to its classiﬁcation by the New Zealand Department of Conservation as Nationally Critical (Hitchmough 2002), despite discoveries of more populations (Elkington and Charteris 2005). The species was recognised and described too recently for assessment by IUCN (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006), but was listed in the EBF threatened species series (McDowall and Allibone 2004).

The Patagonian (Chilean and Argentinean) Galaxias species

The Chilean puye, Galaxias globiceps: The taxonomic and conservation status of the Chilean Galaxias globiceps (Fig. 63A), as also its habitat preferences and ecology, are uncertain, because it has only been reported with certainty:

- 1. When the type series was collected in southern/central Chile in the 1920s (Fig. 64, w—arrow 1) (Eigenmann 1928); and
- 2. More recently from a stream on Isla Chiloe, a little further south (Murillo and Ruiz 2002) (Fig. 64, w—arrow 2).

An attempt to obtain further specimens in the area around the type locality in the late 1960s failed (McDowall 1971b), as also more recently by Berra and Ruiz (1994) and Berra and Barbour (1998). It has been reported up to 119 mm, but this ﬁgure is based on only the type series (McDowall 1971b). Virtually nothing is known about the ecology and life history of this species, though it may resemble those in G. platei, another Patagonian species (see below), though this species is also poorly known.

The question of extinction of G. globiceps has been mooted by Berra and Barbour (1998), who suggested that ‘‘Further collecting is needed to

- Fig. 63 Two Patagonian Galaxias species: (A) G. globiceps—once feared extinct (known to >110 mm; (B) G. platei (known to 330 mm) (probably a species complex)

- Fig. 64 Distributions of two Patagonian galaxiids, puye grande, Galaxias platei (d) (probably a species complex), and G. globiceps (w). Arrow 1—type locality; arrow

- 2—recent site on Isla Chiloe

determine if G. globiceps is still extant’’; Murillo and Ruiz (2002) also canvassed this question, but the ﬁsh is too poorly known to be deﬁnite. Equally, little is known about its habitat preferences or life history and behaviour. Based on knowledge of other species, it seems likely to be a benthic, perhaps cryptic, cobble-gravel stream species, though study is urgently needed.

CONSERVATION RANKING: In addition to deﬁcient knowledge of the distribution and biology of this ﬁsh, there is also uncertainty about the taxonomy of Patagonian galaxiids covered at present by the names G. globiceps, and also G. platei (see below), making an unequivocal determination of the status of G. globiceps problematic. Campos et al. (1998) classed it as Rare, whereas Murillo and Ruiz (2002) thought that it was In Danger of Extinction. Overall, this seems to be an example of ‘informed ignorance’. More work is needed. This species was listed as Data deﬁcient in the 2004 IUCN Red List of Threatened Species (IUCN 2006).

The Patagonian puye grande, Galaxias platei: Galaxias platei (Fig. 63B) is widely distributed from central Chile and the Argentinean lakes around Bariloche, along the Patagonian Andes and south to Tierra del Fuego (Fig. 64, d). It is largely a species of lake shores and substrates but is also in streams, often associated with lakes.

Galaxias platei coexists widely with brown and rainbow trout but, as noted by Macchi et al. (1999), ‘‘Whether the salmonid introductions created a new trophic niche (e.g., such as a top predator), or whether the salmonids share an existing niche with local endemics, are questions that have been unanswerable until now...the lack of lakes and streams free of salmonids in Patagonia hinders comparative experiments [again]. Although it is impossible to reconstruct the fauna before and after salmonid introduction, it is possible to assess the probable impacts of salmonids on the predatory guilds in Patagonian lakes and reservoirs on the overlaps observed’’. Milano et al. (2002) thought that ‘‘Predation may...have an inﬂuence on the spatial distribution of G. platei’’ which they found to be ‘‘mostly absent from the littoral zone’’ of lakes, but present at depths >30 m, and thus from below the thermocline and deeper than the euphotic zone. The role of salmonid predation in the lake shallows is uncertain and could usefully be investigated. I found G. platei to be moderately common in shallows around rocky shores of some southern Patagonian lakes northeast of Punta Arenas in 1968 (McDowall 1971b), but that was before trout were recorded in some of the lakes.

Whereas Macchi et al. (1999) reported G. platei to be absent from littoral ﬁsh captures from Argentinean lakes, studies by Milano and Vigliano (1997), Vigliano et al. (1997), and Milano et al. (2002) have shown its presence at depths below 30 m, and they found it in the diet of introduced brown trout taken at 50 m depth. The absence of G. platei from the upper littoral zone of Patagonian lakes may be associated with predation in these waters, possibly partly by the native Percichthys trucha (f. Percichthyidae—though not in the far south), as well as trout species.

Pascual et al. (2002) reviewed the conservation status of freshwater ﬁshes of Patagonian Argentina, ﬁnding that three exotic salmonids, brown trout, rainbow trout and brook charr exhibited broad overlap with native species, especially galaxiids, at a broad spatial scale. Because of this, they found that ‘‘documentation on the impacts that salmonids have on native communities ...is scarce, and largely inconclusive’’, with information on interactions with galaxiids being particularly deﬁcient. They thought that more speciﬁc analysis of the interactions between salmonids and native ﬁshes was needed, and at a smaller scale—a type of research they described as ‘‘virtually non-existent for Patagonian rivers’’. They did, however, recognise that salmonids feed heavily on some ﬁsh, particularly galaxiids, with large brown trout being the most piscivorous. Consistent with what is stated so often already: ‘‘Whether segregation provides a mechanism to alleviate competition and predation, or is itself the result of species’ interactions, remains a matter of speculation...Even when there are good reasons to believe that salmonids have had signiﬁcant effects on...the freshwater biota, the lack of an articulate vision of how virgin communities might have looked before introduction’’ seriously impedes an understanding of the effects of salmonid introductions.

CONSERVATION RANKING: Bello and Ubeda (1998) summarised past analyses of the conservation status of G. platei in Argentinean lakes, citing its ranking as variously Insufﬁciently known by

Bertonatti and Gonzalez (1992), Indeterminate by Chebez (1994), and Vulnerable by Glade (1993) (categories translated from Spanish). Bello and Ubeda (1998) ranked the species as Near Threatened in Argentina, whereas Campos et al. (1998) considered it Vulnerable in Chile, where it is affected adversely by introduced salmonids and habitat deterioration.

Details of the taxonomy of both G. globiceps and G. platei, as well as information on their distributions and abundance, are deﬁcient, and therefore an understanding of the impacts of trout, if any, is lacking. These facts, and uncertainties about taxonomy, indicate the need for study before this species’ conservation status can be dealt with. This species is not listed by IUCN (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006).

Galaxias in South Africa

The South African galaxiid, Galaxias zebratus—a species complex: In South Africa only a single, quite widely distributed species of Galaxias is presently recognised (Barnard 1943; see also McDowall 1973a) (Fig. 65), and is part of a quite impoverished freshwater ﬁsh fauna of the Southern and Western Cape Region (Impson et al. 2002) (Fig. 66, d). Only 19 freshwater ﬁsh species are native to the area, 16 of them endemics (Skelton 1993, 2002). It seems certain, however, (Waters and Cambray 1997; McDowall 2001 and unpublished; Skelton 2002) that there are several, perhaps as many as ﬁve galaxiid species in South

Fig. 65 Galaxias in South Africa: two morphotypes (A and B) of a species complex currently regarded as Galaxias zebratus (known to 75 mm)

Fig. 66 Distribution in southern Africa of galaxiids of the Galaxias zebratus species complex

Africa, most of these awaiting formal recognition and description (Fig. 65).

South African Galaxias are rarely >70 mm long, and are variously open-living pool to cryptic rifﬂe-dwellers. Little is known of their natural history, but they are certainly non-diadromous, and mostly river/stream dwelling—though they sometimes occur around lake or pond shores. The taxonomy of Galaxias in South Africa is so poorly understood that making categorical statements about conservation, distribution or natural history is problematic. McVeigh (1977a), while providing no data, noted that ‘‘In contrast to many other Cape endemics this species has been able to maintain itself despite predation by introduced game species and habitat destruction such as pollution, siltation and water extraction’’. He clearly envisaged G. zebratus as having a role as a food for introduced trout, mainly rainbows (McVeigh 1977b), writing that ‘‘It is obvious that the galaxias is the ideal ﬁsh species to be used as a forage ﬁsh for game species...The galaxias breeding season is at the right time of year to supply young trout ﬁngerlings with a large supply of food in the form of galaxias fry... [which] ...are the perfect sized food for these ﬁngerling trout...and ...increase in size at a rate that causes the ﬁngerling trout to [prey] upon them continually...This is borne out by the fact that galaxias are often found in the stomachs of trout caught by local anglers’’. The life history/behavioural attributes discussed above make the South African taxa highly vulnerable to predation by introduced salmonids and centrarchids. These comments are reminiscent of discussion above of breeding by New Zealand galaxiids being so timed that the larvae hatch and emerge into streams, to provide an easily accessible food for juvenile trout emerg-

ing from their redds at about the same time of the year, but which are of much greater size than the galaxiids. McVeigh did not seem to consider the potentially adverse impacts of trout predation on the galaxiid prey, and provided no data on, or even any reference to, long-term coexistence of G. zebratus and trout species. I doubt that it happens.

Gaigher et al. (1980) reviewed the conservation status of freshwater ﬁshes of the Cape Region of South Africa, an area that covers the range of G. zebratus, but they expressed no explicit concerns for the galaxiid’s conservation status. They described it as abundant, a distinct contrast with concerns about the more taxonomically diverse cyprinids in the same area (Skelton 1993).

De Moor and Bruton (1988), however, pointed out that the largemouth bass (Micropterus salmoides—Centrarchidae) had been introduced into habitats of G. zebratus, and while it was initially thought that this ‘‘did not seriously affect populations of indigenous species in the fast-ﬂowing sections of the river’’, a decade later it was reported that ‘‘the populations of ‘coarse ﬁsh’ had declined’’ and some indigenous species had been ‘‘eradicated from fast-ﬂowing sections of the river’’. Similar ﬁndings are reported from elsewhere in Southern Africa.

Bruton and van As (1986) without explicitly naming G. zebratus as threatened, generalised that many introduced exotic species were serious threats to native species. Skelton (1990), similarly, noted that ‘‘Game ﬁsh species’’ such as brown and rainbow trout and especially smallmouth bass (Micropterus dolomieu) have ‘‘had a severe impact on the highly endemic faunas of the Cape rivers’’, these being mostly localised endemic cyprinids (genus Pseudobarbus—Skelton 1993), but he did not list G. zebratus among the threatened species. Nor was it listed as threatened in a review of the fauna by Skelton et al. (1995). Impson and Hamman (2000) later addressed threats to, and the conservation status of, freshwater ﬁshes of the Western Cape Province of South Africa, and they also found no reason to express serious concern for G. zebratus, in contrast to grave concerns for various cyprinids formerly described as widespread in the western

Cape region. Skelton later (2002) listed alien ﬁsh introductions (as well as habitat destruction) as threatening the survival of G. zebratus. Alien species that he listed included brown and rainbow trout, largemouth, smallmouth and spotted bass (Micropterus punctulatus). He thought that ‘‘ﬁshes of the Cape are notoriously naı¨ve in the presence of predatory species and were rapidly eliminated following the introduction of an alien

- top predator’’, and he hoped that populations of some Cape endemics were protected from trout invasion by barrier waterfalls (once again). Much of Skelton’s commentary was focussed on species of ‘‘barbs’’ and ‘‘redﬁns’’ (f. Cyprinidae), which had been his personal research focus for many years; that the Cape galaxias was not discussed as explicitly as the cyprinids undoubtedly partly reﬂects lack of clarity in galaxiid systematics in southern Africa, and it will be interesting to see what changes arise in our understanding of the threat status of African galaxiids once systematics and distributions are clariﬁed. It would be unsurprising if galaxiid populations, like those of the cyprinid barbs and redﬁns, are found to be present only upstream of falls that are impenetrable by trouts and basses, or in streams that alien predators have yet to invade.

Cambray et al. (1995) extended the eastern range of G. zebratus in southern Africa, to include the Krom and Kouga [Gamtoos] Rivers, and thought it likely ‘‘that in the past G. zebratus were more widely distributed in the two river systems. Man-made impacts, damming, and water abstraction for agriculture and, especially, the introduction of alien ﬁsh such as Micropterus species [basses of the family Centrarchidae] have probably eliminated Cape galaxias from the lower reaches of the Krom and Gamtoos River systems’’, adding that ‘‘in the Krom River there is a new threat that the alien ﬁsh Oncorhynchus mykiss could be introduced into the last areas where the Cape galaxias occurs...It is a sobering thought that new introductions of alien ﬁsh species could eliminate the Cape galaxias from these rivers where they have existed for tens of millions of years ...Alien ﬁsh, dams and water abstraction threaten what remains of these important populations of Cape galaxias’’. Woodford et al. (2005) in a survey of a mountain stream of the Cape Floristic Region of

western South Africa, found that G. zebratus as well as several cyprinids and a catﬁsh, were entirely absent from a stream invaded by smallmouth bass, with only a single native species persistent, though in seriously reduced numbers.

In a review of the impact of ‘‘alien trout species’’ Cambray (2003b) claimed that ‘‘negative impact of alien trout species in South African biodiversity has been evident for some time’’, pointing to published warnings that date back almost a century (Hey 1926). Cambray (2003b) pointed to a site in the Keurbooms River where [G. zebratus] had previously been recorded and no [G. zebratus] were collected in 1996...This is a tragedy since [G. zebratus] is South Africa’s only freshwater ﬁsh link with Gondwanaland...and it is even more serious now that we know there is more than one galaxiid species in South Africa’’.

As G. zebratus, at least in some places, is a small, open-living, free-swimming species, its long-term survival in artiﬁcial sympatry with introduced predators like brown and rainbow trout, and large- and small-mouth bass, seems unlikely. If we recognise what happened when galaxiids and trout were brought into sympatry in other parts of the world, and recall the comments of McVeigh (1977a, b) about the suitability of the species as a ‘‘forage ﬁsh’’ for trout, it seems that G. zebratus may be being protected from local extirpation by trout and bass only where there are different habitat preferences or tolerances, or the predators are absent for either biological (different habitats) or historical (no releases of alien predators) reasons.

Perceptions of the conservation status of ‘G. zebratus’ are bound to alter substantially when the taxonomy of populations in South Africa is better resolved, i.e. the apparent absence of adverse ‘species-level’ impacts of exotic species on G. zebratus, and the actual conservation status of the galaxiid, may be both being obscured by taxonomists’ failure, hitherto, to recognise the taxonomic diversity encompassed by what is currently called Galaxias zebratus (Barnard 1943; McDowall 1973a; Waters and Cambray 1997; author, unpubl. data).

Once the taxonomy of the populations is clariﬁed, there seems likely to be a need for careful mapping of the distributions of introduced

predators and the galaxiid lineages across their ranges in South Africa. Surveys of ﬁsh distribution patterns in South African rivers where G. zebratus is present, along with introduced trouts or basses, would be both interesting and, I suspect, urgent. As Impson and Hamman (2000) remarked, generally, ‘‘freshwater ﬁshes of the Western Cape, South Africa, need urgent conservation attention...Most of these species are endemic to single catchments. The primary causes for their threatened status are thought to be the impact of predatory alien ﬁsh and habitat alteration, fragmentation and destruction, particularly by agricultural development’’. Impson et al.

- (2002) noted that G. zebratus has been recorded from 12 South African nature reserves, but this does not mean that this offers refuge from introduced predators, as Impson et al. (2002) noted that these predators ‘‘are frequently recorded from provincial nature reserves’’.

CONSERVATION RANKING: Skelton (2002) included G. zebratus in a listing of the conservation status of SouthAfricanfreshwaterﬁshes,rankingitasLower Risk under the IUCN threat categories (Baillie and Groombridge1996),andgavealienintroductionsas the primary threat and habitat destruction as the secondary threat. Impson et al. (2002) also listed G. zebratus as Lower Risk, but added that evolutionarily signiﬁcant units (ESUs—Waples 1994) ‘‘have already been identiﬁed’’ for the species, and this clearly has implications for conservation status.

The IUCN threat ranking of this species is Lower Risk-Near Threatened (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; 2004 IUCN Red List of Threatened Species: <www.redlist.org>); it will be interesting to see how the rankings will change when the various, as yet undescribed, morphs and lineages are clariﬁed (Waters and Cambray 1997; McDowall 2001) and their conservation status is reconsidered.
