Australian and New Zealand species of Neochanna mudﬁsh

The galaxiid genus Neochanna includes one southeastern Australian species, four New Zealand mainland species and another at the Chat-

ham Islands. They are generally referred to as ‘mudﬁshes’ in both Australia and New Zealand (McDowall 1997d, 2000a, 2004a) (Fig. 67). The scope of the genus has gradually expanded, as broader limits have been established to ensure inclusion of the entire clade (McDowall 1970, 1997d, 2004a; Waters and White 1997; McDowall and Waters 2004; Waters and McDowall 2005), and as further species have been discovered and described (Gleeson et al. 1999b; Ling and Gleeson 2001). They share an idiosyncratic suite of specialisations that relate to their distinctive habitat preferences, tending towards an anguilliform morphotype (McDowall 1990b, 1997d; Waters and McDowall 2005) and exhibit dis-

Fig. 67 The Australian and New Zealand mudﬁshes: (A) Australian mudﬁsh, Neochanna cleaveri (southern Australia and Tasmania—reaches >140 mm); (B) Canterbury mudﬁsh, N. burrowsius (New Zealand—>150 mm); (C) Black mudﬁsh, N. diversus (N.Z.—>155 mm); (D) Brown mudﬁsh, N. apoda (N.Z.—>175 mm); (E) Burgundy mudﬁsh, N. heleios (N.Z.—>90 mm); (F) Chathams mudﬁsh, Galaxias rekohua (N.Z. at Chatham Islands—>175 mm)

tinctly anguilliform swimming mode, with multiple half-wave ﬂexures along the body (McDowall

- 2004c). These include greatly elongated anterior tubular nostrils, and small eyes (especially in large individuals—Fig. 67). The dorsal and anal ﬁns tend to be long and low, and the tail distinctly rounded. Fleshy ﬂanges along dorsal and ventral edges of the caudal peduncle are strongly developed, and tend to become conﬂuent with the hind margins of the dorsal and anal ﬁns, concentrating propulsion towards the posterior of the body, and this may be important for swimming amongst proliﬁc instream debris in habitats generally occupied. They also exhibit a strong tendency to lose their pelvic ﬁns (present, though small, and with 7 rays in N. cleaveri and N. rekohua, much reduced and with 4–5 rays in N. burrowsius (Fig. 67B), or ﬁns and girdle lacking in N. apoda, N. diversus and N. heleios—Fig. 67C–E). Viewed together, the six species now included in the genus (McDowall 2004a) form a transformation series, with the Australian N. cleaveri and the Chatham Islands (New Zealand) N. rekohua being the least specialised and the New Zealand N. apoda and N. heleios the most specialised.

Neochanna species typically occupy wetlands, wetland streams and spring heads, and tend to be much elongated, almost cigar-shaped ﬁshes that live in habitats with much plant debris and aquatic macrophytes. Some, perhaps all, Neochanna species have the ability to survive in ephemeral streams and wetlands. This is attributed by Fulton and Pavuk (1988; Fulton 2000) to the Australian N. cleaveri and to all New Zealand species by Gleeson et al. (1999b) and Davey et al.

- (2003), and though probably true, I am not aware of any explicit published observations of this in N. heleios or N. rekohua. When their habitats dry up, ‘aestivating’ Neochanna mudﬁsh may be found, semi-torpid, in damp hidden recesses such as caverns formed by rotted-out tree roots, beneath tree buttresses, under fallen tree trunks and logs, and similar places (Fig. 68), and they seem capable of enduring loss of water for weeks, if not months (Eldon 1978, 1979a, b; Eldon et al. 1978; O’Brien 2005). Survival during these dewatered phases depends on ﬁsh ﬁnding moist locations, in combination with their known ability to respire cutaneously (Meredith et al. 1982).

Fig. 68 Aestivating Canterbury mudﬁsh, Neochanna burrowsius (photo: G.A. Eldon)

They do not fully aestivate, as they are not entirely torpid when found. They respond to being touched or exposed, rapidly become mobile, and are not known to form a mucous-lined protective ‘‘cocoon’’ (Eldon 1978, 1979a). Caution needs to be used when considering the ability of these ﬁsh to aestivate. Eldon et al. (1978) monitored dewatering of a natural habitat occupied by N. burrowsius, and found that ‘‘only 2 weeks after the habitat became dry...many ﬁsh were already dead and many others were in situations where their survival for a further 2 weeks seemed problematical....[even though] In more favourable habitats, where moistureretaining material and shade are present, N. burrowsius is known to survive for periods of 4 weeks or more...’’. There is a complex of causes and beneﬁts associated with this partial aestivation by mudﬁsh. It allows them to occupy habitats that are often inaccessible to other ﬁsh and, also, these habitats provide conditions that few other ﬁsh species can cope with, such as stresses of seasonal dewatering that may persist for several months. At least one mudﬁsh species’ life history seems adapted to exploiting ephemeral wetlands by reproducing almost immediately after surface water returns (Eldon 1978).

When held in containers with limited water, and probably as a response to reduced oxygen levels, at least some Neochanna species seem able to exploit oral breathing; they will ‘hang’ near vertically, snout at the surface, gulping and discharging air bubbles (Meredith et al. 1982; Eldon 1978; McDowall 1999b; O’Brien 2005).

- Fig. 69 Distribution in southeastern Australia and Tasmania of Australian mudﬁsh, Neochanna cleaveri, d

Depending on the timing of these ﬂuvial disconnections, upstream and downstream migrations of the sea-migratory life stages of N. cleaveri may be compromised, and it may also, potentially, suffer from ‘‘source and sink’’ mechanisms (Pulliam 1988; Dunning et al. 1992—dispersion of progeny from reproductively positive sites to reproductively negative sites/habitats), that would increase its vulnerability to decline.

Authorities in Tasmania have described N. cleaveri as: ‘‘reasonably common at low elevations all around the coast, although adults are not often seen’’ (Anon. n.d.). Cadwallader and Backhouse (1983), however, thought that its range had become fragmented in Tasmania by drainage of swamps (threats may resemble those discussed below for Galaxiella pusilla).

The Australian mudﬁsh, Neochanna cleaveri: Australia’s N. cleaveri (Fig. 67A) inhabits low elevation wetlands and swamp creeks in Tasmania and the southern fringes of Victoria (Fig. 69) (Koehn and Raadik 1991). It is largely cryptic by day, but emerges at night to forage; it grows to about 140 mm and has small eggs that probably number several thousand. Spawning is in winter. Unlike New Zealand Neochanna, N. cleaveri is amphidromous, having a sea-living whitebait juvenile that spends about three months at sea—so the N. cleaveri whitebait is smaller than those of other galaxiids, being only about 30 mm long. It is exploited in the Tasmanian whitebait ﬁshery (Fulton 1986), Fulton (1987) reported it to be ‘‘abundant and widespread in whitebait migrations’’ around coastal Tasmania, though he provided no data. The presence of this marine life stage means that the ﬁsh must have access to and from the sea to complete its life cycle (no nonmigratory populations are known, but this species is little studied). Maintenance of sea access seems likely to be a restrictive requirement for a species that can aestivate when its habitats become dehydrated, because these habitats will be vulnerable to becoming physically disconnected from other water bodies, at least temporarily. Retaining access to and from the sea is perhaps facilitated by adults living in swampy areas close to or around river estuaries (Fulton 1996).

CONSERVATION RANKING: Though it has achieved no formal listing among Australia’s threatened galaxiids (Wager and Jackson 1993; Crook 2001; Crook and Pogonoski 2002, 2003), Allen et al. (2002) classed it as a ‘‘rare species that is listed as Vulnerable, particularly in Victoria where large areas of suitable habitat have been destroyed’’. It is not listed, however, by IUCN (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006). This question needs to be re-examined—we may be in a state of ‘uninformed ignorance’.

Wetland drainage and the mainland New Zealand Neochanna mudﬁshes: New Zealand’s four mainland Neochanna mudﬁshes (Fig. 67B–E) are widespread across the country at mostly low elevations (Fig. 70). Typically they are found in habitats not occupied by other ﬁsh species (Eldon 1978, 1979b), but mudﬁsh tend often to be absent where other ﬁsh species are present. Minns (1990) for instance, in a country-wide cluster analysis of species associations at nearly 6,500 freshwater habitat sampling sites, found that N. apoda, N. burrowsius and N. diversus each ‘‘showed no association with [any] other species’’. New Zealand’s Neochanna species, unlike their Australian counterpart (discussed above) are not amphidromous, and so do not require access to and from the sea.

Vulnerability of these Neochanna species to decline involves their tendency to form popula-

- Fig. 70 Distributions of the New Zealand mudﬁshes (· - black mudﬁsh, Neochanna diversus; m—burgundy mudﬁsh, N. heleios; n—brown mudﬁsh, N. apoda; d—Canterbury mudﬁsh, N. burrowsius; w—Chathams mudﬁsh, N. rekohua); arrow 1—North Cape; arrow 2—presence of burgundy mudﬁsh within range of black mudﬁsh; arrow

- 3—Hikurangi Swamp, now largely obliterated; arrow
- 4—brown mudﬁsh on both sides of Cook Strait, a consequence of former land connections; arrow 5—brown mudﬁsh widespread on west coast of South Island; arrow 6—Canterbury mudﬁsh spreading as far south as Waitaki River valley; arrow 7—Chatham Island

tions in small, often isolated and vulnerable, areas of wetland, which have suffered severely from habitat perturbations in New Zealand. Wetlands have long been a target of developers throughout New Zealand (Park 1995; Cromarty and Scott 1996; McDowall 1998b). The farming potential from land produced by wetland drainage was recognised very early in New Zealand’s colonial history, Joseph Banks, naturalist with James Cook in New Zealand in 1769, alluding to this (Beaglehole 1962; Park 2002). The soils of these wetlands are typically fertile and highly productive (McDowall 1975, 1998b; Anon 1982), and their pastoral and cropping potential has been realised with vigour throughout New Zealand for 150 years. Park (2002), for instance, thought that, ‘‘No New Zealand environment has seen greater loss of its indigenous life than its swamps’’. Moreover, many of the wetlands that were once favoured by mudﬁsh were probably those around the margins of podocarp

forests, and these forests have also been widely modiﬁed, being felled for their timber, and the land cleared to exploit its farming potential (Park 1995, 2002).

As a general statement, the New Zealand Department of Conservation’s recovery plan for Neochanna species (Barrier 2003) drew attention to the ‘‘lack of awareness by local authorities and the public on the state of New Zealand ﬁsh species’’ and recognised that with regard to conservation of Neochanna species this ‘‘needs to be addressed through a public awareness campaign’’. This was said to particularly include a recognition of the need to seek close cooperation of landowners and the general public.

Neochanna burrowsius in the Canterbury Plains, South Island, New Zealand: Neochanna burrowsius (Fig. 69B) is a largely cryptic, nocturnal ﬁsh, foraging in the open at night, but its larvae and juveniles are more open living and may be found in the open by day. Eldon et al. (1978; Eldon 1979a, b) found N. burrowsius to be ‘‘plentiful in places’’, across the Canterbury Plains of the eastern South Island (Fig. 70, d) spreading as far south as the Waitaki River valley (Fig. 70, arrow 6). Spawning is usually in spring, with several hundreds to several thousand small eggs that are laid amongst aquatic macrophytes (Eldon 1979a; O’Brien 2003, 2005).

As with other mudﬁshes, the larvae and small juveniles can be seen in loose shoals in open water until they reach 30–40 mm, when they become cryptic and are seldom seen. Eldon et al. (1978) observed that many ‘‘remaining habitats are at some degree of risk from one cause or another. Numerous streams in Canterbury probably once had short dry periods, but [they] now ﬂow only in times of ﬂood... [and he considered that] ... Continued alterations to and elimination of waterways pose threats’’ to this species’ future. In the New Zealand Department of Conservation’s recovery plan for mudﬁsh species, Barrier (2003) described N. burrowsius as ‘‘now restricted to small relict populations in fragile wetland remnants’’. Climate change, with the potential for landscapes and river systems east of the Southern Alps to received signiﬁcantly less rainfall than at present, combined with the

concomitant additional demand for irrigation water by farmers, and the already fragile conservation status of many known habitats means that this species’ conservation status needs to be monitored with some care.

Skrzynski (1968) long ago thought the Canterbury mudﬁsh to be so rare, that its continued survival ‘‘may depend on setting up reserves or preserving the habitat in areas where it is known to exist’’. Cadwallader (1975c) soon afterwards had difﬁculty ﬁnding populations that he could use for a conservation/population study, though Eldon (1979a, b) had no such difﬁculty, and nor did O’Brien who recently completed a Ph.D. on this species (O’Brien 2003, 2005; Davey et al.

- 2003). This divergent perception does not seem to have been due to a change in this species’ abundance.

Mudﬁsh habitat remnants tend to be vulnerable to lowered water tables, as farmers intensify their landuse practices, with wetland fragments being progressively reduced. Wetland development has driven surviving mudﬁsh populations either into drains dug by farmers to improve drainage of their land, or into small residual wetland streams, often spring heads, sometimes at the toes of gullies at the margins of foothills of the Southern Alps, where water tends to surface and accumulate.

A parallel adverse impact on Canterbury mudﬁsh populations has been that as wetlands and associated streams have been developed and drained, waters that mudﬁsh occupy have become increasingly accessible to invasion by other ﬁsh species, particularly anguillid eels, and introduced brown trout. Eldon (1979b), for instance, found Canterbury mudﬁsh only at localities inaccessible to trout, across a broad area where there is intensive penetration of brown trout. He also examined European perch for possible predation, and found that adult perch and N. burrowsius consistently occupied separate pools in streams. This might be interpreted as indicating that perch rapidly eliminate any co-existing N. burrowsius, so that the two species rarely, and only brieﬂy, co-occur. It could, however, mean that the two species favour different habitats, though it is reasonable to assume that Eldon, who knew the mudﬁsh species well, would have been examining perch diets in places where he expected mudﬁsh to

be present. O’Brien (2003) remarked that this species ‘‘does poorly in competitive situations with other ﬁsh but can tolerate the presence’’ of Gobiomorphus breviceps (f. Eleotridae). She thought it ‘‘very important to exclude brown trout and perch’’, and named these two species, plus the shortﬁn eel Anguilla australis (Anguillidae), and ‘‘herons’’, probably whitefaced heron, Ardea novaehollandiae, as potential predators (O’Brien 2005).

Harraway (2000) summarised the status of N. burrowsius as comprising ‘‘disjointed remnant populations surviving in a diverse range of modiﬁed habitats that are vulnerable to changing patterns in agricultural land use’’. Ling (2001) thought all of New Zealand’s Neochanna species to face threat, not so much because their numbers are particularly low, but because wherever they live, often in small wetland fragments or managed farm drains, their habitats are threatened by farming practices. For many areas, all that is needed to eliminate populations is ﬂuctuation in water levels over a time and spatial scale great enough to exceed the ability of these ﬁsh to aestivate until water returns to their habitat. The issue is probably applicable to all New Zealand species of Neochanna.

A recovery plan for the New Zealand species of Neochanna recognised the extensive, and ongoing loss of wetland habitat, and recommended that there is a need to:

- 1. Reverse this trend;
- 2. Identify, maintain and improve quality of habitats; and
- 3. Conserve genetic diversity in this and other Neochanna species (Barrier 2003).

CONSERVATION RANKING: Neochanna burrowsius has had a long history of conservation concern, dating back to comments by Stokell (1955) and Skrzynski (1968). It was designated Rare in the IUCN Red Data Book (Fishes) by Miller (1977) and also in the New Zealand Red Data Book (Williams and Given 1981). New Zealand Department of Conservation listings have been Category A (top priority) and Category B (second priority) (Molloy and Davis 1992; Tisdall 1994) and then

classed as Nationally Endangered in the Department of Conservations most recent assessment of threatened biota (Hitchmough 2002), which Barrier (2003) interpreted to mean ‘‘acutely’’ threatened. Its listing by IUCN is currently Vulnerable by IUCN (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006).

springs discharge at the surface. Illicit, southwards spread of gambusia into the geographical range of N. apoda probably poses a serious potential threat to this species (Studholme 2002, 2003).

The goals and objectives for a recovery plan for New Zealand species of Neochanna were as listed above for N. burrowsius) (and see Barrier 2003).

Neochanna apoda: widespread but sporadic in central New Zealand: A second New Zealand species of Neochanna, N. apoda (Fig. 67D), is widespread in central areas of the country (Fig. 70, n) and spreading south along the West Coast of the South Island (Fig. 70—arrow 5), its presence on both North and South Islands is a likely consequence of Pleistocene bridging of land across Cook Strait (Fig. 70—arrow 4). It grows to about 175 mm. Studies on the life history and ecology of N. apoda (Eldon 1978) revealed the same behaviours and issues for conservation discussed above for N. burrowsius. It is highly cryptic and nocturnal as an adult, emerging from cover to forage at night, although larvae and juveniles can be found in small shoals, in the open, by day. Neochanna apoda has several hundred to more than a thousand eggs, and seems to be facultative in timing its spawning, being adapted to spawning rapidly after water returns to formerly dewatered habitats (Eldon

- 1978). Though New Zealand has no measure of the explicit, relative impacts of the various perturbations affecting mudﬁsh distributions, it is clear that wetland drainage and conversion to pasture have caused pervasive damage to N. apoda habitats, which are perhaps dramatised most clearly in land development practices in northern Westland, an area that has been developed relatively recently. Viewed broadly, N. apoda has disappeared from many areas and, in some, persists in often small fragmentary forest wetlands whose long-term survival is precarious. There are now extensive areas of countryside where former mudﬁsh habitats have been entirely destroyed, and sometimes relictual mudﬁsh populations may be found only in tiny wetland remnants that have escaped development, either by accident, because they happen to be in fauna reserves, or because beneﬁts from draining them have outweighed the costs, or where long-lasting

CONSERVATION RANKING: Neochanna apoda was not listed by Miller (1977) or Williams and Given (1981) in the IUCN Red Data books for ﬁshes, or New Zealand, but it was classed as Category A (top priority) (Molloy and Davis 1992) and Category B (second priority) (Tisdall 1994), and ﬁnally as in Gradual Decline (Hitchmough 2002) in the New Zealand Department of Conservations listing of fauna and ﬂora under threat; Barrier (2003) interpreted this as ‘‘chronically’’ threatened. IUCN listed it as Lower Risk-Near Threatened in both 1996 and 2004 (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006).

Neochanna diversus: widespread in northern New Zealand: Neochanna diversus (Fig. 67C) is less well known than N. burrowsius and N. apoda, having not been subjected to detailed study that these other two species have (Eldon 1979a, b; Eldon et al. 1978; O’Brien 2003, 2005—but see Thompson 1986; Ling 2001; Ling and Willis 2000). Most details of this species’ ecology match those of other species discussed above. Neochanna diversus grows to about 160 mm, and has quite small eggs, fecundity is quite high, with one to a few thousand eggs. Larvae have been observed from autumn through winter, which is indicative of spawning season.

This mudﬁsh is widespread from the Waikato region to near North Cape at New Zealand’s northern extremity (Fig. 70, ·—arrow 1). The distribution of N. diversus in northern New Zealand has clearly been drastically reduced by wetland drainage, threatening the species’ survival there. An example is the virtually complete loss of the large Hikurangi Swamp, inland from Whangarei, over the past 20–25 years (Fig. 70—arrow 3), where this species was widespread in the 1960s. Gleeson et al. (1999b) observed N. diversus habitats tended to be small

fragments of old kauri (Agathis australis —f. Araucariaceae) forest wetlands, with the ﬁsh surviving in holes left by kauri gum (‘‘amber’’) diggers from the early 20th century (and see also McDowall 1970, 1990b); they thought that this made the Northland populations vulnerable to population/genetic bottlenecks, raising their conservation risk compared with those in larger areas of wetland in the Waikato and Hauraki Plains, further south—even though most of the wetlands in the latter area have been lost to farm development.

A recent and developing impact on N. diversus has been rapid spread of the poeciliid, Gambusia afﬁnis, which is long known to have adverse impacts in ﬁsh communities (Myers 1965; Arthington 1989, 1991; Rowe 1998). Barrier and Hicks (1994) investigated interactions between gambusia and N. diversus in captivity, and found that N. diversus was increasingly able to compete with gambusia as the mudﬁsh grew from fry to adults. Adult N. diversus were found to be aggressive towards gambusia, though it cannot be assumed that N. diversus avoids/is unaffected by the well-known ﬁn-nipping and other damaging impacts of G. afﬁnis (Myers 1965; Rowe 1998). Barrier and Hicks (1994) regarded three factors as facilitating coexistence of the two species:

- 1. N. diversus reproduces in winter so that highest abundances of its larvae are when G. afﬁnis numbers are least;
- 2. N. diversus can survive habitat dewatering by aestivation, whereas G. afﬁnis cannot, so that gambusia is excluded from some mudﬁsh habitats, or has to reinvade them when water returns; and
- 3. N. diversus is largely nocturnal, whereas G. afﬁnis is diurnal, so that there may be temporal separation during feeding (though larval and juvenile mudﬁsh are diurnal).

None of these factors excludes impacts of gambusia on N. diversus, and Ling and Willis (2000) presented evidence that gambusia ‘‘significantly affected...growth and condition’’, particularly ‘‘in the smallest’’ N. diversus. Ling (2001) went rather further, stating that gambusia ‘‘eat mudﬁsh fry and will attack adults, although the

nocturnal habits of mudﬁsh will protect them from day-active’’ gambusia. Ling and Willis (2000) concluded that gambusia constitutes ‘‘a clear threat to [N. diversus] recruitment in wetland margins but probably do[es] not impact on adults, which seem to prefer the seasonally dry centres of sedge wetlands’’. Gleeson et al. (1999) also expressed concerns, asserting that long-term survival of N. diversus, as for all species of Neochanna is potentially threatened by invasion of habitats by gambusia.

The goals and objectives for a recovery plan for New Zealand species of Neochanna were listed above (N. burrowsius) (and see Barrier 2003).

CONSERVATION RANKING: Neochanna diversus has long been a focus for conservation concern. It was ranked as Indeterminate by IUCN Red Data Book (Fishes) (Miller 1977), and also in the New Zealand Red Data Book (Williams and Given 1981). The New Zealand Department of Conservation listings varied from Category B (second priority) (Molloy and Davis 1992), to Category C (third priority) (Tisdall 1994), and ﬁnally Gradual Decline (Hitchmough 2002) (which Barrier (2003) interpreted as meaning ‘‘chronically’’ threatened. IUCN classed this species as Data Deﬁcient (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006).

Neochanna heleios: a newly described species from far northern New Zealand: The recent description of N. heleios (Fig. 67E), from a small area within the much broader range of the long recognised N. diversus in Northland (Ling and Gleeson 2001) (Fig. 70, m—arrow 2), reveals the extent to which conservation protocols for some of New Zealand’s freshwater ﬁsh have been in the ‘uninformed ignorance’ phase. With recognition of an additional species in Northland, Gleeson et al. (1999) called for additional exploration of the area as the two then known populations were both in sites under stress, with proposals for ‘development’. Additional exploration (M. McGlynn, pers. comm.) identiﬁed numerous additional sites over a substantially wider area than envisaged by Gleeson et al. (1999) and Ling and Gleeson (2001), somewhat alleviating conservation concerns.

Even less is known about this species than about other species of Neochanna, partly owing to its recent description, but it can safely be assumed that most of the issues discussed, above, with regard to the ecology, life history and habitat preferences of other Neochanna species, apply to N. heleios. In as much as gambusia is also widely present in habitats of N. heleios, similar harmful interactions between the two species are likely, and are of greater concern because of this mudﬁsh’s more limited geographical range (Fig. 70—arrow 2).

The recovery plan for New Zealand Neochanna in Northland (Barrier 2003) clearly identiﬁed impacts of gambusia as an explicit threat, and speciﬁed as signiﬁcant research priorities, the investigation of impacts of gambusia on ‘‘mudﬁsh population structure and long term health through controlled ﬁeld trials, and monitoring current invasion sites’’. Barrier (2003) also recommended the development of techniques for excluding and eradicating gambusia relevant to conservation of N. heleios.

The goals and objectives for a recovery plan for New Zealand species of Neochanna were listed above (see N. burrowsius) (and see Barrier 2003).

CONSERVATION RANKING: Hitchmough (2002) ranked N. heleios as Nationally Endangered, which (Barrier 2003) interpreted as ‘‘acutely’’ threatened. There is no IUCN listing of this species (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006).

An endemic Chatham Island species of Neochanna: Neochanna rekohua (Fig. 67F) was described by Mitchell (1995) as a species of Galaxias. However, his description was based on juveniles only ca. 75 mm long. Collection of additional material from both type locality and another lake nearby, as well as studies on the species’ osteology (McDowall 2004a) and genetics (Waters and McDowall 2005) show it to have closest afﬁnities with the Australian and New Zealand species of Neochanna. This material shows that N. rekohua grows to at least 175 mm in length.

Neochanna rekohua is known only from two small lakes in isolated and largely unmodiﬁed scrub and wetland in the south of the remote Chatham Islands, ca. 800 km east of mainland New Zealand (Fig. 70, w—arrow 7) (McDowall 2004a). Habitats there are undisturbed by either human development or introduced exotic species, and for as long as this remains true, the species is unlikely to be threatened, other than by vulnerability that arises from its restricted distribution. The greatest threat may be from ﬁre in vegetation that surrounds the small lakes. This species may eventually be found to be present more widely than the two known lakes,

- as several additional nearby lakes on Chatham Island have never been examined for their freshwater ﬁsh faunas.

CONSERVATION RANKING: This species description (Mitchell 1995) was too recent for some rankings for threat (Miller 1977; Williams and Given 1981; Molloy and Davis 1992; Tisdall 1994). Habitats occupied by N. rekohua are virtually unmodiﬁed, but involve just two small lakes, and for this reason the New Zealand Department of Conservation has listed it as Range Restricted (Hitchmough 2002). The 1996 IUCN Red List (Baillie and Groombridge 1996) listed it (in the genus Galaxias) as Vulnerable, as did the 2004 listing (IUCN 2006).

The Tasmanian species of Paragalaxias

Four species of Paragalaxias (Fig. 71) comprise a distinctive galaxiid radiation in lakes of the high elevation central plateau of Tasmania (Fig. 72). They differ from other galaxiids in having a dorsal ﬁn that is high on the back (a little behind the level of the pelvic ﬁns) and they lack an adipose ﬁn. Paragalaxias species have never been found in ﬂowing waters, and are amongst the relatively few galaxiids that are so strongly linked to lacustrine habitats (McDowall 1998c).

Paragalaxias dissimilis in Great Lake, central Tasmania: Paragalaxias dissimilis (Fig. 71A) is a small species growing to ca. 75 mm that by, day, is usually cryptic amongst substrate boulders, but

- at night it emerges to forage in midwater (unlike

- Fig. 71 The four Tasmanian species of Paragalaxias: (A) Shannon paragalaxias, P. dissimilis (reaches >75 mm); (B) Western paragalaxias P. julianus (>100 mm); (C) Arthurs paragalaxias, P. mesotes (>80 mm); (D) Great Lake paragalaxias, P. eleotroides (>55 mm)

- Fig. 72 Distributions in central, sub-montane Tasmania of the four species of Paragalaxias

P. eleotroides which is distinctly benthic in habit (see below; Fulton 1982; McDowall and Fulton

- 1996). Its life cycle is entirely lacustrine (Fulton

1982) and it spawns in summer, laying only about 100 largish eggs (ca. 2 mm diameter). Larval and early juvenile life is in the lakes’ pelagial zone. Otherwise little is known about its reproduction. It is found primarily in Great Lake, but has spread into Shannon and Penstock Lagoons, which are artiﬁcial impoundments of the Derwent River system downstream of Great Lake (Fig. 72, m), and is present down to about 7 m depth in the lakes.

Though these submontane lake habitats are largely unimpacted by human development (rural or urban), P. dissimilis may face adverse impacts from lake level ﬂuctuations which may exceed 21 m in Great Lake. This could make it vulnerable both with regards to habitats occupied by adults, and possibly also dewatering of egg masses (its spawning habitat is undiscovered). Major draw-down of lake levels may also seriously impact upon larval survival.

Frankenberg (1966) long ago expressed serious concerns for the future of a population now known as P. dissimilis (he called it P. shannonensis), thinking it possibly ‘‘already too late [as] a recent visit to the type (and only) locality [Shannon River] failed to reveal any specimens’’. However, the species has since been found in Great Lake, Shannon Lagoon, Penstock Lagoon (McDowall and Fulton 1978; McDowall and Frankenberg 1981) and, most recently, Allen

et al. (2002) described P. dissimilis as ‘‘relatively abundant’’.

Paragalaxias dissimilis faces potential threat from predation by introduced predators, principally brown and rainbow trout, and possibly also European perch, and it appears that P. dissimilis is a component in the diet of at least the trout species. Paragalaxias dissimilis also shares Great Lake with P. eleotroides (Fig. 71D), as well as two other native galaxiids, G. truttaceus and G. brevipinnis, that could prey upon it, perhaps especially when P. dissimilis is a juvenile. However, P. dissimilis has shared Great Lake with these salmonids for more than 100 years and the two Galaxias species probably for much longer, and yet remains common. Davies and Fulton

- (1987), though they found P. dissimilis to be ‘‘common elements in the diet of brown trout... [and] taken quite widely’’, thought that ‘‘from a conservation point of view, the populations are in a healthy state and there is no concern that [they] are in any way at risk from trout predation’’. Nevertheless, predation by these salmonids has been mentioned as one possible cause of decline (Anon. n.d.), and trout predation may be limiting Paragalaxias abundance.

be captured, by day, from beneath the same or nearby rocks on the lake bed, though the two species clearly partition the habitat available. Whereas P. dissimilis is a distinctly midwater foraging species (Fig. 71A), P. eleotroides (Fig. 71D), is distinctly benthic (McDowall 1998c), and is a substrate forager (Fulton 1982; McDowall and Fulton 1996). Davies and Fulton (1987) reported that P. eleotroides tends to be present in deeper parts of Great Lake especially among weed beds (Fig. 72, ), though later studies by the Tasmanian Threatened Species Unit (2005) do not support this view. Being such a small species, fecundity is low, with up to about 150 eggs spawned in spring (Fulton 1982; McDowall and Fulton 1996). Larvae are pelagic in the open waters of the lake.

As was stated, above, for P. dissimilis, P. eleotroides also shares Great Lake with populations of Galaxias brevipinnis, and G. truttaceus, as well as brown and rainbow trout and European perch, and has done so, at least with the trout, for over 100 years. And though it appears that P. eleotroides is a component in the diet of the two trout species, Allen et al. (2002) described it as ‘‘common despite the presence of introduced trout’’.

CONSERVATION RANKING: Paragalaxias dissimilis was ranked Vulnerable under the Tasmanian Threatened Species Protection Act, 1995 (Jackson

- 2000d) and it has retained this ranking in the most recent Tasmanian evaluation (Threatened Species Unit 2005). The Australian Society for Fish Biology’s Threatened Species Committee initially listed it as Data Deﬁcient (Crook 2001) despite some quite substantial ecological study (Fulton

- 1982); a more recent review by the Australian Society for Fish Biology listed it as Endangered, primarily owing to its limited geographical range (Crook and Pogonoski 2003). It has not been listed by IUCN as in any way threatened (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006).

CONSERVATION RANKING: As with P. dissimilis, P. eleotroides was ranked Vulnerable under Tasmania’s Threatened Species Protection Act, 1995 (Jackson 2000d). The Threatened Species Committee of the Australian Society for Fish Biology initially listed P. eleotroides as Data Deﬁcient (Crook 2001) despite the study of Fulton (1982), and the 2003 list of the Australian Society for Fish Biology ranked it as Endangered, primarily owing to its geographical range being limited to Great Lake and the associated lagoons, downstream (Crook and Pogonoski 2003). This species is not listed by IUCN (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006).

Paragalaxias eleotroides, also in Great Lake: Paragalaxias eleotroides shares Great Lake, Shannon and Penstock Lagoons with P. dissimilis, and is the smallest species in the genus, growing to ca. 60 mm. The two species can

Paragalaxias julianus in the western lakes of upland Tasmania: Paragalaxias julianus (Fig. 71B) occupies a series of rather more remote lakes in the west of the Tasmanian central plateau (Fig. 72, d). Surveys published by the Tasmanian Threatened

Species Unit (2005) have shown it to be widespread in the Western Lakes, northwest of Great Lake. It grows larger than the other Paragalaxias species, reaching ca. 100 mm length, but its ecology and life history are virtually unstudied Fulton

- (1982), included just a few comments in his study that primarily addressed the life histories and ecology of the two Paragalaxias species in Great Lake—P. dissimilis and P. eleotroides discussed above). Paragalaxias julianus is a benthic, cryptic species found by day amongst substrate boulders of lake shallows, though it seems less explicitly benthic than P. eleotroides. Larvae are pelagic in lakes (Threatened Species Unit 2005). Little is otherwise known about it, though clearly it spends its entire life in the lakes. Given the species’ small size, fecundity is likely to be low—a few hundred eggs at most.

Paragalaxias julianus was described by Allen et al. (2002) as being ‘‘locally abundant’’, again despite presence in its habitats of brown and rainbow trout, as well as G. brevipinnis and G. truttaceus. Both adults and larvae of P. julianus are apparently taken as prey (Threatened Species Unit 2005). The remoteness of lakes that this species occupies somewhat protects P. julianus from adverse human activities apart, that is, from introductions of exotic species.

The Tasmanian Threatened Species Unit

- (2005) expressed concerns about trout predation on P. julianus, considered that all populations and habitats within the species’ range needed protective management, suggested a need to ﬁnd trout free lakes, and that careful management of trout populations was needed to protect this species from predation. However, as with other Paragalaxias, P. julianus has been exposed to trout predation for many years.

CONSERVATION RANKING: The Tasmanians classed P. julianus as Rare under that State’s Threatened Species Protection Act, 1995 (Jackson 2000d), and identiﬁed threats from trout predation and water level ﬂuctuations, but little seems to be known. Conservation concerns discussed in a later review suggested elevation of ranking to Vulnerable (Threatened Species Unit 2005), additional concerns including limited distribution,

predation by introduced species (brown and rainbow trout and European perch), and changes in habitat quality. The Threatened Fishes Committee of the Australian Society for Fish Biology (Crook and Pogonoski 2003) listed this species as Endangered. There is no IUCN listing of this species (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006).

Paragalaxias mesotes in two upland Tasmanian lakes: The fourth species of Paragalaxias in upland Tasmania, P. mesotes (Fig. 72 C), is found in Woods and Arthurs Lakes (Fig. 72,w), and grows to ca. 80 mm. Little is known about it (though snippets of information are available in Fulton 1982). Its habitat is primarily among rocky and vegetated shores around the shallows of the lakes, but it is known down to a depth of 4–5 m (Threatened Species Unit 2005); and the entire life cycle is completed in the lakes.

Paragalaxias mesotes shares the two lakes with G. tanycephalus, and it is of interest that the two galaxiids have the same distributions, being found in only these lakes. As well, brown and rainbow trout are present, and some trout predation impacts seem evident. Crook (2001) and the Tasmanian Threatened Species Unit (2005) both noted that P. mesotes had not been seen in Woods Lake during several previous years of electric ﬁshing monitoring, and additional sampling using ﬁne-meshed fyke nets had also failed to reveal any P. mesotes there—although some were caught using that gear in Arthurs Lake. It was concluded that P. mesotes may have disappeared from Woods Lake at some time between 1988 and 1998. Recall that Woods Lake is clear, whereas Arthurs Lake is more turbid (see discussion of these lakes in relation to the status of G. tanycephalus, see above). Possibly the clarity of Woods Lake makes P. mesotes more vulnerable to trout predation. There may be adverse impacts on P. mesotes from lake draw-down (both lakes are used for hydro-electricity generation, with shore habitat becoming dewatered at low levels). Crook and Pogonoski (2002) alluded to reintroduction of the species into Woods Lake using stock from Arthurs Lake, with 174 ﬁsh being transferred. What had changed to make it possible for the translocated ﬁsh to survive in

Woods Lake any better than the previously resident stocks that had disappeared, is unclear, as are the results of the transfer.

CONSERVATION RANKING: Jackson (2001b) described P. mesotes as ‘‘threatened due to the very limited distributions, and threat from trout predation and habitat changes in these hydromanaged lakes’’, but the Tasmanian Inland Fisheries Service later considered this species to be Endangered under both the Tasmanian Threatened Species Protection Act and the Environmental Protection and Biodiversity Conservation Act (Threatened Species Unit 2005). The Australian Society for Fish Biology Threatened Species Committee thought that ‘‘reasons for its disappearance [were] unclear, but [that they] may relate to effects of water level or water quality management’’; the level of threat classiﬁcation of P. mesotes was raised from Data deﬁcient (Crook

- 2001) to Endangered (Crook and Pogonoski 2002,

- 2003). IUCN has it as a cause for conservation concern and listed it as Vulnerable (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006).

Two related diminutive galaxiid genera—Galaxiella in Australia and Brachygalaxias in Chile

Despite some long-lasting debate about how Galaxiella and Brachygalaxias are related to one another (EOG Scott 1971; McDowall 1973c), the latest information suggests that they share a closest common ancestry, relative to other galaxiids (McDowall and Waters 2004), possibly being associated with Galaxias platei from Patagonian South America and G. zebratus from Africa (Waters et al. 2002b). There are three diminutive species of Galaxiella in southern Australia (Fig. 73), and (probably) two species of Brachygalaxias in central/southern Chile.

Galaxiella pusilla in southeastern Australia and northern Tasmania: Galaxiella pusilla is sexually dimorphic, with males growing to ca. 30 mm and having a bright orange lateral longitudinal stripe, whereas females reach 40 mm, and lack the orange stripe. It lives in wetlands and small associated

- Fig. 73 The three species of Galaxiella: (A) Eastern little galaxias, G. pusilla (reaches ca. 40 mm); (B) Black stripe minnow, G. nigrostriata (>35 mm); (C) Mud minnow, G. munda (>50 mm)

- Fig. 74 Distribution of eastern little galaxias, Galaxiella pusilla, in southeastern Australia and Tasmania

swampy creeks and stream that are sensitive to lowered water tables, and which may become dewatered during droughts. Habitats are often sensitive to wetland drainage. The onset of ﬂooding facilitates dispersal to new habitats.

The distribution of G. pusilla is southern Victoria, extending west as far as eastern South Australia, and also Tasmania, in both the northeast and north-west (Fig. 74, d)—thus it is found on either side of Bass Strait.

This semi-solitary, midwater-swimming species probably has a largely annual life cycle, with occasional individuals surviving for two years

(Romanowski 2004). As a result, substantial seasonal ﬂuctuations in abundance are likely, with few adults around soon after autumn–winter spawning. Females are mostly lost from populations by July, males by November (Romanowski

- 2004). Fecundity is low, commensurate with the small size of adult females (66–247 ova—Humphries 1986), and the eggs are deposited singly on aquatic vegetation.

Galaxiella pusilla seems able to survive through dewatering of habitat by aestivation/ dormancy of some life stage, though precisely how remains undiscovered (Backhouse 1983; Beck 1985; Humphries 1986; Romanowski 2004) and needs clariﬁcation. Humphries (1986) discounted suggestions of McDowall and Frankenberg (1981) that G. pusilla may be able to survive droughts by having desiccation-resistant eggs, as adults were found soon after water returned to dried-up habitats, and Humphries’ ﬁndings suggest survival of drought by adults, and this has been conﬁrmed by Romanowski (2004). It may survive by taking refuge down crayﬁsh burrows that extend down to the water table, or it may aestivate in small depressions beneath rocks or logs, in moist mud (Threatened Species Unit

- 2005), or in the moisture beneath a dehydrated silt pan (Romanowski 2004). This species’ ability to survive in the presence of Gambusia holbrooki may relate to Galaxiella pusilla’s tolerances of lowered temperatures—numbers of G. pusilla declined dramatically when Gambusia invaded the habitat, but recovered when temperatures dropped and numbers of Gambusia were reduced.

Galaxiella pusilla is a cause for some conservation concern (Koster 2003). Tucceri (2005) described one area of habitat for this species as ‘‘one of the fastest growing residential and commercial areas in the greater Melbourne region’’. Tasmania’s Threatened Species Unit (2005) reported the species from >20 sites, but its distribution there is patchy. A host of adverse activities may apply, including:

- 1. Fragmentation and loss of habitats as a result of lowered water tables and wetland drainage;
- 2. Removal of aquatic vegetation;
- 3. Forestry;

- 4. Water abstraction;
- 5. Urbanisation;
- 6. Ploughing of paddocks; and
- 7. Stock management including fouling and trampling of habitats by cattle.

Vulnerability may be due in part to habitats often being small areas of shallow wetland that are very easily perturbed, or drained to exploit their fertile soils. Not surprisingly, some known Tasmanian habitats have been lost to wetland drainage. There is concern that most sites are on private property, accentuating the need for local publicity about the species’ values and conservation. There are concerns in Tasmania, too, about arrival of Gambusia holbrooki, and the Threatened Species Unit (2005) and Lynch (2005) mentioned the need to eradicate this invasive species, which has been addressed at some sites (Wisniewski 2005). Additional measures include rehabilitation of degraded habitats.

Much the same threats are cited for Victorian populations of Galaxiella pusilla (Koehn and O’Connor 1990; Allen et al. 2002; Romanowski 2004), though the higher human populations may accentuate the threat. Romanowski (2004) suggested that cold winter temperatures may favour survival of G. pusilla by reducing abundance of Gambusia holbrooki, which might otherwise share its habitat and tend to dominate. He also speculated that habitat dewatering may provide control of potential predators like dragonﬂy nymphs and notonectids. A largely annual life cycle (Romanowski 2004) and low fecundity render G. pusilla vulnerable to very rapid population decline, and this is undoubtedly not helped by its habit of living in temporary wetlands and creeks. Littlejohn (2001) called this species ‘‘the little Aussie Battler’’ owing to its ability to survive through widespread and intensive anthropogenic habitat perturbation! Hammer and Horne (2004) described how G. pusilla has declined dramatically in southeastern South Australia, and throughout its range ‘‘with massive loss of wetland habitats (>90%) associated with [human] settlement and drainage’’. They examined the species’ potential to shift into karst geological formations (cave systems in limestone rocks through which streams have subterranean penetration), and drew attention to

environmental effects of excavation, waste disposal and introduction of such alien ﬁsh as brown trout and European perch. They summarised that ‘‘The challenge to protect these systems is significant but not insurmountable, and is worthwhile’’. Captive reproduction is evidently easy to achieve, though Victorian State legislation, designed to protectspecies meant that retention of populations in small ponds became unlawful without a special permit, and such permits proved almost impossible to obtain (Romanowski 2004): sometimes conservation legislation can confound its own goals. Proposals to clear an area of wetland south of Melbourne and to conﬁne ﬂows to underground pipes threatened a signiﬁcant population, leading to translocation of the population to a less threatened site, nearby, and drainage from the wetland modiﬁed to ensure maintenance of water in the habitat, providing 800 m of wetland stream that will sustain a population of G. pusilla (Tucceri

- 2005). The Tasmanian Threatened Species Unit

(2005) mentioned occasional co-occurrence with trout in Tasmania, but that G. pusilla tended to inhabit shallow, weedy margins where co-occurrence with trout is unlikely. There are reports in Victoria (Jackson and Davies 1983) that G. pusilla and brown trout co-occur in some swampy areas, but the known behaviour and predatory habits of brown trout would suggest that this coexistence is likely to be possible only in very complexly structured habitats, perhaps with heavy growths of aquatic macrophytes or instream debris that provide a refuge for the diminutive, mid-water, slow-swimming, and open-living G. pusilla. Alternatively, perhaps the ability of the latter to aestivate enables it to form dense populations in areas where trout densities are kept low by periodic habitat dewatering. Again, there is little deﬁnitive information.

There have been suggestions that there is unrecognised taxonomic diversity among the Victorian and South Australian of populations Galaxiella pusilla (Littlejohn 2001), so that taxonomic studies are needed, and if Littlejohn’s suggestion proves to be correct it would raise the conservation threat owing to additional taxa inevitably having more limited geographical ranges.

CONSERVATION RANKING: Crook (2000), reporting on deliberations of the Australian Society for Fish Biology’s Threatened Fishes Committee, stated that surveys for G. pusilla in northwestern Tasmania had shown loss of habitats, and described the decline of this species as ‘‘death by a thousand cuts’’—as wetlands are progressively drained, trampled, and grazed by cattle, or are inundated following construction of farm dams. This species is clearly at least locally threatened in Tasmania, where it has been included in a renewed Tasmanian Galaxiidae Recovery Plan and, according to the Tasmanian Threatened Species Unit (2005), qualiﬁes to be ranked as Rare under the Tasmanian Threatened Species Act, Vulnerable under provisions of the Environmental Protection and Biodiversity Conservation Act, and a threatened species under the Victorian State Government Flora and Fauna Guarantee Act (1988) (Tucceri

- 2005). Galaxiella pusilla is classed as Vulnerable in the rankings of the Threatened Species Committee of the Australian Society for Fish Biology (Crook and Pogonoski 2002, 2003) and also by IUCN (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN
- 2006).

Fig. 75 Distributions of black stripe minnow, Galaxiella nigrostriata (m), and mud minnow, G. munda ( ) in southwestern Australia (adapted from Morgan et al. 1998)

Galaxiella nigrostriata in Western Australia: Galaxiella nigrostriata (Fig. 73 B) closely resembles, and was formerly thought to be a subspecies of, G. pusilla (EOG Scott 1971). However there is little doubt that it is a distinct taxon (McDowall 1978c; McDowall and Frankenberg 1981; Allen et al. 2002; McDowall and Waters 2004). Unlike G. pusilla, it seems that G. nigrostriata is not sexually dimorphic (Kimberley Smith, pers. comm.). It is small (to ca. 40 mm) and is probably also an annual species. It frequents wetlands and streams of the ‘‘southern acid peat ﬂats...a lowlying area of seasonally inundated wetlands in the lower southwest’’ (Pusey and Bradshaw 1996). There, it forages for food in the water column and from surface waters, with terrestrial insects being important in their diet (Pusey and Bradshaw 1996). It has deeply fragmented range (Fig. 75, m) with a few populations north of Perth, occasional areas south of Perth along the Swan Coastal Plain in western fringes of Western Australia, and then numerous sites across southern Western Australia eastwards to a little beyond Albany, this latter area being the species’ core distribution (Smith et al.

- 2002a) (Fig. 75). Absence of this species and, equally, absence of the congeneric G. munda, from much of the western fringe of Western Australia, might suggest that there has been extirpation from a former, broader range around and south of Perth (Smith et al. 2002b).

Galaxiella nigrostriata occupies habitats that are ephemeral, and which tend to dry up in summer, suggesting that this species, like G. pusilla, is capable of aestivating. Gill and Morgan (1996) reckoned that G. nigrostriata can remain ‘‘buried for extensive periods’’, but provided no explicit data on this behaviour. Smith et al. (2002a) found well-grown adults in G. nigrostriata populations immediately after water was restored.

The water in which G. nigrostriata lives is usually darkly tannin stained and acid (pH may be as low as 3—Allen et al. 2002a). Smith et al. (2002a) found that G. nigrostriata is highly sensitive to elevated water temperatures, and this might seem a fatal ﬂaw in its ecology in an area of high sunshine and small, exposed, deeply tannin-stained water bodies. However, they discovered several fascinating features of how this

little ﬁsh uses its habitat. One is that the highly tannin stained water, in the pools where it lives, develops very strong temperature stratiﬁcation during hot days, with bottom water much cooler than upper layers. They determined the preferred water temperature to be ca. 14.5 C, but that the waters in which it is found may be as high as 26 C at the surface. The ‘‘extremely dark ...water’’ of their study habitat ‘‘facilitates the development of a strong thermal stratiﬁcation on hot days’’ (Smith et al. 2002a). Further, they found cool ground water upwelling at the bottoms of the pools, and that it is in these deeper, cooler waters that the ﬁsh live when surface water temperatures are elevated. Galaxiella nigrostriata, according to Smith et al. (2002a), is unable to ‘‘tolerate for more than a few seconds temperatures >26 C’’, and when they netted ﬁsh from cooler bottom waters of these pools, they reportedly died ‘‘instantly ... [when] lifted through the warm epilimnion’’ (Smith et al. 2002a). Morgan (2003) drew attention to autumn spawning in G. nigrostriata providing the ﬁsh with cooler temperatures for larval and juvenile development.

Smith et al. (2002b) found no evidence for genetic divergence between a population north of Perth and others across the south coast of Western Australia, despite their separation by over 350 km, and they discussed the prospect of recent wide dispersion by G. nigrostriata and as well as other small Western Australian freshwater ﬁshes.

McKay (1977) suspected that European perch were guilty of causing declines in the population of G. nigrostriata but published no supporting evidence. Gill et al. (1999) concluded that the ephemeral nature of at least some habitats occupied by G. nigrostriata resulted in exclusion of gambusia, and thought that this provided protection from them. Given the capacity of gambusia to proliferate in congenial conditions, and especially if seasonal dry periods do not develop, the validity of this conclusion may need to be re-visited in a long-term strategy for survival of G. nigrostriata. It has been suggested that G. nigrostriata is unlikely to be threatened by salmonids ‘‘owing to habitat differences’’ (Anon 2002). Certainly, salmonids would be no more tolerant of elevated temperatures than G. nigrostriata is, and probably would have lesser

ability to ﬁnd cool refuges in deeper water owing to trout being much bigger ﬁsh than G. nigrostriata. Morgan et al. (2002) thought that the European perch ‘‘may be, or has the potential to become, a major factor contributing to the loss or reduction of G. nigrostriata’’.

Smith et al. (2002a) considered that populations of G. nigrostriata north of Perth were threatened by urban expansion, groundwater extraction and highway construction, and recommended that conservation management of their study site should include ‘‘maintenance of a vegetation buffer against wind mixing of the water’’ to ensure that the water continues to stratify on hot, sunny days. Landscape devegetation may have distinctive conservation impacts on this species.

CONSERVATION RANKING: The Australian Society for Fish Biology ranked G. nigrostriata as Lower Risk—Near Threatened (Crook and Pogonoski

- 2003), and the same rank was assigned to by IUCN (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN

- 2006).

Galaxiella munda in Western Australia: This species (Fig. 73C) was ﬁrst recognised much more recently than the other species of Galaxiella (McDowall 1978d), and is rather less well known. It grows somewhat larger (to ca. 50 mm—McDowall and Frankenberg 1981). However, like other Galaxiella species, G. munda appears to have an annual life cycle, and seems to be a multiple spawner, spawning small numbers of eggs over a protracted period in winter and early spring (June–October) (Pen et al. 1991; Allen et al. 2002). It inhabits gently ﬂowing waters (Allen et al. 2002—compared with generally still waters in which other Galaxiella species live), and it appears unable to aestivate when habitat dries up. The distribution of G. munda is much the same as that of G. nigrostriata (see above), except that no populations are known from the western-coastal fringes of Western Australia south of Perth (Smith et al. 2002a) (Fig. 75, ).

Morgan et al. (1996) expressed concerns that native ﬁshes in Western Australia, including G. munda, tend to be absent in many water bodies where Gambusia holbrooki was present; more-

over, with gambusia present other ﬁsh often exhibited ﬁn damage (as often been attributed to gambusia—Myers 1965; Rowe 1998). Gill and Morgan (1997) reported Galaxiella munda to be ‘‘rare throughout most of its distribution, but...occasionally locally abundant’’, and Morgan et al. (1998) attributed ‘‘virtual’’ extirpation of one population of G. munda to the ‘‘combined effects of habitat alteration, through the construction of dams, and the introductions of [European perch], trout species and G. holbrooki’’, though the evidence they presented was subjective and circumstantial. Morgan et al. (2002) later explored the potential role of European perch in native ﬁsh conservation in Western Australia, generally, and considered that it ‘‘may be, or has the potential to become, a major factor contributing to the loss or reduction of some populations of native ﬁsh’’. They reported that ‘‘large populations’’ of G. munda (and two other ﬁsh species) in Big Brook Dam had essentially disappeared since perch had been introduced; G. munda had formerly been common enough for Pen et al. (1991) to undertake a detailed study of the species’ biology in that area (reproductive ecology, growth and diet). When Morgan et al. (2002) sampled the location in 1997–1999, by which time European perch had become abundant, they found no G. munda above the dam and collected only a single specimen below it. Both brown and rainbow trout were also present, but rarer than perch. Large perch were observed to mainly eat ﬁsh, mostly smaller perch and gambusia. Though they were not observed to consume G. munda, this could simply have been that they had consumed them all and the galaxiid was virtually extirpated. Morgan et al. (2002) attributed disappearance of G. munda to ‘‘a combination of both abiotic (habitat change through creation of the dam) and biotic (predation and/or competition from feral ﬁsh) factors [which] may be responsible for the demise of the native freshwater ﬁsh fauna in the headwaters of Big Brook...It is not possible, however, to discount the presence of the predatory S. trutta and O. mykiss, or the agonistic G[ambusia] holbrooki, which is known to cause ﬁn damage to the endemic ﬁshes of southwestern Australia’’.

Thus Morgan et al.’s (2002) results conﬁrmed prognostications by Pen and Potter (1992) that

under certain conditions, perch threaten conservation of native ﬁsh in southwestern Western Australia, highlighting the adverse consequences of introducing alien species.

Because Galaxiella munda typically occupies ﬂowing streams, a recent analysis (Anon 2002) described it as at high ‘‘risk of extinction’’ by salmonids with ‘‘the small adult size that [G. munda] attains, the similarity in habitat preferences between the two species, and the relatively restricted distribution of this species within the South-West’’ of Western Australia, making it vulnerable to negative impacts.

Gill and Morgan (1996, 1997) drew attention to introduction of several exotic ﬁsh species (gambusia, perch, rainbow and brown trout), as well as Australian endemics such as Macquaria ambigua (Percichthyidae) and Bidyanus bidyanus (Terapontidae) that had been translocated from eastern Australia. They thought, moreover, that introduction of these ‘‘large and piscivorous eastern Australian species, which were well adapted to similar harsh environments, probably posed the greatest threat to small endemic ﬁsh of south-western Australia’’.

CONSERVATION RANKING: Galaxiella munda was listed as Lower Risk—Near Threatened by the Australian Society for Fish Biology (Crook and Pogonoski 2002, 2003) and also by IUCN (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006). Threats, again, derive from agricultural and forestry development, and degradation and loss of wetland habitats.

Brachygalaxias: one or two species in Chile?: A Chilean endemic genus Brachygalaxias (Fig. 76) is distributed mostly at low elevations in central Chile (Fig. 77). Its taxonomy is debated as,

Fig. 76 The diminutive Chilean Brachygalaxias bullocki (reaches >55 mm)

although Berra et al. (1995) argued that a second nominal species, B. gothei (Busse 1982), is a junior synonym of B. bullocki, Cuevas et al. (1999) considered that B. gothei is distinct, based, among other things, on differences in karyotypes and hybridisation experiments. There also seem to be differences in colouration between the two Chilean lineages (McDowall and Waters 2004). Campos et al. (1998) did not accept the synonymy of Berra et al. (1995), and further resolution of this question has signiﬁcant implications for the conservation of Chilean galaxioids. Brachygalaxias gothei has narrow range at the northern extremities of the pair (Fig. 77, m), while B. bullocki is much more widespread (Fig. 77, d).

Not much is known about the ecologies and life histories of these ﬁsh, but, like Galaxiella, species of Brachygalaxias are midwater swimmers that

Fig. 77 Distribution in central/southern Chile of: arrow m—Brachygalaxias gothei; and d—B. bullocki

are found in wetland margins and small, gently ﬂowing streams. Brachygalaxias bullocki appears able to aestivate during periods of dewatering of its habitat (Fischer 1963), and in at least some lineages Chilean Brachygalaxias has marked longitudinal bright orange stripes. These ﬁsh are typically found in slow-moving weedy streams and around lake margins (McDowall 1971b; Campos 1972). They are tiny ﬁshes, reaching ca. 56 mm (McDowall 1971b). Fischer (1963) found the species seasonally present in a stream near Concepcion, in central Chile; juveniles appeared in his study population in the spring (October), and Fischer implied that renewal of the population was driven by overwintering eggs, though his data suggest that some ﬁsh also overwintered, emerging as adults in spring. I found spent ﬁsh in autumn (McDowall 1971b), and it appears that this species may live for up to two years. Campos (1972) found that spawning may also take place in winter, suggesting that it may occur across a 4 month period and it is possible that Fischer (1963) was wrong in suggesting overwintering by fertilised eggs. Campos (1972) reported that the eggs are laid individually on aquatic macrophytes (as takes place in Galaxiella—see above).

The relationship of Brachygalaxias to the Australian Galaxiella has been debated over the decades (EOG Scott 1971; McDowall 1973c), but recent work suggests that the two genera do share

a common ancestry (Waters et al. 2002b; McDowall and Waters 2004).

CONSERVATION RANKING: Campos et al (1998) classiﬁed B. bullocki as Data deﬁcient, expressed concern about reduced water quality, and listed B. gothei as Vulnerable owing to its restricted distribution. IUCN applied the same categories to both taxa (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006).

An endemic genus in New Caledonia?

The single species of galaxiid in New Caledonia, long known as Nesogalaxias neocaledonicus (Fig. 78), is the only galaxiid that can be described as sub-tropical—it occurs in Lac en Huit, a small lake at about 250 m altitude in southern New Caledonia (Fig. 79, d) which, from its elevation and southern position, is possibly the coolest freshwater ﬁsh habitat available on the island. Its generic classiﬁcation is in a state of ﬂux. McDowall (1968a) supported its assignment to

Fig. 78 The diminutive New Caledonian Nesogalaxias neocaledonicus (reaches >75 mm)

Fig. 79 Distribution of Nesogalaxias neocaledonicus in southern, submontane New Caledonia

Nesogalaxias by Whitley (1935), whereas Sere´t

- (1997) suggested that allocation inappropriate. Clarifying this question is not crucial to discussion of its conservation status—but it is important to recognise here that, whether placed in Nesogalaxias or Galaxias, the New Caledonian species is an unusual little ﬁsh (reaching ca. 75 mm) when viewed in the context of wider knowledge of the family. Its relationships among other galaxiids are presently uncertain, though Waters et al. (2002b) presented molecular data that placed it among some New Zealand species of Galaxias.

Nesogalaxias neocaledonicus is found in the lake shallows, and also in pools in streams near the lake, being semi-benthic and mostly cryptic within the substrate (which consists largely of bedrock with furrows and overhangs in Lac en Huit). Nothing is known about its life history, though it is clearly a lacustrine and non-migratory species, and might be guessed to prey on small aquatic insects and crustaceans in Lac en Huit.

Of present importance, the conservation of this species is a serious concern to New Caledonian conservation/environmental ofﬁcials (Boulet

- 1997). It is considered threatened from introduction of two exotic species of cichlid (Oreochromis mossambicus and Sarotheradon occidentalis) into Lac en Huit and also largemouth bass, all of which are described as predators ‘‘on the different [life] stages’’ of the galaxiid. Decline in N. neocaledonicus has also been attributed to deterioration of rivers ﬂowing into Lac en Huit (water quality) resulting from nickel mining and pollution (Keith 2002).

adipose ﬁn), known to reach ca. 360 mm (McDowall and Nakaya 1987), but like galaxiids, they lack scales. They seem likely to share a closest common ancestry (probably along with the Australian Lovettia sealii), among the Galaxiidae, the composite group being a likely sister taxon of the northern Osmeridae (McDowall 1969, 1984b; Johnson and Patterson 1996).

Patagonian peladilla/Falkland Islands zebra trout, Aplochiton zebra: Aplochiton zebra (Fig. 80A) is known widely through Patagonian South America (Fig. 81, d), and also on the Falkland Islands (McDowall 1971a; McDowall et al. 2001, 2005) (Figs. 81, 82). It is an open-living, semishoaling, midwater swimming species that is found in both gently ﬂowing pools and in lakes. It grows to about 284 mm in length (McDowall and Nakaya 1987). Very little has been published about this species’ reproduction and life history, though Eigenmann (1928) reported that it reproduces in autumn. It probably spawns large numbers of small eggs, in fresh water, and seems amphidromous, with marine larvae, the small juveniles returning to freshwater at ca. 50 mm length, during spring. However, detailed assessment of all these points is needed. There are also land-locked lacustrine (non-diadromous) populations of A. zebra in both Patagonia and the Falklands (McDowall 1971a; McDowall et al. 2001, 2005).

Pascual et al. (2002) commented on the introduction of salmonids in Patagonian Argentina and their potential effects on G. platei, and the

CONSERVATION RANKING: Knowledge of the conservation status of this ﬁsh is basically anecdotal, but Keith (2002) ranked it as Critically Endangered. Its has not been evaluated by IUCN (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006).

The genus Aplochiton in Patagonia and the Falkland Islands

The genus Aplochiton comprises only two recognised species from Patagonia and the Falkland Islands. Very little is known about these small to moderate-sized, trout like ﬁsh (complete with

Fig. 80 The two species of Aplochiton peladillo from Patagonia and the Falkland Islands: (A) A. zebra (reaches >280 mm); (B) A. taeniatus (>325 mm)

Fig. 81 Distributions of Aplochiton taeniatus (.) and A. zebra (d) in Patagonian South America

same may apply to A. zebra. They found Aplochiton particularly rare with various exotic species (primarily salmonids), whereas salmonids feed heavily on some native species, including A. zebra, where it still co-occurs with trout, large brown trout being the most piscivorous (Macchi et al. 1999; Pascual et al. 2002).

In the Falkland Islands (Malvinas) survival of this species is a matter of serious conservation concern—A. zebra is certainly very seriously threatened, there. Arrowsmith and Pentelow (1965), when enthusing about past introductions of brown trout to the Falklands, wrote of hopedfor introductions of Atlantic salmon, and stated rather blandly, and without apparent concern, that ‘‘The imported brown trout found [Aplochiton zebra] very much to their liking and they have virtually disappeared from all rivers where there are now brown trout’’. How true, and how sad!

The abundance and geographical range of Falklands Islands A. zebra has plummeted over the past 30–40 years (Simpson 1991; Strange 1992; Lapsley 1993; Oldﬁeld 1999), to the point where it is now widely absent across the islands where it was formerly abundant. McDowall et al. (2001, 2005) found it at only 17 of 146 sites sampled during the spring of 1999 (Fig. 82); moreover, A. zebra was collected at only two sites where introduced brown trout were also present (arrows in Fig. 82), and was generally found only in broad areas that brown trout had not yet reached. Brown trout, however, were found at 57 sites, widely across the islands. These results corroborated earlier and persisting views of local conservationists that decline in zebra trout abundance and contraction of its range are a consequence largely, if not totally, of introduction to the islands of brown trout, about 50 years ago (Strange 1992; Lapsley 1993).

Fig. 82 Largely complementary distributions in the Falkland Islands of: d—introduced Salmo trutta; and m—indigenous Aplochiton zebra (two sites arrowed are those where both species were found)

Though brown trout were released only locally in Falkland Island streams, and though it was originally thought that stocks shipped to the Falklands were of non-migratory brown trout (Nolan 1993; Lapsley 1993), it is abundantly clear that trout have spread naturally and rapidly through coastal seas around the islands, invading nearly all accessible, suitable streams, and they are probably still spreading. They must rapidly have become anadromous, as is common in brown trout, and have been migrating to sea to feed and grow. These sea-migratory ﬁsh support much of the angling effort at the Falklands on account of the large size the trout reach at sea. Their movements to sea result in faster growth, increased predatory behaviour, and increased fecundity, all of which are adverse to A. zebra populations. And clearly, as they have spread, the words of Arrowsmith and Pentelow (1965) have proved to be not only a ‘status report’ for the situation some 40 years ago, but ‘prophetic’ for ensuing decades with regard to the status of A. zebra (McDowall et al. 2001, 2005) (and it is perhaps fortunate that Atlantic salmon released there disappeared to sea and were never seen again, as is true of anadromous Atlantic salmon introductions, everywhere else—Clements 1988; McDowall 1990b, 1994a).

The future of A. zebra on the Falkland Islands is clearly in serious jeopardy. Because it seems to be amphidromous, with its larval and early juvenile life spent at sea (McDowall 1988), new cohorts are moving to and from the sea at regular times and ﬁxed life history stages. Thus, maintaining access for A. zebra migrating to and from the sea is a crucial element of population viability. This means that, in general, A. zebra is found only in streams and lakes it can invade from the sea, and it follows, from this, that any habitats accessible to upstreammigrating, juvenile A. zebra are equally accessible to sea-migratory brown trout as they, too, enter fresh water. If brown trout continue their spread, and eventually reach areas where there are still populations of A. zebra, then there will likely be continued, catchment by catchment extirpation of A. zebra across the Falkland Islands (McDowall

- et al. 2001). There are at least landlocked (non-diadro-

mous) populations of A. zebra on the Falklands

(e.g. Red Pond, in northeastern West Falkland—McDowall et al. 2001, 2005; McDowall and Pankhurst 2004). These provide some conservation ‘backup’ for this species in the Falklands. However, landlocked populations without their sea migrations do not represent the complete natural genetic and behavioural diversity of A. zebra. Thus, although better than nothing, landlocked populations fail to meet reasonable conservation objectives for the species on the Falklands. Local conservation concern has prompted total statutory protection of the species (Anon 1999).

What has happened so rapidly in the Falklands could be a foretaste of what is to come (or is already well progressed) for A. zebra in Patagonian Chile and Argentina, as various introduced salmonids become increasingly widespread there. As in many countries the passion for trout ﬁshing, and potential tourist income that can be earned from visiting anglers, seem likely to be leading to serious decline of indigenous ﬁshes in South America. Changes in the status of native ﬁsh populations, and particularly of A. zebra and its conservation status there, need to be watched with care. There is a realistic likelihood that now obvious events relating to A. zebra in the Falkland Islands may be taking place, perhaps at slower rate, but largely undocumented, across Patagonia.

CONSERVATION RANKING: Campos et al. (1998) classed A. zebra as in Danger of Extinction in heavily populated and developed northern provinces of Chile, and Vulnerable further south where habitat changes and human exploitation are less intensive. However, there are few data and not much is known. This species is regarded as Vulnerable in Argentina (Bello and Ubeda 1998), its decline there being attributed, at least in part, to impacts of introduced species, presumably various salmonids that are widespread in the montane lakes of Argentina (Navas 1987; Macchi et al. 1999) (primarily rainbow trout, brown trout and brook charr). Aplochiton zebra has not had explicit IUCN conservation rankings assigned to it (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006).

Another Patagonian peladilla, Aplochiton taeniatus: Aplochiton taeniatus: (Fig. 80B) is known only from Patagonian South America, where it is widespread (Fig. 81, .) and seems substantially less well known than A. zebra, but perhaps it is the clarity of events pertaining to A. zebra in the Falklands that have pointed clearly to that of A. zebra more generally. As for A. zebra, A. taeniatus is possibly amphidromous (McDowall

- 1988; McDowall and Nakaya 1987), and is likely to suffer the frailties of ﬁsh species subject to habitat invasions by introduced salmonids. Lacustrine populations are also known, and Campos

(1969), stated that this species spawns in winter (May–July) and produces 2,500–3,000 eggs 1–

- 2 mm in diameter (though he did not say whether these were mature eggs in the ovaries or were fertilised and water-hardened, nor how big the ﬁsh were).

Decline has been attributed to exploitation (no mention is made of impacts of salmonids, despite brown and rainbow trout being very widespread in southern Chile and Argentina) (Campos et al.

- 1998; Pascual et al. 2002). Documentation even of the geographical distribution of A. taeniatus is sparse, there is no quantitative, or even qualitative published information on abundance, and effects of trout remain unclear and little explored.

CONSERVATION RANKING: Aplochiton taeniatus is classiﬁed as Vulnerable in Argentina (Bello and Ubeda 1998), whereas in Chile (again as with A. zebra) it was described as Endangered in northern provinces and Vulnerable in the south. This species has not been ranked by IUCN (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006).

A third Chilean Aplochiton, A. marinus: Campos

- et al. (1998) recognised a third species of Aplochiton as distinct—A. marinus, described from central Chile by Eigenmann (1928). Despite this name having been long ago reduced to synonymy of A. taeniatus (McDowall 1971a) it continues to be recognised by some Chilean biologists, though without justiﬁcation. Retention seems to reﬂect history rather than biology. No one has published any information on the distribution, life history, or ecology of this species.

CONSERVATION CONCERN: With nothing known about this ﬁsh (uninformed ignorance) it was ranked as Data Deﬁcient by Campos et al. (1998), without further comment. IUCN accepted recognition of this species as valid, and also ranked it as Data Deﬁcient (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006).

Lovettia: an Australian aplochitonine

Lovettia sealii (Fig. 83) is a small galaxioid known widely from Tasmania (Fig. 84, d) (and also recently recorded in coastal Victoria—T. Raadik pers. comm.). It is placed in a monotypic genus that has long been regarded as allied to Aplochiton (McDowall 1969, 1971a, 1984b; Fink 1984; Williams 1996), and was included in Aplochitoninae by Johnson and Patterson (1996). This makes for some complex biogeography as Lovettia is present only in Australia, whereas Aplochiton is

- Fig. 83 The Tasmanian Lovettia sealii (reaches ca. 75 mm)

- Fig. 84 Tasmanian distribution of Tasmanian whitebait, Lovettia sealii (is recently found on mainland—T. Raadik, pers. comm.)

only in Patagonia and the Falklands (McDowall 1971a). The two genera are different in a multitude of ways, both morphologically and behaviourally/ecologically. Though they share some quite fundamental morphological attributes (McDowall 1969; Williams 1996), these may be attributed, at least partly, to very reduced osteology in Lovettia

- (McDowall 1969).

Lovettia sealii is anadromous, and no landlocked populations are known. The mature adults are only about 50–60 mm long and, during spring, it swarms into Tasmanian estuaries in huge shoals, to spawn. It once supported a valued ﬁshery. Mature adults are strikingly sexually dimorphic, the male having strongly developed nuptial tubercles, especially on lower surfaces of the head. As well, the male’s urinogenital apertures migrate forwards during maturation, to lie in the isthmus, below the gill openings (McDowall 1996c). The ecological signiﬁcance of this dimorphism to spawning behaviour is undetermined. Fecundity is low, as expected from such a small, slender ﬁsh, eggs numbering 150–200 eggs, up to about 350 in large females (Blackburn 1946; Fulton and Pavuk

- 1988). The adults spawn in lower reaches/upper tidal estuaries of rivers during spring, and then die, so the life cycle is semelparous, though Fulton and Pavuk (1988) reported occasional two year olds in the spawning populations. The eggs are deposited on woody debris and substrate boulders, where they develop and hatch after about three weeks, and the larvae move immediately to sea (Blackburn 1950; Fulton and Pavuk 1988). How far they move at sea is uncertain.
