Blackburn (1946) postulated that they do not move any great distance from the river mouths where they hatch, and Fulton and Pavuk (1988) wondered whether they are substantially estuarine, as genetic studies identiﬁed ﬁve separate stocks around Tasmanian coasts—suggesting that there is little broad-scale coastal/oceanic dispersal.

A Tasmanian ﬁshery for L. sealii (Fig. 85) dates back to the 1930s, but developed most rapidly during the early 1940s (Blackburn 1950). It harvests mature adults as they migrate into estuaries from the sea during spring. They were formerly captured in huge numbers together with lesser numbers (ca. 5%) of returning juveniles of several

amphidromous species of Galaxias—all of them being around 50–60 mm long (weighing about 0.5 g) at migration, taken in large numbers, and eaten en masse. Catch rose rapidly, to reach ca. 480,000 kg in the late 1940s, then collapsed to ca. 150,000 kg, and the ﬁshery was closed in 1949. Although potential annual sustainable yield was estimated at 300,000 kg (Blackburn 1950), the ﬁshery never returned to anywhere near that level. Fishing resumed in 1950 when ca. 50,000 kg were taken and in following years the ﬁshery declined to only 1,500 kg in 1955 and little more than 1,000 kg in 1972 (Fulton 1984—Fig. 85). The ﬁshery was again closed in 1974, but failed to recover.

It was reopened, again, following a series of experimental ﬁshing seasons from 1985 to 1987 (Fulton and Pavuk 1988), before being closed again. There was a trial season in 1990 and an open season in 1991, but the ﬁshery remained very depressed—the 1991 harvest was 1,300 kg (Anon 1992). One measure introduced to allow the populations to recover was to delay opening of the season to ensure plenty of escapement for spawning, but the effectiveness of this provision

Fig. 85 Catch ﬁgures for the Tasmanian whitebait ﬁshery for Lovettia sealii (after Fulton 1984)

seems dubious. The ﬁshery remains open at present, and the ‘‘upstream spawning migrations [of Lovettia sealii] still form a large component of the whitebait runs’’ (Anon. n.d.). Galaxias species that were always part (though formerly a minor part) of the ﬁshery now make a greater contribution to catches, primarily owing to the massive decline of L. sealii.

Responses from Tasmanian ﬁsheries ofﬁcials to the decline of L. sealii has interesting sidelights, and speciﬁcally portray an attitude that seems to regard the ﬁsh as more important as a food for estuarine/marine introduced brown trout than for human consumption. Hobbs (1960), for instance, wrote that: ‘‘There has long been grave concern among anglers at the alleged effect of netting [Lovettia sealii] on trout stocks. Again there is the view that formerly, whitebait provided trout with an abundant seasonal source of food...But...the whitebait stocks have diminished greatly with consequent harm to the trout ﬁsheries’’. The following year, Hobbs (1961) wrote, with regard to the northern Tasmanian whitebait ﬁshery, that: ‘‘...the quality of trout ﬁshing in early spring was dependent on the seasonal availability of whitebait ... [which] ...is stated to have brought regular tourists from the mainland...[It] provided a bountiful source of food for trout...and induced the trout to come into areas where they became more available to anglers’’, who wanted the whitebait ﬁshery terminated. Hobbs expressed concern that destruction of the populations ‘‘may already have proceeded beyond the point from which a recovery might be possible’’, though this concern seems to have been driven more by likely consequential impacts on trout angling than out of conservation concern for Lovettia. Lynch (1979b) later thought that it would be ‘‘a tragedy if the species were ﬁshed to extinction’’ though, again, it is ambiguous whether his concern was for Lovettia itself, its ﬁshery, or its availability as trout food. More recently, Fulton and Pavuk (1988) hinted at further pressure to terminate whitebait ﬁshing in Tasmania to make the ﬁsh available as trout food in the lower reaches and estuaries of Tasmanian rivers.

A report on the ﬁshery by Fulton (1990) yet again expressed ‘‘considerable concern from many trout anglers that stocks of whitebait may again be wiped out. The resultant regulations

Fig. 86 The southwestern Australian salamanderﬁsh, Lepidogalaxias salamandroides (reaches ca. 75 mm TL)

were therefore a compromise and an attempt to cater for all interests’’. Responses to a public questionnaire on the ﬁshery came primarily from trout anglers (76%). Again, it seems that interest in trout ﬁshing is taking a primary role in moves to conserve galaxioid ﬁshes.

The decline in this ﬁshery seemed to have resulted, initially, from overexploitation, but its failure to recover after it was closed from 1974 to 1992 is enigmatic, suggesting that other forces may have been implicated in the initial collapse, and/or could be contributing to sustained low production. Perhaps Hobbs (1961) seems correct in his concern that decline may have gone so far that recovery was not possible. Fulton (1984) pointed to the vulnerability to rapid overﬁshing of a ﬁshery exploiting an annual species just prior to reproduction, especially in the face of environmental perturbations such as ﬂoods and droughts, and he suggested that pollution of estuarine spawning grounds may also have been inﬂuential. He thought serious impacts from brown trout introduced to Tasmania, from about the 1860s onwards, were unlikely, as trout had been introduced about 75 years earlier than the late 1940s peak in the whitebait ﬁshery, but perhaps once seriously depressed by a combination of ﬁsheries exploitation and trout predation, the latter is enough to keep the populations depressed.

CONSERVATION RANKING: Lovettia sealii, though in serious decline from a ﬁshery perspective, has not been discussed in terms of its conservation status as a taxon in Tasmania, nor has it been regarded by Australian authorities as endangered (Crook 2001; Crook and Pogonoski 2002, 2003). There is no IUCN listing (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006).

Lepidogalaxias in Western Australia

Lepidogalaxias salamandroides (Fig. 86) is a little Western Australian species that is one of the most perplexing and extraordinary ﬁshes known, and well deserves a place as a biological icon amongst all the other diverse and bizarre animals and plants that inhabit Australia (McDowall and Pusey 1983; Pusey 1986, 1989; Pusey and Stewart

- 1989; Berra and Pusey 1997; Berra and Allen

- 1989, 1995; Allen and Berra 1989; McDowall

- 1998d). Its lack of proﬁle in Australian natural history derives substantially, probably, from its small size (to ca. 75 mm).

The relationships of L. salamandroides are obscure and much debated. Though it is generally agreed that it belongs somewhere among the lower euteleostean ﬁshes (where galaxiids, retropinnids, osmerids, salmonids and esocids all belong), details of the afﬁnities of Lepidogalaxias are much less clear (McDowall 1969; Rosen 1974; Johnson and Patterson 1996; Williams 1997; Waters et al.

- 2002b). I found its relationships uncertain in an early study (McDowall 1969) but thought that it is not a galaxiid. Rosen (1974) controversially suggested closest afﬁnities with the northern cooltemperate esocid ﬁshes (pikes), but no-one seems to have agreed with him (e.g. Fink 1984). Equally controversial, in my view, Johnson and Patterson

(1996) argued that Lepidogalaxias shares a common ancestry with Lovettia sealii. Williams (1997) did not support this afﬁnity, based on morphological data, and nor did Ishiguro et al. (2003) using molecular data, and that afﬁnity seems bizarre to me. Nelson (2006) regarded the evidence presented for a close relationship to Lovettia to be ‘‘strong’’; I ﬁnd this difﬁcult to agree with this, and suspect that this ﬁsh has become so specialised that few morphological characters remain that provide clear and reliable indicators of its afﬁnities, and that we may need to depend on molecular information to clarify this question. Since it seems to probably belong somewhere among the lower euteleosts, and because it is found in the southern cool-temperate zone, I include it in this account. It has numerous features that differ from ‘run-of-themill’ galaxiids, including, as the generic name indicates, the presence of scales, which are found in no ‘core’ galaxiids (including Lovettia).

Lepidogalaxias salamandroides has adnate eyes (there is no circumorbital sulcus, so that movements of the eye are limited). It can bend its ‘neck’—hence the common name salamanderﬁsh (ﬁshes usually do not have a neck; see Fig. 87); possibly this is aided by large gaps between the ﬁrst three or four vertebrae. The ability to bend its neck seems likely to be an adaptation associated with the adnate eyes—instead of rolling its eyes to locate food, etc., as most ﬁsh do, it bends its neck, a behaviour that resembles an English thrush cocking its head to listen for worms on a lawn.

Lepidogalaxias inhabits acidic, ephemeral pools of water across southern Western Australia (Fig. 88, d), and can aestivate when habitats become dewatered. It has internal fertilisation of its eggs. This could, perhaps, be an adaptation to the wetlands where L. salamandroides lives, as low pH may have impacts on sperm activity and viability). It shares many of these wetland habitats with Galaxiella nigrostriata, discussed above. The peat ﬂats wetlands tend to be inhospitable to ﬁsh that are not specially adapted to such conditions and, like G. nigrostriata, L. salamandroides is capable of aestivating when water in its habitat dries up during summer. Morgan et al. (1998) cautioned that exotic ﬁsh species ‘‘may pose a serious threat to the small endemic ﬁsh of southwestern Australia’’; they listed L. salamandroides as co-occurring with only Gambusia holbrooki (among these exotics), and doing so only once. Quite probably, the ability that L. salamandroides has to aestivate allows it to live in wetland pools

Fig. 87 Photo of salamanderﬁsh, Lepidogalaxias salamandroides, show its ability to bend its ‘neck’

- Fig. 88 Distribution in southwestern Australia of Lepidogalaxias salamandroides (after Morgan et al. 1998)

where Gambusia is prevented from long-term occupation.

CONSERVATION RANKING: The Australian Council of Conservation Ministers ranked Lepidogalaxias salamandroides as Rare (Michaelis 1986), as also by the Australian Society for Fish Biology (Crook and Pogonoski 2003). Allen et al. (2002), however, regarded it as ‘‘common, but restricted to drainages and wetlands within 70 km of the south-western coastline of Western Australia, between Margaret River and Albany’’. Impacts of salmonids on L. salamandroides seem unlikely owing to their very different habitat preferences. IUCN ranked this species as Lower Risk–Near Threatened (Baillie and Groombridge 1996; IUCN 2006).

Part III: Discussion and synthesis

Invasiveness as a biotic process in the context of galaxioid conservation

One of the emerging features of this analysis of galaxioid conservation is the frequent implication of introduced ﬁshes, especially salmonids. What is it that renders invasive species so damaging and receiving ecosystems so vulnera-

ble? The impacts of invasive species on indigenous biotas, and especially the role of humancaused invasions in causing biodiversity loss, have become a major issue for conservation ecology, globally, and there is an increasingly widely held view that introduced species are amongst the most damaging contributors to the current biodiversity crisis (Fausch 1988; Vitousek et al. 1997; Low 2002; Sakai et al. 2001; Kolar and Lodge 2001; Lee 2002; Marchetti et al. 2004), not the least in New Zealand (McDowall 1968b, c, 2003b, 2004b; Townsend and Crowl 1991).

The biodiversity crisis in the galaxioid ﬁshes is at least substantially created by the impacts of exotic species, sometimes called the third great extinction episode in earth history (Brown 1989; Pimm 2001), biological invasions being second only to habitat destruction as a cause of native species decline (Ruzycki et al. 2003). The place of salmonid ﬁshes in processes of ecosystem invasion in cool Southern Hemisphere lands seems obvious. Dealing with these impacts, should there be a will to do so, is clearly both complex and costly, and the will to do so may be the primary issue, given the economic value ascribed to salmonid ﬁshes almost universally across the range of the galaxioids. Much of the process of establishment of salmonids, though deliberate, was planned with no thought for the consequences. Lassuy (1995)

cited a report of the United States Ofﬁce of Technology Assessment as stating that non-native species are ‘‘here to stay and many of them are welcome’’. And, there is a paradox that applies globally, because human societies depend heavily on introduced species for food production, aesthetic pleasures, household pets, and recreational hunting and angling.

All sorts of analogies have been drawn to illustrate the introductions of exotics and their impacts. Crosby (1986) called species introductions ‘‘ecological imperialism’’. More often it is called ‘‘homogenisation’’ (Dill and Cordone 1997; Scott and Helfman 2001); others have called it ‘‘mongrelization of the global landscape’’ (Meffe and Carroll 1997); ‘‘zoogeographic pollution’’; an ‘‘introduction frenzy’’ (Courtenay, cited in Cambray 2003a); and ‘‘Danubization’’—the introduction of Danube River ﬁsh species into Italy (Bianco and Ketmaier 2001) But, this ﬂorid language aside, it is not a new idea, Leopold (1949), for instance, long ago alluding to ‘‘worldwide pooling of faunas and ﬂoras’’.

What is true, generally, of the global biota, seems to be true also of freshwater ﬁshes. It is nearly 40 years since I expressed concern about the impacts of exotic ﬁshes on the New Zealand freshwater ﬁsh fauna (McDowall 1968b, c). Stiassny (1996) thought the role of exotic species to be ‘‘a major and growing problem, worldwide’’, and Moyle (1997) suggested that the impacts of introductions are almost impossible to predict, calling this unpredictability the ‘‘Frankenstein Effect’’. Allendorf (1991) alluded to the ‘‘ratchet effect’’, this being the cumulative effects of incremental biodiversity losses. Lassuy (1995) found that many introduced species were deemed to be of more immediate beneﬁt to humans than what persisted of the native biological communities, but also pointed to the dilemma of shortterm gain for long-term loss. Townsend (1991) alluded to the ‘‘knock-on’’ effect, the ecosystem consequences of introductions, and he argued that not much is known about the vulnerability of receiving environments. Allendorf (1991) had difﬁculty balancing the short-term human beneﬁts of introductions against their long-term biodiversity losses, asking: ‘‘How much money is a species of ﬁsh worth? How about an endemic inverte-

brate species?’’ As someone once perceptively said: ‘‘It depends what sort of world one wants to live in?’’ And in the present instance all these issues apply to galaxioid conservation, being very substantially an issue relating to trout introductions. Some of the changes imposed by exotic species are subtle and difﬁcult to identify, though, as Allibone and McIntosh (1999) urged in their assessment of trout impacts in New Zealand, ‘‘lack of information and the difﬁculty of the problem should no longer be seen as an excuse for ignoring it’’. Pascual et al. (2002) raised similar issues for Patagonian South America, as did Cambray (2003b) for southern Africa and Sanger and Fulton (1991) in Australia—and so in virtually all countries where galaxioid ﬁshes are found.

A contemporary idea with an old history

Concerns about the impacts of introduced species on indigenous faunas of often remote, depauperate and disharmonic biotas of lands of the southern cool-temperate zone, like those of New Zealand, are not new. The notion that impacts of invasion of ‘successful continental’ species originating in large and complex faunas in the Northern Hemisphere into impoverished, sometimes island, faunas in the south is old enough to have its conceptual origins with Darwin (1859). The idea was developed by Matthew (1915), Elton (1958) and Darlington (1957, 1965), among others, and may have some merit. Elton (1958) long ago argued that invasion into an existing community is easier in relatively simply structured ecosystems, a view that is still held widely (Ross 1991; Lodge 1993). If true, it is likely to apply at least to New Zealand, and Falkland Islands and New Caledonian freshwater ecosystems, but what this means in practice is unclear. And the question is very much a ‘relative’ one: What is a ‘simple’ ecosystem?

Thomson (1922) long ago expressed concerns about impacts of alien species in New Zealand, articulated in part in the context of what was then burgeoning interest in Darwinian evolution. He explored a litany of impacts from a multitude of introduced species that, even then, demonstrated quite clearly that the implications could often be

severe for the indigenous biota. He asked: ‘‘What effects have been produced on the native fauna by the introductions of foreign animals and plants into the islands of the New Zealand group?’’, and his response, even then, was that: ‘‘The effects have been so far-reaching and so complex that it is impossible to present any summary of them, and all that can be done is to show various aspects of the problem, and to consider facts in detail’’. And that seems no less true today, more than 80 years later.

For authority, Thomson turned to Darwin (1859), who wrote in response to his visit to New Zealand on the Beagle in 1835, that: ‘‘From the extraordinary manner in which European productions have recently spread over New Zealand, and have seized on places which must have been previously occupied, we may believe, if all the animals and plants of Great Britain were set free in New Zealand, that in the course of time a multitude of British forms would become thoroughly naturalised there [and so they have] and would exterminate many of the natives [which has also been true]. On the other hand, from what we now see occurring in New Zealand, and from hardly a single inhabitant of the Southern Hemisphere having become wild in any part of Europe, we may doubt, if all the productions of New Zealand were set free in Great Britain, whether any considerable number would be enabled to seize on places now occupied by our native [British] animals and plants’’. There are, no doubt, elements of truth in these comments, particularly the view that depauperate faunas are susceptible to invasion by non-native species (Ross 1991), but these notions may also be somewhat simplistic. Especially when Darwin was in New Zealand the process of acclimatisation of foreign biota in lands of the cool southern temperate zone was quickly gaining momentum. Human emigration from Great Britain, especially to Australia and New Zealand, was increasing rapidly. The emigrants took all sorts of animals and plants with them. Partly this was because they needed them to produce food and as ‘beasts of burden’ or as companion pets, but it was also partly because they wanted to re-create a microcosm of their homelands in the colonies (Hursthouse 1857) (making one might wonder why they

went!). And fatefully for New Zealand’s freshwater ﬁsh, salmonids were among the desired importations—this was Crosby’s (1986) ‘‘ecological imperialism’’ in action. Recall that seafarer James Cook in the 1770s had taken various culturally important species like potatoes, pigs, goats and sheep to New Zealand, and that he also gave the native Maori people seeds of various vegetable plants with the expectation that they would multiply and proliferate under the guidance of the local population (details in Thomson 1922, from Cook’s journals). Thus ‘‘propagule pressure’’ for establishment of exotic (and largely British) biota, for centuries, strongly favoured British/European biota in the lands of the antipodes. So too did deforestation then taking place in New Zealand, with establishment of broad areas of disturbed habitat and ecotones of changing ﬂora from forest to scrubland to pasture. Pastoral development happened very quickly (Park 1995). In part, this alien invasion was simply that so many species were transported to New Zealand, so often, and for so long (and it was still going on well into the mid-20th century). It is therefore no wonder that establishment of exotics had a directional component, with species originating from northern cool-temperate zone lands becoming established in New Zealand. Similar patterns can be described for Australia (Flannery 1994; Low 2002) and as well, it seems, for Patagonia and South Africa. The likes of Matthew (1915), Darlington (1957) and others have perpetuated these ideas, perhaps making them seem more defensible. Trout species were a signiﬁcant part of the alien invasion process.

I suspect, however, that this may underestimate what was going on. It is easy to attribute causes to such effects, but far harder to test such ideas rigorously. In at least in some instances there is evidence that biotas of southern lands are not always as ‘inferior’ in competitive ability as might be thought. Why, for instance, has the small endemic New Zealand freshwater hydrobiid snail, Potamopyrgus antipodarum, been so strikingly successful in Australia, Europe and North America where it is causing serious environmental concern (Zaranko et al. 1997; Schreiber et al. 1998, 2003; Strayer 1999; Carlsson 2000) (though whether it has created serious ecological disrup-

tion is yet to be ascertained). And relatively fewer southern biotic elements have ever been introduced into the northern, continental biotas, so that there are few tests of this idea.

Allibone and McIntosh (1999) argued that, in New Zealand, ‘‘It has been common and convenient to blame decline in native ﬁsh on changes affecting the quality of habitat occupied by native ﬁsh’’, but they concluded that this cannot be considered the only impact. Any invasive species will have impacts on receiving ecosystems, precipitating changes possibly in both physical and biological attributes of the receiving habitats (Taylor et al. 1984). Moreover, impacts of habitat degradation and exotic ﬁshes may compound each other—the stresses of habitat degradation may reduce the viability of native ﬁsh populations making it easier for invasive ﬁshes to displace native ones, especially if invasive species are accustomed to disturbance, as they often are. Some, however, argue that intact ecosystems are easier to invade than disturbed ones (Ramakrishnan and Vitousek 1989; Lodge 1993). Moreover, in novel habitats, invasive species are freed from traditional ecological constraints, e.g., there may be less or different competition for foraging sites, fewer parasites and pathogens, etc. in the receiving ecosystems (Font 1997; Poulin and Mouillot

- 2003). Some ecosystem changes are bound to be subtle and, according to Ross (1991), ‘‘few studies have really looked [for such changes] in assemblage structure or niche shape in native species following the addition of non-native species’’.

Duncan and Lockwood (2001) searched for intrinsic characters of various threatened ﬁsh families that might contribute to their being threatened, and were pessimistic about deriving generalisations that assist in understanding and predicting causes for species becoming threatened. They concluded that ‘‘our ability to generalize globally appears futile’’, and that ‘‘Sweeping generalisations at the broad scale of global freshwater ﬁsh families are replete with exceptions’’. They also wondered whether extinction-prone families are more likely to inhabit more thoroughly studied regions of the world, perhaps because they are better known. A cause–effect relationship here

seems elusive; but faunas of such developed countries may be better known because of a country’s likely afﬂuence. At the same time faunas may be more seriously impacted by means used to produce that afﬂuence, such as high human population densities, land development, industrialisation, water management, and efﬂuent discharges.

Possibly, however, the scale at which Duncan and Lockwood (2001) viewed the question of intrinsic change of invasive species was altogether too broad, and perhaps intrinsic characteristics only take on meaning when viewed in the context of extrinsic effects and when ﬁsh taxa are grouped more ﬁnely on the basis of their intrinsic characters. Interestingly, too, despite widespread and growing expressions of concern about the role of introduced species on threatened species, Duncan and Lockwood (2001) did not examine this question with relation to the conservation status of ﬁsh species.

Any invasive species will have impacts, precipitating changes possibly in both physical and biological attributes of the habitat or ecosystem receiving the introduction (Taylor et al. (1984). Moreover, impacts of habitat change and of exotic ﬁshes on indigenous ones may possibly compound one another—stresses of habitat degradation may reduce the viability of native species’ populations making it easier for the invasive species to displace native ones.

Wilson (1992) expressed a hope that: ‘‘Except in ignorance or malice, there is no longer an ideological war between conservationists and developers. Both share the perception that health and prosperity decline in a deteriorating environment’’, but with regard to the aspect of this ‘‘deteriorating environment’’ that relates to issues of biodiversity and species’ introductions, it is unclear that he is right. Nevertheless, there is a changing mood in most parts of the world, a growing tendency to view biodiversity issues across an evolutionary time-scale rather than human-life-time scale (Magnuson 1976), and to adopt a global and indigenous perspective. Ecological imperialism (Crosby 1986) is no longer any more acceptable than human colonialism to many people. It remains highly uncertain, however, that changed attitudes have permeated the salmonid angling culture. Moreover, suggestions that much-

vaunted salmonids have had serious impacts on native species, often galaxiids, are resulting in reactionary responses from anglers widely across the ranges of the galaxioids, to the point where some of those favouring salmonids are accusing conservationists of plotting salmonid extermination. Such individuals are beginning to sound somewhat ‘embattled’. The place of salmonids and the future for galaxioid conservation are certainly uncertain!

Salmonid introductions into southern lands

Earlier sections of this paper have implicated salmonids as a signiﬁcant force in the decline of galaxioids. The salmonid ﬁshes are amongst the most favoured of all sports ﬁsh by anglers, in the lands of the cool to cold Northern Hemisphere where they are native. There has been much translocation of species between Europe and North America, in both of which salmonids are naturally present, to augment trout species already naturally available to anglers there (MacCrimmon and Marshall 1968; MacCrimmon 1971; Welcomme 1988; Behnke 1992, 2002; Lever n.d.) There has also been extensive translocation of salmonids within their home continents, and between catchments within the broad-scale of various species’ natural ranges, and there has as a result been extensive hybridisation, introgression among, and displacement of, native salmonid species in western North America (Behnke 1992, 2002). There has particularly been widespread introduction of North American rainbow trout in Europe as well as European brown trout in North America, leading to both brown and rainbow trout being regarded as among the ‘now relatively cosmopolitan species that are useful to humans’. But this has led to brown and rainbow trout being listed amongst the globally most troublesome introduced species (Lowe et al. n.d.). They have become very widespread in southern lands—where they have been brought into ‘confrontation’ with galaxioid ﬁshes.

Looking at the global ranges of brown and rainbow trout:

• Rainbow trout are recorded as introductions into 97 countries around the world (Welcom-

me 1992) and this includes unlikely places like Hawaii, Papua New Guinea, Iran, Iraq, Israel, Malaysia and Sri Lanka where, in many instances, the salmonids have retreated to high elevations to ﬁnd cold enough waters in which to survive. This vast range prompted Cambray (2003a) to write somewhat scornfully of the opportunity this provided for anglers to catch the same ﬁsh in over 90 countries, all over the world.

• Brown trout are present as an alien/exotic in around 30 countries, and the much lower number of countries (compared with rainbow trout), is substantially because the brown trout is indigenous to many of the countries of Europe into which rainbow trout have been introduced. Brown trout have become widely distributed in North America, being present in 41 states in the United States (Courtenay et al. 1984) and in all but three provinces in Canada (Crossman 1984)—despite those countries’ rich diversity of indigenous salmonids.

When it is realised that much of the colonisation of southern cool-temperate lands has been by peoples of European ancestry, it should then cause no surprise that these same salmonids have also been widely introduced into southern cooltemperate lands. For New Zealand, at least, one of the goals of 19th century colonists was to try to re-create something of the countryside and culture that they had left behind in Great Britain, with New Zealand being viewed as potentially a ‘‘Britain of the south’’ (Hursthouse 1857). There have been similar events and processes in Australia. But Atlantic salmon and brown trout were being discussed in early colonial New Zealand by the 1840s, as potential importations (McDowall 1994a). Success with shipment of salmonids took a while when based on sailing ships and with no then-proven protocols to get ova across the tropics, but in the last third of the 19th century, it became more and more successful, especially when motorised ships became involved (McDowall 1994a). As a result, trout ﬁsheries in New Zealand were world-famous by the late 1800s (Spackman 1892). Once stocks of these ﬁsh

reached one or other of the southern lands, the principal impediment to their wider spread, bringing the stocks through the tropics, had been obviated, and salmonids could spread among southern lands without recourse to material from the north.

In total, about ten Northern Hemisphere salmonids have been introduced into southern lands, with widely varying success. Brown and rainbow trout are by far the most successful, brook charr are quite widespread in some areas in Australia, New Zealand, Patagonia and South Africa, there are occasional landlocked stocks of both Atlantic salmon and sockeye salmon, and also instances of other salmonids having become locally established. And chinook salmon are established as both anadromous and landlocked stocks in New Zealand (McDowall 1990b, 1994a), and perhaps recently in Patagonia (Ciancio et al. 2005), as well as the presence of artiﬁcially stocked populations in some Australian lakes and in Patagonia. Coho salmon are present in Kerguelen in the Southern Ocean. In general, however, it is brown and rainbow trout that are most widespread in southern lands.

Successful translocation of salmonids

Elements related to the widely successful introduction of salmonids into the ranges of southern galaxioids and to their consequential impacts include:

- 1. Attributes of the introduced species, which are of pivotal importance with regard to their place in the biological communities in which they originate and into which they are introduced;
- 2. Attributes of the receiving biological communities will have affected how introduced species become part of new communities, especially food organisms and the general lack of ﬁsh predators and troublesome diseases in most cool southern lakes and rivers;
- 3. Most important, is the match in physicochemical and other conditions between receiving environment and the introduced species.

Ecological preferences and tolerances have clearly been of critical importance for success of introduced salmonid species. In some ways the needs of salmonids are generalised, other than demands for cool water, coarse gravel spawning substrates, and generalised aquatic invertebrate foods.

Certainly, freshwater habitats into which salmonids have been introduced in cool-temperate southern lands have provided physicochemical conditions that quite closely resemble those in their Northern Hemisphere native ranges. Particularly in New Zealand and parts of Patagonia widespread successful acclimatisation of brown and rainbow trout is, with the advantage of hindsight, no surprise. Success could have been predicted, based just on trout environmental preferences and the physico-chemical characteristics of southern freshwater ecosystems where they were introduced (Townsend 1996).

Salmonids as ersatz native species in southern lands

Scott and Helfman (2001) recognised how widely some salmonids have become across (mostly) cool-temperate latitudes of the globe, including much translocation within continental areas where they are indigenous (MacCrimmon and Marshall 1968; MacCrimmon 1971; Welcomme 1984, 1988; Behnke 1992, 2002; Lever n.d.). This led them to describe brown trout and rainbow trout as among the ‘‘small group of [now] relatively cosmopolitan species that are useful to humans’’. So entrenched have some of these introduced salmonids become, that anglers often seem unaware that they are ﬁshing for alien ﬁsh species. And for some ﬁsheries managers, salmonids have become such a routine, accepted part of their ecological landscape, that they too seem sometimes unaware that these ﬁshes are not native (Hocutt 1984). Cassey and Arthington (1999) cited Mack’s (1996) suggestion that a naturalised species should not be classed as invasive if the alien does not have any detrimental effects on the receiving ecosystem. However, this proposal creates many problems:

- 1. Introduced aliens virtually always have some impact (Ross 1991);
- 2. Often no one would know whether or not there have been adverse impacts, even when the matter has been investigated (Fausch 1988);
- 3. The damaging impacts may take time to emerge and by then it is generally too late to reverse the action;
- 4. This is not a lot of use if the species has already become invasive (apart from efforts to limit further spread); and ﬁnally
- 5. Choosing between the beneﬁts to anglers and others, and the costs to native species/ecosystems, is a value judgement, anyway, and in most countries there is no-one who seems willing to make a decision against aliens and in favour of indigenous biota, especially when salmonids are invasive.

In some countries, there is a ‘culture’ tending to favour conservation of alien species. In some regions these salmonids (and other exotics) are so well entrenched, have been established for so long, and perhaps are so valuable, that attitudes of conservationists concerned for impacts on indigenous biota are thought to need ‘revision’. Chadd (2004 ) reckoned that in Britain ‘‘many organisations devoted to collecting distributional data regard some of the rarer ‘aliens’ as ‘‘naturalised elements of the ﬂora and fauna’’, and he suggested ‘‘two pillars for greater tolerance of ‘aliens’. The ﬁrst centres around the invasiveness and destructive potential of a chosen species, the second around how long it has lived here and how much a naturalised part of the fauna and ﬂora it has become’’. That may be just ﬁne for a region like the British Isles, where habitat modiﬁcation is so pervasive that there may be few natural areas left, but as a generalisation it seems to me to be certainly dangerous, and have no concern for indigenous biota.

Pascual et al. (2002) found that in Patagonia, exotic ﬁsh are not really regarded any differently from indigenous species by the public or most policy makers, and the Argentineans are probably not alone among cool-temperate Southern Hemisphere lands. And here, it is perhaps time to recall repeated instances discussed in earlier sections

where indigenous galaxioid species in several southern lands are regarded as having preferential value as food for trout, over availability of the galaxiids for human consumption, let alone their conservation.

The Maruia Society, one of New Zealand’s leading nature conservation organisations, sells T-shirts with pictures of introduced trout on them (but none with native ﬁshes). Cambray (2003a) noted a ‘Tourism New Zealand’ advertisement, under the title ‘Pure 100% New Zealand’, featuring an angler catching an introduced rainbow trout in a New Zealand river. These happenings, of course, only foster the notion that trout belong there. Rainbow and brown trout have featured in several postage stamps in New Zealand, including celebrations of their ﬁrst centuries in New Zealand rivers and lakes, but native ﬁsh have never been featured (despite repeated efforts to remedy this), though there was recent issue of a $5 coin that features the endemic Galaxias argenteus, the largest species in the genus (Fig. 52A). Cambray (2003a) reported that in South Africa, some anglers ‘‘want alien rainbow trout to be declared an ‘honorary indigenous species’ because it has been in that country for over 100 years’’.

Some aspects of the introduction and management of salmonids have reached a staggering scale. Atlantic salmon were being introduced to New Zealand over a period of more than a century, with at least 24 consignments, a total of nearly 5 million ova imported and 2.75 million young salmon liberated (fruitlessly—McDowall 1990b, 1994a). Repeated millions of trout have been stocked into New Zealand rivers and lakes over the decades. One New Zealand acclimatisation society (of up to >40 at various times) reported releases of 9 million brown trout in 35 years, and in some years >700,000. Another liberated 23 million ﬁsh in 55 years. These ﬁsh had to come from somewhere, and a third acclimatisation society harvested ova from a stream in which the spawning population was estimated as up to 65,000 ﬁsh, which might have produced over 90 million ova (McDowall 1999c). The society listed a take of up to 1.7 million ova a year for stocking purposes. So the scale of hatchery stocking was immense. Similar stories and attitudes can be related for Australia (Cle-

ments 1988) and probably also for South Africa, Patagonia, and even the Falkland Islands, though records are less accessible. Salmonid stocking is still argued as justiﬁable and is said to be having no serious impacts in South Australia (Fulton

- 2004), though that is clearly a matter of opinion for Australia generally (Cadwallader 1996; Raadik 1995, 2001, 2002; Sanger 1988, 1993).

Salmonid stocking persists into the modern era, some ﬁsheries still being augmented by hatchery releases (McDowall 1994a). Some salmonid stocking in New Zealand’s was simply reckless—what Cambray (2003a) called the ‘‘Wild West approach to stocking aliens in industrialized countries’’. It seems as though attitudes to recreational ﬁsheries values of these salmonids have blinded governments, ﬁsheries managers, and angler groups to the actual or potential damaging impacts of these ﬁsh to native ﬁsh and ecosystems. Thus, Allen (1961) attributed negative attitudes towards invasive species to ‘‘undue sentiment’’ and therefore presumably of little account, though application of the precautionary principle would probably have suggested that other approaches to ensuring the conservation status of New Zealand’s galaxiids were needed.

The role of life history strategies in salmonid spread and establishment

One element that may help to explain some of the widespread success and impacts of brown trout in southern cool-temperate ecosystems, in particular, and when compared with other species, is that this species has a highly ﬂexible life history strategy. Often brown trout populations involve some, and sometimes a majority, of individuals that spend a part of their lives in the sea—brown trout are facultatively and very ﬂexibly anadromous (McDowall 1988, 1990b). Migrating individuals in some populations undergo a minor metamorphosis (smolting) and move to sea as small ﬁngerlings, and they may spend several years feeding and growing there before returning to fresh water to spawn (so-called ‘‘sea trout’’). Often they will return to their ‘home’ river, but there is undoubtedly a signiﬁcant amount of straying (Quinn 1993; Unwin and Quinn 1993; Thorpe 1994). Straying results in the species spreading into other rivers (as

in New Zealand for Chinook salmon in the early 1900s—McDowall 1990b), and as is evidently still occurring in the Falkland Islands. Other individual ﬁsh may spend most of their lives in river estuaries where food supplies may be rich, and predation risks low compared with life at sea, and such ﬁsh (sometimes called ‘‘slob trout’’) will be encountering elevated salinities at times. Thus, brown trout often have the capacity to move to and from the sea, enabling them to invade river systems around sea coasts of countries into which they have been introduced (if sea temperatures are appropriate). Sometimes this ability to move to and from the sea is present even though it is believed that stocks being introduced were derived from a ‘nonmigratory’ stock (as in Tasmanian, early New Zealand, and Falkland Islands history—Clements 1988; McDowall 1990b, 1994a; Nolan 1993; Lapsley 1993). Also, salmonids may grow much faster and larger at sea, and this has several consequential effects, the ﬁsh becoming increasingly piscivorous, having higher fecundity, and so more rapid proliferation.

It seems that, regardless of the provenance of source stocks (where precisely known), some individual brown trout retain, or revert to, a tendency to become sea-migratory. Clearly, this has been an important process in fostering widespread invasion of brown trout into rivers systems, at least in Tasmania, New Zealand and the Falklands (McDowall 1990b; McDowall et al. 2001), and the same may have been true in Patagonia. Broad geographical ranges developed by brown trout are the outcome in these lands, as the species invades everywhere that upstream access from the sea is possible. Brown trout seem to have established sea-migratory stocks almost everywhere—except in the north of their translocated ranges, probably because sea temperatures are too highinsummer.Scott and Poynter (1991) observed that warm ambient water temperatures in most habitats in New Zealand north of about latitude 37 N exclude self-sustaining stocks of salmonids (though hatchery stocking may in part be ‘compensating for’ this exclusion). The same limitations probably apply in the other southern lands.

Add the human factor to this, and particularly the habit that anglers or ﬁsheries management

authorities have of ‘helping’ the trout get past obstructions by liberating them into places into which they could not naturally disperse, and it is easy to see why brown trout have become so widespread (McDowall 1990a, b; Sanger and Fulton 1991; McDowall et al. 2001). Distances travelled at sea by brown trout do not need to be great to facilitate their gradual spread because they can move gradually from one river system to another along coastlines. Nevertheless, movements at sea may sometimes be substantial—there are New Zealand instances of tagged brown trout moving hundreds of kilometres between rivers, through the sea, between being tagged and recaptured (McDowall 1990b).

The scenario with other salmonids in southern lands is rather different. With rainbow trout, even though sea-migratory stocks were introduced, at least into New Zealand, (Scott et al. 1978; McDowall 1990b, 1994a), and therefore also Australia (since the Australian stock came from New Zealand—Clements 1988; McDowall

- 1990b), they have never become anadromous. Considerable effort was invested in New Zealand in actually trying to ‘force’ rainbow trout into becoming anadromous by releasing them into rivers not associated with lakes (McDowall

- 1990b, 1994a), but all such efforts failed. Furthermore, it seems that, in many New Zealand rivers, rainbow trout have failed to become established at all, or are restricted to small, residual, headwater stocks, possibly because most juveniles retain their sea-migratory behaviour, smolt, and go to sea, but generally fail to return to fresh water (though one might expect very rapid selection against migration to sea, given the failure of such ﬁsh to return). Quite possibly cues that the ﬁsh use to navigate back to land/fresh water in their northern Paciﬁc home territories fail when rainbows have been introduced elsewhere and they become lost at sea. Even though it is possible that occasional, individual rainbow trout may move to and from the sea, it seems that this is not a predominant life history attribute of rainbow trout in any areas of their acclimatisation. And a consequence of this is that rainbows are much less widespread than brown trout in Australia and New Zealand (McDowall 1988, 1990b).

The migratory/life history status of some Patagonian populations of rainbow trout and various Paciﬁc salmons is somewhat obscure. Pascual et al. (2001) have presented evidence for anadromy in rainbow trout in Patagonia, and Ciancio et al. (2005) of Chinook salmon in Argentina, but it is possible that in some instances returns of ﬁsh to fresh waters are driven by escapes from sea cage salmonid farms, and it will be interesting to see if locally, naturally spawning, anadromous populations become established and spread more widely in Patagonia. Certainly, in the past in New Zealand, returns of chinook salmon to some river systems were strongly driven by liberations of large hatchery smolts, and once hatchery releases terminated, the returns declined very rapidly (McDowall 1990b).

Conﬂicts of interest for ﬁsheries and conservation managers

Much of this account discusses the potentially damaging impacts of introduced salmonids around the Southern Hemisphere, and particularly impacts on galaxioid ﬁshes, the dominating freshwater ﬁshes around the cool Southern Hemisphere. As discussed earlier, a crucial point in coming to grips with widespread introductions of salmonids (and in attempting to deal with their impacts on Southern Hemisphere freshwater ecosystems) is that these alien ﬁsh have become extremely highly valued in many recipient countries, as outlined above. Thus, in most countries having galaxioid ﬁshes, ﬁsheries based on introduced salmonids support highly valued recreational ﬁsheries. Salmonids are not only ‘‘there to stay’’ (Lassuy 1995), but are widely valued, and ﬁsheries they sustain are signiﬁcant assets—valued by local anglers, as a source of income for a substantial biological/ economic infrastructure, and for luring tourists from countries all over the world.

Given the values ascribed to these alien salmonids and their adverse impacts, ensuring protection of the native galaxioids is a bureaucratic nightmare, and here we confront the global human conﬂict of interests between native and exotic fauna and ﬂora, seldom so explicitly and deeply etched, right around the Southern Hemisphere, and thus across the whole range of the

galaxioids. This creates a potential for substantial conﬂict of interests between nature conservation and economics in such countries, and there have been such conﬂicts.

In the United States, Hocutt (1984) recognised a ‘‘clear mandate to maintain the biological integrity’’ of United States waters, and the knew that exotic ﬁshes ‘‘pose a distinct potential source of disruption of these waters’’. This was, of course, written about a country where salmonids are naturally widespread, and in a book examining the problems that introduced ﬁshes have caused. Moreover, the account involves a nation where there has been massive translocation of native freshwater ﬁsh, as well as extensive importations from beyond the country’s borders. Interestingly, though, Hocutt (1984) recognised the conﬂict of interests involved: on one hand ecologists and ichthyologists—interested in ﬁsh and their ecology and relationships and, on the other hand, ﬁsheries biologists and anglers—more interested in managing and exploiting ﬁshes. He criticised functional separation of research and management functions, at all levels from local to national, because they allowed managers to become isolated (insulated?) from the effects of exotics. He recognised that some ﬁsheries managers do have grave concerns for native ﬁsh species, and that these conﬂicts are then ‘internal’, taking place in single human individuals, and Hocutt admitted personally this facing dilemma!

For the native galaxioid ﬁshes and introduced salmonids in southern lands, there are several areas of conﬂicting values.

- • One is directly related to whether salmonids or galaxiids should have management priority in rivers and lakes—it seems that often we cannot have both natives and aliens;
- • Another relates to whether galaxioid ﬁshes should be primarily the focus for conservation, available as a resource to be harvested by humans, or be available in lakes and rivers as a food for salmonids;
- • In some instances there are issues relating to whether threatened species should be exposed to human harvest; and
- • Yet further conﬂicts apply to policies and practices concerning salmonids in parks and

reserves that are set aside for conservation of indigenous biota.

Thus what Hocutt described for the United States applies equally to southern cool-temperate lands where galaxioid ﬁshes ‘belong’ and salmonids do not, except ‘by invitation’ (and where in the view of some they have long ‘worn out their welcome’). How are we to manage highly valued ﬁsheries based on introduced alien species without serious adverse impacts on indigenous biota? Is it desirable? Moreover, can it be achieved?

Conﬂicts of interest are at times quite explicitly within administering agency jurisdictions within countries. New Zealand’s Department of Conservation, for instance, has multiple conﬂicts, between:

- 1. Caring for threatened species;
- 2. Managing a ﬁshery that includes exploitation of these threatened species; and
- 3. Managing ﬁsheries for introduced salmonids that prey upon the galaxioids.

No doubt such problems are much wider than just New Zealand.

In New Zealand, despite statutory requirements for managers of National Parks to eliminate exotic biota within Park boundaries, National Parks authorities have for decades managed, and even fostered, development and maintenance of exotic salmonids in National Park rivers and lakes (including hatchery stocking, at times). It is almost as if aquatic biota belong in a different category from terrestrial animals and plants (‘‘under water, under valued’’—McDowall 2004b). Other government agencies, such as New Zealand’s Ministry for the Environment, have expressed concerns about impacts of introduced ﬁsh species on native ones. I mentioned above the liberations of rainbow trout into a virgin lake in one New Zealand National Park by the country’s then lead agency for fauna conservation, and that the same agency sought approval for control of indigenous anguillid eel stocks to allow proliferation of introduced Atlantic salmon within a National Park.

In Tasmania, Fulton (1978b) has suggested that some threatened galaxiids may be protected by their habitats being in World Heritage

areas—though the streams and lakes are full of brown trout, and sometimes rainbows, as well as sometimes invasive, if indigenous, galaxiids. And Fulton (1987) pointed to the Tasmanian Inland Fisheries Commission adjusting its trout stocking policy ‘‘to avoid conﬂict with sensitive native species’’ but was not speciﬁc, and provided no indication of data that allowed such sensitive adjustments of salmonid stocks. As well, one may wonder how that could be possible, in the many waters where the trout populations were selfsupporting. Fulton (1987) further argued that, in Australian ﬁsheries management, ‘‘it is unrealistic to expect the licence fees of freshwater [i.e. primarily trout] anglers to be devoted to research and survey work on threatened native species’’. This seems a curious position to adopt, especially when the threatened species have been subject to the sorts of damaging impacts from trout introductions, competition and predation, discussed above and including by Fulton himself, at length. Ingram et al. (1990) viewed it rather differently. They recognised the conﬂict, and pointed to a role of the Tasmanian Inland Fisheries Commission (now Service) in ‘‘balancing the conservation of galaxiids and the recreational values of brown trout’’. It is unclear where this balance lies.

Similar types of problems are present in Patagonian South America, the Falkland Islands and South Africa. Pascual et al. (2002) put it well for Argentina, writing: ‘‘The true challenge for ﬁsh conservation at the regional level, we believe, is to ﬁnd ways to protect native ﬁsh compatible with maintaining highly priced sports ﬁsheries. Is this at all possible? Are there management strategies that could balance ﬁshing quality and conservation of native species? Such an approach may not appear completely satisfactory from a conservation viewpoint, but the current management scheme simply leaves native species out of the picture’’. It is probably no different elsewhere. They described how, in Patagonia, the goals for ﬁsh communities ‘‘effectively encompass (a) preserving world-renowned trout sports ﬁsheries, the dominating and exclusive focus of provincial ﬁsheries administrations; and (b) preserving native biodiversity, the guiding principle of the National Parks Administration, and a much relegated objective...Patagonian ﬁsh have been

largely absent from the conservation agendas of provincial and federal governments, as well as NGOs’’. Cambray (2003a) observed that in some National Parks in South Africa, ‘‘the number of alien freshwater ﬁsh species exceeds the number of indigenous species’’, and the situation there seems little different.

Apart from all other issues, all countries that are signatories to the International Convention on Biodiversity have accepted a commitment, where practicable, to prevent the introduction of non-indigenous species, and to manage biological resources so as to protect biodiversity values. That would seem to include controlling or eradicating alien species that are a threat to indigenous habitats or species, and would presumably apply, at least in principle, to the control or eradication of stocks of introduced trout! It does not happen.

The conﬂicts do not need to be absolute, i.e. a case of ‘either salmonids or galaxiids’’. Pascual et al. (2002) thought that in Patagonia the challenge was to ‘‘ﬁnd ways to protect native ﬁsh compatible with maintaining highly prized sports ﬁsheries’’. Allibone and McIntosh (1999) commented, for New Zealand, that: ‘‘The integration of sport [salmonid] and native ﬁsheries should be considered the ﬁnal goal of freshwater ﬁsheries management...and the objective that ﬁsheries research aims to provide high quality sports ﬁshing while protecting native ﬁshes and communities’’. And they thought that: ‘‘It is certainly possible to carry out programmes that mitigate sports ﬁsh impacts without reducing the recreational ﬁshing opportunities that New Zealand enjoys’’. They argued that the goal for freshwater ecosystems in New Zealand should be to maintain ‘‘a sustainable recreational sports ﬁshery, with its huge international reputation...while also protecting the unique biodiversity of the native freshwater biota’’. But I fear that not many New Zealand trout ﬁshery managers would agree with this, and even fewer anglers. Allibone and McIntosh (1999) suggested that for this to be effective there is a need for active participation of the agencies that manage sports ﬁsheries in fresh waters’’ (basically the national network of Fish and Game Councils—McDowall 1994a). And this participation does appear to be developing in New

Zealand, given that the National Fish and Game Council (according to its National Director—Johnson 2004) has ‘‘a long-standing organisational policy prohibiting salmonid releases into catchments that have never had them before’’. Note, though, that this policy applies only to catchments, and there may not be any such catchments except in the far north. Moreover, to make such releases Fish and Game Councils have to obtain site-speciﬁc approvals from the Minister for Conservation, and would be unlikely to do so. In a sense what they have agreed to no longer do is something that they would probably not be allowed to do, if they wanted to. Also, with brown trout, dispersal through, and invasion from, the sea has resulted in the species being present almost everywhere that conditions are suitable. A similar policy has evidently been in place in Tasmania since the 1980s (Fulton 2004) but its effects seem, at best, elusive.

Allibone and McIntosh (1999) suggested that this question needs to be taken a step further. They argued that many of the salmonid populations in New Zealand, especially those in higher elevation, steep, small streams, are of little or no value either for recreational angling or as nurseries for producing salmonids for downstream waterways. For some of these essentially ‘useless’ salmonids in higher elevation southern streams, cold water temperatures and the small size of streams may prevent trout from reaching a size that interests anglers (Allibone and McIntosh

- 1999), and yet they are likely to be having adverse impacts on streams’ native ﬁsh populations. These issues need to be viewed in the context of the pivotal role of headwater streams for galaxiid ﬁshes, at least in both Australia and New Zealand. These headwater salmonid populations thus, according to Allibone and McIntosh (1999), ‘‘represent a cost to the natural environment with no compensatory beneﬁts’’. On the other hand it is likely that in many trout-occupied waters the native galaxiids never grow large enough for them to have size refuge from trout predation. The National Fish and Game Councils in New Zealand has agreed in principle and ‘‘do not have a closed mind to proposals for [the removal of trout] in minor sub-catchments where a reasonable case can be made for that to occur on biodiversity grounds’’

(Johnson 2004—and note that it is now only ‘‘minor sub-catchments’’ that are included); however, regional Fish and Game Councils seem to have been less compliant in practice (R.M. Allibone and C. Miskelly pers. comm.). There has been some localised eradication of trout in waterways occupied by sensitive and threatened galaxiids. Johnson (2004) added: ‘‘...water quality and stream ﬂows protect trout and the pastime of angling, but also beneﬁt indigenous ﬁsh and other recreational users’’, a somewhat specious argument, if it is recognised that protection or fostering of increased salmonid populations by recreational angling agencies, like Fish and Game, have concomitant negative impacts on the same indigenous ﬁsh species.

Allibone and McIntosh (1999) recommended that trout populations in New Zealand ‘‘should be assessed to determine whether they have negative impacts upon native ﬁshes, and if native species would beneﬁt from their removal... [and they further suggested that] such ﬁsh could be removed and used as ‘‘stream-wise’’ ﬁsh for stocking elsewhere, and the costs of recovery could be set against hatchery costs’’. As well, they recommended a cessation of stocking into waters that do not sustain natural salmonid reproduction, wherever salmonids have serious negative impacts on native ﬁsh. And, they recommended no removal of barriers to upstream migration by trouts if doing so provided upstream access to signiﬁcant native ﬁsh populations and habitats. In addition, there is a beginning of construction of barriers in some New Zealand streams that permit differential upstream access—that facilitate movements by galaxiids but exclude salmonids (Fig. 89). This is not hard to achieve owing to the different swimming styles of galaxiids and salmonids as well a difference in body size, salmonids being much larger than galaxiids at migration from the sea.

At the regional scale the impacts of salmonids on galaxioids has been highly pervasive. Only at northern limits of the family Galaxiidae’s range may some galaxiids ﬁnd streams that lack salmonids owing to wider temperature preferences of some galaxioids. Although galaxiids tend to be cold water ﬁsh, some species have upper temperature preferences which allow them to occupy

- Fig. 89 A v-notch weir designed to facilitate passage of galaxiids that can climb but exclude trout that jump obstructions (photo: P. Ravenscroft)

streams too warm for salmonids (see discussion on this question by Closs and Lake 1996 for G. olidus).

The conservation status of the galaxioids and causal mechanisms

The foregoing documentation of the conservation status of galaxioid ﬁshes conveys a very strong sense of massive to moderate decline in the distributions and abundances of a large number of galaxioid species, and this has taken place right across the broad southern temperate range of the group—often local extirpation, rarely species’ extinction. Time after time, narratives mention introduced predatory ﬁshes, especially brown and rainbow trout. Additional salmonids have also been introduced into other southern lands but are either not widely enough introduced and/or are not well-enough known to allow explicit judgements about their impacts. The potential for impacts by some of the other, less widely introduced salmonids, such as brook charr, Atlantic and Chinook salmon should not be ignored (and some impacts are suspected—as for brook charr in New Zealand (Allibone and McDowall 1997; Allibone 2000a), and perhaps also Atlantic salmon in Chile. Michaelis (1986) expressed concern at the conservation implications for native ﬁsh of proposals for the reintroduction of Atlantic salmon to Tasmania.

The impacts of salmonids on galaxioid ﬁshes form only a tiny fraction of the whole global question of salmonid introductions and their impacts—in temperate latitudes, there are probably few groups of aquatic animals that have been more widely and deliberately stocked and acclimatised in natural habitats than salmonids (Welcomme 1988, 1992; and see Behnke 1992, 2002 for information on the impacts of salmonids on salmonids in North America). That there have been serious, adverse, ecosystem impacts from salmonid introductions across almost the entire natural and introduced ranges of the family is almost unarguable, resulting, as Lassuy (1995) put it, ‘‘from a history of poor species choices and complacency regarding their potential harm’’. This certainly includes much of North America where many salmonids are both native and widely introduced or translocated. Thus, what is seen of the impacts of salmonids in southern cool-temperate lands is just a subset of what has happened with these ﬁsh globally (Fausch 1988; Behnke 1992, 2002; Ruzycki et al. 2003; Peterson and Fausch 2004). Welcomme (1984) seemed to imply that the salmonids were now so widely distributed that additional transfers would not greatly inﬂuence their history. This, I view as a very dangerous attitude, as a careful look at the distributions and ﬁsh communities in southern rivers and lakes at ﬁner scales would, I believe, show how untrue this is. Whether adverse impacts appear to have happened may depend substantially on the scale at which comparisons are made, as Pascual et al. (2002) observed for Patagonian Argentina. Negative effects of other invasive exotics in southern lands are implicated in fewer instances, but include European perch (Cadwallader and Backhouse 1983; Morgan et al. 1998), various cichlids (f. Cichlidae), freshwater basses (Centrarchidae) (Keith 2002); and two species of Gambusia (f. Poeciliidae) (de Moor and Bruton 1988; McDowall 1990b; Skelton 1993, 2002; Barrier and Hicks 1994; Morgan et al. 1996; Rowe 1998; Gill et al. 1999; Ling and Willis 2000). The ‘apparently’ fewer damaging impacts of these additional invasive species may (apart from gambusia), relate primarily to their much less widespread introductions and more restricted geographical ranges in receiving habitats, but it

is also probably related to their having less similarity to galaxiids in habitat preferences in the receiving habitats, and/or to less overlap in distributions in their ﬁne scale ranges as exotics. Reach-scale analyses are needed, as David et al. (2002) revealed for populations of Galaxias argenteus in southern New Zealand. Salmonids are still spreading in some areas (McDowall et al.

- 2001), their effects on galaxioids are still emerging, even in places where they have long been present, and I believe that there are (perhaps new) attitudes about that we should seek to make better provision for the protection or enhancement of native galaxioid populations, where they persist and where exotic salmonids can be excluded, and where this might facilitate the restoration of the indigenous galaxiids.

Information presented in the preceding species’ narratives is summarised in Table 1, which illustrates the extent to which galaxioid ﬁshes are considered threatened by expert authors/agencies in countries where the group is present One species is Extinct; an additional eight species are regarded as Critically Endangered (or have had similar descriptors applied to them—see * in the ‘‘species threatened’’ column in Table 1); and many others are a matter for local conservation concern. Not surprisingly, threatened galaxioid species are most numerous in Australia and New Zealand, since that is where most galaxioid species are found, and it is possibly also the area where the indigenous freshwater ﬁsh faunas are best known, taxonomically and ecologically (Merrick and Schmida 1984; McDowall 1990b, 2000a; Allen et al. 2002). There are nevertheless serious concerns about the status of taxa in the less speciose regions such as New Caledonia (Keith

- 2002), Patagonian South America (Pascual et al. 2002), and the Falkland Islands (McDowall et al. 2001). Only in South Africa is there an apparent absence of published, explicit, serious concern about the local galaxiid (but see Cambray 2003a, b), though I suspect that this may be due partly to inadequate knowledge of the distribution and abundance of the South African galaxiid (see Swartz et al. 2004, for a discussion of these issues for redﬁn barbs of the family Cyprinidae, where there has been intensive study and serious impacts are clear). Also, as discussed elsewhere,

this perspective on the South African galaxiid fauna may change across a wide geographical front when the taxonomy of the group is better resolved and when, inevitably, some newly recognised taxa have more limited ranges than the range of the species complex of which they are a part—as is already explicitly clear in New Zealand, and is becoming evident in Australia. Moreover, discussion of the sorts of concern felt for the Falkland Islands biota reveals a level of concern that is only recently reﬂected in the conservation status of its galaxioids (McDowall et al. 2001, 2005).

Major problems across a wide geographical area in southern lands are caused by brown trout which, as McIntosh and Townsend (1995) generalised for New Zealand, have ‘‘replaced native galaxiids as the major predators in many streams’’. Presence of brown trout is speciﬁed as having adverse effects on galaxiids in Table 1 in 38 instances (the Retropinna species are notable exceptions). The narratives also implicate rainbow trout in galaxiid declines, although in ‘only’ 14 instances (Table 1). But, as with brown trout, almost everywhere that rainbow trout have entered galaxiid habitats there have been significant adverse impacts on the galaxiids. The impression that the rainbow trout appears to be less of a threat is almost certainly only because it is rather less widely invasive, partly, no doubt, due to its general failure to become anadromous when introduced beyond its natural range! It seems, then, that there is an inescapable conclusion that invasion of habitats by brown trout has repeatedly had very serious repercussions for the conservation of numerous populations and species of galaxioids and that, where its introduction has been successful, the same can be said of rainbow trout.

Wilson (1995) sought to absolve rainbow trout of responsibility for a decline in native galaxiids in New Zealand, and it may be true that rainbows have had less wide-ranging adverse effects, but I rather doubt it when a comparison is made of where rainbow trout is present and where it has caused problems. Certainly, the ‘jury is still out’ on this question.

The above discussion prompts questions about why the galaxioids seem to have been unable to

withstand the impacts of a combination of direct predation, habitat displacement, and competition for space and food. The issues relating to the adverse effects of these salmonids are all the more explicitly etched because of the continuing popularity of trouts and salmons among anglers, and the very obvious conﬂicts of interest that are involved in managing trout ﬁshing in southern lands.

Salmonid impacts have been especially strong in Australia and New Zealand. In their review, Jackson and Koehn (1988) mentioned that an ‘‘area of concern is the possible adverse effects of introduced species’’. Decades ago (McDowall 1968c), I expressed similar concerns for New Zealand, stating that ‘‘It seems that for the survival of the existing fauna, the New Zealand freshwater ﬁshes need a degree of protection from the introduced Salmonidae, and that the potential effects of any further introduced predatory ﬁshes should be thoroughly investigated before any introduction is made’’. Nothing that I have learned or read in the ensuing >38 years suggests that this view was erroneous or exaggerated. Allibone and McIntosh (1999) similarly, but much more recently, summarised for New Zealand: ‘‘There should be no doubt that the introduction and dispersal of sports ﬁsh [salmonids] in New Zealand has impacted on native ﬁshes, some species more than others. In very simple terms it would be highly unlikely that the present biomass of sports ﬁsh supported in New Zealand waters could have been attained without impacts upon the native communities. Possibly the only reason these impacts are not more obvious to ﬁsheries managers and the general public is the lack of knowledge about native species, the complete lack of information on native ﬁsheries and distributions prior to sports ﬁsh releases, and an unwillingness to investigate the impacts...If this decline is to be reversed and the impacts mitigated, then sports ﬁsh management in New Zealand need to carefully consider interactions between sports and native ﬁsh’’. As discussed above New Zealand came ‘within a whisker’ of having largemouth bass introduced, and the potential impacts for the native species are almost unimaginable. But it was probably the potential impacts on salmonid ﬁsheries that was a pivotal

reason for rejection of the bass—the people who know little or nothing of history are still suggesting that bass could be introduced, though it is smallmouth bass, Micropterus dolomieu that is now being suggested (McKenzie 2004; McDowall 2005a). It will not happen.

I fear that were the distributions and abundances of galaxioids from other areas as well known as they are in Australia and, especially New Zealand, there would also be much the same level of concern. Pascual et al. (2002) have certainly hinted of this in Patagonia, remarking that ‘‘Salmonids feed heavily on some native ﬁsh, particularly on galaxiids’’ (both Galaxias and Aplochiton). And so has Cambray (1997, 2003a, b) in South Africa, who (Cambray 1997), appears to concur, stating that ‘‘there are many problems caused by the stocking of natural waters with ‘wanted species’ [but] to the detriment of indigenous ﬁsh stocks’’. Introduced sports ﬁsh there include brown and rainbow trout, largemouth, smallmouth and spotted bass, also various cichlids, as well as gambusia. In New Caledonia it seems that centrarchid basses and cichlids have been the main culprits (Keith 2002).

Allibone and McIntosh (1999) argued that in New Zealand: ‘‘It has been common and convenient to blame decline in native ﬁsh on changes affecting the quality of habitat occupied by native ﬁsh’’, but they agreed that this cannot be considered the only impact. ‘‘Substantial reductions in the range and abundance of some native ﬁsh species in New Zealand are certain, their presentday distributions rarely overlapping with sports ﬁsh, indicating [that] displacement has occurred...some freshwater ecosystems are also rarely free of sports ﬁsh ...the continuing legal, illegal, and natural range expansions by sports ﬁsh threatens the remaining sport ﬁsh free areas of mainland New Zealand’’. Allibone and McIntosh (1999) further thought that ‘‘Generalisations about the nature and degree of interaction are difﬁcult to make due to the various galaxiid species involved and the varying physical nature of the streams, but negative interactions certainly occur’’, and they concluded that displacement of ‘‘non-migratory galaxiids in all cases appears [to be] associated with two factors: stable streams

that provide cover for adult salmonids (>150 mm) and self-reproducing salmonid stocks (Allibone and McIntosh 1999).

Leidy and Moyle (1998) generalised that for ‘‘most described [ﬁsh] species, little is known about their life histories, environmental requirements, or present status’’. The preceding sections of this paper suggests that this broad generalisation does not apply across the diversity of the galaxioid ﬁshes, though inevitably some species are very much better known than others, and some of them remain poorly known. As a global generality, the galaxiids are probably quite well known, though when it comes to addressing species-speciﬁc issues of ecology and details of behaviour, life history and conservation, all sorts of knowledge gaps quickly emerge, and this prompted Wager and Jackson (1993) to write of Australia that: ‘‘Many aspects of the biology and ecology of native ﬁshes are poorly known. In addition, nearly all of the...threatening processes are inadequately understood. Options for the conservation and management of threatened and non-threatened species are inhibited by lack of knowledge. Appropriate research is seldom funded and there are few long term monitoring programmes to allow proper assessment of the effects of threatening processes’’. How true, and yet would they have written anything much different if a great deal more was known?

Knowledge of species and their conservation status and needs varies with:

- • Whether their areas of distribution are well explored;
- • How well the group is understood taxonomically;
- • Whether or not species have commercial or ﬁsheries interest;
- • Whether or not there are major conﬂicts of interest from either a ﬁsheries or water management perspective;
- • How big they are and how easily they can be observed;
- • Whether they can be kept in captivity and how attractive they are to look at;
- • Whether some iconic label can be attached to them from any of the above perspectives; and as well:

• Whether or not what is known about them attracts attention from a conservation perspective.

Clarifying the effects of salmonids on galaxiids is substantially obfuscated by their development of distributions complementary to those of introduced trouts (Fausch 1988; Allibone and McIntosh 1999; Pascual et al. 2002)—it seems that the trouts tend to invade streams, either (usually?) physically displacing the galaxiids totally, or forcing them to occupy sub-optimal habitats, and/or consuming them. The outcome can be that surveys of streams demonstrate what might seem to be different habitat ‘preferences’ and thus a lack of impacts by the salmonids, e.g. brown trout and Galaxias argenteus when observed at the stream reach scale (David and Closs 2002; David 2003), but in fact it is an outcome of agonistic interactions. This could also apply to predation. Minns (1990) examined the distributions of indigenous and exotic ﬁshes in New Zealand based on an extensive dataset (nearly 6,500 sites) and concluded that, although brown trout quite frequently co-occurred with native galaxiids in river systems, ‘‘At the site level, native species were generally not grouped with exotic species, indicating species segregation within distributional overlaps. This contrast between regional and site level clusterings might be interpreted as being the result of exclusion owing to competition and/or predation, or as reﬂecting the occurrence of different species in different habitats within regions’’. He found that most of the negative associations in the dataset between native and exotic species were accounted for by salmonids. Thus, general distribution patterns derived from a broadscale analysis at the familial level sometimes indicated complementarity in distributions of brown trout and native species, which is consistent with the complementarity revealed by detailed species by species comparisons. This is especially true at the level of site comparisons (rather than river reach or river system scales, or even broader).

Allibone and McIntosh (1999) found it ironic that ‘‘the most serious consequences of predation are unlikely to be obvious from present day records of [salmonid] stomach contents...native

ﬁsh are likely to be quickly eliminated’’, and therefore be no longer available as prey for trout. It is of course both simple and simplistic to argue that salmonids are having no effects on contemporary galaxiid populations when the galaxiids have been extirpated from salmonid habitats by salmonid predation and competition and we are looking at the outcome rather than the process. And, of course, often, it may be hard to know just what has happened (Fausch 1988).

Allibone and McIntosh (1999) found that dietary surveys of New Zealand salmonids showed that bullies (eleotrids of the genus Gobiomorphus) occur most frequently in sport ﬁsh [salmonid] stomachs, but they argued that it is the galaxiids that are most vulnerable to trout predation, leading to complementary distributions. These are general problems aired also by Pascual et al. (2002) for Argentina.

Fig. 90 Varying species richness with distance upstream from the sea in diadromous species in Grey River, West Coast, South Island, New Zealand

The place of diadromy in galaxiid conservation

About 15 of the 56 galaxioid species are diadromous, i.e. they have regular, seasonal, and sometimes obligatory migrations to and from the sea during their life cycles (McDowall 1988). Because each species occupies such divergent and spatially separated habitats during its complex life cycle, it is crucial that conservation of diadromous species should address their needs at the ecosystem scale (Skelton 1990). Diadromous (especially amphidromous) species tend to have distributions that are focussed on downstream habitats, simply because their juveniles progressively settle into suitable habitats as they migrate upstream from the sea (McDowall 1998a)—although there is substantial variation in the extent to which diadromous species penetrate inland.
