This tends to lead to a downstream–upstream increase in species richness in faunas where diadromous species abound, as in New Zealand (Fig. 90); the prevalence of downstream occurrence renders diadromous species vulnerable to land development, river modiﬁcation, deforestation, construction of impoundments and pollution because these processes tend to be more intense at low elevations where topography may be ﬂatter, temperatures warmer. Also, the land is more accessible and

suited to pastoral or horticultural development and human settlement, and because, at least at some stage in each individual ﬁsh’s life, lowland habitats are important, even if only as pathways to habitats at higher elevations.

The existence of diadromy may increase ﬁsh species’ vulnerability to population decline and extirpation, especially where decline results from anthropogenic barriers to migration (such as dams and weirs, but also river mouth blockage and stream reach dewatering). As a result there are distinctive and complex conservation problems for diadromous galaxioids (McDowall 1992a, 1999a) that relate speciﬁcally to their need for upstream/downstream access for migrating life stages. It is therefore no surprise that many diadromous galaxioid species are regarded as threatened (see Table 1).

There is no hint that diadromous galaxioid ﬁshes are capable of homing on return from the sea to fresh water as small juveniles (though it would make some sense if they did), and some evidence suggests that they do not home. In at least four species egg development takes place in the humid atmosphere amongst gravels and/or forest or other vegetation and litter along the margins of the stream ﬂows, i.e. not in the water of the stream involved (G. brevipinnis, G. post-

vectis, G. fasciatus, and G. maculatus). The fact that in some species embryonic development is out of the stream ﬂows, and the small size of the larvae (<10 mm), plus their emigration to sea immediately on hatching, suggests that there is minimal opportunity for imprinting on the odours of the spawning streams. The stage of morphological development of the larvae emigrating to see is rudimentary, perhaps also reducing the likelihood of imprinting. Moreover, at least in New Zealand, there is clear evidence that, when the ﬁsh return to fresh water some 4–6 months after emigrating as larvae to sea, there are migrations into streams that have no spawning grounds (so that the returning ﬁsh could not have originated there—McDowall and Eldon 1980); also river systems that have blocked mouths, sometimes for years, may break out at times of high ﬂows, and the juveniles are known to rapidly start running into those systems if the season is right (McDowall 2003c). Finally, there seems little reason to conclude that the size of the return migration bears any relationship to the spawning productivity of river systems. All the above said, however, the possibility should not be ignored that the ﬁsh are capable of doing far more than we credit them with, and also that a stream’s odour signature could be derived from the vegetation and litter amongst which the eggs develop.

There have been suggestions of a role for kin recognition in guiding amphidromous galaxiids into speciﬁc rivers on their return from the sea (Rowe et al. 1992), though this question needs additional study. Baker and Montgomery (2001) described attractions between conspeciﬁcs as due to ‘‘adult pheromones’’, but did not identify the presence of attractant chemicals, and did not distinguish between: (1) pheromones, as explicitly evolved, produced and released chemicals that function to attract conspeciﬁcs, and (2) other emitted chemicals such as body wastes that may function as attractants without an explicit, purposeful function as attractants that may facilitate kin recognition. This difference, which appears to have been unimportant to Baker and Montgomery (2001), seems to me to be crucially important in terms of interpreting migratory behaviour in an evolutionary/phylogenetic context.

These points remain unresolved and require further study. They are important to two quite contrary aspects of the role of the return of stock from the sea:

- • On the one hand they are important in relation to the conservation management of groups such as the diadromous galaxioids in the context of ‘‘sources and sinks’’ in community ecology (Pulliam 1988; Dunning et al. 1992). It has been suggested elsewhere (McDowall 1990b, 1999a) that conservation of diadromous ﬁshes could be seriously impaired if progeny from reproductively positive habitats (sources) are dispersed at sea, in the absence of explicit guidance mechanisms such as kin recognition, pheromones or homing. This absence could result in them being spread across a diversity of habitats, including some with variable, and sometimes no effective, reproductive output (sinks). In theory, at least, this process may be crucially important to conserving diadromous species, and may increase the likelihood that some mechanisms have evolved for guiding the returning juveniles back into favourable habitats in fresh water.
- • On the other hand, there must be recognition that populations of diadromous galaxiids are dispersing in the sea around the coastlines and that this gives these species powerful abilities to restore populations to freshwater habitats in which perturbations have caused local extirpations (as discussed generally for diadromous ﬁshes in McDowall [1996b] and in relation to a particular perturbation scenario, a massive volcanic eruption in New Zealand, in McDowall [1996a]) or allows them to invade habitats that become newly congenial for ﬁsh to inhabit, as with the retreat of glaciers (Legendre and Legendre 1984; Black et al. 1986; McDowall 1996a, b; Milner and Bailey 1989).

There are general issues of importance to galaxioid conservation that also involve the presence of anadromous (sea-run) stocks of introduced brown trout. The presence of diadromy in some galaxioids, and the associated need that they have for upstream/downstream access in river

systems, mean that the access that permits the migrations of these diadromous galaxioids, is also available for invasion of the same rivers by anadromous stocks of brown trout—which are now widely present across the geographical range of the galaxioids. The implications could be very serious for conservation of diadromous galaxioids, as has been particularly articulated, above, for Falkland Islands populations of Aplochiton zebra. Diadromous populations of A. zebra are predicted to disappear if brown trout continue to expand across the Falklands (McDowall et al. 2001, 2005).

Some of these diadromous Galaxias species are remarkable climbers, moving up vertical rock faces tens of metres high (Fig. 91). They climb falls by moving lateral to the main stream ﬂows and wriggle up the rock of the wetted margins by attaching themselves to the rock faces of falls simply by surface tension; ﬁsh observed climbing have always been small juveniles, which have low weight to surface area ratios. Their climbing ability affords them some protection from trout invasion as they can penetrate upstream past falls that salmonids are unable to breach—though this presupposes that upstream-migrating juvenile galaxiids are also able to survive the gauntlet of predatory salmonids below the falls and in the lower reaches of river systems leading to the falls (McDowall 2003a).

Landlocking in diadromous species as a conservation escape

Diadromy is a facultative life history trait in many diadromous species (McDowall 1988). More than half the known diadromous galaxioid species can establish landlocked populations in which migrations to and from the sea are abandoned (see Table 1). This nearly always happens in lakes —although not always, as in Western Australian populations of Galaxias maculatus, which are ﬂuvial.

Landlocking thereby offers some protection for populations from some of the hazards discussed in the previous section, viz. from interference to migrations from anthropogenic inﬂuences, and from invasion of habitats by anadromous brown

Fig. 91 Falls in a small stream up which some diadromous galaxiids can climb

trout. To that extent landlocked populations can provide a limited kind of ‘back-up’ resource for diadromous species under threat. But landlocked populations should be regarded as distinctly reduced representations of such species, rather than as acceptable substitutes for conservation of the full behavioural and genetic diversity of diadromous species. The likelihood, should diadromous populations become extirpated, that these landlocked populations could be used to resuscitate the diadromous stocks is unknown—it may depend, among other factors, on the extent to which natural genetic diversity has been preserved in the landlocked stocks and how long the landlocked population has been established. The longer the time, perhaps, the lower the likelihood that the stock has retained its ability to make the physiological adjustments involved in the biome shift between marine and freshwater habitats. Surely, a landlocked stock of an otherwise extinct

species is far better than nothing, but it is a minimal rather than optimal representation.

The question of species complexes

Additional to the identiﬁed conservation concerns for galaxioid ﬁshes, discussed above, is the likelihood that some of the more widespread, non-diadromous galaxioid ‘species’ may comprise species complexes. This is not a novel situation, and Lundberg et al. (2000), for instance, recognised the widely ‘‘important need for [taxonomic] studies in order that [freshwater ﬁsh] species be properly conserved and managed’’. Galaxioid taxa involved include Galaxias occidentalis (Watts et al. 1995, in Western Australia); perhaps also G. truttaceus there (Morgan 2003); G. olidus (Raadik 2001; Raadik and Kuiter 2002) in eastern Australia, G. platei in Patagonian South America, G. zebratus in southern Africa (Waters and Cambray 1997; McDowall 2001 and author, unpubl.), and perhaps also Brachygalaxias bullocki in Chile (Busse 1982; Berra et al. 1995; Cuevas et al. 1999). Notably, among these possible species complexes, G. platei and B. bullocki are regarded as threatened as a whole.

The extent to which more ﬁne-scale resolution of the taxonomy of these complexes results in identiﬁcation of new taxa with highly restricted distributions that make them seem vulnerable to decline and extinction, remains to be seen. Several of the newly revealed species in the G. vulgaris complex in New Zealand have highly restricted ranges, e.g., G. pullus, G. eldoni and some additional lineages that are not yet formally recognised, as well as others in the pencil galaxias complex such as G. cobitinis, G. macronasus, and the mudﬁsh N. heleios (McDowall and Wallis 1996; McDowall 1997b; McDowall and Chadderton

- 1999; McDowall and Waters 2002, 2003; Ling and Gleeson 2001). This point will almost certainly emerge as true also in South Africa (McDowall 2001). Some of these newly recognised taxa or lineages are already threatened, the degree of threat perhaps being hitherto obscured by the coarse levels of taxonomic resolution in some instances (instances of ‘uninformed ignorance’).

When the taxonomy of the Australian G. olidus is addressed, it certainly will be shown to be a

complex of several species (Raadik 2001; Raadik and Kuiter 2002; T. Raadik pers. comm.) at least some of which seem highly likely to be shown to be endangered by the invasion of their habitats by exotic salmonids. Recognition of G. fuscus from among the G. olidus species complex, and its designation as seriously endangered (Raadik et al. 1996; Raadik and Kuiter 2002), thus may be a foretaste of what will be discovered when the wider group’s taxonomy is clariﬁed. As ongoing taxonomic work recognises additional species within various of these complexes, it seems inevitable that the level of conservation threat and concern among the galaxioid ﬁshes will rise since it is certain that the geographical ranges of newly recognised groups will be narrower than the broader species-groups to which they belong.

Whether or not the various genetically and/or morphologically distinct populations of galaxiids require formal taxonomic recognition, there is a need to give some attention to the conservation of different genetic stocks within species. Studies of galaxiids trail well behind the sort of work that has been done, particularly, on salmonids both in Western Europe (Ferguson and Taggart 1991) and North America (Behnke 1992, 2002), where the recognition of stocks as evolutionarily significant units has played an important part in developing conservation protocols (Waples 1994). The issues raised in these works on salmonids apply to freshwater ﬁshes generally, and are relevant to galaxiid conservation.

The question of hybridisation

Hybridisation is often mentioned as an important aspect of freshwater ﬁsh conservation (see Krueger and May 1991, and Behnke 1992, 2002 for discussion of this in the trouts of western North America). There is some evidence among the Galaxias vulgaris species complex that hybridisation has been taking place between different lineages, and to the extent that this is so, it is an element that needs to be considered in ensuring survival of unmodiﬁed galaxiid biodiversity. Allibone et al. (1996) recognised a low level of probably naturally occurring hybridisation between two sympatric species of Galaxias, later identiﬁed as G. anomalus and G. depressiceps

(McDowall and Wallis 1996), in Healy Creek, a tributary of the upper Taieri River, in Otago, New Zealand. Esa et al. (2001) described a population comprising hybrids of G. depressiceps and Galaxias ‘‘sp. D’’, another galaxiid lineage not presently formally recognised as taxonomically distinct, in another tributary of the Taieri River; in this latter instance it is believed that the two lineages may have been brought into sympatry by construction of water races by gold miners during the late 19th century, which created ﬂuvial connections between separate river drainages that contained different galaxiid lineages—which were then brought into artiﬁcial sympatry. Allibone (2000b) postulated that presence of physically deformed ﬁsh in this hybrid zone may have been a consequence of hybridisation.

Additional hybrid stocks of various other members of the Galaxias vulgaris species complex appear to be present in the nearby Shag River, again possibly as a result of goldmining-related stream diversions. The extent to which these instances of hybridisation involve introgression between two closely related sister lineages, as opposed to more distantly related lineages, among the G. vulgaris species complex, is uncertain. Phylogenetic, biogeographical and ecological studies are needed.

Diseases and parasites

It is sometimes suggested that another damaging impact of introduced species is that they may carry with them diseases and parasites that could have catastrophic impacts on species in the receiving ecosystem. There are few instances where this has been seriously suggested among galaxioids. There was a purported outbreak of ‘‘fungus’’ in a population of the Tasmanian Prototroctes maraena in the 1860s and 1870s (Saville-Kent 1888). In this instance cause and diagnosis are far from certain. Hall (1983) reported the presence of the parasitic copepod Lernaea cyprinacea also on P. maraena whereas Langdon (1989) accused European perch of being the vector for a viral disease, epizootic haematopoietic necrosis that can be fatal for Galaxias olidus (though he did not claim widespread mortalities). Otherwise there is no suggestion that introduced species carrying

diseases are causing problems for indigenous galaxiids at release sites, unless the almost universal presence of Ichthyophthirius in New Zealand fresh waters is originally a result of ﬁsh introductions. Salmonids have generally carried few diseases with them into receiving habitats (Hewitt and Hine 1972; Hine et al. 2002; Beumer et al. 1983; Semenas et al. 1989; Ortubay et al. 1994). This has probably been because many trout importations have been of fertilised eggs, and there is no evidence to indict any of them for carrying parasites of signiﬁcance to indigenous biota anywhere within the range of the galaxioid ﬁshes—in fact the reverse has been true. Salmonids have picked up local parasites from indigenous species (McDowall 2000b).

Why have the salmonids been so damaging for the galaxioids?

Why Northern Hemisphere salmonids have had such impacts on the southern galaxioids is not explicitly clear, Although galaxioids and salmonids comprise somewhat related taxa among the lower euteleostean ﬁshes (McDowall 1969; Johnson and Patterson 1996; Waters et al. 2002b) it would be simplistic to suggest that the intensity of salmonid impacts on galaxioids is due to the two groups sharing a close common evolutionary ancestry. A common ancestry does not necessarily imply ecological similarity, or give one of the groups ‘mastery’ over the other and, anyway, why salmonids over galaxiids (though the great size advantage of salmonids over galaxiids is likely to make the results of any interactions inevitably go the way of the salmonids)? The galaxioids are much more diversiﬁed than salmonids, morphologically, behaviourally, and in habitats occupied. And, of course, no galaxiids have been introduced into natural salmonid habitats. Moreover, the shared evolutionary roots of galaxiids and salmonids must be very ancient, dating back at least to the mid-late Mesozoic. Fossils of recognisable salmonids and osmerids are known back to the upper Cretaceous to early Tertiary times (Romer 1966; Wilson and Williams 1991; Wilson and Li 1999), and even extant species of Oncorhynchus have been found in the Pliocene (Cavender and Miller 1972; Stearley and Smith 1993). Fossil

galaxiids resembling extant species are known from the Miocene of New Zealand (McDowall

- 1976b; McDowall and Pole 1997; Lee et al. submitted). Thus the northern salmonoid/osmeroid radiations and the southern galaxioid radiation seem likely to have had very long, independent, evolutionary histories, and attributing impacts to common ancestry is far-fetched. (My description of the introductions of salmonids into the ranges of galaxiids as a ‘‘family reunion’’ (McDowall 1990a), which recognised the relationships of galaxioids and salmonids, was intended entirely as a literary device rather than to point to any biological phenomenon involving galaxioid or salmonid ﬁshes). It may be nothing more than that when anglers were choosing which species to introduce into New Zealand, they looked for ﬁsh that grew large, were predatory, active and traditionally popular among Northern Hemisphere anglers. Other introduced species, such as various cyprinids, came to both New Zealand and Australia from the same British ecosystems, but are not identiﬁed as causing problems in southern lands (McDowall 1990b). Furthermore, galaxiids seem to have suffered serious impacts from invasive trout, whereas retropinnids in the same supposedly ‘simple’ ecosystems in New Zealand and Australia have not. And so, regardless of the phylogenetic relationships between salmonids and galaxioids, a cause for adverse impacts of salmonids must be sought primarily in ecology rather than history. They have become immensely widespread in southern cool lands, as indicated by their distributions in New Zealand (Fig. 92).

Townsend and Crowl (1991) followed Thorp (1986), in considering that ‘‘the unequivocal effects of predation seem to have been demonstrated mainly in systems...where prey had no common evolutionary history with the predators’’. Moreover, they thought that, for galaxiids in New Zealand, ‘‘the strong predation effect is probably a result of New Zealand’s comparative paucity of native piscivorous ﬁshes, resulting in a native ﬁsh fauna having no evolved defences [or] evolved predator escape mechanisms’’. As argued, the intrusion of large, proliﬁc predators (like salmonids) into stream ecosystems generally lacking such predators seems likely to have

contributed to decline of galaxiid prey species. In as much as salmonids introduced into Australian, New Zealand and Patagonian waterways are, like the native galaxioids, primarily generalised aquatic invertebrate predators, it should perhaps occasion little surprise that impacts on galaxiids have been serious. To these potential dangers may be added the vulnerability of often small populations to the combination of low genetic diversity and stochastic genetic and environmental processes.

McIntosh and Townsend (1995) pointed to brown trout having evolved in the Northern Hemisphere with many co-occurring ﬁsh species, and thought that this would have been inﬂuential in the impacts of brown trout in New Zealand’s relatively impoverished freshwater ﬁsh communities (and the same argument could be made for rainbow trout). They considered that this advantage, as well as the trout species growing to a large size, being aggressive and competitive in the battle for optimal foraging sites, would have given the trout species an advantage. However, it is hardly true that brown trout ecosystems in the United Kingdom (Maitland and Campbell 1992: 51 species, 13 of them introduced) are really so much more species rich than those they encountered in New Zealand, Australia or Patagonia (McDowall 1990b; Merrick and Schmida 1984; Allen et al. 2002; Pascual et al. 2002). There are as many or more species in some rifﬂes in North American rivers (Taylor 1969), than in all of New Zealand (or the United Kingdom), and no doubt the same is true in some parts of Asia, South America, and Africa. Furthermore, though both trout and various of the galaxiids may prey upon benthic invertebrate drift, Allibone and McIntosh (1999) observed that whereas trout adopt a mid-water feeding position, the galaxiids tended to rise from the bottom when taking drifting insects, and this may give the trout an additional competitive advantage. As well, the galaxiids were shown to be more nocturnal and are likely, also, to also have much lower reaction distances when drift feeding. In practice, it may simply be that salmonids can ‘‘control the supply of key resources to native ﬁsh’’ (Allibone and McIntosh 1999).

Much of the detailed work and commentary on galaxioid/trout interactions has related to

Fig. 92 Very wide ranges in New Zealand of alien salmonids: (A) Brown trout, Salmo trutta; (B) Rainbow trout, Oncorhynchus mykiss

streams. However, there has been examination of some of these questions in lakes, including G. brevipinnis in several New Zealand lakes, G. gracilis in a group of small coastal dune lakes, and also G. pedderensis in the new Lake Pedder and G. tanycephalus in other lakes in Tasmania. Effects on galaxioids in lakes, and probably in estuaries, may also be equally serious, though data that explicitly document these impacts are less available (see discussions above), and also much harder to deﬁne.

With regard to New Zealand’s freshwater ﬁshes Thomson (1922) was already implicating salmonids, long before there was any nascent understanding of invasion theory. He stated that: ‘‘The introduction of species of Salmonidae, of perch, carp, etc. into the lakes and rivers of most parts of [New Zealand] has produced equally important changes. Some ﬁsh species have been exterminated wherever the introduced forms have established themselves’’. Thomson also included diverse aquatic macroinvertebrates within the ambit of his discussion, summarising that: ‘‘Among naturalists of the last half century there has been a strong belief that the introduced fauna and ﬂora have been directly responsible for the

diminution of many, and the disappearance of some indigenous animals and plants’’ (see Tillyard 1920).

A contrary view

Fulton (2004) seems almost alone in seeking to absolve salmonids from harmful impacts on galaxioid ﬁshes. In a review prepared for the State Government of South Australia, Fulton found himself unable to conclude that there was a case to answer for salmonid impacts. He wrote that ‘‘The degree to which...competition may impact on the native fauna is often not readily apparent due to the time scales involved since the ﬁrst introductions were made, and our lack of prior information on native ﬁsh distributions and numbers ... in most cases it would be impossible to determine what particular element of change has been detrimental to the native fauna’’. While that may be substantially true, these impediments had not stopped Fulton from earlier pointing to damaging salmonid impacts on a diversity of Tasmanian galaxiids, e.g. Fulton (1978b) considered that wholly freshwater galaxiids were not able to coexist with trout, and (Sanger and Fulton 1991) that the primary

environmental requirement for G. johnstoni was isolation from trout. Fulton (2004) was commissioned by the South Australian Government to review the relevant literature, but his choice of published material was incomplete and selective (including neglect of his own earlier assertions) so that the conclusions that he could derive from this material do not reﬂect the overall weight of past views expressed, including his own. Moreover, some of his interpretations of earlier publications seem inconsistent with what these publications said. He argued, for instance, that the paper of Closs and Lake (1996) demonstrated that it was climatic factors that were leading to the decline of G. olidus in some Victorian streams, whereas this is not what Closs and Lake said, or anything like it. What they did say was that climatic factors were affecting the upstream distribution limits of brown trout. In effect, what was happening was that in hot, dry summers, the middle reaches of a stream were becoming restricted to a series of isolated pools and, as a result, water temperatures rose and the trout were locally extirpated. This allowed the G. olidus stocks from reaches further upstream, to move down into reaches from which the trout had disappeared. But, when normal ﬂows returned, the trout moved upstream again, extirpating the downstream-moving galaxiid stocks. So local climatic factors were controlling the range of the trout, and how this control was exercised determined the distribution of the galaxiids (discussed in detail, above). Fulton (2004), when arguing that there was no evidence to support a conclusion that trout were affecting galaxiid distributions and abundance, cited the work of Lintermans (2000). But he failed to acknowledge that the explicit purpose and outcome of Lintermans’ study was to demonstrate, as he did, that when trout were removed from a reach of stream, this reach was immediately colonised by downstream movements of G. olidus. The only logical conclusion is that the trout had hitherto been displacing the galaxiid. Similarly, Fulton cited the work of Fletcher (1979), but failed to acknowledge that the primary outcome of this work, and based on experimental protocols that controlled for the presence or absence of trout, was to show that G. olidus responded adversely to the presence of trout.

Fulton (2004) also cited work undertaken on this issue in New Zealand, and pointed to the papers of Hayes et al. (1989), Hanchet (1990), Minns (1990) and Swales and West (1991) as demonstrating that there had been no inﬂuence of trout on galaxiid distributions. But of these papers, only Minns addressed the question of trout impacts and that paper several times pointed explicitly to trout and galaxiids having distinctly complementary distributions. He referred to the New Zealand work of Townsend and Crowl (1991) and cited them as stating that habitat change was not one of the widespread factors likely to have contributed to decline in galaxiid distributions. In that, Fulton reﬂects Townsend and Crowl’s conclusion, but he failed to cite Townsend and Crowl’s (1991) other conclusion that the factor most likely to contribute to presence of galaxiids was the absence of trout. Fulton (2004) referred to decline to near extinction of Galaxias pedderensis following formation of the new Lake Pedder, stating that the ‘‘decline in numbers of G. pedderensis are [sic] ...at least in part...due to translocation of Australian native species’’. What he did not say was that the other ‘part’ of this decline is widely considered to be proliferation of brown trout in the lake (Sanger 1988; Gaffney et al. 1992; Hamr 1992a, b, 1995, and see above). Fulton (2004) cited Fulton (1990) for this view, which is not to be found in the latter publication (a popular booklet on the Tasmanian freshwater ﬁsh fauna), at all. And so Fulton (2004) did not consider ‘‘that trout are a major impediment to the security of native ﬁsh populations, nor do they appear to have been the major determinant of the present discontinuous distributions of native species such as G. olidus’’. He perceived no need to change the trout stocking programme in South Australia as, in his view, ‘‘there is only marginal overlap with the existing [G. olidus] habitat’’, but in reaching this conclusion he failed to address the real likelihood that the present complementary distributions of G. olidus and trout in South Australian streams are an outcome of displacement of the galaxiid by trout, quite apart from this conclusion being contrary to numerous studies of impacts of trout on G. olidus. He did say that trout stocking practices in Tasmania were adjusted to limit

predation of trout on galaxiids, and one might ask both:

- 1. Why was this done, other than to try and avoid adverse effects on the galaxiids?
- 2. Was it effective?

Finally, it is curious that in his analysis, Fulton

- (2004) mentioned that there has been a policy since the 1980s of not stocking salmonids into Tasmanian waters where they do not already exist, this presumably being primarily to avoid adverse impacts of trout on the indigenous biota, though Fulton (2004) evidently believed that there was no case to answer. Thus in the only explicit defence of introduced trout in relation to concern for the conservation of native galaxiids in streams (apart from Allen 1961), Fulton (2004) presents a rather incomplete picture of the general state of knowledge and of the attitudes of other ﬁsheries biologists in both Australia and New Zealand.

One, almost subversive, aspect of the impacts of salmonids on galaxioids may well have been the tendency for trout invasion to isolate small, headwater populations of galaxiids, often above falls by extirpating the populations downstream of falls—and this has been suggested again and again (and again) in the history of galaxioid conservation. Headwater fragmentation of stocks may have had the effect of reducing galaxiid population size to the point where genetic effects and/or stochastic population ﬂuctuations may become inﬂuential in population viability and survival. It also means that the above-fall populations are totally genetically isolated from populations elsewhere in the system (not that this makes much difference to populations above falls that the galaxiids cannot climb, either).

An unpredictable aspect of the potential ranges and impacts of species introduced into new ecosystems is the extent to which they may appear to spread into areas with wider ranges of habitat characteristics than they occupy in their natural ranges. This may have several explanations.

• One is that the species’ natural range may be constrained by historical/biogeographical effects rather than environmental causes, and

that they tolerate far broader conditions than those represented by the localities in which such species are naturally found.

- • Another is that when translocated, species may be under less pressure from competitors for space, foraging positions, and food itself, predators, or pathogens, and these reduced pressures may, for instance, enable them to cope with more stressful minimum winter temperatures than they can in natural habitats.
- • Also, environmental preferences or tolerances, or fundamental life history parameters, may evolve rapidly in new environments (Kinnison et al. 2002).

As argued, the intrusion of large, proliﬁc predators (like salmonids) into stream ecosystems generally lacking such predators certainly seems likely to have contributed to a decline of galaxiid ‘prey’ species. In as much as salmonids introduced into Australian, New Zealand and Patagonian waterways are, like the native galaxioids, primarily generalised aquatic invertebrate drift feeding ﬁshes, it should perhaps occasion little surprise that impacts on galaxiids have been serious. To these potential dangers may be added the vulnerability of small populations to the combination of low genetic diversity and stochastic genetic and environmental processes.

Other galaxoiod ﬁshes as invasive species

The galaxioid ﬁshes cannot themselves be entirely exonerated as invasive species, as shown by the discussion above of the likely impacts of introduced Retropinna retropinna on lacustrine populations of Galaxias brevipinnis in Lakes Taupo and Rotopounamu (New Zealand—Rowe 1993) and, in turn, the effects of G. brevipinnis on various other galaxiid species in both New Zealand (McDowall and Allibone 1994; Allibone 1999) and Australia (Sanger 1988; Sanger and Fulton 1991; Hamr 1992a, b; Gaffney et al. 1992). These impacts of one galaxioid species on another seem also to point to vulnerability of some galaxioids to invasion, competition and predation. The ﬁnal effects of these galaxiid translocations and invasions may be still to emerge.

Retropinnids and salmonid predation

The apparent ability of lacustrine stocks of New Zealand’s Retropinna retropinna to withstand enduring and heavy predation by huge stocks of exotic rainbow and brown trout, in Lake Taupo, New Zealand’s single biggest trout ﬁshery, as well as in other lakes, is an enigma for which there is no obvious, present explanation. Perhaps tolerance of intensive salmonid predation relates partly to the fact that R. retropinna exploits a different feeding niche from the salmonids (surface lake plankton for much of the time) which may isolate Retropinna from some aspects of trout predation. By contrast, only larval and juvenile phases of lacustrine G. brevipinnis exploit surface-living lake planktonic foods, whereas the late juveniles through to adults live around the lake shores, are benthic in the lakes, or inhabit lake tributaries where they remain vulnerable to trout predation. Perhaps, too, lacustrine, pelagic species can build up much larger populations than the galaxiids—which tend to be benthic in the lake or live in tributary streams as adults.

Cowx (2002) presented an analysis showing that ﬁsh species introductions were mentioned as likely causes of conservation threat for native ﬁsh more often than almost any other type of ecosystem perturbation, ranking it a little more common than the construction of dams and weirs, and water quality problems. Had his analysis dealt with only the galaxioid ﬁshes, the pre-eminent impacts of introduced species would have been much more obvious, substantially driven by introductions of salmonids—so much so that the salmonid introductions rank very high among the litany of introduced species’ impacts. Moreover, of course, impacts of salmonids on galaxioid ﬁshes, discussed above, form only a tiny fraction of the global question of salmonid introductions and their impacts—in temperate latitudes of the world, there are probably few groups of aquatic animals that have been more widely and deliberately translocated and acclimatised to natural habitats than salmonids (Welcomme 1988, 1992, and see Behnke 1992, 2002, for information on the impacts of salmonids on salmonids in North America). And that there have been serious

ecosystem impacts across the entire natural and introduced ranges of salmonids is unarguable, resulting, as Lassuy (1995) put it, ‘‘from a history of poor species choices and complacency regarding their potential harm’’.

Ruzycki et al. (2003) considered that: ‘‘When ... introduced species become established a clientele often develops that exploits the resource and lobbies for maintaining the introduced species’’, and this is most certainly true of introduced salmonids in many parts of the world. Lassuy (1995) believed that there is still a great need for ‘‘a change of attitude from one dominated by value judgements based on immediate human beneﬁt to one that values the integrity of native ecosystems and all of its component species’’, which he considered to be ‘‘a huge long-term beneﬁt to our children’s children, indeed to the human species’’. And, again, this is certainly an issue with regard to salmonid introductions—there is a substantial conﬂict of values and interests where salmonids have been introduced into new habitats and areas, as they become popular ﬁsh for anglers and generate revenue to human communities where salmonid ﬁsheries become established. Anglers in many countries seem to become used to having access to salmonids, regarding it as their right—even in places where they are introduced aliens. In many instances they not only fail to realise that the ﬁsh they are angling for are introduced; they seem to seldom know or care anything about the native ﬁsh faunas (except perhaps where they are useful forage species for salmonids). Also, eradication of salmonids, even if practicable, is often not desired owing to their perceived value. Despite possible opposition, Wager and Jackson (1993) thought that ‘‘speciﬁc eradication programs in streams containing threatened ﬁshes are a valid management tool’’, and it has been applied in some instances, as described: by Lintermans (2000), where rainbow trout had eliminated G. olidus from a stream reach in the Australian Capital Territory; by Raadik (1995, 2002) to protect populations of G. fuscus in Victoria; was proposed to protect populations of G. pullus from predation by brook charr in New Zealand (Allibone and McDowall 1997); and has been

undertaken after brown trout invaded a population of G. eldoni when a ﬂood facilitated access of trout to upstream habitats that had previously been inaccessible to them.

Krueger and May (1991) argued that in the cool-temperate Southern Hemisphere, ‘‘the risk posed by new introductions to native faunas is too high and unpredictable to justify further introductions [and that] ... the era of widespread, intentional introductions of salmonids and other ﬁsh species as a ﬁshery management activity justiﬁably is drawing to a close’’. But, though their views on the question of risk are supported by history, one may wonder whether they were right about closure, about how soon that closure will come for salmonid ﬁshes in southern cooltemperate lands, and whether it will be soon enough for the survival or recovery of many threatened populations of galaxioid ﬁshes found in those lands. I somehow doubt it.

A ﬁnal summation

Viewed from the perspective of mostly narrative accounts of the status of galaxioid ﬁshes, presented above, and notwithstanding the obvious fact that much of the information presented is anecdotal and lacks the sort of scientiﬁc rigor that might be desirable in attempting to quantify the levels of conservation threat exhibited by the galaxioid ﬁshes and in seeking causal explanations (Fausch 1988), description of the status of the group as in a ‘biodiversity crisis’ seems appropriate—it is not ‘crying wolf’. Stiassny (1999) examined use of the word crisis and concluded that it is the only one appropriate to: ‘‘describe the current state of biological diversity in American freshwater systems’’, and in my view the same applies to the status of the galaxioid ﬁshes across their southern cool-temperate range. Overall, there has clearly been a strong tendency for many galaxioid species to be driven upstream (or exterminated) by invasion from downstream of their habitats by alien ﬁshes, most often brown trout, often rainbow trout, and occasionally other species, such as the brook charr, chinook salmon and the centrarchids largemouth and smallmouth bass. In most instances these species were introduced to sup-

port recreational angling, and virtually never with any prior, detailed assessment of the potential introductions of such predators. As a result it is certain that some, and likely that many, populations of native galaxioids were displaced by predation, competition, and various other feedback effects on the way that ecosystems function. In addition these effects would have caused genetic and ecological isolation of populations in small headwater streams, sometimes the galaxioids driven into sub-optimal living and foraging habitats, and exposed to all manner of stochastic ecological and genetic processes that have threatened their long-term survival. Most of these effects have, not surprisingly, been observed in Australia and New Zealand, as this is where the majority of the galaxioid ﬁshes are to be found. Nonetheless impacts have been serious in all other centres of occurrence of this group of ﬁsh.

Leidy and Moyle (1998) cited evidence that ‘‘intensively studied ﬁsh faunas have more threatened species than regions with poorly known faunas, an implication being that much of the loss of aquatic biodiversity is going unrecorded’’. This statement is relevant to the galaxioids, which must rank as relatively well known, but it does not alleviate concerns for their conservation. The risk is real and widespread, and is especially clear in Australia and New Zealand. That it is less clear in Patagonia and South Africa may result from lack of knowledge. The generality of the known/apparent threats to galaxioids from introduced salmonids, in particular, ought to alert conservation authorities to the types of risks present. It emphasises the need to clearly understand the taxonomies, geographical distributions, life histories and ecologies of galaxioids across the group’s broad range. And it is critical that taxonomic details of the various unresolved species complexes should be unravelled as urgently as possible to ensure that the entities/ lineages are well enough known to provide a sound basis for risk assessment and conservation management (‘‘informed understanding’’).

The introductions of salmonids widely, almost pervasively, across the ranges of the galaxioid ﬁshes, could well be regarded as ‘crying foul’—the

galaxioids have been exposed to widespread, intense, pervasive and prolonged inﬂuence of human-introduced exotic salmonid species translocated for largely recreational angling purposes, and generally with little (or often no) thought for the biodiversity or ecosystems consequences, as long ago predicted by Darwin (1859), and recognised by Thomson (1922) in New Zealand and Gray (1929) in Australia.

Of particular concern is the prevalence of introduction, maintenance and management of salmonids in national parks or other faunistic reserves in the various countries where galaxioid ﬁshes are endemic, though the apparent double standard of having introduced predatory ﬁshes present in the waters of national parks is not conﬁned to the cool-temperate Southern Hemisphere (Richter et al. 1997). The incessant, often little-considered, and frankly reckless, liberation of salmonids into pristine, cool to cold fresh waters has been a feature of the history of all the major, and some of the more minor southern lands from the mid 1800s, and it continues, today, as in an exceedingly remote, small island such Kerguelen (Davaine and Beall 1997). I suspect that the impacts are, in many instances, still emerging. Wilson (1995) reckoned that a matter of great concern to New Zealand anglers is the country’s Department of Conservation’s ‘‘statutory responsibility under the National Parks Act (1980) to, as far as possible, exterminate all introduced animals within National Parks’’. Aware that brown and rainbow trout were introduced, he recognised that ‘‘at least in theory we could see extinction campaigns of trout in some of our ﬁnest ﬁsheries’’, and he thought it ‘‘absurd that the premium trout ﬁsheries of the Tongariro, Urewera, Nelson Lakes and Fiordland National Parks are not acknowledged as valid recreational resources’’. He hoped that ‘‘surely no government would willingly take responsibility for such a decision’’. It seems to me that it is not quite as clear-cut as Wilson rather naively argues, there may be distinct conﬂicts of interest in such decisions, and further that anglers for introduced alien salmonid ﬁshes ought not always have unalienable preference to the production of the lakes and rivers of New Zealand’s national parks. Moreover, he exempliﬁes the typical angler

stance that generally gives little thought to conservation of endemic biota.

Furthermore, it is clearly both ‘shameful’ and a ‘crying shame’ that so many galaxioid species are under such serious threat. For most of them it is hopefully not too late, though assertive responses to the impacts of invasive species (primarily salmonids) are needed to prevent signiﬁcant future biodiversity losses. Though salmonids were mostly introduced into the ranges of galaxioid ﬁshes more than 100 years ago, it cannot be assumed that salmonids have attained their ﬁnal ranges in southern lands, nor that their impacts have reached some sort of equilibrium. Clearly salmonids are continuing to spread, partly by movement of marine anadromous life stages (especially in the Falkland Islands—McDowall et al. 2001, 2005), and partly as a consequence of continuing deliberate human translocations, some ofﬁcial and approved, others clandestine and illicit. It cannot be assumed, even where salmonids and galaxioids have coexisted for many decades, that the full gamut of adverse impacts of salmonids has already been felt by the galaxioids, partly because the impacts take time to become evident. Anecdotal evidence, at least in New Zealand, suggests that impacts are still developing, and may in some stream ﬁsh communities be expected to become more serious as time passes (as is especially true on the Falkland Islands). Richter et al.’s (1997) ‘‘quiet crisis...taking place beneath the surface of the world’s rivers and lakes’’ seems an appropriate description of the status of the galaxioid ﬁshes (again, a case of ‘under water—under valued’).

The impacts of exotic ﬁshes, primarily salmonids, on the galaxioids can thus be summarised as due to:

- 1. Direct predation;
- 2. Competition for space, especially optimal foraging sites, which may in turn lead to:
- 3. Displacement of native ﬁsh species into suboptimal feeding locations and/or modiﬁcation of the timing of feeding activity;
- 4. Conﬂicts and tradeoffs by native ﬁsh species between obtaining adequate food and avoiding predation, compromising survival, growth and reproductive output;

- 5. Feedback effects from the impacts of introduced species on the benthos, which can have cascading impacts on periphyton production, benthic insect behaviour and production, and ultimately production of food available for ﬁsh predators (McDowall 2003b; Townsend 2003); and
- 6. Less accessible foods, slower growth, lower size at maturity and lower fecundity.

Despite the long list of identiﬁed impacts on many galaxioid ﬁshes from exotic species, which are demonstrated from explicit interactions between the two groups of ﬁsh, there is a strong and serious possibility that where there do not appear to be negative interactions, this could simply be because spatial overlaps between the exotics and galaxioids have been eliminated by historic impacts—what may seem to be a lack of present impacts may rather be mutually exclusive distributions that result from past impacts. Identiﬁcation of scenarios of this sort requires an intensity of research that has hitherto not been undertaken (Pascual et al. 2002).

Disturbance may have a role in allowing/ facilitating invasion of galaxiid habitats by introduced species (Moyle 1986; Simberloff 1981; Fausch et al. 2001), but since many salmonid introductions are into already disturbed ecosystems, the observation that invasive species do best in disturbed environments may simply reﬂect the locations of most salmonid introductions. Disturbance may give introduced species an advantage by fragmenting or breaking down community interactions, and/or may create space for invasives to enter and/or reduce the ‘biotic resistance’ of the receiving ecosystem. Ramakrishnan and Vitousek (1989) thought that exotic species clearly alter ecosystems, perhaps facilitating invasion by other alien species. Invasion, itself, is of course a form of disturbance, and possibly the impacts of early introductions may pave the way for increasing abundance, dispersion, and greater ‘success’ of later invaders. Which ever of the above are true, conclusions about introductions into pristine habitats may be different from those about introductions into disturbed habitats. Impacts are likely in both.

Species with long evolutionary histories in close relationship to ecosystems disturbed by human cultural activities may have evolved adaptations that promote their invasiveness in newly settled lands. Loope and Mueller-Dombois (1989) argued that: ‘‘Disturbance has been such an important evolutionary force in continental situations that opportunistic species have evolved that are adapted to persistence, dispersal and colonization of unoccupied areas’’. While this seems intuitively probable, and brown trout could be an example, it seems unveriﬁed by rigorous study.

Thus, while disturbance may be a ‘‘novel experience’’ for some endemics, and therefore disadvantageous, it may not be novel for invading species, and Moller et al. (1993) cited trout, which they thought could be ‘‘taken as one example of an old world species that evolved through a period of increasing human impact on the landscape’’. Thus the pervasively pernicious effects of trout on galaxioids around the southern cooltemperate zone may be related in part to the differential ability of trout and galaxioids to cope with disturbance.

The conservation response and the problem of knowing

One of the key elements for galaxiid conservation, as for all conservation, relates simply to the problem of ‘knowing’—what Duncan and Lockwood (2001) called the ‘‘basic dilemma between the need to act and the need to know more’’. Just as there is never ‘enough’ time (Lundberg et al. 2000), there is rarely ‘enough’ information to make well supported, rational decisions, i.e. enough is rarely known to ensure that conservation management is always based on ‘informed understanding’. Decisions have to be made that are based on what is known, and we incrementally modify these decisions as knowledge improves. This is a serious problem for conservation globally, and is partly a question of cost and partly of will. For Australia, Wager and Jackson (1993) estimated that A$3.62 million was needed to identify and remedy knowledge shortcomings (quite apart from the costs of instituting mitigation). Should as much be known about galaxiids, as is known about the rhyncocephalian reptile

Sphenodon in New Zealand (a ‘living fossil’ and the only living member of its order—Daugherty

- 2004), or perhaps the lungﬁsh Neoceratodus in Australia (Merrick and Schmida 1984, and references therein), generating attitudes and establishing research and conservation programmes that lead to more informed and acceptable conservation of the galaxiids would be a lot easier. Changes in the conservation status of G. postvectis in New Zealand, with risk declining, relates entirely to growing knowledge of its distribution, abundance and ecology.

Attributing cause to the contemporary conservation/biodiversity crisis of the galaxioids, as with other ﬁsh groups, relates primarily to anthropogenic impacts, much of it related to human population growth. Human impacts (in no particular order) include:

- 1. Habitat loss through physical conversion of land and vegetation to new uses and resulting in loss of wetlands and streams;
- 2. Habitat degradation, partly due to (1) above, but also through pollution, especially deliberate, widespread use of waterways as point source ‘sinks’ and ‘removal systems’ for human and industrial wastes, and also the effects of elevated nutrient levels from nonpoint nutrient sources;
- 3. Habitat change that is driven by river impoundment, stream channelisation, water diversion and abstraction;
- 4. Overexploitation of ﬁsh populations; and
- 5. Translocation and introduction of nonindigenous/exotic species.

All of these impacts clearly apply at varying levels among the galaxioid ﬁshes. In their favour is the fact that southern cool-temperate lands tend to have relatively low human populations and relatively light industrial development (at a global scale). However, these features of southern lands also make them highly suitable for establishment of introduced salmonids. Thus these advantages are clearly counterbalanced by the widespread and pervasive introduction of predatory salmonids into these southern lands, as must be clear from this paper.

Many of the galaxiids are both aesthetically beautiful and ecologically fascinating, but they

tend to be little known to the general public, or even to committed naturalists and conservationists. They are hard to ‘ﬁnd’ and difﬁcult to ‘sell’ in an environment driven by economic growth and quick ﬁx. As Lloyd and Walker (1986) put it, ‘‘Many species are uniquely Australian but unknown to conservation authorities, and those without commercial value are often overlooked by the ﬁsheries authorities’’. Moreover, as Wager and Jackson (1993) stated. ‘‘In many cases detection of declining ﬁsh populations is accidental and anecdotal’’. Without continuous careful monitoring, no other state of affairs is really possible.

Leidy and Moyle (1998) listed a series of measures that they thought were imperative for ensuring the conservation of aquatic biodiversity, including freshwater ﬁshes. These may be summarised as follows:

- 1. Raising awareness of freshwater ﬁsh biodiversity issues amongst the local populace;
- 2. Management of watersheds as fundamental conservation units and managing them as watersheds;
- 3. Recognising the economic value of natural aquatic ecosystems;
- 4. Developing conservation strategies that recognise linkages between aquatic biodiversity, habitat complexity and water quality;
- 5. Increased research, inventory and monitoring;
- 6. Controlling impacts of aquaculture, especially the release/escape of cultured ﬁsh and associated aquatic organisms.
- 7. Curtailing introduction of non-native species through trade and travel, ballast water transport, and aquarium introductions; and
- 8. Having a long-term perspective on the sustainability of exploited ﬁsh stocks.

Few would argue with these points. Curiously, however, Leidy and Moyle (1998) did not mention the role in freshwater ﬁsh conservation/threat from deliberate translocation and stocking of sports ﬁsh, especially salmonids, and the need to control this.

If impacts of introduced species, and particularly salmonids, are as dire as they seem to be,

there need to be some explicit policy changes and actions to achieve protection of the indigenous biota, if the protection requirements of the International Convention on Biodiversity are to he adhered to and if the galaxioid ﬁshes are to receive the measure of protection that this would require. I believe they need and deserve this as part of global biodiversity, and as icons of the evolutionary history of southern cool-temperate lands. Persuading managers of recreational ﬁsheries (which, in the lands of the southern cooltemperate zone are based primarily on introduced salmonids), that there needs to be some limit on how widespread introduced game ﬁshes should be, so as to protect stocks of indigenous biodiversity, would appear to be one of the principal impediments to the conservation of galaxioid ﬁshes. In New Zealand, at least, there has long been a mentality that salmonids ‘belong’ in all of the country’s waters, as evidence by:

- 1. Just how widespread brown and rainbow trout are;
- 2. Moves made in the relatively recent past to remove indigenous anguillid eels from waters within a National Park in New Zealand to allow enhancement of stocks of introduced Atlantic salmon (Salmo salar) (McDowall 1990b, 1994a);
- 3. Retention and active management of introduced salmonid ﬁshes in National Park waters with the acceptance of the country’s Department of Conservation, despite legislation that requires the extermination of exotic biota within Park boundaries;
- 4. Despite this legislation, active release of introduced ﬁshes into virgin lakes within Park boundaries;
- 5. Not infrequent proposals (and sometimes unauthorised) actions by anglers to release salmonids into other virgin waters without statutory approval or consideration of potential impacts.

I suspect that similar attitudes and situations prevail elsewhere within the southern cool-temperate geographical range of the galaxioid ﬁshes in Australia (Raadik 1995; Cadwallader 1996),

Patagonia (Pascual et al. 2002), and South Africa (Cambray 2003a, b). There is clearly scope for elimination of salmonids in some waters without some jeopardy to valued recreational salmonid ﬁshes—especially where their presence is clearly threatening indigenous ﬁshes, and especially where salmonid introductions have not produced worthwhile ﬁsheries. This has had limited acceptance by New Zealand’s Fish and Game Councils (Johnson 2004).

It is crucial to acknowledge that a troublesome issue for galaxioid conservation relates to the existence of diadromy in a signiﬁcant number of species. For diadromy to persist there needs to be free access upstream and downstream from the sea, and where there is such access for galaxioids there is also, in principle, access for sea-migratory brown trout which have become widely established. The substantial differences in size of the upstream migratory life stages in galaxioids and brown trout makes construction of barriers to brown trout migration possible. For example, the installation of coarse boulder ramps at stream mouths can exclude trout, which are large, while permitting migration by galaxioids, which are mostly small, though there is a need to ensure that immigrating galaxioids are capable of penetrating upstream through the boulder ramp. Alternatively, screens could be inserted at stream mouths that are coarse enough to permit galaxioids to move past them but too ﬁne for trout. Implicit in the use of any such structures is a commitment to ensuring that they are maintained in a condition to be effective—and in areas where ﬂooding is prevalent, such a commitment can involve substantial effort.

Most fundamental, however, to ensuring the conservation of galaxioid ﬁshes is the development of attitudes in government and ﬁsheries agencies that include the need to protect galaxioids from salmonids, and to encourage such attitudes amongst anglers. In virtually all lands where galaxioids are present, introduced, invasive salmonid populations seem to engender greater general public concern than the indigenous galaxioids, and while this is so, ensuring protection of threatened galaxioid species is always going to be an up-hill struggle. It is not easy to balance non-commercial indigenous conservation values

against ﬁsheries interests such as those represented by recreational salmonid ﬁsheries, especially where such ﬁsheries support a commercial infrastructure.

As recently stated by Daugherty (2004): ‘‘Some day more people will understand that trout have replaced a whole native fauna in our waterways, just as rats have replaced tuatara [the endemic rhyncocephalian ‘living fossil’, Sphenodon] on the mainland [of New Zealand]. Will such knowledge lead us to create mainland aquatic islands where native galaxiid ﬁsh...thrive once more’’. He wondered whether naı¨ve [native] species can replace introduced ones...The answers to these questions are uncertain and opposing sides will doubtless be ﬁercely debated’’. He asked whether we should accept a ‘‘mainland devoid of the biological wonders of our past... [and suggested that] We may instead choose a future that better reﬂects the natural heritage of New Zealand. Conservation is thus not really about the past. Conservation is about imagining and then creating the future that we wish for our children and ourselves’’. The situation seems no different in Australia, Patagonia, the Falkland Islands, South Africa and New Caledonia.

Gurevitch and Padilla (2004) surmised that ‘‘available data supporting invasion as a cause of extinction are, in many cases, anecdotal, speculative and based upon limited observations’’, and no doubt there is truth in that statement, both generally, and as applied here to the galaxioid ﬁshes. They wished for ‘‘more critical synthesis and evaluation of the available data’’ and for ‘‘a more scientiﬁc, evidence-based approach to understanding the impact of invasive species on extinctions’’, for dealing with the present and future, and don’t we all? While this understanding will greatly assist improving the focus on processes of alien species’ impacts on native ecosystems, at the general faunistic or ﬂoristic level, the task is beyond us and we need to be constantly alert to the potential impacts of alien species and to understand this as well as we can with today’s knowledge. Clavero and Garcı´aBerthou (2004) responded to Gurevitch and Padilla with data that suggested substantial evidence for a role of alien species in the extinction

of North American ﬁsh, but perhaps more importantly, made a plea for recognition of the ecological and evolutionary impacts and, moreover, for the application of the precautionary principle.

A closing, cautionary tale

Several small children with nets and buckets were having fun, catching newly emerged juvenile trout in a small stream ﬂowing into a well-recognised New Zealand trout-ﬁshing lake. The local farmer came by, saw what was happening, and offered the children $1.00 for each trout they caught. He returned a little later by which time the enterprising youngsters had caught 30 small trout. The farmer duly paid them $30, as promised, and took the ﬁsh to a remote lake on his property where he released them. This entire series of actions was unlawful—unlawful to catch trout without an angling licence, to retain juvenile (under-sized) trout, to catch them with a net, to buy them or sell them, to be in possession of them without a permit, and to release them into a different water body without explicit approvals. This story, in a way, encapsulates the attitude towards salmonids and aquatic biodiversity, certainly in New Zealand and probably much more widely, where trout ﬁshing is such a passion. Though what the farmer did was against New Zealand law, and was also done quite possibly in ignorance of the law, it was also equally done in unawareness of the environmental/biodiversity implications. Had the farmer known of the legal and biodiversity implications of his actions, however, I strongly suspect that he might have done exactly the same.

Given such attitudes, as well as the very widespread distributions of salmonids that have resulted from their introduction around the southern cool-temperate regions of the earth, and their apparent impacts on the indigenous galaxioid ﬁshes in these lands, is it, then, any surprise, that the galaxioid ﬁshes are in a biodiversity crisis? It is a crying shame.
